Summary

Rio Vidal is a university professor who hates her dean, Agatha Harkness, for her authoritarian methods.

One night, at a bar, her friend shows her Tinder — and with a smirk, Rio stumbles across a very familiar photo.

What happens when a very drunk Rio accidentally likes her dean’s profile…

…and ends up on a date with her?

Notes

A few days ago, I finished writing this fic in my native language. It means a lot to me, and since it became pretty loved over there, I decided to try sharing it here too.

English isn’t my first language, so for now, I’ve translated just the first four chapters — as a little experiment.

Let me know if you’d like to read the rest — there are 34 chapters waiting 😉

Tinder and Other Academic Disasters

by marickman

https://archiveofourown.org/works/66865429

    Chapter 1: 1| Friday

    Rio sat in the university cafeteria, staring blankly into space, gloomily surveying the noisy crowd of students.

    Their laughter and chatter had blurred into one monotonous hum that was starting to give her a headache.

    She chewed a piece of raspberry gum, trying to extract some kind of entertainment from the dull routine of the day.

    Every bite reminded her of how much she used to love her job— and how now, she was drowning in apathy.

    Rio searched for something, anything, that might spark her interest: sharp minds, bright eyes, a whisper of intrigue.

    But all she saw were young minds consumed by trivialities, while her own thoughts drifted away into an endless ocean of sameness. This morning had been a disaster. A complete one.

    First, in a rush, she spilled hot coffee all over her favorite shirt — the brown stains soaking into the fabric like a curse on her day. She’d forgotten her breakfast, and by the time she remembered, it had gone cold. Trying to salvage a sad little omelet, she misprogrammed the microwave and ended up with something more akin to rubber than food. When she finally decided to do something with her hair and turned on the blow dryer, it gave a loud snap and sputtered out, making a noise like it was personally offended by her mood.

    Rio could feel irritation rising inside her. It was as if even the appliances had turned against her.

    Sitting in the cafeteria, she couldn’t shake the feeling that the day had started badly — and that nothing good was coming.

    Every chew of the gum reminded her of how she'd failed at even the simplest things.

    The plate in front of her still held the remains of her pathetic breakfast, and the stain on her shirt felt like a symbol of her entire morning. Around her, students were deep in laughter and weekend plans. But Rio felt like she was trapped in a time capsule — cut off from their vibrant, noisy world.

    Her gaze drifted over the crowd and landed on a pair of students discussing an upcoming exam.

    It stirred something in her — a flicker of memory from when she actually enjoyed teaching, when she shared knowledge with excitement instead of obligation.

    Rio sighed, trying to shake the heaviness settling in her chest. A nearby group of students caught her attention — they were animatedly discussing some kind of gambling game.

    Their voices buzzed with energy, the kind she hadn’t felt in herself for far too long.

    She watched them for a while, hoping their excitement might spark something in her.

    But instead, all she felt was a quiet ache — a longing for things she’d lost, for joys that used to make her life feel full.

    Bored.

    Folding her arms across her chest, she tried to hide the irritation and restlessness tightening in her shoulders.

    She blew a bubble with her gum, trying to distract herself, but right as her eyes caught a familiar silhouette—

    the gum popped. Loud.

    Agatha Harkness.

    Her dean.

    Of course.

    The disaster of a morning was just the warm-up — the real test was always a conversation with Agatha.

    And of course, the dean noticed her.

    “And here I thought this day couldn’t possibly get any worse,” Rio muttered, rolling her eyes.

    “Ah, Rio,” she said, stepping closer, letting her eyes sweep over the brunette. “It’s comforting to know I have that kind of effect on you. Though, judging by the way you look, I'd say the damage was already done before I even got involved.”

    Then again, I only tried to make an omelet this morning and nearly set the kitchen on fire.”

    “It would be honest,” Agatha replied, her mouth twitching into something like a smile.

    “Though I’d suggest focusing on your lectures.

    If you’re struggling with breakfast, your students may want to start praying.”

    Rio narrowed her eyes, but the corners of her mouth twitched upward. “Oh, how could I forget? There’s no room for personal tragedy in a professor’s life. Only flawless professionalism — and, of course, critiques from my favorite dean.”

    “Vidal,” Agatha sighed with mock regret, “if I were your favorite, you’d look a lot happier.”

    Rio smirked.

    “Happiness is overrated. But learning your art of ‘never losing control’ — now that’s something worth discussing.”

    “It’s not an art, Rio. It’s a habit,” Agatha replied, arching a brow. “Maybe it’s time you developed at least one useful habit yourself.” “Absolutely. New year, new me.

    Though I think I’ll need a subscription to your masterclass on perfect sarcasm and emotional unavailability.”

    “Rio, are you okay? You look like a wardrobe fell on you. Everything alright?”

    “Morning disasters. Nothing serious,” Rio said with a forced smile. “Sometimes you just need to pause and breathe. And maybe pay more attention to your kitchen… and your schedule,” Lilia offered gently. Agatha, never one to miss an opportunity, added smoothly: “And perhaps to yourself in general. Though judging by things, you might need to rewrite your entire list of priorities.”

    Rio snorted.

    “Thanks for the unsolicited wisdom, Agatha. Always right on time.” “I’m not here to say what you want to hear,” Agatha replied breezily, tossing a lock of hair over her shoulder.

    “Shame,” Rio said with a shrug. “Sometimes it’s nice to pretend you care about other people.”

    “I’m already performing a small miracle maintaining the balance between professionalism and your daily disasters.”

    “Don’t strain yourself.”

    “Good luck, Vidal,” Agatha said on her way out.

    “Try to survive until your next lecture — preferably without setting anything on fire.”

    “I couldn’t live in constant fear of making a mistake.”

    Chapter 2: Saturday

    Chapter Notes I swear, I got nervous with them while writing that evening scene 😅 Also — I just saw a TikTok where Kathryn says she’s obsessed with karaoke, and Aubrey’s like: absolutely not.

    The sound was soft but distinct—an alert from the dating app. A new notification had just appeared.

    She didn’t even know what outcome she was hoping for.

    Did she want clarity?

    Closure?

    Or was she just afraid of what the truth might be?

    Maybe she’d simply hoped for something — anything — that could make sense of that night, the one she still couldn’t stop replaying in her mind.     Every step, a mistake.

    Every word, a trap.

    Rio exhaled, ran a hand down her face, and finally turned to follow. She didn’t feel like smoking anymore.

    She picked up her pace, desperate to get outside. The university always felt especially hollow in the evenings — echoing footsteps, flickering lights, silence between the walls.

    She took the stairs slowly and pushed the door open, inhaling the cool evening air. The campus was nearly deserted — just a couple of students by the gate, smoking and chatting under the last traces of sunlight. Adjusting the strap on her shoulder, Rio paused on the sidewalk, suddenly unsure what the rush had been for. Yes, she wanted to go home — but even “home” felt vague and empty now. Lately, she kept slipping into strange, pointless reflections on her life.

    Agatha was still her dean. And yes, it was ridiculous — obsessively checking someone’s profile when there was nothing between them. Nothing real. Just one evening. One evening that had burrowed itself deep inside her thoughts and refused to leave.

    Rio let out a frustrated breath and headed toward the bus stop, doing her best not to glance back at the university windows. Nothing had changed. Agatha was still her poised, untouchable self. And Rio… Rio was just left stewing in her own responses — all these reflexes she couldn’t unlearn. She sat at the stop, arms wrapped around her bag, staring into the distance where car headlights carved lines through the growing dusk. Her eyes dropped to her sneakers, as if they could offer some kind of distraction. She tried not to think about Agatha. Or about how her life lately felt like the plot of a bad, overly dramatic TV show. The cold crept in through the thin fabric of her blazer, but she didn’t bother pulling the sleeves down. The low hum of an engine pulled her out of her thoughts. A sleek, expensive-looking Audi A7 pulled up slowly to the bus stop — dark windows, spotless surface, the kind of shine that spoke of careful upkeep. The passenger window slid down with a quiet whirr, and Rio nearly jumped when Agatha’s face appeared behind the glass.

    “Get in,” the dean said calmly — though her tone carried a distinct edge of command. “Unless you want to be fined for ruining the architectural integrity of this bus stop with your presence.”

    Rio raised an eyebrow, trying to muster something sarcastic, but instead, a strange warmth bloomed in her chest. Her fingers clenched unconsciously around her bag strap. She glanced around the empty street. Useless to resist. For a split second, she almost did — just to prove a point, just to assert some illusion of independence.

    “I’d rather ruin a bus stop than your car,” she muttered. “I can get home just fine on my own, Agatha.”

    Agatha sighed and waved a hand dismissively. “Rio, get in. Don’t make me step out and cause a scene.”

    There was something in her tone — soft but firm — that made Rio roll her eyes and rise from the bench. Her heart was pounding so loudly she was sure it could be heard through her coat. She walked over and slipped into the passenger seat, shutting the door with quiet precision. The interior was exactly what she expected — flawless. It smelled of something expensive and subtle, and the dashboard glowed with cold, elegant lights. Agatha glanced over at her, a small smile curling the corner of her lips.

    “Seatbelt.”

    “God, I hope you’re not planning to kidnap me,” Rio muttered, buckling up. “What would be the point? You’d surrender too quickly,” Agatha replied smoothly, pulling away from the curb.

    The car glided into motion, the engine humming evenly beneath them. For a while, neither of them spoke. Rio kept her eyes on the road, trying not to glance at Agatha — though she felt everything. Every movement, every glance, every subtle flick of her fingers on the steering wheel. “Where am I taking you?” Agatha asked, shifting gears.

    “Seriously? You don’t even know where I live?” Rio raised a brow, attempting a playful tone.

    “I could interrogate one of your students,” Agatha said. “Don’t make me. Just give me the address.”

    Rio snorted and, doing her best to hide her embarrassment, gave her neighborhood. Agatha nodded and smoothly turned onto the appropriate street. At a red light, she said, casually: “Look behind you.”

    “What? Why?” Rio narrowed her eyes.

    “Don’t be so suspicious. Just turn around and look at the back seat.” With a skeptical huff, Rio twisted in her seat. On the leather behind her sat a small box tied with a black ribbon. Her brow furrowed as she turned back to Agatha.

    “What is that?”

    “A gift,” Agatha replied without taking her eyes off the road. “I hope you’ll like it.”

    Rio slowly reached back, taking the box with both hands. For some reason, her heart started beating faster, fingers fumbling slightly as she undid the ribbon. The lid opened with a soft click, and she froze, staring down at what was inside.

    Inside, nestled against a velvet lining, lay a small dagger on a delicate chain. It was thin, elegant, with a handle etched in fine detail — so precise, so intricate, it was immediately clear the piece was expensive. It looked antique, and yet there was something bold about it. Something rebelliously refined.

    “Happy birthday, Rio,” Agatha said suddenly.

    Rio looked up at her, caught off guard. For a moment, words escaped her. “I…” She swallowed, feeling a ripple of goosebumps down her arms. “I really like it. It’s… unexpected.”

    “Good,” Agatha said with a short nod, a faint smile flickering at the corner of her lips. “I’m glad.”

    Rio glanced back at the dagger and ran a finger along the blade, feeling the cool metal. It was a strange gift — but a perfect one. Like Agatha herself — always a surprise, always balanced between poise and something dangerous that drew her in all the more.

    “Where did you even find something like this?” Rio asked, trying to sound casual, but her voice betrayed her.

    “That’s a secret. Don’t you like surprises?”

    “Usually? No. But this one…” Her eyes lingered on Agatha’s face. “This one’s different.”

    “Then I guessed right,” Agatha said softly, with just a hint of satisfaction in her voice.

    Rio looked at the dagger again, then smiled — the first genuine smile of her day.

    “So, how are you planning to celebrate?” Agatha’s tone was calm, but there was a subtle undercurrent of curiosity. She threw a quick glance at Rio as the car turned smoothly onto the right street.

    “Cry over soup,” Rio deadpanned, closing the box and resting it gently on her lap.

    “Sounds like an ideal evening,” Agatha replied dryly, the corners of her mouth twitching upward.

    Rio chuckled, trying not to show the warmth blooming quietly in her chest from that small, almost intimate exchange. She turned to the window, then shrugged.

    “Well, seriously — maybe go out with some friends on Saturday. Sit in a bar. Talk about how fast civilization’s collapsing.”

    “Very constructive,” Agatha said with a nod, suppressing a smile. Rio hesitated. Then, almost without thinking, she added quietly: “Do you want to come?”

    A charged silence filled the car. Agatha turned to look at her, surprised — clearly not expecting the invitation. For a brief second, something uncertain flickered in her gaze, before it was quickly buried under her usual composure.

    “I don’t think that’s a good idea,” Agatha replied, eyes fixed on the road again. “The world’s not ready for a catastrophe like us behaving normally in public.”

    Rio scoffed, rolling her eyes theatrically — though, in truth, she silently agreed. Agatha, as always, had a point. And yet, the refusal didn’t sting. It left no bitterness behind — just a faint trace of disappointment softened by the quiet warmth of having at least tried.

    The car turned onto a narrow street in the city center and slowed to a stop in front of a sleek four-story building. Its façade was all clean lines and soft lighting, with floor-to-ceiling windows and minimalist balconies enclosed in clear glass. A heavy glass door with a digital lock stood at the entrance, catching the glow of the streetlights.

    Agatha glanced at the building and said dryly, “Charming.” They sat in silence for a moment, both aware it was time to say goodbye. Rio sighed and reached for the door handle — then paused, her voice laced with a smirk: “So, did you already find yourself another weekend date on Tinder?” Without looking at her, Agatha let her fingers glide slowly over the steering wheel.

    “Maybe it’s time you finally ask for my phone number.”

    Rio froze, her hand still on the handle. She turned to Agatha slowly, narrowing her eyes.

    “Finally? As in, you offered it before and I just… missed it?” “I hinted,” Agatha replied evenly — but the curve of her mouth betrayed the tiniest flicker of smug amusement. “Let’s be honest, Rio. That’s not the kind of information you want slipping past you.”

    Without a word, Rio rummaged in her bag, pulled out a small notebook, and tore out a blank page. She held it out with a lopsided grin. “Go ahead. Dazzle me.”

    Agatha took the paper without comment and, with a smooth motion, drew a pen from the inside pocket of her blazer. She jotted down a number in neat script, folded the page carefully, and handed it back to Rio — her eyes gleaming with the quiet satisfaction of someone who never really doubts the outcome.

    “See you around, Rio.” She leaned back in her seat, watching her with a knowing smile, as if waiting for the inevitable response. Rio, cheeks warming despite herself, snatched the folded paper and pushed the door open.

    “Have a good evening, Agatha,” she said over her shoulder before stepping out and closing the door with a soft thud.

    As she climbed the stairs to the fourth floor, the slip of paper still clutched in her hand, she couldn’t shake the ridiculous feeling blooming in her chest — a mix of quiet delight and mild annoyance at just how warm one stupid moment could make her feel.

    Around nine in the evening, a sharp, confident knock sounded at Rio’s door. She sat up from the couch, frowning — it was a strange hour for visitors — and reluctantly went to open it.

    Standing on the threshold was Regina Mills, looking as if she’d just stepped off the cover of a magazine. She wore a flawless black dress that hugged her statuesque figure, her dark hair styled in loose, polished waves. Her gaze, as always, was piercing — straight to the bone. There was something about Regina’s elegance and restraint that always stirred mixed feelings in Rio: admiration, tinged with a bit of irritation.

    “Regina,” Rio arched an eyebrow and leaned against the doorframe, crossing her arms. “What a surprise.”

    “I’m the mayor. I’m allowed to drop by unannounced,” Regina replied smoothly, holding a large bag in one hand. “I came to brighten up your solitude. And,” she added pointedly, lifting a paper bag, “I brought a selection of teas for all emotional emergencies. Since you’ve sworn off alcohol after that… intense weekend — which, judging by your face, still hasn’t let you go.”

    Rio rolled her eyes but couldn’t help smiling.

    “Next time, try a text. I could’ve been... not dressed.”

    “Unlikely,” Regina snorted, stepping inside without waiting for an invitation. “Though I still say giving up apple cider is a crime.” “I’ll keep that in mind,” Rio muttered, closing the door. An hour later, Rio was sprawled on the couch, legs stretched out, eyes on the ceiling. Her voice was heavy with exhaustion: “...and that’s more or less how I’ve felt since that night.” Regina sat across from her like royalty on her throne, perched in an armchair with a steaming mug of tea. She sipped delicately, watching Rio with the kind of knowing calm that made it feel like she already knew every detail.

    “You’ve really outdone yourself,” Regina said at last. “The dean. With a kid. That’s a plot twist even I didn’t see coming.”

    Rio pushed herself up on one elbow and gave her a tired look. “Don’t start. I already know how… wrong it is.”

    “Who said it’s wrong?” Regina set the cup on the table, her brows slightly raised. “Look, if you genuinely didn’t want anything serious, I’d understand. But you can’t stop thinking about her. You’re spiraling in your own head, going in circles. Maybe try taking just one step toward her? Then you’ll know if it’s a mistake — or something more.”

    Rio went quiet, staring at her hands. Her fingers tightened around the edge of the blanket, like she was afraid to let herself take Regina’s words seriously.

    “It’s not that simple,” she finally whispered.

    “Nothing worth having ever is,” Regina replied. There was no judgment in her voice — only firm warmth and a kind of grounded wisdom. For a moment, silence settled between them, broken only by the sound of wind rustling outside the window.

    “You know,” Regina said suddenly, “you’re still here alone on your birthday. You want to know why?”

    Rio looked at her, confused.

    “Agatha gave you the green light today,” Regina said, a smile tugging at her lips. “So why aren’t you doing something about it?”

    Rio bit her lip, at a loss for words.

    “Give me your phone,” Regina continued. “Or at least check the address on Uber. If you don’t want to go alone, I’ll drive you.”

    “Regina, I’m not sure…”

    “Stop,” Mills said firmly, rising from the armchair and grabbing her bag like the conversation was over. “You either go, or you keep sitting here and drowning in your own thoughts. Let’s go.”

    Rio opened her mouth to protest, but the words stuck somewhere in her throat. A flutter of nerves rose in her chest, tightening her breath, blurring her thoughts. Everything was moving too fast. Regina was so confident, so relentless — as always. And Rio… Rio had been stuck for a long time somewhere between what she wanted and what she was afraid of. “Ten minutes,” she finally exhaled, raising her hands in surrender. Regina gave a knowing hum and sank back into the armchair. “I’m waiting. And don’t even think about sneaking into a bottle of cider — I know how you like to dodge reality.”

    “Don’t tempt me,” Rio muttered as she darted past her and disappeared into the bedroom.

    She shut the door and leaned her back against it. Her entire body was buzzing. Her hands were trembling so badly she could barely unzip the garment bag. Lately, even the simplest tasks — like picking out clothes — had turned into impossible choices. Everything felt wrong, inappropriate. Either too dressed up or far too casual.

    “It’s just a birthday,” she whispered to herself, staring into the closet. “Unofficially.”

    Oh, gods.

    Her eyes scanned rows of uninspiring, insecure-looking outfits. The situation was quickly becoming dire, and she was on the verge of spiraling — until she spotted a pair of jeans she hadn’t worn in ages. Wide-legged, soft-printed, and fitting her just right — like they were made for her. She paired them with a pale blue cropped top and a lightweight cardigan in the same tone. Altogether, it looked… simple, but stylish. Relaxed, but still special enough to step outside her apartment in.

    Spinning once in front of the mirror, Rio gave herself a once-over, adjusted the strap of her small crossbody bag, and — surprisingly — didn’t hate what she saw.

    “Well… that’ll do,” she sighed.

    On her way through the kitchen, she opened the fridge and spotted a single leftover cupcake — chocolate, the last one. She stared at it for a while like it held all the answers, then snatched it and added: “Happy birthday to me.”

    Digging through one of the drawers, she found an old candle — slightly bent, but still usable. She shoved it into her bag alongside the cupcake and felt her pulse kick up again. What if Agatha wasn’t even happy to see her? What if it was all just… stupid? Her hands trembled again, but before she could spiral any further, Regina’s voice echoed from the living room: “We’re not late for an airport transfer, but if you don’t hurry, I’ll change my mind!”

    “Coming!” Rio called back, tucking a lock of hair behind her ear and casting one last glance at herself in the mirror.

    She had no idea how Agatha would react to her showing up. What she would say. How she would look at her. It was their first real encounter after all those strange, scattered conversations, half-steps, and careful retreats. Maybe this was a mistake.

    But deep down, Rio knew — if she didn’t take the leap now, the regret would eat at her even more.

    “All right. Let’s go,” she whispered, closing the bedroom door behind her.

    Some time later, they pulled up to Agatha’s now-familiar building. The car rolled to a gentle stop by the curb, and Rio, sitting in the passenger seat, clutched her seatbelt so tightly her knuckles turned white. Everything inside her was shaking, and her heart pounded so loudly and distinctly she was convinced Regina could hear it.

    “I’m not entirely sure about this...” she murmured, her eyes fixed on the dark windows of the building.

    “Perfect,” Regina cut in, unbothered, already unfastening her belt. Her voice was firm, decisive—just as it always was. “That doesn’t matter in the slightest.”

    With a small, knowing smile, Regina leaned forward and, before Rio could get another word out, pushed the car door open. Cold air rushed in, sharp and immediate, dragging with it the blistering awareness that the moment of truth had arrived.

    “Go on,” Regina said softly, resting one hand on the steering wheel. “Good luck.”

    “You’re a terrible person,” Rio muttered under her breath. Regina gave a short laugh. “Which makes me an excellent friend.” She didn’t give Rio time for another ‘but.’ The car pulled away, smooth and silent, the headlights disappearing into the night within seconds. Left alone on the sidewalk, Rio exhaled—and immediately caught her breath again. The ringing quiet around her only made everything worse. Her hand slipped into her coat pocket, fingers finding the folded scrap of paper with Agatha’s number. Just a few tiny digits, and yet somehow, they weighed like a whole universe.

    “Shit,” she whispered, heart still pounding, and pulled out her phone. With trembling fingers, she dialed the number and held it to her ear. The first ring. The second. The third.

    “Come on…” she breathed, gripping the phone tighter. The wave of anxiety rose so high it briefly clenched her throat, cutting off air. Her eyes lifted to the building’s façade, as if staring at it might somehow help. And then—light. A lamp flicked on in a second-floor window, flooding the glass with a warm, soft glow.

    She froze. Her gaze locked onto that window as though it held something fated. Maybe it did.

    Each ring stretched endlessly. And then, finally, a calm voice—slightly husky, and maybe still touched by sleep—came through on the other end: “ Yes?”

    Rio closed her eyes, swallowed, and let out a quiet breath, trying to steady herself.

    Chapter 7: Friday

    Chapter Notes This is one of my favorite chapters.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The door opened, and Agatha Harkness appeared on the threshold. She looked nothing like the woman Rio was used to seeing at the university — where sharp suits and flawless hair were her daily armor. Now, she was almost domestic. And yet, even like this, it was impossible to look away from her.

    She wore a deep violet robe with wide sleeves, the soft fabric clinging gently to her figure. Her hair, usually immaculately styled, was slightly tousled — as if she'd just risen from bed. Without makeup, her face looked a touch softer, but her gaze still held that distant, glacial depth that so often unsettled people.

    She wrapped her arms around herself, clutching the robe tighter as if it could shield her from the sharp night air. For a moment, she just stood there, staring at Rio, who stood at the edge of the walkway. The flame of the candle on the small chocolate cupcake Rio held flickered in the dark, casting a warm glow across her face and lighting up her smile — sincere and almost provocatively so.

    “What are you doing here?” Agatha asked, her voice quiet and low, and somehow softer than the moment seemed to warrant. “Did something happen?”

    Rio gave a little shrug, her smile widening, her gaze turning bolder — as if to say she had no shame showing up here in the middle of the night. “Nothing happened,” she replied. “I just wanted to see you.” And just like that, she admitted it — not only to Agatha, but to herself. Agatha stepped forward, narrowing her eyes slightly as if trying to see Rio more clearly. Her movements were fluid, but there was tension in them. Her face showed something like confusion — quickly replaced by a more composed, stern expression, as if she’d reminded herself to keep a distance. “Rio, why are you here?” she asked again, this time firmer. Without breaking eye contact, Vidal stepped a little closer. The streetlight caught the edge of her jacket, and the candle on the cupcake trembled in the wind — but didn’t go out.

    “I don’t know,” she said with a small smile that was honest beneath its playfulness. “I just wanted to end the day with you.”

    Agatha didn’t respond immediately. She looked at Rio with an expression that was hard to read — a mix of disbelief, hesitation, maybe something else. Her fingers gripped the robe a little tighter, like she was bracing herself. But the silence stretched on too long, and eventually she broke it with a quiet sigh.

    “Rio…” she began, then paused, glancing at the cupcake. “That’s sweet. But we’re not seventeen,” she said finally, her voice carrying a quiet reproach, though not anger. “I have a child. I can’t just run outside in the middle of the night because you suddenly felt like showing up.” But Rio didn’t back down.

    She looked at Agatha, and her smile softened — though not her resolve. “I know,” she said simply, her voice warm but calm. “And I’m still here. Because I genuinely wanted to be with you tonight.”

    Agatha didn’t answer. This time her face gave nothing away. Only the faint breeze moved her robe, and the flickering candlelight cast shifting shadows across Rio’s face. It looked as though she was about to speak, but couldn’t bring herself to. Instead, she gave a small shake of her head and finally stepped back into the house.

    “Come in,” she said over her shoulder. Her voice was almost dry, but it was impossible to tell what emotion lay beneath it. “You’ll freeze out there.” The dean’s home was dim and quiet. A soft light from a wall sconce in the hallway painted the space in warm hues, casting long shadows and a kind of intimate stillness. Agatha walked ahead without turning, and Rio followed, gently closing the door behind her. Their footsteps echoed faintly through the silence.

    “Are you hungry?” Agatha asked suddenly, without stopping. Rio shook her head, caught slightly off guard. Her voice came out softer than she expected.

    “No. I brought cake.”

    Agatha glanced back at her, but said nothing. Her steps were steady, her movements precise. She made her way to the kitchen, where the light under the cabinets gave a quiet golden glow to the countertops and wood-paneled walls. She stopped at the island and placed both hands flat on its surface, leaning forward slightly. The light caught her profile, softening her features — though her gaze remained sharp, focused.

    “Looks like it’s time we finally had a conversation,” she said, evenly. Not harsh, but serious.

    Rio tensed slightly, biting the inside of her lip. A flicker of nerves raced through her like static electricity, and she instinctively ran her tongue over her lips, trying to find words.

    “What exactly are you expecting from this… from us, Rio?” Agatha asked. Her tone was gentler now, but still firm — as if testing the weight of truth. Rio exhaled, trying to ground herself.

    “I…” she began, then hesitated, her eyes dropping as though searching for the right words. “I’ve been thinking about that night. This whole time.” A flicker of surprise passed across Agatha’s face. Her brows lifted, and then she straightened slightly — as if trying to put the mask of composure back in place.

    “That night?” she repeated, her tone even — but her eyes revealed more than she likely intended.

    “Yes,” Rio said, meeting her gaze and holding it — even though it cost her something. “I’ve been thinking about you. And I… I can’t seem to shake it.”

    Agatha blinked. Her fingers shifted slightly on the counter. Her stare turned more guarded, but within it was something else — maybe uncertainty. Maybe something closer to fear.

    “I’m not a habit, Rio,” she said, her voice firm.

    “I didn’t mean it like that,” Rio replied quickly, stepping forward, rounding the edge of the island. Her voice trembled, but she fought to sound sure of herself.

    “Then what did you mean?” Agatha asked. Her tone softened again, but remained cautious.

    Rio stopped, looking directly at her. She knew this was not a moment to hide behind sarcasm or careless charm.

    “I meant… you’re not something I can just ignore.”

    Agatha raised her chin slightly, her posture subtly straighter. Her gaze remained critical, yet somewhere within it, something began to thaw. She folded her arms across her chest — a small, protective gesture — and in it, Rio saw something delicate, something quietly raw.

    “That’s better,” Agatha murmured, allowing herself the briefest of smiles. Then her voice shifted — serious again, calm, composed, with a steadiness that asked not to be interrupted. “Rio, let me be clear. I do have feelings for you. I always have. But what draws me to you… it’s also what keeps me away. You’re free. Impulsive. Unpredictable. And I have responsibilities. I can’t afford to take risks for something fleeting or unserious.” Rio listened without flinching, her expression tightening just a little, her shoulders dropping in quiet acknowledgment. She nodded. Fair. Agatha continued. Her voice didn’t harden — but it carried the weight of someone who’d repeated this reasoning more times than she cared to admit: “At the university, relationships between faculty are prohibited. And beyond that, I’m a mother. I have a son. I can’t disappear in the middle of the night for spontaneous drama or an unplanned movie date. My life isn’t a stage for impulse.”

    Rio exhaled slowly, letting the weight of Agatha’s words settle over her. She gave a small nod, searching for a response. When she finally spoke, her voice was quiet but steady.

    “That’s fair. And it’s right,” she said, pausing for a moment. “I can’t promise you a forever kind of love. All we had… was one evening. A really beautiful one.” Her gaze drifted, distant for a heartbeat, before returning to Agatha. “I’ve never had a serious relationship. I don’t know how to be with someone. To cook together, go grocery shopping, make plans… But I do know this much — I don’t want to hurt you. And I especially don’t want to hurt your career. Or your son.”

    She looked away for a breath, as if gathering her courage, then turned back to Agatha — steady, but with something tender and exposed flickering in her eyes.

    “I want to be as honest as you are,” she began again, her tone soft but grounded in sincerity. “You said you can’t afford to take a risk for something fleeting or unserious. And I agree with you.”

    She hesitated, then let a small smile curl her lips, softening what came next. “I don’t want just sex, Agatha.” Her eyes didn’t waver. Her smile widened a touch, teasing now — playfully disarming. “Although, to be fair, you’re ridiculously good at that.”

    The corners of Agatha’s mouth twitched — almost a smile. But she caught herself quickly, pressing her lips together, keeping her expression guarded. “I’m being serious,” Rio said, gently steering them back to where the words mattered most. “What happened between us — it wasn’t just a moment. It meant something. I can feel it. But I’ve never been in a relationship. I don’t know how to exist with someone — not just in bed, but out there in the world.” She faltered briefly, but steadied herself again. “And I’m scared of hurting you. Or worse… hurting Nicholas.”

    Agatha remained silent. Her eyes had sharpened, but there was no judgment in them. Only the tension of holding something back — as if she were still weighing every syllable.

    “I know how you see things now,” Rio went on, her voice trembling just enough to reveal how hard she was fighting to stay composed. “I understand you have commitments. Boundaries I need to respect. You’re right: we’re not seventeen. And I can’t just crash into your life like some damn hurricane and expect everything to fall into place.” She took a step back — a small, deliberate movement, like she was making room for reality to breathe.

    “Maybe I need time,” she said. Then, more quietly, with a flicker of resignation, “Time to figure out if I’m capable of this. Capable of not damaging you… or the life you’ve built.”

    Agatha didn’t move. Her lips were drawn in a tight line, her gaze intense — but there was something in her that softened, almost imperceptibly. “I appreciate your honesty, Rio,” she said at last. Her voice was low, almost rough, and beneath it lay an unmistakable weariness.

    Rio looked at her, and something in her chest ached at the sound of those words. They rang true — too true. She stepped forward, bridging the space between them with deliberate gentleness, until their bodies were only inches apart.

    Her eyes met Agatha’s — surprised, watchful — and held.

    “But, Agatha…”

    She didn’t say another word.

    Instead, she slowly reached up, fingertips brushing lightly against Agatha’s cheek — a touch so cautious, so reverent, it felt like a confession in itself. Then, with a soft inhale, Rio leaned in and kissed her.

    Not with hunger. Not with heat.

    But with tenderness so deep, so quietly honest, it stilled the moment around them.

    It wasn’t a kiss meant to change the future. It was one meant to honor the present.

    A silent vow — that even if this was all they ever had, it would be enough to remember.

    To keep.

    To ache for.

    There was no urgency in the kiss. No hunger, no attempt to possess. It was quiet, slow, filled with meaning — as if every second might be the last. And in that kiss, there was everything: her pain, her fear, her hope. She wrapped her arms around Agatha — gently, but firmly — like she was trying to memorize the shape of her body, the warmth of her skin, the rhythm of her heart against her own. Her face pressed into Agatha’s hair, and Rio inhaled deeply, letting the scent fill her lungs before she pulled back with a soft, bittersweet smile.

    “I should go,” she whispered. The words barely broke the air, like the rustling of leaves in stillness, and yet they filled the room with a tension so thick it became hard to breathe.

    Agatha didn’t answer at first. Her gaze drifted to the side, as though the answer might be waiting somewhere beyond the walls. When she finally spoke, her voice was hoarse with emotions held too tightly for too long. “Probably,” she said.

    Her fingers moved through Rio’s hair — light, tentative — as if trying to hold her for just a moment longer, and yet unwilling to make her stay. It was a gesture that said both goodbye and please, don’t go. Rio nodded. Her eyes lingered on Agatha for a final heartbeat, and then she stepped back, leaving behind a silence shaped exactly like her absence. She turned and walked toward the door, her footsteps echoing too loudly in a room that now felt cavernous with things unsaid. Agatha didn’t follow. She stayed where she was, watching her leave, unmoving.

    At the door, Rio paused. She looked back.

    Their eyes met across the room — Rio’s dark with pain, but also gratitude. Her face was calm, but in that stillness there was a quiet kind of resolve. “Thank you,” she said, her voice barely audible. “For being real.” She opened the door and slipped into the night, leaving Agatha standing alone, surrounded by the echo of her words.

    Chapter 8: Saturday

    Rio was sitting by the window at Starbucks, lazily stirring her long-cold cappuccino. The motion was automatic, stripped of intention. Her gaze, distant and slightly dazed, drifted over the street outside the glass, where life was pulsing along at its usual pace: someone rushing by with shopping bags, someone else pausing to talk to a friend, the muted hum of engines somewhere in the distance. Sunlight spilled into the café, casting shifting reflections across the tabletop in front of her—but Rio didn’t seem to notice.

    She leaned forward slightly, resting her chin in her palm. A few strands had slipped loose from her messy bun, falling across her face, but she didn’t brush them away. The simple gray T-shirt hung loosely on her narrow frame, and the beige leggings clung to her legs, softening her into something quietly undone. There was a kind of unintentional ease in the way she looked—as if she had stopped thinking about appearances altogether.

    Rio tried to focus on anything else: the scattered patches of light dancing across the table, the muffled chatter from the table beside her, the melting ice in an abandoned glass nearby. But her thoughts kept circling back— magnetized by something she couldn’t break away from—back to Agatha. How long had it been? A week? Two? She wasn’t sure. Time had thickened somehow, making it hard to understand how quickly their bond had shifted into... something else. When had it happened? When did the sharp, needling back-and-forth between them give way to real conversation? When did Agatha’s pointed, almost dismissive interest turn into something that felt like it mattered?

    Rio bit her lip, trying to stop the spiral—but it didn’t help. Her heart thudded in that strange, aching rhythm every time Agatha’s image flashed through her mind. That woman...

    Part of her still swelled with quiet joy. She could still hear Agatha’s voice— so steady, so certain, so free of hesitation. It was strange and a little disorienting to hear such openness from her. From her—a woman who always seemed like she was built of composure and control. But now Rio knew: this had been real for Agatha too. Not casual. Not a game. And yet... that certainty carried weight. Their conversation had shifted something. The lightness was gone. The rhythm they’d had—the teasing, the verbal sparring—it now felt like a relic of another time. Every new word from Agatha now came coated in caution, as though they’d both frozen at the edge of something undefined.

    Rio missed her. The version of Agatha she used to know. The one who would dryly mock her for a dumb remark or smile just barely, like a secret. But she missed the new version too. The one who had opened up— honestly, almost vulnerably. The one whose fingers had been hot against her skin, whose voice had trembled into whispers.

    Those memories clung to her body like heat. She knew she shouldn’t dwell on them, but every time she closed her eyes, she could smell Agatha’s skin again. See that look in her eyes. Agatha was a drug. She wanted more. And more. And more. And Rio knew—it was dangerous.

    She exhaled and set her cup down. Her fingers brushed her chin, lingering there for a second, as if she were waiting for something to click. She had never been in anything like this before. It scared her. It made her question everything.

    She thought back to the brief flings that had flickered in and out of her life like match strikes, leaving nothing but vague outlines behind. But with Agatha... this was different. There was something real in it. Something that felt larger. And Rio didn’t know what to do with that.

    Maybe that’s why she felt so lost right now. How do you move forward with someone like Agatha? What was this between them? Could it become something more? Her history with love was a graveyard of bright, short- lived things. None of them had ever grown roots.

    These thoughts pressed sharp and insistent at the edges of her mind, refusing to let go. But somewhere underneath, beneath the questions and confusion, a quiet desire was blooming—unexpected, almost childish in its urgency.

    She wanted to see her.

    Right now.

    Immediately.

    It was strange. Just a week ago, in this very same Starbucks, she’d heard a familiar voice behind her and almost ran. Back then, panic had surged through her at the thought of her little game being discovered. Anger too— as if she refused to admit how much those encounters were beginning to affect her. More than she wanted them to.

    But now… everything had changed.

    Rio wanted to see Agatha—this reserved, almost cold woman who clung so tightly to her sense of distance. She wanted to see that look again—how Agatha glanced at her over the rim of her glasses, how her fingers wrapped with perfect control around a coffee cup, how she smoothed back her sleek hair with just the barest shift of her hand.

    Rio sighed, remembering their conversation the night before. The night had been unbearable. Agatha’s words had spun in her head like shards—cutting, scattered, impossible to piece back together. Sleep didn’t come. She had tossed and turned in bed, caught somewhere between regret and the maddening wish to rewind time—to say something different. When she finally dozed off near dawn, it gave her no peace. Only the illusion of rest, not relief.

    When she woke up a few hours later, she felt like a stranger in her own skin.

    Everything around her seemed distant, unreal. Her thoughts moved sluggishly, as if trying to swim through fog.

    That daze had led her back to the lecture hall.

    Rushing between classes that morning, she’d left behind her notes. She hadn’t even checked the schedule—her brain too clouded for that kind of logic—and just went back to the room.

    She opened the door.

    And her heart stopped.

    Agatha was there.

    Standing by the whiteboard in her signature black suit, perfectly tailored to fit her figure. A crisp white shirt hugged her shoulders, and her hair was pulled back into a sleek ponytail.

    Rio watched her draw something on the board with a marker, every movement deliberate, clean.

    She looked entirely composed, as if the world didn’t exist outside her precise lines.

    For a second, Rio froze—afraid to disturb the careful symmetry of the scene.

    But the silence stretched too long.

    So she said softly, “Hey.”

    Agatha turned.

    “Hello, Rio,” she replied calmly, with the faintest smile. Her voice was even.

    But in that calm, Rio heard it—something worn, something tired. Turning back to the board, the dean resumed writing, as if she hadn’t been interrupted. But Rio noticed it—something in her eyes had shifted. Subtle, but there.

    She stepped toward the desk where her forgotten papers were still lying. “I left something this morning,” she said, the words catching slightly in her throat.

    “Something important?” Agatha glanced at her briefly, still writing. “Lecture materials,” Rio answered simply, gathering her things. Agatha paused mid-motion. The marker in her hand stopped cold. “Damn,” she muttered under her breath, inspecting the tip. It had run dry. Rio watched as she placed the marker down with a trace of annoyance and began searching for a spare.

    “I think the extras are just below,” Rio offered, moving closer to the shelf. Agatha stopped and looked at her. Her expression held more than just irritation at the malfunctioning marker—there was fatigue there. Longing, maybe. Rio couldn’t quite read it.

    She found a working marker quickly and handed it over.

    “Here.”

    Their fingers touched. Only for a second—but in that second, something passed between them.

    Something Rio couldn’t name.

    Her hand lingered just a moment too long. Her fingertips brushed lightly across Agatha’s skin, as if testing whether she’d pull away. But she didn’t.

    “Rio,” Agatha said quietly. Her voice barely above a whisper. Rio looked at her, feeling a strange surge of warmth and apprehension at once. Agatha didn’t break the gaze, but in her eyes, something fragile flickered.

    Something close to pain.

    And then—

    Footsteps in the corridor.

    The moment collapsed. Agatha drew back her hand, the mask slipping instantly back into place.

    She turned toward the board as if nothing had happened, but Rio saw the slight tremor in her shoulders.

    She stood there, holding the folder against her chest. Inside, everything was spiraling—questions, want, uncertainty.

    She took a step back, watching Agatha resume writing with her usual calm, clean precision.

    The same perfect posture. The same control.

    But Rio saw something now.

    Something had changed in her.

    And maybe it was her fault.

    Or her doing.

    The bar was in full swing. Rio sat at a tall round table with her friends, music pulsing around them while colored lights danced in the cut glass of their cocktails. She was a sight to behold: a black mini skirt adorned with metal chains highlighted her long, toned legs wrapped in fishnet tights with a butterfly pattern just above her right knee. A tight black top and a sheer, shimmering mesh blouse left just enough to the imagination, hugging the delicate shape of her figure. Her hair was swept up into a high bun, accentuating her refined features, and her bold makeup added a touch of drama—thick black eyeliner, deep raspberry lips. Rings sparkled on her fingers, and the sharp heels completed a look that was confident, a little provocative, and entirely her own.

    “To Rio!” Regina shouted, raising a shot glass with a grin that could only mean one thing: she was here to party.

    The shot glasses emptied in unison. Rio knocked hers back, grimacing at the harsh burn before quickly covering her mouth with her hand. “You’re losing your edge, babe,” Regina teased, bumping her shoulder. “Oh please, says the professional,” Rio shot back, though a reluctant smile played at her lips.

    Laughter rippled through the group. With the alcohol flowing and the music vibrating through their bones, the mood was pure freedom. For a moment, Rio felt weightless—detached from the heavy thoughts that had plagued her for days.

    Regina leaned in, lips almost brushing Rio’s ear to be heard over the music. “So? Gonna open my gift?”

    “God, I hope it’s a key to the city,” Rio smirked, squinting slightly as she turned her head toward her friend. “Nothing less will do. A little power wouldn’t hurt.”

    Regina laughed, clearly enjoying the banter.

    “Go on then, open it. Find out.”

    Rio reached for the small box wrapped in glittery paper. She paused for a beat, turning it over in her hands before ripping it open. “Ow!” she exclaimed suddenly, pulling her hand back.

    “What now?” Jen called out, amused.

    “Nearly broke a nail,” Rio grumbled, flashing her perfectly black manicure. “Regina, this better be worth it—or you officially owe me a key.” Under the wrapping was a small box. Rio eyed it, then rolled her eyes dramatically before turning to Regina.

    “You’re kidding me?” she said with mock outrage, holding it up for the table to see. “A vibrator?”

    “With remote control,” Regina answered smugly, taking a sip of her drink. “And what exactly do you plan to do with that?” Jen asked dryly, propping her elbow on the table and lifting a brow. “Considering you, Rio, ghost everyone who’s even remotely alive.”

    Rio leaned in, still holding the box, her tone suddenly thoughtful. “You know,” she began slowly, “I could give it to you. Might come in handy.”

    Jen burst out laughing and waved her off.

    “Oh, no thanks. I’m doing just fine. But you, sweetheart? You should be looking for keys to hearts—not to city halls.”

    Regina snorted into her drink, covering her mouth. Rio, accepting the challenge, leaned back in her chair, lifted her cocktail with exaggerated elegance, and glanced around the table.

    “Who knows,” she said, smiling slyly. “Maybe my keys are universal.” She raised her glass in a new toast. “To freedom of choice—and the full spectrum of possibilities.”

    An hour later, the night had unraveled into a chaotic yet enchanting whirlwind of laughter, stories, and the warm buzz of alcohol. The table felt like its own little universe, humming with the kind of unfiltered joy that comes only when everyone lets their guard down. Sharp jokes gave way to silly toasts, bursts of laughter drowned out even the loud music, and though most of the women barely knew each other, the mood was strangely intimate. Jen, Mari, Ginny, Regina, and Lilia — what tied them all together was Rio. And somehow, that one thread was enough to weave them into a perfectly mismatched, perfectly functional little crew.

    The first signs of deep intoxication had already crept into their faces: flushed cheeks, glossy eyes, looser limbs. Lilia — the oldest among them, with her oversized glasses constantly slipping down her nose — looked particularly charming in her tipsy form. She was staring at her phone with intense focus, her fingers clumsily swiping the screen until, suddenly, she looked up.

    “Girls, you won’t believe this!” she announced with exaggerated importance and a slightly slurred voice. “The dean liked all of my stories!” Jen, half-sprawled across the table and propping up her head with one hand, glanced lazily at Lilia.

    “Unthinkable,” she drawled, teasing. “You’ve officially outdone me. My best move is forwarding TikToks.”

    Lilia’s face turned serious, as if she’d just been challenged to a duel, and she lifted her chin.

    “I posted five stories today,” she said proudly, puffing out her chest. “So I’m basically an influencer now.”

    That declaration sent the whole table into another round of laughter, and even Lilia cracked, unable to maintain her faux-grandiosity. Rio smiled too—but just at the corner of her mouth. Her gaze drifted for a second, as if something had pulled her far away from the noise and brightness. The mention of the dean had conjured an image: Agatha. Still present in her thoughts, still lingering. That stern gaze, that nearly invisible smirk, the fleeting touch when their hands had brushed. It flashed through her memory, leaving behind a sharp heat that tingled along her skin. She didn’t even realize at first that the chair beside her—Regina’s—was no longer empty. A small shift caught her attention, followed by a voice—soft, but confident: “Hi.”

    Rio turned, meeting the eyes of a stranger. The woman sat angled slightly toward her, one elbow resting on the back of the chair. Her face was striking —high cheekbones, soft lips, and dark, intent eyes that seemed to study Rio with genuine interest. Curly dark hair framed her features, and a fitted black dress hugged her figure. Around her neck, a delicate gold chain shimmered with a tiny pendant.

    “Feeling lonely?” she asked, tilting her head flirtatiously. Rio gave a small smile and lifted her glass.

    “Just a little empty without a fresh drink,” she replied, aiming for lightness. The woman chuckled, gaze fixed on her.

    “Bella,” she said, offering a hand.

    “Rio,” she replied simply, reaching to meet it.

    “Rio…” Bella repeated, as if tasting it. “A beautiful name for a beautiful woman.”

    “Do you say that to everyone?” Rio quipped, leaning back just slightly, her eyes giving Bella a once-over.

    “Only to the ones who earn it,” Bella replied, narrowing her eyes and inching closer.

    Rio could feel the warmth radiating from her, and the faint scent of vanilla with something sharper—spicier. There was confidence in Bella’s presence, but not the forceful kind. It was inviting. Like she already knew Rio would want to stay.

    “Do you always open like this?” Rio asked, folding her arms across her chest.

    “Only with people who intrigue me,” Bella said, her voice dropping into something quieter, more velveted.

    Rio hesitated for a beat. Bella was magnetic—everything about her, from the tilt of her chin to the cadence of her words, pulled you in. It was the kind of night made for a fast, fleeting connection. Something easy. Something physical.

    But somewhere at the back of her mind, Agatha’s face emerged. Cool, reserved, impossible. That unreadable glint in her eye. And instead of whatever reply Bella was expecting, Rio surprised even herself. “Sorry,” she said gently. “Not tonight.”

    Bella blinked, clearly taken aback, but recovered with a soft, crooked smile. “Really?” she lifted an eyebrow. “Did I lose my touch?”

    “Tonight I just want to be alone,” Rio answered calmly, taking a sip from her glass.

    Bella leaned in slightly, her scent lingering in the air, then stood up, tracing the rim of her glass with one finger before casting one last look at Rio. “Maybe next time.”

    “Maybe,” Rio replied simply, watching her walk away.

    She let out a slow breath, raised her glass in a silent toast to herself, and allowed her thoughts to drift—once again—back to Agatha. The one she couldn’t seem to get out of her head.

    Rio barely managed to shut the apartment door behind her, slumping against it with a tired thud. Her fingers fumbled for the light switch more than once before she finally flicked it on, flooding the hallway with a soft, muted glow. The silence inside was piercing—taunting, almost—after the blaring chaos of the bar. She kicked off her heels with a sway, nearly collapsing, but caught herself on the wall. The shoes stayed wherever they landed.

    Her purse, along with a few shopping bags, hit the floor. “I’ll deal with it in the morning,” she thought, staggering vaguely in the direction of the bedroom. Her thoughts were scattered, blurred by the fog of alcohol, and all she could do was keep moving—imperfectly, but forward.

    In the bedroom, she sank onto the edge of the bed and let out a shaky breath. She reached for the waistband of her tights, trying to peel them off, but her fingers kept slipping, and it felt more complicated than it should’ve been. Eventually she managed to tear them halfway down—one leg still caught—and gave up. She let herself fall back, legs sprawled, arms flung wide open across the mattress.

    The ceiling spun. Her breathing was uneven, and flickering memories of the night danced through her mind: her friends’ laughter, the clink of shot glasses, the buzz of shared warmth and celebration. But slowly, those images began to dissolve, giving way to something else. Something that made her heart stutter… and then pound.

    Agatha.

    Rio clung to that image—first vague, like a shimmer in the dark, but soon sharpening into clarity. Her eyes came first—so deep, you could drown in them, and want to. Then her hands, long-fingered and graceful, always moving with that peculiar elegance. That cold, cutting gaze she cast at the world from a distance. And the other one—the one only for her. Warmer. Almost burning.

    “Perfect,” Rio whispered, barely audibly.

    She slid a palm slowly over her chest, squeezing gently, as if trying to feel the ache with her whole body. Her skin lit up under her own touch—but it was nothing compared to the fire building below. She imagined Agatha’s hands trailing over her, the ragged edge of her whisper against Rio’s ear, the way her name sounded when it passed those lips. Agatha… always composed, always cold, always untouchable. But not with her. Not behind closed doors.

    And that contradiction—God, it was driving her insane.

    She rolled onto her side, trying to shake off the vision—but it was impossible. Her thoughts had already captured her, and all she could do now was surrender. With effort, she pushed herself up to her knees and reached for the nightstand, slowly pulling open the drawer. Her movements were sluggish, almost lazy, like someone too tired to act but too restless to stop.

    Her mind was far from sober, but one name pulsed with every beat of her heart. And her fingers, as if drawn by instinct, found the vibrator. Oh, she needed this. If she couldn’t have the dean tonight, then at least this small, pulsing toy would do.

    Rio let out a sharp breath and bit her lower lip as she pushed her panties aside and brought the vibration directly to her clit.

    Too much. Too good and too painful at once. The alcohol, the ache, the vivid images of Agatha — it all melted together, stirring a fever under her skin.

    She furrowed her brows and let out a quiet moan, increasing the intensity with trembling fingers.

    God, how she wished she could trade this toy for Agatha’s tongue. To feel her fingers sliding inside, to see the woman savor her — teasing, controlling, stopping just before the edge, then starting again. No, Rio knew — Agatha would never allow an easy finish.

    She saw it all, down to the smallest detail.

    She imagined her own fingers winding into Agatha’s thick, soft hair, letting the strands slip obediently between her palms. Her hand tightened slightly, tugging, and Agatha would meet her gaze. That look — dark, dazed, heavy — thick not with wine, but with the intoxicating pull between them. Every movement, every flicker of shadow on Agatha’s face would slow, as if time itself froze to frame them at the center of the universe. Rio’s breathing deepened, ragged and uneven. Her head tipped back, exposing the delicate line of her throat. The rustle of fabric filled the silence as her back arched in one fluid motion. Her eyes shut tight, as if she could trap the moment inside her, hold it — pure, unbearably sweet. Then a sudden thought, sharp as a blade, cut through the haze. She hadn’t expected it to go this far. She hadn’t expected Agatha to become the one — the only woman Rio craved like this. So deeply it made her fingertips tremble and heat spread low in her belly.

    Control slipped through her fingers, dissolving in the whirl of sensation. There was nothing left in the world but this thick, shadowy warmth spun from fantasy, gasping breath, and the dizzy fog of a few drinks too many. Rio let herself sink into it. For the first time in a long while, she didn’t hide. Didn’t hold back. The orgasm took her off guard, crashing through her in a wave so raw it ripped a sound from her throat — loud, honest, feral. In that moment, she was nothing but a woman who had allowed herself to feel. And it was terrifyingly beautiful.

    Because the scales had already tipped.

    A few minutes later, the air in the room was still heavy — warm, thick with the ghost of pleasure — slowly cooling into something quieter. Rio lay on her back, her chest rising and falling in a slower rhythm now. Her eyelids fluttered before opening to the dim glow of streetlights seeping through the cracks in the curtains. The alcohol still clung to her thoughts, but clarity was returning — sharpened by what had just passed. She lifted a hand to her face, fingers brushing hot skin, the sweat at her temple. And then it hit her — the phone. Where was her phone? A flicker of annoyance passed across her face. The bag — she remembered — was still in the hallway. Right where this whole night had started. Reluctant but determined, Rio sat up, the sheet slipping from her bare shoulders. Her feet touched the cold floor, and she shivered. Her skirt, still not fully removed, had twisted around her waist. She adjusted it with a tug and stepped toward the door. With every step, her stride grew steadier, her mind clearer. The need — still smoldering under her skin — burned quietly now, like embers waiting for breath.

    The hallway greeted her with silence and half-light. Her bag sat on the chair against the wall. Rio grabbed it, rummaging quickly until her fingers found the cold surface of her phone. Got it.

    Back in her room, she closed the door behind her. The screen blinked to life, lighting her face. Her thumb scrolled through her contacts until it stopped on one name: Agatha Harkness.

    She paused. Her thumb hovered. What if she doesn't like this? What if she thinks it’s inappropriate? A drunk, late-night message? Should I wait until morning?

    But the voice of hesitation died quickly—drowned out by desire, raw and demanding. She had to do it.

    She began typing. No long text. No explanation.

    Just one word.

    And she hit send.

    The screen dimmed, and Rio brought the phone to her lips, smirking at her own boldness. In that moment, she was proud—of the heat in her blood, of the recklessness she didn’t regret.

    The phone dropped onto the bed, and she, brushing hair from her face, walked toward the bathroom.

    Warm water wrapped around her as she stepped into the shower, washing away the sticky sheen of sweat, the lingering traces of need. She closed her eyes, letting it run down her back and shoulders, down her spine. Her thoughts still spun — flashes of Agatha’s gaze, her voice, the possibilities that might unfold. But she chose not to race ahead. The consequences could wait until morning.

    Chapter 9: Sunday

    Chapter Notes I try to describe everything in as much detail as possible. I just really enjoy imagining not only the conversations between my favorite women, but also all those little daily routines Rio goes through. So yeah… sorry (but not really) 😅 See the end of the chapter for more notes Rio had woken up closer to noon and was already standing under the shower. A faint beam of sunlight filtered through the bathroom curtains, casting soft reflections on the tiled walls. Hot water streamed down her body, washing away the remnants of last night — the smell of alcohol, the echo of loud laughter, the gentle ache of fatigue. It was her ritual: to spend time beneath the stream, allowing herself to drift, to simply enjoy the warmth.

    But nothing grounded her quite like a cold shock. She inhaled deeply, held her breath, and turned the handle toward the blue mark. Ice-cold water poured over her skin, and her body froze for a second, then flinched, jolting her fully awake.

    Rio began counting slowly in her head: “One, two…” Freezing droplets ran down her face, shoulders, trickled over her stomach and thighs. By fifteen, she was almost used to the cold. At thirty, she turned the knob and shut off the water.

    She ran her palms down her face, wiping away the water, lingering at her temples. Inhale, exhale. A few seconds of silence. Then she reached for a soft white towel hanging on a hook.

    Moving slowly, Rio dried her face, then her shoulders, her chest, her back. The towel glided gently across her skin, soaking up the last drops of water. She caught her reflection in the large mirror opposite her. Her gaze lingered for a few moments. Sharp cheekbones, dark circles under her eyes, skin a shade paler than usual. She frowned. Over the past few months, she’d stopped taking care of herself. Alcohol, late nights, junk food — it was all written across her face. What used to be soft curves had slimmed down noticeably; her shoulders looked too narrow, and her flat stomach felt less like the result of fitness and more like a side effect of chaos and stress.

    “I should probably do something about this,” she thought, tossing the towel onto the rack.

    She pulled on a pair of black panties, then slipped into a white tank top — slightly snug, barely covering her hips. Barefoot, she padded across the bedroom floor and made her way to the kitchen.

    Flipping on the small TV on the kitchen shelf, she switched to a music channel. A familiar beat filled the room, and Rio, recognizing the rhythm, turned the volume up a notch. Her feet began to move to the music, her hips swaying lightly as she walked to the fridge.

    She opened the door and peeked inside. A shelf of vegetables, a few yogurt cups, leftover cheese. “Not exactly a feast,” she thought, but the hunger she suddenly felt was real and insistent.

    Her mood was surprisingly good. For the first time in a long while, she felt light — not from exhaustion, but something real. Something fresh and alive.

    She grabbed everything she could use for breakfast: eggs, tomatoes, a bit of ham. Tossing it all on the counter, she started slicing vegetables, softly singing the chorus and dancing in place. Her hands moved easily, confidently, the knife tapping against the board in sync with the rhythm, her body naturally falling into step.

    “The more days like this, the better,” Rio thought as she looked at the pan, where the eggs had just started to sizzle. Breakfast promised to be simple, yet at that exact moment, she felt truly alive.

    She got distracted from cooking when she remembered she'd left her phone in the bedroom. Absentmindedly, she set the knife down on the edge of the cutting board and headed toward the room. The phone was exactly where she’d left it — on the nightstand.

    By the time she returned to the kitchen, a strange smell hit her. The pan! Dropping the phone on the table, she rushed to turn off the stove and pulled the pan off the heat at the very last second. The food was saved — though the edges of the omelet had browned a bit more than she’d intended. “Thank God,” she muttered to herself, transferring the contents onto a plate. Then she grabbed a bottle of orange juice from the fridge, filled a glass, sat down at the table, and picked up her fork while the music still played in the background. She carefully brought the first bite of omelet to her mouth, closed her eyes, and smiled. The warm, tender flavor contrasted perfectly with the cool sweetness of the juice. It was, unexpectedly, just right. A few seconds later, she unlocked her phone and began checking her notifications. The feed was its usual mess — group chats, ads, news alerts. But one name made her stop cold. Agatha Harkness.

    Rio sucked in a breath too fast, nearly choking on her omelet. She started coughing, her throat burning from the rush. Taking a few sips of juice, she finally exhaled, clutching the glass like a lifeline.

    She shut her eyes, remembering how — drunk and reckless — she’d finally dared to message her first.

    A stupid, impulsive, absolutely thoughtless: “Hey.”

    And now, on the screen — a simple message: “Hey :)”

    The parenthesis. That small but meaningful little curve. Rio stared at the screen, blinking in surprise.

    Well. Maybe this wasn’t going to be a disaster after all. She took another sip of juice, feeling her mind clear as a sudden wave of excitement crept in. Her fingers hovered above the screen. Should she answer? Of course. Why not? She quickly typed: “Glad I didn’t wake you.”

    The reply came almost instantly, as if Agatha had been waiting. “Oh, don’t worry. I get it. Birthdays, alcohol... courage by the glass.” Rio let out a soft chuckle, smiling. Her fingers paused again. That flicker of boldness rose in her chest, and this time, she didn’t feel like hiding behind caution.

    “Well, apparently not even that could stop me from thinking about you.” She hit send and bit her lip, watching the message disappear into the void. The reply came just as fast.

    “I like that.”

    Rio held her breath, her heart slowing. She set the phone down on the table, fingers grazing the edge. It was stupid, really — such a short confession, and yet it had set something alight in her. She didn’t hesitate. “Really?”

    “Of course. It’s not every day you take a step toward me. Especially after shots.”

    Rio laughed aloud, picturing Agatha’s delivery — that perfectly honed sarcasm, the trademark half-smile. She was just about to reply when the next message made her freeze: “By the way, I’d be curious to see you uninhibited by alcohol again.” A low thrum stirred deep in her, pulling tight in her stomach. Her breath caught. It was a bold move — nearly too bold. A challenge wrapped in silk. She shut her eyes for a moment to regroup, then looked back at the screen, fingers flying.

    The answer came without hesitation.

    “Oh, Rio, I’d love to give you another extracurricular lesson.” She bit down harder on her lip. Heat pooled beneath her skin. Rio turned off the screen, set the phone on the table, and closed her eyes. She needed to process what had just happened. Agatha. Her messages. That direct, unfiltered flirtation — the kind that reached into parts of Rio she’d spent so long trying to bury.

    After their late-night call, everything had been left hanging. They hadn’t decided anything. And Rio herself wasn’t sure what she wanted. It was all too strange. Too uncertain.

    But one thing she knew: she didn’t want Agatha thinking she only saw her as a desire. It was more complicated than that. Much deeper. And, god, it was stronger than her.

    “I’m a bitch, I’m a lover, I’m a child, I’m a mother...” Rio belted out, shimmying a little in her seat.

    Agatha smiled — small, quiet, but unmistakably warm. Her eyes gleamed in the low light.

    “Well. Thank you for your midnight message. Though I have to say, a voice memo would've been better than—”

    “Forty seconds,” Rio cut in with a smirk.

    “Right. Even better.”

    Rio opened the door and stepped out, the cold air nipping at her skin. Before heading to the door, she glanced back over her shoulder. Agatha was still behind the wheel, watching her.

    Rio smiled — a warm, grateful smile, full of all the things she hadn’t quite said aloud. Then, as if reminding herself not to stay in the moment too long, she gave a small nod, turned around, and headed inside.

    Agatha sat for a few seconds longer, eyes fixed on the spot where Rio had disappeared. Then she sighed, started the car, and pulled slowly away into the night.

    Chapter 10: Tuesday

    Chapter Notes I loved how you all reacted to the word ‘sadder’, haha. I actually told that to my readers who’ve made it all the way to the end of the story — and they said, 'Oh, they have no idea how much they’ll be crying by the time it’s over.'

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Rio sat by the window of the university cafeteria, watching as the cool autumn light filtered through the frosted glass, flooding the room with a soft, diffused glow. In that light, her fingers — slowly tracing lazy circles with a fork across her plate — looked longer, more delicate. Almost elegant. Though she had never thought of her hands as beautiful. In front of her sat a plate of lasagna, still steaming with fragrant warmth, the scent of melted cheese and tomatoes mingling with the coffee aromas drifting from nearby tables. The smell was rich, enveloping — but Rio barely tasted anything. She brought another bite to her lips, but barely registered eating it. Something inside her still pulsed, something that made her heart beat with a quiet, nearly imperceptible echo.

    She felt strange. Light, almost giddy — but also slightly unmoored. As if the entire world had shifted ever so slightly, and the familiar now looked different.

    This lightness wasn’t shallow. No — it flowed deep, under her skin, in her chest, along her spine. Yesterday had been ordinary: lectures, students, a workout at the gym. She even ran more than she’d planned to, and had smiled at her reflection in the mirror, cheeks flushed pink from the exertion. It felt good. For the first time in ages, she wanted to do something — something other than just going through the motions.

    But now, sitting in the cafeteria, she understood: it wasn’t the run. It wasn’t the food. Or the coffee.

    It was her. Agatha.

The heat of their kisses, the slight trembling, the impatience in every movement — it still lingered on her skin. Her lips, she swore, still remembered the taste. As if Agatha had left a mark on them that couldn’t be erased.

    And it was a goddamn unsettling feeling.

    She wasn’t used to emotions like this. Normally, it was simpler: a spark of desire, a night of passion, a fleeting distraction — and by morning, she’d already forgotten. No attachments. No obsessions. But Agatha wasn’t simple. This woman rolled over her like a tide, wrapped around her, sank into her thoughts even when she wasn’t anywhere near.

    Rio inhaled deeply, forcing herself back into the moment. She reached for her water glass, took a small sip, and turned back toward the window. Outside, it was undeniably autumn. Students, wrapped in light scarves, hurried from one building to another, ducking from the sharp wind. Leaves spun through the air and settled onto the wet pavement. The chill in the landscape only sharpened the contrast to the heat that still simmered low inside her.

    And then there was the last message.

    “Be a good girl.”

    Fucking message.

It lingered — the way Agatha seemed to play with her, teasing, always just out of reach, but never really letting her go. As if they were both suspended in this magnetic pull neither of them could quite define, yet neither could resist.

Just a fleeting moment. One brief second. But it had been enough. Agatha had looked at her over the rim of her glasses, one brow arched, before returning to her papers like nothing had happened.

The kind of silence that comes before a storm, when the air grows heavy and electric, swollen with the sense that something irreversible is about to happen. Agatha didn’t rush to speak. She turned the tulip gently between her fingers, as if studying it, as if giving herself time to absorb everything that had just been said.

    Rio watched her every movement, feeling her heart still pounding out an anxious, uneven rhythm. She had just taken a step into the unknown—a terrifying step, yes, but one that felt undeniably right. Finally, Agatha looked up. Her pale eyes shimmered with a quiet, almost contemplative light.

    “You may doubt yourself, Rio,” she said, her voice low and a little hoarse, but not even a trace of mockery in it—only depth, “but the very fact that you’re thinking about all of this… that already makes you mature.” Rio tensed involuntarily.

    “I just…”

    But Agatha shook her head, gently cutting her off.

    “Listen to me.”

    She brushed her fingers along the tulip’s stem before continuing. “Experience, knowledge, knowing how to ‘do the right thing’—those aren’t things we’re born with. They come with time. No one knows how to be the perfect partner, how to belong to something greater than themselves, how to not be afraid of what they feel.”

    She leaned in slightly, their faces now just a few inches apart. “But the most important thing… you already have.”

    Rio pressed her lips together, trying not to look away.

    “And what’s that?”

    Agatha smiled faintly, a soft gleam in her eyes.

    “The willingness.”

    Rio blinked, caught off guard.

    “The willingness?”

    “The willingness to try. To be honest. To stay, even when it’s easier to run.” Agatha tilted her head slightly, her fingers gently brushing the edge of the tulip’s petals.

    “And if you have that… then everything else is just a matter of time.” The silence between them was velvet-thick.

    Rio tried to process what she’d just heard, but deep down, she already knew —Agatha was right.

    It wasn’t about perfection.

    It was about wanting.

    About the courage to step forward even when nothing is guaranteed, even when fear grips your chest, even when the path ahead is unclear. Rio swallowed, then slowly exhaled and met Agatha’s gaze. “So… you accept me?”

    Agatha looked at her long and hard, her gaze like a quiet storm—deep, piercing, steady.

    “I wouldn’t have taken the tulip… if I wasn’t sure of my answer.” Rio felt her heart stumble.

    Warmth spread through her chest—slow, tender, but piercing all the same. She allowed herself a smile—not cocky, not teasing, but real. “You have no idea what you’re getting yourself into, Agatha Harkness.” Agatha gave a short, amused laugh.

    “Oh, I think I have a pretty good idea.”

    She turned the tulip in her fingers again, then slowly offered it back to Rio. “But you know what?”

    Rio raised a brow.

    “What?”

    She hesitated, looking Agatha straight in the eye, then slowly—almost reverently—lifted her hands and cupped her face. The warmth and softness of her skin made Rio’s fingers instinctively press in, as if trying to memorize every contour.
