    She leaned in, their breaths mingling, and brushed a soft kiss on the corner of her lips. Then another. And another. As if tasting something forbidden— something she couldn’t stop wanting.

    “This all happened so fast,” she whispered, her lips grazing Agatha’s cheek. “Not long ago, I almost hated you.”

    Agatha smirked, her lashes fluttering before she arched a brow. The corners of her mouth lifted into a knowing smile, something sly flickering behind her eyes.

    “Almost?”

    Rio rolled her eyes and gently traced her fingers along Agatha’s cheekbones.

    “I’m not a psychopath. I don’t hate people that intensely.” “How charming,” Agatha drawled. “So you already wanted me back then, but you were too proud to admit it?”

    “No. I just wanted to push you down a flight of stairs.”

    “What a sweet, harmless little fantasy. You do realize what that means?” Rio tilted her head, her fingers lazily tracing a line from Agatha’s ear to her chin.

    “Enlighten me.”

    “You’re in denial.”

    “Ah. The obvious.”

    Agatha gave a slow, deliberate nod.

    “Exactly.”

    Rio narrowed her eyes slightly, her fingers drifting down to Agatha’s neck —light as air, but enough to make her catch her breath.

    “Maybe you’re the one in denial—thinking I couldn’t have actually hated you.”

    “It’s hard to hate someone you’re drawn to,” Agatha replied, her voice low and velvety. “That’s the cruel irony of it.”

    “Oh sure, now you’re a philosopher.”

    “And you’re the one who can’t sit on the same couch with me and have a proper conversation.”

    Rio raised a brow, then slowly slid lower and nipped at Agatha’s chin, playful and daring.

    “You realize that sounds like a challenge?”

    “Maybe because it is a challenge?” Agatha asked innocently, her hand gliding along Rio’s lower back—a subtle reminder that she knew exactly how tightly coiled Rio was inside.

    But before Rio could answer, a sound cut through the air. The ring of a phone.

    Rio froze, shut her eyes, and let out a groan of frustration. “Again?” she groaned, flopping backward.

    Agatha gave a slow smile, tilting her head, her hair slipping over her shoulder.

    “I think it’s our curse.”

    She stretched lazily, hand slipping between the couch cushions until she finally pulled out the phone. For a second, her eyes stayed on the screen, unreadable. Not a flicker of emotion. Like she’d put a mask back on. “Yes?” Her voice turned cool, formal.

    Rio sat nearby, watching her posture shift, watching the way her previously relaxed expression tightened into something distant. She couldn’t hear the voice on the other end, but Agatha’s responses were brief. “I understand. I’ll be there.”

    A pause.

    “If it’s truly urgent, I’ve got a few minutes.”

    Rio crossed her arms, openly annoyed.

    “Someone important?”

    Agatha lowered the phone, let her head fall back against the cushions, eyes closed. Then she pinched the bridge of her nose.

    She exhaled slowly and murmured: “It’s my wife.”

    Rio went still.

    One second. Two. Three.

    She blinked, as if to make sure she hadn’t misheard.

    “Your... what?”

    Chapter 13: Thursday

    How much do you need to know about a person to start hating them? Step one — be Agatha’s wife.

    That’s enough.

    Rio lay in bed, wrapped in soft sheets still warm from her body. But the comfort of this room was a lie. Beneath her ribcage, where her certainty used to live, a slow, creeping cold was spreading. She stared at her phone screen, its dim glow casting sharp shadows across her face. Her fingers kept scrolling down, over and over again, as if the motion itself could deliver answers. As if, if she just kept going long enough, this feeling — strange, thick, unpleasant — would dissolve on its own.

    She found the page almost immediately.

    Emma Swan.

    It cut into her gaze like a blade, echoed heavy and hollow in her skull. Like the sound of a door closing. Something familiar, half-forgotten. Rio wasn’t even sure what exactly she had expected. She hadn’t been searching, not really. Right?

    She was just checking. That’s what she told herself.

    Checking. She wanted to know. She wanted to see. She wanted to make sure she wasn’t imagining things — that the sick knot twisting in her gut had a reason.

    But hell, who was she trying to fool?

    She wanted to understand.

    Who was she?

    What was she like?

    What had she done to earn a place in Agatha’s life?

    There were only two photos on the profile. Two goddamn photos — and that was enough to send something inside Rio spinning, folding in on itself, rebelling.

    The first one.

    She recognized the place immediately — the playground near the park. She and her colleagues sometimes passed it on their way to the university. And there, on the wooden climbing frame, under the pale autumn sun, sat Nicholas. Still a kid, his arms wrapped around his knees, a faint furrow between his brows. He looked like he was just about to say something but held it back, stubbornly quiet.

    And next to him, sitting just a little lower, was her.

    Emma.

    The sunlight hit her hair, turning it into molten gold. Her jawline was sharp, clean, her lips touched by the barest trace of a smile — soft, unbothered. Her eyes were half-closed, as if lost in thought, breathing in the crisp fall air. A fucking perfect shot. A woman out of a movie. In a red leather jacket, ripped jeans, hair tousled just enough to make her look effortlessly magnetic.

    Rio clenched her jaw.

    She knew women like that.

    The kind of women who never try to be liked — they just are. The kind who fill a room just by being in it, even in silence. The ones whose voices carry power without pressure, confidence without arrogance, openness without the need to explain or defend.

    The kind who linger.

    Rio flicked to the next image abruptly, as if swatting away the thought. But the second photo was worse.

    Because Agatha was in it.

    And that photo made the ground beneath her tilt.

    A porch, probably belonging to some house deep in the woods. Lit by soft yellow lights, glowing gently against the coming dark. And at the heart of it — Agatha, sitting in a rocking chair. Nicholas curled into her chest, his small hands clutching at her waist. In her hands, a large, worn-out storybook. She was reading to him.

    Rio could almost hear it.

    That low, slightly raspy voice of Agatha’s, the slow, deliberate way she spoke, as if every word carried weight. That uncanny ability she had — to sound as though her words were carved into stone, and yet somehow wrap themselves around you like velvet.

    Fuck.

    She had never seen Agatha like this.

    So… at home.

    So relaxed. So natural.

    No blazer, no tailored pants, no heels. Just a soft cardigan, simple jeans, her hair twisted up in a messy bun, a few loose strands brushing her neck. She looked warm.

    She looked like family.

    Rio felt her throat go dry.

    She suddenly realized that Agatha had never mentioned Emma. Not once. Not even a hint.

    She knew about Nicholas. About the university. About Agatha’s habits. Her occasionally infuriating confidence, her weakness for expensive things, her sharp, precise opinions.

    But a wife?

    Ex?

    Current?

    Who was she?

    Rio leaned back, staring up at the ceiling.

    She felt like a stranger in this story. Like she had stepped into a book already written, all the chapters laid out, all the characters in place. And she wasn’t in it.

    There was no space for her. No role to play.

    Because in that world, there was already Emma.

    And that was something Rio couldn’t quite grasp.

    Agatha was always stingy with details. She rationed out information like oxygen, revealing only what she wanted, when she wanted.

    And Rio...

    Rio didn’t like that feeling.

    She didn’t like realizing there were things she didn’t know. Didn’t like the way it made her feel like Agatha was hiding something. And, worst of all, she didn’t like how that photo made her feel— Replaceable.

    She wasn’t supposed to feel that way.

    But the feeling had already lodged inside her, spreading with a hot, brittle burn. It pulsed, throbbed, scratched at her chest with sharp edges. What was it?

    Rio sat up suddenly, clutching the phone as if its chill could put out the fire rising inside her. Jealousy? No, that would be too easy. Possessiveness? Maybe. Fear? Unlikely. She’d never been afraid of competition, and she wasn’t one to shy away from someone’s past.

    But this past—

    It felt too solid, too tangible.

    As if it had already taken all the space…

    And left none for her.

    God, how stupid this was.

    She shouldn’t be thinking about it. Shouldn’t be letting that feeling sink its teeth into her, leaving behind a bitter aftertaste. But it was already lodged under her skin, sharp and itching, scraping at her from the inside out, making it impossible to breathe evenly.

    Rio looked at the screen again.

    At that photo — where everything looked far too perfect.

    At Nicholas, pressed close against Agatha, his small arms wrapped around her in absolute trust.

    At Emma, who looked like someone who never lost.

    And then it hit her.

    They were alike.

    She and Emma.

    But only on the surface.

    Both magnetic, confident, with that same look that said, “I don’t belong to anyone.” Charming, a bit sharp-edged, effortlessly composed. The kind of women who couldn’t be ignored, who couldn’t be broken.

    But deeper down…

    Deeper down, they were different.

    Emma looked unshakable. Like a mountain. The kind who stayed when everything else fell apart. The kind who didn’t leave. In her posture, in the set of her eyes — it was clear she was someone who fought to the end. And Rio?

    Rio could walk away.

    She could say she didn’t care.

    She could prove she needed no one.

    But…

    She couldn’t not feel.

    And that—

    That threw her off balance.

    She ran her tongue over her lips, tasting dryness there. Inside, something twisted, something sharp. Like she’d just stepped across an invisible line she couldn’t come back from.

    The phone still rested heavy and warm in her hand.

    She should’ve closed the photo.

    Wiped it from her mind.

    Just let it go.

    But her fingers trembled — and before she could stop herself… She opened her messages.

    She opened the thread with Agatha.

    The screen lit up with the last message. Simple. Concise. "I'll be there soon."

    Rio exhaled.

    For a moment, her finger hovered over the keyboard.

    Ask? Or swallow it down, shove the feeling deeper, pretend like she didn’t care?

    She hated self-doubt.

    But she hated silence even more.

    "How are you?"

    The message sent.

    It shouldn’t have mattered.

    But that anxious, sticky feeling had already settled beneath her ribs, rising in waves like a tide before the storm. It squeezed her chest, refusing to let her breathe easy, refusing to let her just let it go and forget. Two simple, neutral, nearly harmless words.

    She could’ve asked something else.

    "Are you still there?"

    Or "Are you coming back?"

    Or hell, "Why am I alone right now?"

    But she didn’t say any of that.

    Just "How are you?"

    Enough for Agatha to know she was waiting.

    But not enough to look weak.

    Rio exhaled again. Slowly. Deeply. As if trying to push every last trace of that maddening, unbearable unsaidness out of herself. She locked the screen, tossed the phone onto the pillow beside her, and went still, watching how the dim streetlamp light fractured across the fabric. Her fingers curled into the sheet, as if trying to hold onto something— anything.

    She rolled onto her stomach, buried her face in the pillow—but it hardly helped. Almost immediately, she turned her head again, squinting toward the window.

    The faint glow from the streetlamps bled through the glass, painting long, blurred shadows across the floor. Autumn had already begun to announce itself—nights colder, the air thicker, the sky deeper, filled with velvet-dark silence. Somewhere in the distance, a plane passed overhead, its red lights blinking once, then vanishing into the void.

    And god, it irritated her.

    Everything irritated her.

    Herself.

    Agatha.

    This evening that ended all wrong.

    That damn phone call that shattered it all like a glass ornament, scattering shards of their night across the floor.

    She hadn’t expected it to sting this much.

    That everything —

    their conversation, those glances, their kisses, that almost tender note in Agatha’s voice when her fingers brushed over her skin —

    could vanish so quickly.

    That someone else could step into her place so easily.

    Rio clenched her fists, feeling irritation morph. Shift. Slowly, hungrily, it filled the hollows inside her, transforming into a sharp, needling sense of loss.

    But what exactly was she losing?

    The problem wasn’t Emma.

    Not just Emma.

    The problem was them.

    Their inability to find time.

    Their reluctance to do something real about this.

    Their eternal caution, which now seemed almost laughable. Agatha was a mother.

    Agatha was a dean.

    She had work, responsibilities, a schedule crammed to the edge. And Rio?

    Rio waited.

    Just waited.

    But for what?

    She hated waiting.

    She had never been the one who waits.

    An hour ago, she’d been straddling Agatha.

    Looking into her eyes.

    Confessing.

    Unfolding.

    And now she was alone.

    In an empty room.

    In her own bed.

    With a phone screen that said nothing.

    Fuck.

    It was awful.

    It was stupid.

    It was wrong.

    She didn’t want this.

    She didn’t want to feel this damned emptiness.

    She didn’t want it to all collapse again into endless waiting. She didn’t want to be this grown-up, patient, restrained version of herself. She wasn’t someone who stayed quiet.

    She wasn’t someone who held back.

    Rio hated this bitter taste of something new — foreign, unfamiliar patience settling on her tongue.

    Hated that she didn’t throw her phone against the wall.

    That she didn’t demand an explanation.

    Didn’t start a scene.

    Instead, she typed a message.

    And waited.

    As if that would change anything.

    Rio clenched her jaw, staring out the window.

    She would’ve said something before.

    She would’ve started a fight.

    She would’ve just walked out.

    But now…

    Now, it wasn’t that simple.

    She shut her eyes.

    Took a deep breath. Held it.

    And let it go.

    Her chest fell heavily, but it didn’t bring relief.

    Everything inside her tightened — a knot of frustration, dissatisfaction, and something else.

    Something she didn’t want to name.

    It wasn’t supposed to feel like this.

    Wasn’t supposed to press so hard, unravel her like this, leave her in this unbearable vacuum of things unsaid.

    The problem wasn’t that Agatha had left.

    And not even that Rio was alone.

    Not the silence either — the kind that felt like an abandoned cathedral, windows shattered, each step echoing endlessly without ever filling the space.

    The problem was what it all meant.

    What it felt like.

    Rio hated this feeling.

    The feeling of not being chosen.

    Of being an option, not a decision.

    Of being what’s left for later, when all the real things have been sorted. She had always hated being the second choice.

    Fuck.

    She snapped her eyes open, exhaled harshly through gritted teeth as if she could spit the resentment out of her, and reached for the nightstand. Too many thoughts.

    Too much anger.

    Too much of everything that couldn’t be shut off like a phone screen. She yanked the drawer open, her fingers closing around the smooth body of her vibrator — familiar, small, light, like an extension of herself. It slipped into her hand with that same ease that usually sparked anticipation deep in her belly.

    Click.

    The room filled with a soft, steady hum.

    She shut her eyes and slowly, out of habit, slid her hand down beneath the waistband of her underwear.

    Her skin burned.

    Her breath was uneven.

    But…

    Nothing.

    Rio moved slowly, like always. She knew how she was supposed to feel in moments like this. She knew what her body was supposed to do. But she didn’t feel it.

    She let the vibrator dip lower, holding her breath, searching for a rhythm, trying to shut off her thoughts and focus only on sensation. But…

    Silence.

    Thick, dense, vacuum-like silence inside her.

    Instead of the usual heat — tension.

    Instead of that soft, tingling spark beneath the skin — irritation. Like static electricity that only shocks and aggravates but never catches fire. Fuck.

    She clenched her teeth and tried again.

    Harder.

    Rougher.

    No tenderness this time.

    But…

    Nausea.

    It rose from somewhere deep, spreading like a sticky, suffocating wave. Dull.

    Twisting.

    This wasn’t how it was supposed to be.

    This was wrong.

    Not like this.

    Not this.

    She didn’t want it.

    She didn’t want to feel like this.

    She didn’t want to feel… herself.

    She jerked, yanking the vibrator from her underwear and hurling it to the far side of the bed with enough force to guarantee it wouldn’t just bounce back.

    A dull thud.

    Silence.

    And then…

    Rio covered her face with her hands.

    Her fingers clawed into her hair, her nails dug into her temples, her chest heaving with jagged breaths.

    She inhaled again.

    Held it.

    But nothing disappeared.

    The pain didn’t dissolve.

    The frustration didn’t fade.

    There was only this.

    The emptiness.

    “Fuck!”

    The scream ripped from her throat suddenly, as if something had finally snapped inside her.

    Sharp. Loud. Almost painful.

    As if every string pulled too tight had snapped at once.

    As if she had shattered into a thousand pieces.

    Rio hated this feeling.

    She hated herself in that moment.

    She hated that she couldn’t distract herself.

    Hated that she couldn’t just let go.

    That she couldn’t just pull herself together.

    She groaned into the pillow, muffled and raw, as if trying to bury herself away — even from herself.

    But it didn’t help.

    Her throat tightened so hard it felt like breathing had become physically impossible.

    Her heart pounded like a broken metronome, erratic and desperate. And through it all, one thought pulsed, over and over in her skull: Why the hell do I have to feel like this because of her?

    Rio didn’t wake up to the gentle, muted light slipping past the curtains. Not to peace. She woke to the brutal shriek of her alarm — merciless and sharp, vibrating on the nightstand like it was punishing her for surviving the night. The sound was uneven, intrusive, tearing her out of an already fractured sleep and leaving only irritation in its place. She didn’t move at first, as if stillness could undo the morning, dissolve it back into something softer, something she could bear.

    Her fingers eventually found the cold edge of her phone, and with a single blunt swipe across the screen, silence fell again. One long, uneven breath escaped her lips. She didn’t sit up. Her face remained half-buried in the pillow, hair strewn messily across her cheek, shielding her eyes. Her head throbbed. A dull, persistent pulse behind her temples, heavy and unrelenting. Something sticky and slow — frustration, maybe — crept beneath her skin like a second, ill-fitting layer she couldn’t peel off. She had barely slept. The night dragged on in fragments — pockets of silence that didn’t soothe but instead sharpened every raw edge inside her. Each time she stirred awake, her hand reached for the phone on instinct, unlocking the screen like muscle memory. And each time… nothing. No message. No word. Not a single reminder that someone — anyone — gave a damn.

    She couldn’t decide what stung more — the silence itself, or the fact that it mattered so much to her.

    A sharp breath tore through her chest, hot with anger. It bubbled up in her ribs, wrapped tight around her spine. Her fingers clutched the sheets until her knuckles whitened. Then she pushed herself out of bed, tearing off the blanket like it could erase the weight of the night with it. Enough. That was enough.

    The kitchen met her with the kind of silence that pressed down instead of offering peace. It felt like a room that hadn’t been opened in days. Stale. Hollow. She walked to the window, eyes dragging slowly across the street. The city was already breathing — chaotic, unbothered. Cars moved in lazy aggression. A woman rushed somewhere in heels. A man walked his dog with coffee in hand. The air outside looked crisp and clean, lit with that dry autumn light that made the gold leaves glitter across the pavement like forgotten promises.

    She opened the cupboard, pulled out a glass, and filled it with cold water. Each sip was slow, deliberate, as if the chill might bring her back into focus, piece her together molecule by molecule. The water slid down her throat in an icy wave — sharp, clean — but it didn’t soothe anything. And then her eyes landed on the table.

    Right in the center of the otherwise spotless surface stood a paper bag. Chinese takeout.

    Rio froze, her jaw tightening on instinct.

    That takeout.

    The one that had arrived at the exact moment Agatha opened the door to leave. A cruel little punchline from the universe. A symbol so absurdly on the nose it could’ve been a joke — if it didn’t sting like hell. As if the damn paper bag had become the physical embodiment of everything left unsaid between them.

    As if it existed solely to remind her: Agatha walked out. No explanations. No closure. Not even the courtesy of a proper goodbye with enough weight to hold onto.

    Rio snatched it off the table without even checking what was inside. Screw the food.

    With one dry, irritated motion, she dumped it into the trash. Slammed the lid shut like she could seal away the memory with it. Like a click of plastic could erase what clung to her ribs and pressed against her chest. But it didn’t help.

    If anything, it made the ache worse.

    Because it was never about the food.

    It was about being left. Again.

    Rio clenched her fists so tightly that her nails dug painfully into her palms. Then, without hesitation, she turned on her heel and stormed into the bathroom. There was no way she was starting the day like this. No way she was letting herself spiral, trapped in the same endless loop of thoughts — the kind that stuck in your head like a warped record. She wasn’t that kind of person. She didn’t wait around. But fuck, she couldn’t get any of this out of her system.

    Cold water slammed against her skin, sharp and merciless. It streamed down her shoulders, her back, biting into her body with the sting of needles. But even that shock — that brutal contrast — couldn’t wash away the tension burning in her veins. Not even the punishing chill could extinguish the fire smoldering inside her.

    She threw her head back and braced her palms against the smooth tile, chest tightening as her breath came in sharp bursts. Inhale. Exhale. The water drummed steadily against her skin, a rhythmic pulse that should have soothed her — but inside, her thoughts were chaos. Everything spiraled back to one thing.

    Waiting.

    She felt like a schoolgirl. Lovesick. Off balance. Flushed with embarrassment.

    And god, she hated it.

    With a sharp motion, Rio leaned forward, pressing her forehead against the cold wall. Her eyes squeezed shut. This wasn’t her. She didn’t wait up for texts. She didn’t replay someone’s words in her head, searching for subtext. She didn’t feel abandoned just because someone didn’t respond right away. But now?

    Now, that’s exactly how she felt.

    It was ridiculous. It was beneath her. It was more than she could stand. Gritting her teeth, she shoved her soaked hair back, fists clenching tight around the dripping strands. The urge to hit the wall, to scream, to bleed out all this frustration — it clawed at her. She hated uncertainty. Hated waiting. Hated the feeling that someone had sucked the air out of her lungs and left her hollow.

    Agatha had gotten under her skin.

    Deeper than she should have. Stronger than Rio had allowed. Without permission. Without an exit.

    And now… now she had no idea how to live with that.

    Rio ran her tongue slowly across her lips and closed her eyes, feeling the heat building inside her like a warning. She couldn’t let this go on. She wasn’t going to live suspended in someone else’s silence. She wasn’t going to sit around, waiting for someone else to decide her worth. She needed a conversation.

    A real one. Raw. Alive. No deflections, no clever little smiles meant to distract or derail.

    Lifting her head, Rio’s eyes burned with resolve. If she wanted something, she went for it. That had always been her way. And she wasn’t about to make an exception now.

    She had to see Agatha. She had to hear the truth. Not through veiled glances or suggestive remarks. Not through the dance they were always performing around each other.

    She wanted it all laid out.

    Every goddamn detail.

    And yes — she desperately hoped Emma Swan was the biggest, most irritating secret Agatha Harkness had been keeping.

    Because if there was something more…

    Rio’s jaw tightened as she reached up and turned off the water, the sudden silence in the bathroom as sharp as a blade.

    If there was more, then fuck — it would be so much harder to forgive.

    Rio leaned back slowly, sinking deeper into the chair, arms crossed tightly over her chest.

    Her gaze drifted lazily upward, fixing on Lilia Calderu, who was waving her arms and laughing uncontrollably.

    There were only three people in the room — her, Lilia, and another professor whose voice had long since faded into background noise. Just static.

    "Picture it!" Lilia lifted her hands again, her dark curls bouncing back in rhythm like they, too, were in on the joke. "Red rash, little blisters, and the worst part — the itching! The unbearable, soul-crushing itch!" Rio blinked slowly, her face unreadable.

    "You’re seriously speculating on what Agatha Harkness would look like with chickenpox?" Her voice carried a distinct note of skepticism, though if she were honest, a flicker of interest sparked somewhere beneath it. Lilia clasped her hands together in mock gravity, tilting her head as if pondering the image.

    "You don’t get it — this is vital," she said, leaning forward like she was trying to conjure the vision into existence. "Dark circles under her eyes… hair so disheveled not even the best stylist could salvage it..." She drew an invisible aura around herself, fingers sketching out the tragic energy.

    "And no blazers. She wouldn't be able to wear a single one. She’d have to settle for something ancient. Maybe even—"

    "Duck-print pajamas?" Rio cut in, raising an eyebrow.

    "No!" Lilia gasped, affronted. "Lilac roses. Or something with the Harvard crest."

    Rio rolled her eyes, but inside… something tightened. Quietly. Slowly. Agatha was sick?

    No. Not her.

    Her son.

    Rio exhaled, long and quiet.

    Everyone knew.

    Except her.

    What the hell?

    Her fingers unconsciously tightened around the armrest, the only outward sign of the irritation coiling inside her.

    Agatha had never mentioned the child. Never said a word.

    And now...

    Now everyone knew — except her.

    She tried to chalk it up to disinterest — as if she’d never cared about Agatha’s personal life.

    But that wasn’t true anymore, was it?

    Now it mattered.

    Now she wanted to know.

    And...

    Why the silence?

    She pulled her phone from her pocket, unlocked the screen in one smooth motion.

    Messages.

    Nothing.

    Rio felt a slow, sour churn in her stomach.

    Perfect.

    After everything she said last night.

    After opening up to Agatha.

    After admitting she wanted to try.

    And what did she get in return?

    Silence.

    Her lips pressed into a flat, bitter line.

    A fucking perfect closing note to that idiotic confession. She wanted to be angry.

    But anger wasn’t what filled her chest.

    It was emptiness.

    Cold. Sticky. Unbearable.

    Like stepping on the same damn rake all over again.

    “Rio?”

    She looked up sharply.

    Lilia had leaned in, grinning with that maddening sparkle in her eye. “Are you even with us? Or are you off in your erotic daydreams about Agatha with a rash?”

    Rio blinked once, then slowly turned her gaze to her.

    “Lilia, if you don’t shut up right now, I will break your neck.” Lilia burst out laughing, tossing her head back.

    “Coming from you, Vidal? That almost sounds personal. Maybe we’re onto something here.”

    Rio didn’t answer.

    Her eyes dropped back to her phone screen.

    Still nothing.

    And fuck, she didn’t like it.

    With a flick of her thumb, she turned off the screen, feeling a slow, simmering frustration stir inside her — thick and dull, like background noise that wouldn’t shut off. One thought kept looping, circling back like a bad song: Why hasn’t Agatha said anything?

    She knew how absurd it sounded. She had said she was ready — for the secrets, the messy past, the whole package. But the silence about Emma, the fact that not a word had been said about Nicholas being sick — it hit her like cold water. Sudden. Jarring.

    She felt like an outsider again. Like someone kept on the doorstep, not trusted enough to be let in, not important enough to be told. Rio took a deep breath, trying to push down the sting of rejection. Anger surged — at herself. Had she really thought this would be easy? That she could just walk into Agatha’s life, with all its carefully guarded rooms, and claim a space?

    In her younger years, she would’ve bolted. She’d always left first. She didn’t stay to untangle someone else’s damage. She didn’t show her own vulnerability.

    But now... now it was different.

    And that difference made her sick.

    She closed her eyes for a second, letting herself exhale. Inside, everything was burning with that twisted blend of hurt and anger. She didn’t want to feel it. Didn’t want to lose control.

    She needed clarity. She needed to know where they stood.

    After last night — after everything she said — to be met with this silence? Too much.

    Even for Agatha.

    Rio bit her lip hard, holding back the sharp words she wanted to spit. Why the hell had she let herself believe she had the right to know everything?

    Her fingers began to drum nervously against the table, and Lilia, catching the stormy look on her face, fell silent, tilting her head in mild confusion. But Rio didn’t offer an explanation. She stood abruptly.

    “I have to go,” she said curtly, avoiding eye contact with anyone in the room, and turned on her heel toward the door — leaving behind that familiar weight of silence and unfinished thoughts.

    Outside, the first breath of autumn air hit her face — sharp and cool, sweeping away the lingering stuffiness of the classroom. Day had surrendered to evening; the sun no longer warmed the city, having slipped beneath the horizon and left only a fading reflection on the glass façades of the university buildings. The air was thick and damp, heavy with the scent of wet asphalt and crumbling leaves swirling along the paths. That unmistakable perfume of fall — earthy, bitter, tinged with exhaust fumes and the distant smell of fresh coffee from the campus café. Rio pulled her blazer tighter across her chest, but the chill slipped beneath the cotton of her white T-shirt anyway, a clear reminder: the season had turned. She pulled a cigarette from her jacket pocket and lit it with a slow, deliberate flick of the lighter. The smoke hit her lungs hard, acrid and biting. She wasn’t a regular smoker, not really — but sometimes, in moments like this, when the weight of her own frustration made it hard to breathe, it was the only thing that helped. She inhaled deeper, letting the smoke fill her chest, tricking her body into stillness. Her fingers trembled slightly, but she ignored it. Just bit down gently on the filter and closed her eyes, listening to the hum of the city: the distant drone of traffic, the echo of students chatting about lectures, and beneath it all, the dull, stubborn thud of her own heart.

    When she opened her eyes, she stared into the distance. The trees, once vivid in yesterday’s light, now loomed in heavy greys, draped in the dusk of early evening. Down the street, neon signs blinked lazily, promising a night of distraction to those hoping to outrun their thoughts. But Rio didn’t want distraction. She didn’t want to be anywhere. She just stood there — cigarette burning between her fingers, wondering how quickly everything had dissolved into uncertainty. She hated this limbo — this purgatory of not knowing, of waiting, of nerves stretched thin across the silence. Why wasn’t Agatha saying anything?

    Why, after everything Rio had said, after last night — after laying herself bare — was she left in this goddamn quiet?

    She flicked the ash with a sharp motion, narrowing her eyes. She hated feeling this way. Dependent on someone else’s choices. She craved clarity — a line in the sand she could see and touch — but there was none. Only the cold evening, her spiraling thoughts, and a cigarette burning down faster than she needed it to.

    Rio took another deep drag, feeling the acrid smoke coil inside her chest, clinging to her lungs, and finally — finally — leaving behind something that resembled calm. She leaned back against the cold brick wall, her gaze fixed on the empty stretch of autumn evening. Her thoughts wandered somewhere between irritation, exhaustion, and a gnawing, unbearable pull toward the woman who still hadn’t answered her message.

    She was silent.

    And Rio hated that silence.

    She bit down on her lower lip, flicked the ash from her cigarette, and exhaled slowly, lazily, through gritted teeth. Everything around her seemed suspended — the city moved, yes, but to her, it all felt frozen. Caught in pause. In this suspended state that clenched her chest and made the air feel twice as thick. She wasn’t supposed to be this attached. She wasn’t supposed to be stuck in a loop, replaying every moment, every glance, every breath they had shared.

    And yet here she was.

    Waiting.

    And then—

    A sharp, muffled ringtone sliced through the quiet.

    Shit.

    Rio flinched, nearly dropping her cigarette. She fumbled for her pocket, yanked out her phone, and when the name flashed across the screen, her heart skipped a beat.

    Agatha.

    She squinted at it, letting herself have one long, deliberate second of inaction — just watching the name pulse against the screen while the device buzzed again in her palm. Only then did she swipe her finger across the green icon.

    "You finally decided I was worth a reply?" Her voice was low, laced with sarcasm — but something inside her tightened, sharp and sudden. On the other end, a muffled groan.

    "God. This is the worst day of my life."

    Rio blinked.

    "Noted: Dean Harkness rated my call a zero out of ten."

    Agatha snorted, but there was too much pain folded into that short sound. "I'm not talking about you, Vidal."

    "How shocking."

    "I…" Agatha took a heavy breath, then exhaled again — a pause, thick and quiet, settling into the line. "I’m locked in."

    Rio raised an eyebrow.

    "Come again?"

    "Literally locked in." Agatha’s voice was sharp, almost angry — but underneath the irritation, there was exhaustion. "I'm in fucking quarantine." Rio blinked again.

    "You’re infected?"

    "Oh, you're clever today." Agatha’s tone was venomous, but the kind of venom Rio knew too well — not dangerous, just a bitter blend of sarcasm and helpless frustration. "Yes, Rio. I’m infected. Nicholas couldn’t let me enjoy this hell alone, so now I’m covered in spots too — like a goddamn Dalmatian."

    Rio froze — and then… chuckled.

    "That’s what I call family bonding."

    "Screw you."

    Rio let out a soft huff, the corners of her mouth twitching with the first real hint of a smile that day.

    "You sound absolutely fabulous."

    "Oh yes, I’m at the very peak of my allure," Agatha groaned again — a sound caught somewhere between a whimper and the exhaustion of someone on the edge of collapse. "I have a fever, I’m locked in my own house, and the cherry on top of this miserable cake? I can’t drink wine." Rio couldn’t hold back a laugh.

    "Are you seriously mourning the wine right now?"

    "Vidal, the alcohol level in my blood is usually higher than my patience." Rio tightened her grip on the phone, staring into the empty evening. "So what, you’re just going to sit there and suffer?"

    "You got a better idea?"

    Rio didn’t respond — but something had already started forming in her mind. She didn’t say it. Not yet.

    She flicked the ashes off her cigarette, stubbed it out against the wall, and tossed it into a nearby trash bin without looking.

    "I’ve gotta go."

    There was a brief pause on the other end.

    "Not even a 'get well soon'?"

    "You’ll survive, Harkness."

    She heard a quiet chuckle through the receiver, but didn’t let her speak again — she hung up.

    And then…

    Exhaled.

    She knew exactly what she had to do.

    The cold autumn air crept under her blazer, piercing her skin like fine needles. It hadn’t been warm to begin with — far too thin for this weather — but Rio hadn’t even considered grabbing something heavier. When she stepped out of the taxi, that damn blazer still clung to her shoulders, and in her hands were two heavy paper bags.

    She looked around.

    The path leading to the house was familiar now — narrow, lined with damp yellow leaves, slightly slick from the evening moisture. Tall trees whispered quietly on either side, their wet trunks shimmering faintly under the streetlights. Everything around seemed frozen, wrapped in the hush of autumn.

    Rio stepped forward slowly, unhurriedly, letting her thoughts finally settle. Reaching the door, she carefully set the bags down on the porch, pulled out her phone, and dialed Agatha’s number while pressing the doorbell. Silence.

    One ring.

    Two.

    Three.

    "Hello."

    Agatha’s voice was groggy, irritated — but laced with that subtle note of interest Rio knew too well.

    "Nice to know you’re alive."

    "Were you in doubt?"

    "Well, I wasn’t the one locked up with a fever and red spots all over my body."

    "Are you mocking me?"

    Rio smirked, then looked down at the bags at her feet.

    "I brought you something. For you and Nicholas."

    A beat of silence echoed through the line. Then: "You’re not joking?"

    Rio shook her head instinctively, even though she knew Agatha couldn’t see her.

    "There are two bags on the porch. Inside, you’ll find the usual things people need when they’re dealing with chickenpox: cooling lotion, anti-itch cream, children’s paracetamol for Nicholas, ibuprofen for you, some fresh fruit, and a couple packs of good green tea."

    She paused — then added with a hint of dry amusement: "Also, two bottles of non-alcoholic wine, fresh orange juice for Nicholas, a Lego Iron Man set, two takeout boxes from that Chinese place you like, and... one secret little toy just for you."

    She heard a quiet laugh on the other end of the line.

    "I’m almost afraid to open it."

    Rio tilted her head slightly, imagining Agatha lying somewhere on the couch, wrapped in a blanket, squinting suspiciously at her phone. "Oh, and — a small bouquet of tulips."

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    Then, after a few seconds, her voice came back lower, softer: "You really brought me flowers?"

    "Surprised?"

    "Honestly? Yeah."

    Rio let out a quiet chuckle, then added with mock casualness: "And don’t expect a photo report from me tonight. I know it’s my turn, but let’s face it — you’re not exactly in any condition to keep our tradition going right now."

    A scandalized noise came through the line, but before Agatha could form a retort, Rio took a step back.

    "And one more thing. As soon as you start feeling better..." — she lowered her voice, paused, gauging the silence — "we need to have a serious talk." Agatha stopped breathing for a couple of seconds.

    "...Yeah. Of course."

    Rio glanced back over her shoulder, eyes on the darkened windows of the house.

    "Will you come out?"

    "No. Not until you’re gone."

    Rio smirked, biting the inside of her cheek.

    "Well then... suit yourself."

    She turned and walked slowly back down the path, the autumn air biting at the skin beneath her unbuttoned blazer. She didn’t look back. But she knew Agatha was watching her go.

    And that was enough.

    Chapter 14: Quarantine Days

    Friday. Day two of quarantine.

    Agatha was irritable. Extremely.

    Rio had never seen her like this. Not commanding, not sarcastic, not in control of everything and everyone — but genuinely irritable, tired, and trapped in her own house like a beast in a cage. And it was, quite frankly, hilarious.

    When she picked up the call during her lunch break, barely stifling a yawn, she hadn’t expected that voice. Low, husky, with a hint of resignation and… anger?

    “If this child asks for another ‘magic bath,’ I swear I’m throwing myself out the window.”

    Rio blinked.

    “Magic bath?”

    “Yes.” Agatha’s voice was flat, but far too weary. “With glitter, salts, bubbles, and some kind of magical foam that I’m apparently supposed to summon out of thin air. Because, according to him, ‘Mommy’s a witch.’” Rio chuckled.

    “And what’s stopping you? You are a witch.”

    “Very funny, Vidal.”

    Rio could picture her — slumped in a chair, one hand rubbing her temple, the other idly tugging at the cuff of her sleeve. Pale, no doubt. Tired. Hair thrown up in a messy knot.

    But the irritation in her voice wasn’t the kind that came from bad parenting. No, Agatha wasn’t like that. She was still gentle, still patient. Just sick. Just confined. Just not used to having no option but to stay home. “You’re not snapping at him, are you?” Rio asked with a grin. “Are you kidding me?” Agatha scoffed. “I bathe him three times a day because he screams that he’s itchy, make him cocoa with marshmallows, and then listen to him complain that he’s bored and wants to go outside. I feel like I’m a mom to a toddler again.”

    “Oh, so you are a good mother.”

    “I’m a terrible mother,” Agatha muttered, then yawned. Her voice grew deeper, slower. “I let him watch The Avengers just so he’d leave me alone for a couple of hours.”

    Rio laughed.

    “And did he like it?”

    “He’s now yelling that he’s the Hulk and that he’s going to smash everything.”

    “Perfect. He’s officially my favorite child.”

    Agatha snorted, then sighed.

    “I’m tired.”

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    Partly because she didn’t know what to say.

    It was rare to hear Agatha like this.

    Not sarcastic. Not guarded. Just honest.

    Her fingers tightened slightly around her phone.

    “Are you at least eating properly?”

    “No. I don’t have an appetite. All I want is to get out of this damn house.” Rio smiled.

    “Then don’t go out. I’ll bring you something.”

    “I don’t need charity, Vidal.”

    “You need real food, Agatha.”

    And before she could reply, Rio hung up.

    Later that evening.

    Rio stepped out of the taxi, clutching two heavy paper bags tighter against her chest. The sweater she’d thrown over her shirt was warm enough, but completely useless against the chill of the autumn night. The dampness clung to the air, and a sharp wind crept beneath the fabric, scraping at her skin with icy fingers — but Rio didn’t pay it any mind.

    She was standing in front of Agatha’s house.

    Again.

    And once more, she was doing something she’d never done before. She wasn’t thinking about herself.

    Rio set the bags down on the porch, slipping her hands into her pockets without thinking. Fresh groceries, light snacks, herbal teas with honey, medications for fever — all packed neatly. Two bottles of non-alcoholic wine, fresh-squeezed juice for Nicholas, a set of bright bath bombs, and a brand-new Hulk-themed pajama set.

    She imagined that little monster sitting there in his green pajamas, stuffing his face with marshmallows and demanding another “magic bath.” Rio smiled.

    And last but not least — a small bouquet of tulips.

    Just because.

    Pulling out her phone, she quickly typed:

    “There’s something for you on the porch. Grab it before I change my mind and keep it for myself.”

    Taking a few steps back, she tilted her head up and looked at the windows. The lights were on.

    Her world was in there.

    Her irritable, wound-up, guarded Agatha.

    A smile tugged at the corners of her lips before she could stop it. She slipped her phone back into her pocket — but just before leaving, she couldn’t resist sending one last message:

    “And delete Tinder. It’s Friday, there’s wine — wouldn’t want you getting any ideas again. :))”

    Something crashed inside the house.

    Rio grinned wider.

    And walked away.

    Saturday.

    Regina turned onto the quiet street and eased the car to a stop in front of the now-familiar house. Rio traced the inside of her wrist with her fingertips, feeling a prickling restlessness build in her chest. It was her third day in a row doing this, and yet somehow today felt different—like something sharp and uneasy had taken root just behind her sternum.

    “You do realize you’re turning into a free delivery service?” Regina drawled lazily as she killed the engine.

    Rio smirked and reached for the three paper bags sitting at her feet, filled with what, at first glance, looked like random bits and pieces. But in truth, it was exactly what was needed.

    “Jealous?”

    Regina rolled her eyes but still reached for the door.

    “Hand one over. I’ll help.”

    They stepped out into the cold evening air, which slipped beneath the fabric of Rio’s trench coat like a whispered warning. She hardly noticed. She felt composed, in control. The burgundy dress hugged her figure with elegant precision, the trench sat open and effortless on her shoulders, and the sharp rhythm of her heels on the pavement made her steps sound deliberate, assured. Her lips were painted a deep red. Her hair — perfect. Regina gave her a slow once-over, squinting slightly.

    “You could’ve at least thrown on a stained t-shirt or something. This feels less like a drop-off and more like you’re seducing your ex-wife.” Rio let out a dry snort.

    “You know I always show up well-wrapped.”

    “God, don’t I,” Regina muttered, taking one of the bags.

    Rio placed the rest by the front door. A McDonald’s bag — because sick people always crave junk food. Fresh vegetables and vegetarian sausages — because today Agatha had let slip that Nicholas only eats those. A pop-up tent — to make quarantine feel like an adventure, even if just in the living room or out on the porch. Non-alcoholic martini and tonic — because even in lockdown, Agatha deserved the illusion of a proper life. Rio pulled out her phone and typed quickly:

    “Your delivery’s on the porch.”

    She lingered a moment, eyes fixed on the house. In one window, the light shifted — just barely.

    “She’s watching you already, you know,” Regina said, lips curving in amusement.

    “I know.”

    Rio hadn’t even tucked her phone away before it buzzed in her palm. Agatha Harkness.

    “Where are you off to looking that gorgeous?”

    The message lit up Rio’s screen, and she felt a sharp flicker of satisfaction bloom in her chest.

    She slowly lifted her head and looked up at the windows of the house. Agatha was there.

    Watching.

    Regina noticed the direction of her gaze and crossed her arms with a groan. “Don’t even think about it.”

    Rio didn’t type anything in response.

    Instead, she paused — just for a moment — then, with deliberate grace, lifted her hand and blew a kiss toward the window.

    Regina let out a dramatic sigh.

    “Oh God. I want no part in this.”

    But Rio only smirked, turning on her heel and sliding into the passenger seat with that same effortless, elegant confidence she always carried when she knew someone was watching.

    Regina started the engine, shaking her head.

    “You’re impossible.”

    “I know,” Rio said simply.

    She cast one last glance in the side mirror.

    The light in the window hadn’t flickered again.

    But she knew — Agatha was still there.

    And watching.

    Sunday.

    Rio never liked Sundays.

    They always felt… empty. Not as hopelessly gray as Mondays, nor as chaotically alive as Fridays. Sundays were something in-between — suspended in time, like a slow breath between what was and what’s to come. They dragged on endlessly, exhausting her with the stretch between “you can still rest” and “tomorrow, it begins again.”

    But today was different.

    Rio walked down the street with a surprising lightness in her step, as if someone had quietly turned her mood up a few notches. The autumn air was crisp but not biting, leaves fell in slow spirals, settling onto the damp asphalt like confetti from a party long over. A breeze brushed against her skin, cool but gentle, gliding over the exposed parts of her body, never quite slipping beneath the warm, oversized sweater she’d thrown on. She wore leggings and comfortable sneakers, her hair pulled up into a lazy bun. Today, she allowed herself to just be.

    No overthinking. No doubts. As if she’d wandered into a romantic movie — one where everything moved slowly, beautifully, and just felt… right. And for once, she didn’t mind.

    Rio turned the corner, heading toward the familiar Starbucks — that one. The one where she first ran into Agatha outside the university. That memory tugged her lips into a small, amused smile.

    But before she could step inside, her phone buzzed in her pocket. Rio pulled it out and glanced at the screen.

    Agatha.

    "I’m finally feeling better."

    Rio rolled her eyes without meaning to. She stepped into the slow-moving Sunday line at Starbucks, slid her thumb across her screen, and typed without hesitation:

    “Oh, should I start hoping for nudes again?”

    Sent.

    A smirk tugged at her lips as she slipped the phone back into her pocket. Her Sunday was just getting started.

    The line crawled forward with its usual weekend lethargy. Rio let her gaze wander across the café: students hunched over laptops, couples glued to their phones, a man by the window staring at the passing traffic while his latte sat untouched. Time here always felt slower, like it agreed to pause for a while.

    She pulled out her phone again, lazily flipping it between her fingers — and, predictably, the screen lit up with a new message.

    “Wasn’t what I gave you enough?”

    Rio chuckled, shaking her head. Of course. In her mind, she could already hear Agatha’s voice — low, teasing, like she’d just snapped a book shut and adjusted her glasses with that quiet amusement.

    “Greed is your deadliest sin, Vidal.”

    She bit the inside of her cheek, in no rush to reply. Her turn came, and she stepped forward to the counter, skimming the menu.

    “Two almond milk cappuccinos, a raspberry honey tea, and…” her eyes lingered on the glass case full of sandwiches and pastries “four turkey and cheese sandwiches.”

    “To go?”

    “Yeah.”

    While the barista rang up the order, Rio unlocked her phone again.

    “Well, if greed is my deadliest sin… shouldn’t you be the one to punish me for it?”

    She smiled slightly as she hit send.

    The reply came almost instantly.

    “Still hoping for a photo?”

    Rio shrugged, even though Agatha couldn’t see her.

    “I’ve accepted there’s no content today. But I am curious — just how bored are you already?”

    She could almost feel Agatha rolling her eyes.

    “Your order,” the barista placed a cardboard tray with three cups and a paper bag onto the counter.

    Rio nodded in thanks, grabbed everything, and made her way toward the door, carefully navigating through the crowd.

    Another buzz.

    “Bored enough that I already rewatched all the Harry Potter movies. Including the extended editions.”

    Rio snorted, nearly sloshing her coffee as she pushed past a group of teenagers.

    “Jesus. That’s dire.”

    “You have no idea.”

    She pushed through the glass doors, took a few steps, and drew in a breath of crisp morning air. The autumn wind brushed gently against her skin, leaving a soft chill on her cheeks and tangling the few strands of hair that had slipped from her messy bun. The air was rich — a sharp blend of wet leaves, distant bonfire smoke, and the fading aroma of coffee warming her palms.

    And yet, something deeper stirred inside her. Not sweet, not cloying, not saccharine. But quiet. Slow-burning. Like a fire still alive beneath a fine layer of ash. It pulsed under her ribs, tugging at her gently, making her grip the cardboard tray tighter than necessary.

    A stupid feeling. Like she was the lead in some romantic movie, where everything was moving according to an invisible script. Stupid… but for once, she didn’t mind it.

    She walked with purpose, quickly and steadily, while the drinks were still hot and the sandwiches still fresh. Her reflection passed in shop windows, but she didn’t look. Music from a nearby café played softly in the background, and Rio caught herself walking in rhythm with its beat. Her phone buzzed in her pocket.

    Shifting the tray to one arm, she pulled it out and glanced at the screen.

    “You have no idea how done I am with everything.”

    Agatha.

    Rio smirked. Classic.

    Her thumbs moved fast as she typed back:

    “Oh, I can guess. Who’s on your shit list today?”

    She tucked the phone away again, already expecting some scathing response — or maybe something cold, carefully worded but laced with meaning.

    But then… her mood shifted.

    Abruptly.

    Because she saw Her.

    Tall. Blonde. Walking with purpose down the narrow sidewalk. Emma.

    Rio froze.

    A cold jolt ran down her spine, like an invisible hand closing tightly around her throat. Something clenched in her chest — sudden, sharp, as if the air had thinned too fast, become not quite real.

    She didn’t know what she had expected.

    But not this.

    Not her.

    Not today.

    Her mouth went dry. Her breath slowed, but her heart pounded too fast, too loud.

    And then…

    The door opened for Emma.

    No knock. No pause.

    It just opened.

    And she stepped inside.

    As if she had done it a hundred times before.

    As if it was hers.

    As if she didn’t need permission.

    As if Rio was the one who didn’t belong.

    Her lips pressed into a thin, white line, fingers clenching the paper bag so tightly it crackled in protest.

    The anger came fast.

    Hot. Acidic. Boiling just under her ribs.

    Not because of Emma.

    Not even because she was inside that house — the one Rio had slowly started to believe was hers too.

    But because of Agatha.

    Because Agatha hadn’t said anything.

    Because she let Rio believe things were moving forward.

    Because she left her that night, after everything Rio had let fall open. Because Emma was where Rio should have been.

    The air felt too thick, too heavy to breathe.

    She wanted to storm in there.

    She wanted to say something poisonous, something that would hit exactly where it hurt.

    She wanted to see Agatha’s face.

    She wanted to know what the hell was happening.

    But instead…

    She turned around.

    Abrupt. Fast. Almost like fleeing.

    Her feet carried her away as everything inside her exploded — seething, boiling, pulsing with a low, livid fury.

    The food and drinks in her arms?

    Useless.

    Meant for a different reality. One where Emma didn’t exist. Where Agatha didn’t lie.

    Where Rio didn’t feel like a complete idiot.

    She made it a full block before stopping.

    A trash bin stood by the bus stop.

    No hesitation.

    She hurled the bags into it — sharp and final.

    Hot coffee. Tea for Nicholas. Sandwiches.

    All those stupid little signs of care.

    “Hey, hey!” barked an older man nearby. “That’s private property, miss! You can’t just—”

    Rio didn’t even look.

    She just raised one hand and flipped him off.

    Then turned and walked away without glancing back.

    Her heart was pounding too fast.

    Her thoughts burned too loudly.

    And inside her chest, where lightness used to live, there was now only raw, untamed rage.

    Her phone buzzed again as she strode down the sidewalk.

    She knew it was her.

    Knew that the screen would flash with something sharp, sarcastic — that familiar, biting tone.

    But when her eyes dropped to the message, her irritation only deepened.

    “In the fact that they exist.”

    Rio clenched her jaw.

    As if Agatha had no idea just how badly timed her damn sarcasm was. As if she didn’t realize what Rio had just seen.

    As if she chose to act like nothing was happening — like nothing had changed.

    Her fingers hovered over the keyboard.

    One second.

    Then another.

    And then, with a speed born from white-hot clarity, Rio typed:

    “Say hi to Emma.”

    Sent.

    Her thumb moved again — this time without hesitation.

    Block Contact.

    The screen blinked. Then stilled. Quiet.

    Just like her.

    But it wasn’t peace.

    It was the stillness before a storm.

    Chapter 15: Quarantine Days

    Rio hadn’t thought absence could be so… loud.

    The silence inside her wasn’t calm, wasn’t still. No — it was a cruel, sticky emptiness, clinging to her ribs, seeping into her lungs, pulling her down and refusing to let her breathe fully.

    She hated the feeling.

    Hated that she depended on someone so much that now, even after a week, her world still felt buried beneath the wreckage.

    Everything around her was the same. The same streets, the same faces, the same habits.

    But not her.

    She felt like she had been taken apart, piece by piece.

    Life went on — of course it did. She went to work, met up with Regina, pretended everything was as usual.

    But inside...

    Inside, everything was wrong.

    She caught herself staring too long at the same spot until someone called her name.

    Found herself rereading their old messages, even knowing there would be no reply.

    Opened Instagram and instinctively tapped Agatha’s profile — only to immediately shut it before her fingers could swipe through the photos. She couldn’t sleep. The emptiness beside her was maddening. And the worst part…

    She missed her.

    Not just her body. Not just the sarcastic remarks or the way Agatha would squint just slightly before delivering some poisonous quip. Rio missed even her anger.

    Her presence.

    The sound of her breathing nearby.

    What the hell had she done to her?

    What the hell had she made her feel?

    Rio wished she could say she had survived the week, had survived the breakup.

    But the truth was — she couldn’t forget her.

    No matter what she did, no matter what she tried to tell herself — it was all useless.

    Because, goddammit, Agatha was still inside her.

    And the more Rio tried to force her out, the deeper she seemed to sink in. Every time she closed her eyes.

    Every time she heard someone’s voice in the crowd that sounded almost like hers.

    Rio had never been someone who missed people.

    She wasn’t someone who waited.

    She wasn’t someone who ached.

    But now…

    Now she was simply falling apart.

    And the worst part — she had no idea how to stop it.

    Rio sat by the bus window, watching the city lights blur past, reflected faintly in the glass. Familiar streets flickered outside — store signs, occasional pedestrians wrapped in scarves, as if autumn had suddenly remembered it was supposed to be cold.

    But all of it felt like background noise.

    Colorless, empty, meaningless.

    The bus rocked gently, the low hum of the engine filling the space with a rhythm that was almost soothing. The people around her looked just as tired — someone scrolling through their phone with half-lidded eyes, someone gripping the overhead rail, staring blankly ahead.

    Rio caught her own blurred reflection in the window.

    Dark circles under her eyes. Pale skin. Lips pressed too tightly together. She looked like she hadn’t slept well.

    Because she hadn’t.

    Every night, over and over, she relived the same moment. Emma, stepping into Agatha’s house.

    The door closing behind her.

    And then — silence.

    That deafening, brutal silence where no answer came.

    Her fingers tightened around the strap of her bag, as if that might somehow give her back even a shred of control.

    The bus rolled forward from yet another red light, jolting her slightly. Rio clenched her jaw.

    She wasn’t going to suffer.

    No.

    That wasn’t who she was.

    She wasn’t the kind of person who sat staring at her phone, hoping for a message.

    Wasn’t someone who let herself feel unwanted.

    Wasn’t someone who allowed anyone to break her.

    But...

    She’d never let herself get attached like this before — not enough to fall apart without that person.

    And that — that was her biggest mistake.

    The rain drummed against the windows, turning the streets into a blurry mirage of light. Evening shadows thickened outside, and Rio’s apartment was cloaked in silence, broken only by the muffled hum of the city beyond. She stood in the kitchen, lazily sipping water from a glass, when there was a knock at the door.

    Rio narrowed her eyes.

    She wasn’t expecting anyone.

    Couriers usually called ahead — and truth be told, she didn’t have that many friends. She set the glass down, walked into the hallway, and flung open the door, already ready to say they had the wrong address. But no.

    Standing there was a guy in a standard courier jacket, with the tired, disinterested look of someone halfway through a long shift. In his hands — a paper bag. He held it out toward her, but Rio didn’t move. “I didn’t order anything.”

    The guy just shrugged and pointed lazily at the label stuck to the bag, where someone had written in thick black marker: For Rio Vidal. A sharp tightness seized her chest.

    She knew who it was from.

    “Who sent this?” Her voice came out sharp, but the courier didn’t flinch. “I just deliver, ma’am.”

    Rio’s eyes flicked down to the bag. Her fingers clenched into a fist, involuntarily.

    She didn’t need to look inside to know what was in it. Some stupid tea, maybe wine, or some pointlessly thoughtful crap. Agatha. Of course it was Agatha.

    Rio shifted her gaze back to the courier, but he was already turning to leave. Just before he stepped away, he pulled a small envelope from his pocket and handed it to her.

    “They said to give you this.”

    Rio frowned. She pulled out the note, unfolded it.

    There were only two words inside.

    “Unblock me.”

    Irritation flared, sharp and childish. She clenched her teeth, exhaled through her nose, feeling something heavy rise into her throat. Agatha really thought she could just… what?

    Send her a bag of dumb little things.

    Send her a dumb little note.

    And what, Rio was supposed to just unblock her? As if nothing had happened?

    She crumpled the note in her hand, heat building behind her ribs. Fucking manipulation. Fucking arrogance. Fucking… Agatha.

    The courier was gone. And Rio was still standing in the doorway, staring at the bag on the floor.

    She nudged it with the toe of her slipper like it might explode. And maybe it already had.

    Because everything inside her already had.

    Rio didn’t know if she was angrier at Agatha for pulling this stunt — or at herself for letting it get to her.

    The woman clenched her jaw, casting a look at the phone lying innocently on the kitchen table. She could’ve just picked it up, unblocked Agatha’s number, typed something sharp and biting. Reminded her that just a week ago, she had tossed her out of her life as easily as Rio was about to toss that stupid bag.

    But no.

    She wasn’t going to give Agatha that win.

    Unblocking her would mean admitting she still thought about her. That she was waiting. That she still felt. And Rio Vidal did not wait. Not for anyone. With a barely-there smirk, steeped in frustration, she slammed the door shut and grabbed her phone, scrolling through her contacts.

    "Hey, girl," Rio said, her voice lazy but carrying a clear note of fatigue. "Get ready. We’re drinking tonight."

    On the other end of the line, Regina let out a theatrical sigh. "Finally. I was starting to think you’d taken a vow of sainthood." Rio gave a short laugh.

    "I’ve got a direct ticket to hell, you know that."

    "Even better," Regina said. "Where are we meeting?"

    Rio cast one last glance at the note still balled up in her hand. "Somewhere they pour enough for me to forget that fucking name." Regina let out a low chuckle.

    "Oh, so we’re talking that kind of problem. Got it. I’ll bring the heels and that killer red lipstick."

    "Perfect," Rio replied with a grin.

    She tossed the note onto the table, sweeping the paper bag along with it. Then she walked toward the bedroom.

    Tonight, she was going to burn it out of her.

    And if it meant drowning Agatha Harkness in a few glasses of tequila—so be it.

    Rio Vidal walked into the bar like she owned the place.

    A second ago, the street behind her had been cool, soaked in damp autumn air, but inside, it was an entirely different world. Warm half-light, flickering neon signs, the smell of alcohol, perfume, and something musky — sandalwood, maybe. People laughed, kissed, talked, melted into each other and the night, but Rio paid them no mind.

    She knew she looked too good tonight.

    Tight black pants hugged her hips and legs perfectly, accentuating every movement. Her heels — slender, barely visible, but devastatingly elegant. Up top — a bold black camisole with a deep neckline and thin straps, leaving her shoulders and collarbones bare. And over it all — a blazer, carelessly draped like a nod to the restraint she usually lived by. But tonight, she wasn’t going to be restrained.

    Tonight, she wasn’t going to be herself.

    She ran a hand through her hair, combing loose waves with her fingers, and made her way to the bar, her gaze sweeping lazily across the crowd. Her lips were tinted a dark wine red. Her eyes — outlined in shadowed hues.

    She knew she was being watched.

    Knew someone was already planning to come over.

    But she didn’t care.

    She wasn’t here for that.

    Regina was already seated at the bar, sipping a cocktail with a wedge of lime perched artfully on the rim, holding herself with the grace of a queen. When she saw Rio, she raised a brow and gave her a slow once-over. “Well, darling, if this is your attempt to prove you’re not heartbroken,” she said with a smirk, “you’re doing a damn fine job of it.” Rio gave a dry laugh, lowering herself onto the tall barstool. “Funny — I was just about to say the same thing to you.” Regina smiled, tilting her head.

    “Careful. Say another thing like that and I might start thinking you’re into me.”

    Rio rolled her eyes but couldn’t help the faint smirk tugging at her lips. “Wouldn’t want to break your heart.”

    “Too late for that, darling.”

    The bartender approached, and Rio ordered something strong without hesitation.

    “Come on,” Regina said with a playful glint in her eye, taking another sip of her drink. “Just how badly do you want to forget?”

    Rio glanced at her, then lifted the glass that had just been placed in front of her.

    “Enough to not remember why we’re even here by morning.” She took her first sip.

    And in that moment, she realized she had officially declared war on her feelings.

    Half an hour later.

    Rio was spinning her phone slowly in her hand, watching the bluish glow of the screen reflect on her fingers. Another empty notification. No texts. No calls. Just silence.

    Her thumb hovered over the unblock button, but it never pressed down. Something inside her groaned in frustration — that unbearable pull toward breaking. Toward letting herself be weak. Letting herself miss her. But no.

    She slowly placed her phone face down on the bar, as if it no longer mattered. Even though, of course, it did.

    Regina watched the whole performance, sipping lazily from her glass. Her dark eyes narrowed, and a faint smirk curled the corners of her lips — sharp, knowing, laced with just enough sympathy to not feel like pity. “You’re either going to unblock her or finally sleep with someone else. Because honestly? This is getting boring.”

    Rio exhaled through her nose, reached for her drink, and downed half of it in one go.

    “Jesus, Regina. You could at least pretend you enjoy hanging out with me.” “I do enjoy hanging out with you. But it’s even more entertaining to watch you spiral.”

    Rio shot her a look of disbelief.

    “You’re a sadist.”

    “I’m a realist.”

    Regina leaned in, resting her elbows on the bar, and gave Rio a long, assessing stare.

    “Babe, I’m not saying you should run out and fuck the first person who makes eye contact, but seriously…” She swirled her drink. “How long are you planning to torture yourself over a woman who couldn’t even bother to tell you she had a wife?”

    Rio’s jaw clenched.

    God, how it burned when someone shone a direct light on what she was feeling.

    “None of your business.”

    “You dragged me out, got me tipsy, and now expect me not to give you advice?”

    Rio rolled her eyes, lifting her glass again.

    “Can we just drink and shut the hell up?”

    Regina smirked.

    “I can’t help it. I love the sound of my own monologues.” She took a generous sip of her drink, squinting with satisfaction, then turned back to Rio.

    “You just have to decide what you want. If you want her—unblock her. Text her. Ask for her. Do something. But if she disappears again, don’t blame anyone but yourself.”

    Rio swallowed hard, her eyes dropping to the glass in front of her. “And what if I don’t want to decide?”

    Regina snorted.

    “Then your only option is to keep drinking and hope someone hot takes you home before you black out.”

    Rio rolled her eyes.

    “You’re insufferable.”

    “You adore me.”

    Rio didn’t answer. She just took another long sip.

    The warmth of the alcohol spread through her body, slow and heady, leaving a pleasant haze behind.

    She could feel it—how she was starting to lose control.

    How her resistance was unraveling.

    How the world was blurring at the edges, becoming just a little bit easier to bear.

    And honestly? It felt better than facing the truth.

    Regina clapped her on the shoulder, calling over the bartender. “Two more. I think my girl here is finally embracing my philosophy.” Rio gave her a crooked smile.

    “And what is that, exactly?”

    Regina raised her glass in a toast.

    “Life’s too damn short to spend it waiting on one woman.” Rio picked up hers.

    “Fuck, sometimes you’re way too wise for this world.”

    Regina smirked.

    “I know.”

    Glass clinked against glass.

    And they drank.

    Rio slowly swirled the glass in her hand, watching the amber liquid coat the sides, leaving behind thin, sticky traces. She liked this part — the point when the alcohol was pleasantly warm in her veins, but her mind hadn’t yet slipped into a blur. When emotions were still under control, but the tongue had loosened, and thoughts grew bolder, more honest.

    Regina shifted on the tall barstool, crossing her legs, and glanced at her. “All right, enough about you. Let’s talk about me now.”

    Rio smirked, took the last sip, and set her glass down on the counter. “Right. Because that heartfelt monologue about my shattered heart wasn’t quite enough. Sure, let’s focus on your perfect one.”

    Regina tilted her head, a mischievous gleam flashing in her dark eyes. “My heart’s never been broken, darling.”

    Rio shook her head slowly.

    “I don’t buy that. Everyone’s had at least one… real thing.” Regina gave a dry little laugh, tracing the rim of her glass with a fingertip. “There was. But that doesn’t mean it ended in tragedy. Graham was…” — she paused, searching for the right word — “a solid option.” Rio squinted at her.

    “Option?”

    “Yeah.”

    “You talk about men like you’re ordering off a menu.”

    Regina shrugged.

    “Exactly. I choose, I taste, sometimes I come back for seconds — but I rarely order the same thing twice.”

    Rio snorted.

    “That is brutally cynical.”

    “It’s honest.”

    The bartender poured them another round. Regina nodded, accepted her drink, and took a slow sip.

    “You know what’s strange?”

    Rio leaned in slightly, curiosity flickering in her gaze. “Enlighten me.”

    Regina turned her glass, hesitating. Like she wasn’t sure whether to say it out loud.

    “I don’t feel anything anymore.”

    Rio frowned.

    “What do you mean?”

    “I want to fall in love. I want that flutter — that ridiculous warmth in your chest when you look at someone. I really do. But…” — she gave a soft shrug — “nothing. It’s just… empty.”

    Rio pressed her lips together.

    She didn’t know what to say to that.

    It felt like having too many feelings was her own personal curse. She felt everything — deeply, painfully, with no room for compromise. The breakup with Agatha had been a catastrophe because Rio couldn’t just delete her from memory, move on, find a distraction.

    Regina was the opposite.

    She didn’t feel.

    And somehow, that was just as heartbreaking.

    “Maybe you just haven’t met the right person yet,” Rio offered gently. Regina snorted.

    “Classic romantic optimism.”

    Rio crossed her arms over her chest.

    “Come on. You want to feel — that means you’re still capable. It’s just… no one’s hit you hard enough yet.”

    Regina turned her glass slowly, then looked at Rio with a long, measured gaze.

    “And when you got hit hard enough… what did you do?”

    Rio froze.

    A stupid question. Simple.

    But it landed straight in the gut.

    “I…” she hesitated, then let out a nervous laugh. “I blocked her and went drinking with you.”

    Regina rolled her eyes and pinched the bridge of her nose. “Jesus Christ, you’re hopeless.”

    Rio exhaled, staring into her glass.

    “I know.”

    Regina gave her a long, unreadable look, then drained the last of her drink and set the glass on the counter with finality.

    “Well. If no one’s setting our souls on fire tonight… might as well drink until we forget we even have souls.”

    Rio grinned.

    “Hey, I thought you were here to keep things under control.” Regina picked up another shot from the bar and gave Rio a pointed look. “Yeah, well… control is boring.”

    And they drank again.

    Rio wasn’t sure exactly which drink blurred the edges of reality. Everything around her had melted into a kaleidoscope of light and shadow, laughter and music, sharp flashes of awareness and long, blurred moments when nothing mattered anymore.

    She felt lighter than she had all week. Lighter than those nights lying in the dark, staring at her phone. Lighter than the burning frustration simmering under her skin that she could never quite touch or release. Now, it was all gone.

    Here, in the haze of this place, pulsing with music and the bitter tang of alcohol on her tongue — nothing made sense.

    Only sensation.

    She leaned back slightly, feeling the edge of the bar against her lower back, her fingers instinctively curling around the cold wood. Her lips parted as the bassline sank into her bones, thumping against her ribs like a second heartbeat.

    Regina laughed beside her, head tilted back, her dark eyes gleaming in the reflection of the bottles behind the bar.

    “Fuck it, let’s have some real fun.”

    Rio tilted her head lazily, one brow raised.

    “You suggesting I fuck you right here?”

    Regina scoffed, waving her hand dismissively.

    “Sweetheart, you couldn’t handle me.”

    Rio threw her head back in a laugh, feeling the music vibrate through her body, letting it pour over her like warm, smoky water. Every beat skated across her skin, sinking in.

    Her body wanted to move.

    Wanted to let go of the last ghosts of the past — even if just for one damn night.

    She stepped forward, stretching slightly, tilting her head from side to side like she was warming up for a performance.

    Regina narrowed her eyes, watching.

    “What are you up to?”

    Rio slowly turned her gaze to her.

    “I’m about to steal this show.”

    And before her friend could say a word, Rio pulled herself up and gracefully climbed onto the bar.

    All eyes — on her.

    Instantly, a wave of attention swept across the room. Conversations cut off mid-sentence, heads turned sharply in her direction, and the music kept pounding, driving the rhythm under the skin.

    She stood at the center of it all.

    The goddess of the night.

    Long legs wrapped in fitted black pants. A deep neckline revealing just enough to make someone’s mouth go dry. Her hair loose and tousled — just enough to be dangerous.

    She knew exactly how she looked.

    She knew exactly what she was doing.

    A slow smile curved her lips as her fingers trailed along her collarbone, then up her neck, barely touching, like she was testing how good that touch might feel.

    The bartender said something in protest, but it was drowned out by cheers and whistles.

    Someone hollered, “Let’s go, baby!”

    Rio threw her head back, reveling in the moment.

    Her hands sliding along her sides, a slow roll of her hips, the arch of her back — her entire body moved in time with the music, accentuating every beat, every chord, every thump of bass.

    She owned this moment.

    She owned them all.

    The heat of a hundred eyes clung to her like static, and she only burned brighter for it.

    Her fingers slid along her thigh, then upward — slowly, almost lazily. Someone’s eyes widened. Applause broke out. And Regina, down below, was laughing with delight, throwing her an admiring look. Rio was savoring every second.

    She had lost herself in the dance.

    But at that moment—

    That fucking moment—

    The bar doors slammed open.

    And everything went to hell.

    They burst open with such force that even over the thundering music, the sound sliced through the room like a warning shot.
