    Rio caught it in the corner of her eye but didn’t give it much thought at first. Her body was still moving, flushed with alcohol, music, and all those hungry stares. She was on top of the world.

    And then—

    Silence.

    Not complete. But ripping.

    The cheers dulled. Laughter faltered.

    The music still pounded, but something in the air had shifted. Rio stilled mid-movement, her hand paused on her waist, fingertips grazing the skin beneath the hem of her top.

    “What the…?” she muttered, glancing down toward where Regina sat. Her friend was no longer laughing.

    Her smile had twisted—no longer amused.

    Now it was something else.

    That kind of wicked grin people wear when they know shit is about to blow up.

    Rio frowned, then slowly turned her head toward the entrance. And she saw her.

    Emma Swan.

    Uniform. Badge.

    That serious, utterly composed expression.

    And a face that screamed what the fuck louder than any spoken word. “Get down.”

    Emma’s voice rang out firm and clear.

    And Rio knew immediately—it wasn’t a request.

    It was a command.

    She could’ve just jumped down and surrendered.

    She could’ve cracked a joke to lighten the mood.

    But… this was Emma.

    Agatha’s wife.

    And something inside Rio detonated.

    She narrowed her eyes, a slow smirk curling at her lips. She lifted her chin higher, arms crossing lazily over her chest. “Well, well. What a surprise.”

    Emma’s expression didn’t change.

    “Get down. Now.”

    Regina let out a choked laugh, covering her mouth with one hand. Rio glanced at her, then turned her eyes back to Swan.

    “Sorry, officer, but I’m quite comfortable up here.”

    The crowd erupted.

    Someone clapped, someone whistled, someone shouted, “Now that’s what I call resistance!”

    Emma didn’t blink.

    Rio could feel that stare burning through her.

    You really want to do this?

    She tilted her head slightly, narrowing her eyes.

    Oh, absolutely. I do.

    “I’m on duty,” Emma said evenly. “This isn’t a game.”

    Rio smirked.

    “Are you sure? Because I’m enjoying every second of it.” The crowd burst out laughing again.

    Emma didn’t flinch.

    Then she took a step forward.

    “All right. Your choice.”

    Rio barely had time to react before Emma’s fingers wrapped around her wrist.

    It happened too fast.

    Her body was yanked downward — she didn’t resist, but still lost her balance.

    And the next thing she knew...

    Her back hit the bar, and Emma was standing in front of her with handcuffs in hand.

    “You’re under arrest.”

    Shit.

    “Seriously?” Rio breathed out.

    “Yes.”

    Before she could protest, cold metal snapped around her wrists. The crowd went wild.

    Shouts. Laughter.

    Regina was already crying with laughter, slapping the bar with her hand. “Oh, fuck me…” she managed between gasps.

    Rio hissed under her breath.

    “You just ruined my night.”

    Emma smirked slowly.

    “Trust me — you ruined mine too.”

    And before Rio could get another word out, Emma was already leading her toward the exit.

    It was going to be even worse at the station.

    She felt the bite of the cuffs against her skin as Emma gripped her firmly and pushed her through the crowd.

    Her heels echoed sharply against the wooden floor.

    Laughter, yelling, applause — it all blended into one loud, nerve-grating roar, but Rio had already tuned it out.

    She was pissed.

    Drunk and furious.

    Not the best combination.

    “Do you ever fucking relax?” she snapped as Emma dragged her outside into the cool night air.

    Swan didn’t answer.

    She just tightened her grip on Rio’s wrist and kept walking toward the car. Regina practically flew out after them.

    Still laughing.

    “Hey! Wait! Are you seriously taking her in?”

    Emma didn’t even slow down.

    “Yes.”

    Regina caught up immediately.

    “Well, then I’m coming with her.”

    Emma stopped.

    The tension in the air shifted so fast, Rio felt it hit her skin like static. Swan inhaled through her nose and turned her head slowly toward Regina. “You’re drunk.”

    “So what? Is there a law against riding along with your best friend?” Regina teased, raising an eyebrow.

    “No. But it is illegal to be publicly intoxicated and harass an officer.” “Harass?” Regina laughed and stepped closer — bold, slow, running her eyes over Emma from head to toe.

    “Huh… I could, though.”

    Rio burst out laughing.

    “Oh, Swan, that’s it. You’re never getting rid of her now.” Emma’s face didn’t move a muscle.

    “You have no idea what you’ve just stepped into.”

    Regina tilted her head, eyes still locked on hers.

    “Oh? So you like when people step into you?”

    Rio choked with laughter.

    Emma clenched her jaw tightly.

    “Get in the car.”

    Rio shook her head, grinning uncontrollably.

    “Say ‘please,’ Officer Swan.”

    Emma narrowed her eyes.

    “Get. In. The car.”

    Regina smirked.

    “So commanding. Just…” — she sighed theatrically, dragging a finger down the metal buckle of Emma’s coat, then lower, past the badge — “absolutely on fire.”

    Rio was crying with laughter by now.

    But—

    Emma didn’t flinch.

    Didn’t blink.

    “You’re the mayor. Photos of you with your arrested bestie will be everywhere by morning.”

    Pause.

    Regina blinked.

    Rio caught on first.

    “Oh, you bitch…”

    Emma smirked like a predator.

    Regina dropped her hand and took a step back.

    “That was a low blow, Officer.”

    “I know.”

    Rio hissed but finally slid into the car.

    “All right, Swan. But just so you know—next time, I’m going to be your problem.”

    Emma smirked.

    “You already are my problem.”

    The door slammed shut.

    And Rio shook her head, grinning to herself.

    Well then.

    Looked like it was going to be a fun night.

    Chapter 16: Quarantine Days| Monday

    Rio stepped out of the station, squinting weakly against the merciless morning sun that slammed straight into her face.

    Her head wasn’t just aching — it was pulsing, as if someone had locked a wind-up mechanism inside her skull, creaking and banging with every movement. It felt like someone had burned all the oxygen around her, leaving only thick, choking air laced with last night’s alcohol and regret. Her mouth was bone dry, her tongue felt like sandpaper, and her stomach was sending out anxious signals — warning her that any sudden motion could end in disaster.

    She squinted, trying to focus her vision. Her eyes stung, as if someone had replaced them overnight with shards of red glass.

    Rio vaguely remembered Emma shoving her into the car, the cool air slicing through the burning heat of her skin, her body far too heavy, her thoughts crawling like molasses.

    Now, it all came crashing back in one massive wave of consequences. Her clothes were wrinkled, reeking of smoke and alcohol. Her hair was such a mess that even running her fingers through it only made things worse.

    All she wanted was to curl up in the dark and sleep this day into oblivion. But instead of silence and solitude, she was met with a low, familiar voice. “Good morning, Miss ‘I can handle myself,’” Regina drawled — her voice rich with that sweet, barely restrained mockery Rio both hated and loved. She turned her head slowly, cursing the world.

    Regina looked like she had spent the night in a five-star hotel, not drinking at a bar.

    Her hair was perfect. Sunglasses covered her eyes, but the smirk on her lips gave her mood away.

    She wore a deep purple dress that hugged her flawless figure, a long black coat, dark tights, and knee-high boots. In her hands — two coffee cups, one of which she immediately offered to Rio.

    “Kill me,” Rio groaned, grabbing the cup and pressing it to her forehead like it might magically erase the pain.

    Regina raised a brow, smirked, and turned toward the car without another word.

    “Move it before I change my mind about being nice,” she tossed over her shoulder.

    Rio grimaced but obeyed.

    Dragging herself after her friend, she felt every cell in her body screaming in protest.

    The car wasn’t far — a sleek black silhouette among a sea of dull, parked vehicles.

    Regina slid gracefully behind the wheel, started the ignition, and the engine purred with a smooth, satisfying hum. Rio slowly sank into the passenger seat, nearly collapsing into it. Her eyelids were heavy, her temples throbbing. Her whole body felt wrecked, like she’d spent the night running military drills rather than just getting arrested for disorderly conduct. She ran a hand over her face, exhaled deeply, and pressed her forehead against the cool glass.

    “Just a reminder—this was all your idea,” Regina said with a smirk, sipping her coffee.

    “My ideas always end in disaster,” Rio muttered darkly, rubbing her temples. “That’s what makes me interesting.”

    “Well, if interesting means getting arrested by your woman’s ex-lover, then sure…” Regina gave her an assessing glance. “Very impressive.” Rio groaned, covering her face with both hands.

    “I need to delete the last twenty-four hours from my memory.” Regina snorted, took another sip of coffee, and shook her head meaningfully.

    “Well, I don’t know. I’d say your performance on the bar counter deserves to be preserved in the annals of history. Maybe even as cultural heritage.” Rio shot her a tired look, full of dull irritation and hungover suffering. She massaged her temples like she was trying to physically shake the whole night out of her head, then pressed her lips together, turned her gaze to the street, and after a short pause, said: “Agatha called.”

    Regina raised an eyebrow slightly, but her reaction this time was restrained. “And how’d she like your little bar show?”

    Rio glared at her from under her lashes, touching her temple, where the headache still pulsed.

    “Not me.”

    “Hm?” Regina glanced over briefly.

    Rio pressed her lips into a thin line and exhaled: “She didn’t call me.”

    Regina was silent for a moment, then tilted her head slightly, eyes still on the road.

    “You mean…”

    “Emma.”

    The engine is off. The cabin is quiet, save for her own breathing and the occasional murmur of voices outside the window. She knows it’s normal — sitting still like this, unmoving, when her mind is in chaos. But at the same time, it feels wrong, because Agatha always makes decisions quickly, without hesitation. She’s a woman of action. A woman who doesn’t freeze in a parked car, staring into nothing, trying to process what the hell just happened.

    She closes her eyes.

    Her body feels strange. Her muscles are still tight, fingertips tingling with leftover electricity, the hot, shameful memory of how her wrists trembled as Rio untied the ribbon — just as easily as she’d bound them. Heat flares low in her belly, a treacherous echo of desire, and Agatha irritably taps her fingers against the steering wheel, trying to kill the feeling. No. She shouldn’t be reacting like this.

    She should be angry.

    Her chest rises sharply, and she pushes herself back into the seat, eyes flicking to her reflection in the rearview mirror. Still the flawless hairstyle, the perfectly tied bow — only the fingers gripping the wheel betray the tension.

    Agatha inhales deeply.

    She hates that she let it happen. That she didn’t push Rio away sooner. That she gave in, that her body clenched and uncurled with every movement Rio made. That she couldn’t find the right words. That she let go of the control she clings to like breath. That she stood there, wrists bound, head thrown back, feeling herself slip.

    What she hates most is that Rio walked away.

    Just left her there.

    Cold. Empty.

    Agatha rubs her face with both hands, fingers digging into her temples, then suddenly leans back again, staring at the ceiling of the car. She’s not crying — no. She doesn’t know how. It’s just… the sharp weight of realization. She thought she was angry at Rio. But maybe — just maybe — all this time, she’s been angry at herself.

    At her inability to say what needed to be said.

    The fear that saying the wrong thing could ruin everything. The fact that when Rio blocked her, it hurt more than she was willing to admit.

    Agatha’s gaze drifts slowly over the dashboard before she drops her hand onto the pizza box, tapping her fingers against the cardboard. She should drive home. She should return to her son, to his chatter, to the comfort of routine. She should wash this day off her skin, rid herself of the taste of Rio’s name on her lips, of the marks someone else’s control left on her body.

    But instead, she keeps sitting there, trapped in silence. And for the first time in a long while, she feels truly lost. Agatha exhales heavily, but it’s not enough. There’s not enough air. It gathers somewhere between her ribs, sticks in her throat like unsaid words, like emotions with nowhere to go. Irritated, she raises a hand to loosen the bow at her neck, but stops herself almost immediately — as if even the gesture feels too much like surrender. She leaves it tied, slightly more relaxed than before, but still flawless.

    Her fingers find the steering wheel again. Her nails slowly scratch along the smooth leather, drawing invisible lines. Agatha can’t just sit still. Her body demands action, motion — something to shake her free from this spiral of thought. Everything inside her aches with frustration, with everything left unsaid, with that damn ribbon that had been tied around her wrists. She should’ve said something back.

    She should’ve stood her ground.

    She should’ve—

    No. She shouldn’t have.

    Rio had enjoyed it. She knew exactly what she was doing. She played the game. Took control with such ease, as if it was her right, as if it had always belonged to her. And the worst part was that Agatha let her. She let her, because deep down, beneath the layers of irritation and anger, there was something she couldn’t quite admit. Something that responded to the power in Rio’s voice, to her command, to the warmth of her hand tightening around her throat.

    Agatha grips the wheel harder. Her lips press into a thin line, her breathing unsteady. Everything inside is boiling. It’s disgusting. It’s impossible. It’s— Suddenly, the phone screen lights up, and the quiet chime of a message slices through the silence. Agatha jumps. Her hand reaches instinctively for the screen, but freezes the moment she sees the name.

    Emma.

    She stares at the display a second too long. Longer than any normal message would require. Then, slowly, almost lazily, she slides her finger across the screen to unlock it.

    “Are you coming soon? Nicholas already had dinner, but he wants to wait up for you before bed.”

    The message is simple, without subtext. Ordinary. Boring. And, strangely enough, it doesn’t spark irritation or weariness — but... nothing. Absolutely nothing. As if Emma were just a voice on the other end of the line. Just someone she’s obligated to reply to.

    She lowers the phone back onto her lap and closes her eyes, letting her head rest against the headrest. Inhales deeply. Holds the air in her lungs for a second, then exhales slowly, almost silently. She doesn’t want to go home. Doesn’t want to walk through the door and feel someone else’s presence in a space that has always been hers. Doesn’t want to see Emma. Doesn’t want to talk to her. Doesn’t want to face the questions in her eyes — and Emma will undoubtedly ask why Agatha looks like she’s been hit by a storm. The problem is, Agatha can’t even answer that question for herself. Her fingers tap absently on the steering wheel. She takes a sharp breath before glancing back at the screen. And only then does she notice her own reflection in the tinted window.

    Her face looks unfamiliar. The face of a woman who lost more control today than she could afford.

    A woman who was pushed to the edge.

    A woman who was used.

    Her stomach tightens uncomfortably, and it’s deeper than disgust. It clings to her skin, sticky like filth that won’t wash off, no matter how hard you scrub. Agatha tears her gaze away from the mirror and presses her hand to her temple, smoothing the tense line on her forehead. Her lips tighten into a thin, hard line.

    Rio.

    She had been everywhere today. In every step, in every glance, in every word. Rio had walked into the university like an actress stepping onto her stage, and the entire performance had been for her. The game was subtle, predatory, intoxicating.

    Rio knew exactly what she was doing.

    And Agatha knew too.

    But that wasn’t enough.

    Because now, sitting in her car outside the pizzeria, Agatha feels defeated for the first time in a long while. Not because Rio rejected her. But because she didn’t care.

    And that — that’s the worst part.

    She lowers her head and tightens her grip on the steering wheel until her fingers turn white. A shiver runs through her. Flashes from the library replay in her mind. Rio’s voice. The scent of her perfume. That light, mocking tone. Her touch — too confident, too commanding. The thin red lips forming those exact words.

    Unfasten your pants.

    Agatha's eyes close, and her fingers tighten around the steering wheel. Oh, Rio got her little triumph. Her revenge. Her win.

    But what she doesn’t yet understand is that a victory that feels absolute can turn out to be nothing more than a long, drawn-out battle. Because if there’s one thing Agatha does better than anything else — it’s taking control back.

    She pulls one hand from the wheel, presses the screen lock button with sharp finality, and tosses the phone onto the passenger seat. The engine growls to life as she turns the key in the ignition.

    She’s going home.

    To Emma.

    To Nicholas.

    But not because she wants to.

    Because she can’t not go.

    Agatha turns the key in the lock, hears the soft click of the latch, and gently pushes the door open. The house smells like warm wood, something cozy and alive, but inside her everything feels cold, hollow — like something had been burned out of her over the course of the day. She steps over the threshold, pizza box in one hand, while the fingers of the other absentmindedly brush over the lapel of her blazer, as if wiping away invisible dust.

    The house is quiet, but she knows it won’t last long.

    Footsteps. Light, quick — and then a tousled blond head peeks around the corner. Nicholas. His eyes light up the second he sees the box in her hands. “Pizza!” he breathes out, launching himself forward. In a second, he’s at her side, his small hands grabbing at her wrist, tugging the box toward himself like she might change her mind and take it back. “Mom, I’ve been waiting forever!”

    Agatha smooths a smile onto her face, shaping it into something natural. She can’t let Nicholas see anything foreign, anything that doesn’t belong in this house.

    “Forever?” she drawls lazily, raising an eyebrow. “Then you should consider writing a textbook on relativity. Sounds like you’ve mastered the flow of time.”

    Nicholas snorts, undeterred — deftly taking the box from her hands and, with a little bounce, dashing toward the kitchen. Agatha exhales, barely audibly.

    And only then does she notice her.

    Emma.

    She appears in the doorway slower than their son. Her hair is messy, tied back in a loose ponytail, but a few strands have slipped free and fall across her face. She’s wearing plain jeans and a simple t-shirt, one hand tucked into her pocket, the other resting against the doorframe. She looks at Agatha intently, but without any readable emotion — just watching. They stand like that for several long seconds.

    The silence between them is heavier than the warm air inside the house. “You’re late,” Emma says at last, her expression unchanged. “I know,” Agatha replies, calmly.

    Emma tilts her head slightly to the side, watching her. That look — focused, unblinking, as if peeling away layers of skin. There’s no need for her to ask, “Are you okay?” because it’s written all over Agatha’s face that she isn’t. But they’re not the kind of people who talk about emotions. “Nicholas was waiting for you,” Emma says gently — but there’s more to it, a quiet reproach woven into her tone.

    Agatha glances toward the kitchen, where the sound of a pizza box rustling now floats out, then shifts her gaze back to Emma.

    “I’m here.” Her voice is still even, but a flicker of irritation cuts through. “Isn’t that what matters?”

    Emma narrows her eyes slightly, as if weighing whether this is the moment to press further. But she knows Agatha well. If something is already coiled tightly inside her, pushing it will only make it snap into something uncontrollable.

    “He’s happy to see you,” Emma says simply.

    Agatha lifts her chin slightly but doesn’t respond.

    “So am I.”

    The words knock her off balance. Something sharp, involuntary jerks inside, but Agatha only blinks slowly, absorbing it.

    “Don’t start,” she says at last, walking past her and slipping off her blazer, tossing it over the back of a hallway chair.

    “I didn’t start anything,” Emma replies calmly.

    Agatha can feel her watching from behind. It grates on her nerves. Not because Emma is saying anything wrong, but because her voice lacks conflict. Lacks feeling. Emma always had a way of getting under her skin — not with anger, but with that infuriating calmness that drove Agatha mad.

    “You look like you were hit by a truck,” Emma adds suddenly, her tone taking on something more personal.

    Agatha whips around.

    “Thank you for the poetic observation,” she says icily.

    “Agatha…” Emma takes a step forward, but Agatha immediately pulls back, as if even the idea of being touched might push her over the edge. “Don’t.” She lifts a hand to stop her.

    And once again — silence.

    They stand too close.

    For too long.

    And suddenly, Agatha feels that same sticky exhaustion seeping in again, the kind she’d first felt back in the car. That quiet, grinding sense that the entire day has been tearing her apart — but she has no choice but to hold herself together.

    Rio.

    The thought flashes through her like a spark — sudden, scorching, uninvited.

    She feels the anger twitch beneath her skin, thin and trembling, but she swallows it down.

    Not here.

    Not now.

    She exhales slowly, and when she speaks, her voice is flat, stripped of everything: “You can go.”

    Emma studies her for a second longer, then gives a small nod, saying nothing else. She turns and walks toward the kitchen to say goodbye to their son.

    Agatha is left alone.

    And only when she hears the door click shut behind her does she finally let her eyes fall closed.

    Just for a second.

    Just long enough to not feel everything at once.

    Agatha steps into the bathroom, closes the door behind her, and exhales slowly. The room is quiet, save for the low hum of plumbing behind the walls. The light is dim, soft — yet even that feels too harsh after the day she’s had. She reaches for the switch and flicks on only the small lamp by the mirror. In its glow, she sees her own reflection — tired, tight-lipped, with a faint trace of something she refuses to name.

    Her fingers are clutching a piece of fabric. The ribbon. That ribbon. Dark, smooth, slightly crumpled. She brushes her thumb over it, as if trying to summon whatever trace might still linger in the weave — residual heat, the echo of a touch that shouldn’t matter this much.

    Too many feelings.

    Too much she’s taught herself to suppress.

    Agatha stares at the ribbon a moment longer, then — without hesitation — drops it into the small bin beside the sink. It lands silently. She closes the lid, as if that final, quiet gesture might erase the weight of memory it carried.

    She straightens her back, rolling her shoulders to ease the tension. Her body aches — not in any specific place, but in the deep, heavy way that comes from hours of keeping too much inside. And now, alone, her head feels impossibly full. Thoughts flicker and burn like static.

    Rio.

    Rio and her voice, a drop of venom in every word. Rio and her touch — commanding, precise. Rio, whose nearness scorched in ways that felt too raw, too real.

    Agatha sucks in a breath and turns toward the shower.

    She doesn’t let herself think. Just reaches out, twists the knob. Water spills down in thin streams. Then, quickly, mechanically, she undresses — not noticing how the fabric brushes her skin, how it pools at her feet. She wants one thing only: to scrub away every trace that still clings to her from tonight.

    As she steps beneath the stream, at first all she feels is the warmth. It drapes over her shoulders like a heavy blanket, lulling her body into momentary surrender. The water slides down her skin, rinsing away the surface tension, the shallow remnants of emotion — but it doesn’t reach what’s buried deeper.

    She closes her eyes.

    Rio.

    The name echoes in her skull like a curse.

    Rio — her mouth. Her voice. Her cold, calculated words. Her fingers wrapped around Agatha’s throat. The way she looked at her, like power itself, and how Agatha… let her.

    That thought claws at her.

    Agatha breathes slowly, letting the water encase her. The heat should comfort, should soften her limbs — but instead, it makes everything sharper. More vivid. She feels too much. Feels herself, stripped raw, with no mask, no defense.

    Her eyes open slowly.

    She reaches for the faucet and turns it, just a little.

    The temperature shifts.

    Warm turns to tepid. Tepid to cool.

    Cool, then cold — until the water stings.

    The chill rushes across her skin in tight, prickling waves. Goosebumps rise instantly.

    It wakes her.

    Cold water was always her ritual for regaining control. For dragging herself back into clarity. Into focus.

    She feels too much. Thinks too much. She was always the one to command, to orchestrate, to know. But today… today she lost her grip. And Rio knew it.

    Rio fed on it.

    That’s what burns the most.

    She should never have let herself yield like that.

    She should never have allowed Rio to taste her weakness.

    Her fingers curl slowly into fists beneath the stream. Her jaw clenches. And on her lips forms a faint, almost cruel smile.

    She won’t make that mistake again.

    Never.

    Five minutes later, Agatha switches off the bathroom light—and just then, her eyes catch the clothes scattered across the floor. They lie exactly as they fell: thoughtlessly, messily. A rare sight for her. Always composed, meticulous, in control of every detail… and yet tonight, she allowed something else.

    She allowed herself to be human.

    To make a mess.

    To not immediately clean it up.

    And for a fleeting second, she doesn’t want to.

    There’s something oddly liberating about it.

    As if, for the first time in a very long while, she isn’t straightening every crease, stuffing every emotion into well-labeled drawers, pretending to be less than what she is. For a moment, she even considers leaving it like that until morning. As a reminder. But she knows herself. By tomorrow, everything will be folded. Order will return. Still, for tonight — let it be. Agatha steps into the bedroom, letting the silence wrap around her. The room is cast in half-light; a soft lamp on the nightstand glows amber, painting warm shadows across the walls. She walks over to the dresser, opens a drawer, and selects a set of lingerie.

    A deep, saturated shade of violet — dark, enigmatic, regal. A color she’s always associated with power. With authority. With something that belongs to her by right.

    She slips it on slowly, feeling the delicate fabric glide over her skin. Then reaches for the nightgown hanging from a nearby hook — the same rich hue. The material is whisper-light, flowing around her body like liquid silk. Over it, she drapes a silk robe — matching in tone — and ties it loosely at the waist.

    As she moves, damp strands of hair graze her back, leaving cool trails behind. She glances at the mirror — her eyes look darker tonight. Deeper. Filled with something she doesn’t care to name.

    Without thinking, she picks up a hair tie from the nightstand, sweeps her hair over one shoulder, and begins to braid it. The motions are practiced, steady, but looser than usual — less precise, more… human. She adjusts the robe, casts one more glance at her reflection — measured, assessing — then turns toward the door.

    Time to check on Nicholas.

    Her steps are soft, nearly silent, as she leaves the bedroom and walks down the hallway toward her son’s room.

    Agatha opens the door gently, letting a narrow strip of warm light from the corridor spill across the room. Inside, it’s dim and cozy, infused with the faint scent of children’s shampoo, soft bedding, and something sweet — probably the remnants of ice cream he’d somehow managed to smear on himself during dinner.

    He’s already in bed, tucked beneath the covers nearly up to his nose, but he isn’t asleep. His small eyes, wide with expectation, find her instantly, and the corners of his mouth curl into a quiet, content smile. “I knew you’d come,” he whispers, his voice low but certain, as if there could never have been any doubt.

    Agatha closes the door behind her, leaving only a narrow sliver of hallway light, and moves closer.

    “Oh? And why are you so sure of that?” she asks, sitting on the edge of his bed.

    Nicholas shrugs, as if the answer is obvious.

    “Because you always do.”

    She looks at him, and something tightens deep inside — a quiet, unreachable feeling. Her day had been long, exhausting, and she’s used to locking it all away. But here, in the dim light and his quiet certainty, for a moment — she lets herself breathe.

    She reaches out to tuck the blanket more snugly around his shoulders, as if it were the most sacred ritual in the world.

    “You’re not too tired, right?” His voice drops a bit lower now, tinged with hesitation.

    She holds her breath for a second.

    “A little,” she admits softly, but honestly.

    “You look tired.”

    Agatha lets out a small snort.

    “That’s very sweet of you to say.”

    Nicholas grins wide.

    “Just stating the obvious.”

    She rolls her eyes, but the faintest shadow of a smile brushes her lips. “Well then, maybe you can teach me how not to look tired?” “Sleep more. Eat pizza. Watch cartoons.”

    “Brilliant advice,” she drawls, raising one eyebrow.

    “I know.”

    For a while, they just look at each other. In this silence, there’s no need to explain, no need to pretend. Here, everything is simple.

    “Mama?”

    “Mhm?”

    “Is everything going to be okay?”

    She blinks, meeting his gaze.

    He doesn’t need to elaborate. He always senses when something’s off. And even if he doesn’t ask directly, his question… means more. She hesitates. Then slowly lowers herself onto the bed, lying beside him, feeling the warmth of his small body seeping through the fabric of her nightgown.

    “Of course,” she whispers, staring up at the ceiling.

    He doesn’t answer right away but gently tugs at her sleeve, urging her to turn toward him.

    “Promise?”

    Agatha freezes for a moment.

    She doesn’t believe in promises. They too often turn out to be just words, too easily broken under the weight of reality.

    But right now, that doesn’t matter.

    She looks into those wide, trusting eyes and slowly nods. “I promise.”

    His lashes flutter, eyes drifting closed. He believes her. He always believes her.

    She stays beside him for a while longer, listening to his breath settle into a soft, steady rhythm, feeling his tiny fingers relax against the edge of the blanket.

    Only then does she slip carefully out of bed, straighten the covers, switch off the night light, and pause in the doorway for one last look at his face, gently lit by the dim glow.

    Then she steps out, quietly closing the door behind her.

    Agatha moves down the stairs, the silence of the sleeping house settling thick around her. These walls have always felt too calm — a rhythmic, monotonous order where everything follows a pre-written script. But tonight, that order feels broken. Shattered. Each step on the staircase echoes in her mind too clearly, too loudly, like the sound of unspoken unrest. She enters the kitchen without turning on the lights. Her eyes are already adjusted to the dimness, to the soft glow of streetlamps spilling through the tall windows.

    Her movements are automatic. The fridge door swings open, casting a pale artificial light across the room. Agatha spots the half-eaten pizza box, pulls out a single slice, and nudges the door closed with her elbow. She eats it just like that—cold, stiff, with slightly stale edges. No appetite. No desire. Just because it’s easier. Easier than what? Than thinking. She leans against the kitchen counter, one hand holding the edge of her robe, the other raising the slice to her mouth, and she pauses. She wants to call.

    She wants to text.

    Every nerve ending in her body feels strung taut, every impulse pushing to break free, and it infuriates her.

    Rio.

    Agatha’s eyes narrow as she presses her back harder against the counter, biting into the crust as if that could somehow hold back the growing rage. She shouldn’t feel like this.

    Her world has always been structured. Predictable. Comfortable. It left no room for surprises, emotional outbursts, or unchecked reactions. Agatha knew how to stay in control. She mastered composure.

    But not with Rio.

    That woman had always known how to ignite something in her—something primal, something unexplored.

    Now it feels like there’s a grenade inside her with the pin already pulled, and all she can do is wait for it to detonate.

    She needs to regain control.

    She wants her calm back.

    Her routine.

    Her nothingness.

    But instead—anger.

    Sharp, acidic, dripping from her lips like venom.

    Because what Rio did was humiliating.

    Agatha closes her eyes, fingers curling tightly around the edge of the counter.

    She wants to write to her.

    To say everything that’s clawing at her throat.

    To curse her for that performance.

    For walking away like that.

    For making her feel.

    She slams the half-eaten slice back into the box and drags her tongue across her teeth.

    Somewhere deep down, Agatha already knows this game isn’t over. And that it’s only just beginning.

    She presses harder into the marble, as if she could merge with the cold surface, draw strength from it, contain the storm inside her chest. She wants her grip back—on herself, on the moment, on the carefully guarded void she used to live in.

    But she knows—they’re right back at the beginning.

    Where there’s no room for flirtation, no soft touches, no games stoked to a dangerous heat. Only venom now. Only sarcasm and sharp-edged words that cut deeper than knives.

    Everything snapped back.

    Her fingers clenched the edge of her robe, eyes still fixed on the pizza box as if it held some answer to this absurd spiral.

    But she knew.

    She knew it was her fault.

    Again.

    Just like with Emma.

    When she ruined everything, made it unbearable. When the marriage became nothing more than ink on paper, and all the rest—love, intimacy, even arguments—vanished, replaced by obligation.

    And now she was doing it again.

    Agatha closed her eyes, lips pressed tight.

    But she would never admit it.

    Not to Emma.

    Not to Rio.

    Not even to herself.

    Because she was Agatha Harkness.

    And Agatha Harkness does not make mistakes.

    Does not regret.

    Does not repent.

    At least, that’s how the reality is supposed to look.

    With a snap, she slammed the empty pizza box shut—too loud, too sharp for the dense silence of the night. A moment of irritated stillness, then she grabbed it and tossed it into the trash with surgical precision, as if restoring order to the kitchen would somehow fix the fault line cracking beneath her feet.

    She took a breath—slow, calculated—trying to inhale calm along with the air, but all she found was emptiness. Sticky, uncomfortable, crowding out her usual cold restraint.

    Agatha walked to the foyer. No hesitation. Her hands moved faster than her mind. Top shelf. Small box, long forgotten. Another promise to herself, now broken on the edge of this night.

    Her fingers found the smooth cardboard. She pulled out a pack of cigarettes, opened it, found the lighter. Tested its weight—light, nearly full. The cold met her skin the moment she stepped onto the porch. Darkness. The air smelled like autumn—wet earth, rotting leaves, sharp night air. Goosebumps crawled across her skin, but she didn’t turn back. A cigarette between her lips. A small flick—then a brief glow lit her face. She took the first drag, deeper than she should’ve. Her eyes fluttered closed. Her head cleared.

    Her hands stopped shaking.

    She hadn’t done this since the day Emma packed her last box and left. She hadn’t allowed herself. Control. Discipline. Useless principles that crumble the second they come into contact with real emotion.

    But now—she felt lighter.

    Lighter than she had all day.

    She smoked the first cigarette down to the filter, pinching it between her fingers before stubbing it out in the old ashtray she’d once forgotten to throw away. Almost immediately, she pulled out a second one, rolled it between her fingers, brought it to her lips. Inhale. Exhale. Her phone. Agatha reached for it and unlocked the screen. She didn’t need to check. She already knew.

    Still blocked.

    Her lips pressed into a hard line. She hated this. Hated how her mind clung to that absence. How the irritation flickered inside her, burning through the remnants of a fragile equilibrium.

    She opened Tinder.

    The motion was automatic, like she was searching for a new game, a new stage, a new distraction.

    Rio was online.

    Fuck.

    The sting of it was sharp. Something twisted in her chest, but Agatha didn’t let herself linger on it. She updated her profile.

    Four new photos.

    The first: a sharp black suit draped over bare shoulders. A corset cinched tight at the waist, jawline sharp, gaze colder than ice. Control. The second: soft and effortless — a loose black top with ties, her hair tousled. A faint smile on her lips, but her eyes still carried a bite. The third: a night shot. Agatha in a bar, half-lit. A glass of wine in her hand, the background blurred into shadow, leaving only her stare. Daring. The fourth: the boldest one. Just a silhouette. Long gloves, a tight dress, all in shadow. Nothing revealed — only the shape, the tease, the invitation. She updated the bio.

    “Flawless manners, not-so-flawless intentions. Classic in detail, chaos in thought. I love good books, sharp wine, and people who know how to play.”

    Save.

    She started swiping.

    Her fingers moved slowly, mechanically, scrolling through dozens of faces. Men, women, short bios, bad jokes, tired clichés. No one caught her attention. No one held it for longer than a second.

    She finished the cigarette, stubbed it out in the now-crowded ashtray, and took a deep breath of cold air. Refreshed the feed again. And then Tinder served her something new.

    A dark photo. Black gloves. Delicate wrists. A slender body in an evening gown — but no face. Just shadow, just a curve, just a promise. The bio was short:

    “You’re not afraid to lose, are you?”

    Agatha froze.

    Something inside her clicked—subtle, unmistakable.

    She slowly, deliberately, swiped right.

    It’s a match.

    The screen lit up.

    A message arrived almost instantly.

    She looked at it.

    And then realized—she was smiling. Just a little.

    Chapter 18: 3| Sunday

    Chapter Notes What can I say in my defense... Just don’t forget to breathe and maybe take something to calm your nerves 😉 See the end of the chapter for more notes “Fuck.”

    Rio jerks her hand back from the suitcase, wincing in pain. “I literally just got this manicure two days ago.”

    She glares at her nail — the perfect nude polish now ruined by a thin crack along the side. Wonderful. Just perfect.

    The sun is blazing, the wind plays with strands of her long hair, making them stick to her face, and the heavy suitcase with metal corners keeps catching on every uneven patch of the path like it’s mocking her. On the porch, lounging back in a woven chair, sits Regina. One hand holds a bottle of apple cider, the other — a chilled glass of it. She looks like she just stepped off the set of an ad for something expensive and tasteful. Her lips touch the rim for a lazy sip, but her eyes stay fixed on Rio — with that subtle smirk that says: she’s thoroughly enjoying the show. “How lovely to see that some things never change,” she drawls, setting the glass down and crossing her legs. “You still think you can do everything on your own.”

    “I can do everything on my own,” Rio grumbles, yanking the suitcase up onto the porch with a sharp pull.

    Right then, Heidi — her border collie — comes tearing past, eyes wild and tongue flapping. She darts across the yard, scattering a group of birds, then skids to a stop in front of Regina, kicking up a cloud of dust. Regina recoils, grimacing.

    “Fantastic. And here I was thinking this house had already survived its worst days.”

    “You know it’s temporary,” Rio says, out of breath, planting the suitcase by the door. “And I don’t have that much stuff, so movers were unnecessary.” Regina rolls her eyes and peers over the rim of her glass at Rio. “Right. So the four suitcases, three boxes, and one separate container for your damn boots — that’s not much?”

    “They’re special,” Rio replies coolly, arching a brow.

    “Well, if they’re special, then by all means — forgiven.” She takes another sip, watching as Rio yanks off her blazer and tosses it over the porch railing. Underneath — a fitted black shirt. Regina snorts, not even trying to hide her smirk.

    “You’re really putting effort into looking like you’ve got it all together…” Rio tosses her hair and gives a lazy smile.

    “And? Is it working?”

    Regina tilts her head like she’s giving it serious thought. “Considering you nearly died dragging your suitcase and cursed your manicure?” Regina shrugs. “Not particularly.”

    Rio rolls her eyes and grabs the second suitcase, heaving it up onto the porch.

    “You know, sometimes you could just say, ‘Rio, you’re magnificent.’” Regina takes another slow sip of her cider.

    “Why bother? You already know it.”

    Rio chuckles, shaking her head. They both know each other too well — years of sarcastic remarks, playful jabs, and unconditional acceptance. Still.

    Regina watches as her friend hauls the last suitcase across the threshold. “So, Miss ‘I Don’t Need Movers,’ is that everything?”

    “Yup. You may now award me a medal.”

    Regina raises her glass.

    “This is all I’ve got.”

    Rio sighs and folds her arms.

    “Well then — here’s to a new chapter.”

    Without warning, she snatches the cider bottle right out of Regina’s hand, takes a big swig, and saunters into the house with a smug grin. Regina squints after her, but doesn’t protest.

    She just follows with a laugh.

    This really was the start of something new.

    The crisp April air is cool against her skin, but Regina’s backyard feels warm and inviting. A soft yellow light spills from the house’s windows, gently illuminating the wooden deck and the darkening garden beyond. The air carries the scent of damp grass, apple cider, and something smoky— maybe from a neighbor’s fire pit, or perhaps just the wind picking up remnants of other people’s evenings.

    Rio is curled up on a cushioned garden sofa, wrapped in a dark gray blanket. She’s wearing a black tracksuit — cozy, warm, effortless. Her hair is pulled into a messy bun, with a few loose strands swaying lazily in the breeze. Her reading glasses have slipped to the tip of her nose, but she doesn’t bother pushing them back up — she’s too engrossed in the words on the screen.

    Next to her, Heidi — the border collie — is dozing in a tight ball, already claiming this yard as her own. Her chest rises and falls with steady rhythm, her tail twitching now and then in some dream-bound chase. Rio flips the page on her e-reader and continues reading aloud: “...and, naturally, it was exactly then that the door flew open, revealing—” She pauses, leaning back into the sofa and lifting her gaze over the top of her glasses.

    “Wanna guess who it was?”

    Across from her, Regina lounges in a deep chair, bundled in a burgundy blanket. One hand cradles a glass of cider, the other scrolls lazily through her phone. She smirks slightly without looking up.

    “Let me guess. Someone the protagonist absolutely did not expect to see?” Rio squints, pretending to ponder it.

    “You’re frighteningly perceptive.”

    Regina sets her glass down and finally meets her eyes.

    “You know, it’s impossible to read with you. Everything turns into a performance.”

    Rio stretches with a languid grin.

    “Well, yeah. You know I live for the drama.”

    She sets the e-reader down on the armrest of the sofa, pulls off her glasses, and rubs the bridge of her nose, closing her eyes with quiet fatigue. For a few moments, the backyard falls into silence — only the soft rustle of leaves in the breeze, Heidi’s slow, sleepy breaths, and the distant hum of a passing car break the stillness.

    Regina watches her, noting the way Rio absentmindedly swirls the cider in her glass, seemingly hypnotized by the way the light catches in the amber liquid.

    “Are you happy?” she asks suddenly, her voice unhurried.

    Rio cracks one eye open and glances at her.

    “With what?”

    “With how everything turned out.”

    Rio freezes.

    The silence thickens—dense, a little sticky.

    She doesn’t answer right away. First, she takes a slow sip of cider, feeling the cool liquid slide down her throat.

    Then she exhales.

    And smirks.

    “I’m not sure ‘turned out’ is the right phrase.”

    Regina raises an eyebrow, watching her with that look — the one that never bodes well.

    “But you didn’t try to change anything, did you?”

    Rio shrugs, eyes back on her glass.

    “I’m not an idiot.”

    Regina nods, as if confirming something to herself.

    “No, you’re a coward.”

    Rio snaps her head around, her gaze sharpening.

    “Excuse me?”

    Regina doesn’t flinch. She picks up her glass, takes another sip, and says lazily: “You heard me.”

    Rio narrows her eyes, her fingers tightening around the glass. “That’s a stupid analysis.”

    “Of course it’s stupid,” Regina says. “But you know what? Lately, you’ve been too careful. Too…” — she gestures vaguely, searching for the word — “composed.”

    Rio scoffs, tossing her head back.

    “God forbid. I’ve matured. Sound the alarm.”

    Regina leans forward, her gaze sharpening.

    “You can be mature, Rio. Just don’t lie to me.”

    She pauses for a beat. Then, placing her glass on the table, she says quietly: “Do you still flinch when you hear her name?”

    Rio freezes.

    Her hand tightens around the glass.

    Regina sees it.

    But says nothing else.

    Just watches.

    Waits.

    Rio closes her eyes. Inhales deeply. Then again.

    And then, without a word, she sets her glass down, rises, and walks back into the house.

    Heidi, sensing the shift in her owner’s mood, lifts her head and sniffs at the air, alert.

    Regina follows Rio with her eyes.

    And then slowly, quietly, smiles to herself.

    She got her answer.

    Even if Rio didn’t say a word.

    Regina doesn’t look away from the door for a few seconds. Then, exhaling, she reaches for her phone on the armrest of the chair.

    A new message.

    She unlocks the screen, eyes scanning the lines automatically. Her lips curve.

    “You know I hate apple cider. But your lips make it taste better.”

    Damn.

    How good it feels to read something like that.

    Regina closes her eyes for a moment, letting herself savor the message like it’s something tangible resting on her fingertips.

    A slow smile plays on her lips. She tilts her head slightly, presses her mouth together — as if tasting the afterglow.

    Then, without overthinking it, she types a reply:

    “Then maybe I’ll make you fall in love with it.”

    She hits send, puts the phone aside, and leans back, soaking in the quiet. Silence.

    Soft, cozy, steeped in evening warmth and the scent of cider. But something makes her open her eyes.

    Regina feels it — a stare.

    She turns her head and locks eyes with Heidi, who’s watching her closely, almost judgmentally.

    Regina narrows her eyes.

    “What?”

    Heidi doesn’t blink. Just keeps staring, intense and unyielding. “What?” Regina leans back, throwing her hands up. “You’re a dog. You’re not supposed to get this.”

    Heidi tilts her head.

    Regina blinks.

    Feels… caught.

    “Oh God, don’t look at me like that.” She sighs, takes another sip from her glass. “You’re worse than a therapist.”

    Heidi yawns, as if completely unimpressed.

    Regina rolls her eyes.

    “Just don’t rat me out, alright?”

    She reaches over and scratches behind Heidi’s ear, letting herself smile into the evening hush.

    Only she and the dog know the truth.

    For now.

    The door opens.

    Rio doesn’t slam it, but her entrance is so abrupt, so lightning-quick, that even Heidi lifts her head and flicks her ears in alert.

    Rio’s hair is down, the waves cascading over her shoulders, catching the lamplight in warm shades of caramel and chocolate.

    She’s not in the mood.

    Her jaw is clenched, eyes sharp, movements brisk and tight, charged with a tension that has nowhere to go.

    Rio picks up her glass from the table, brings it to her lips, and downs it in one motion — feeling the cider leave a fading trail of warmth behind. Then she sets the empty glass down and turns to Regina.

    Her voice cuts through the night — sharp, curt, laced with strain. “I’m not the coward here.”

    Regina lifts her gaze slowly, deliberately. One brow arches with lazy precision.

    Rio clicks her tongue, grabs the cider bottle, uncorks it with a flick that drips irritation and practiced elegance, and pours herself another glass. But she doesn’t sit.

    She stands.

    Stands over Regina — tense, angry, teetering on the edge of an explosion. “Or do you think it’s normal to just quit the university without even talking about it?”

    She doesn’t say the name.

    But they both know who she means.

    Regina keeps looking at her, quiet and steady. Her glass remains untouched. Her posture is all control.

    Unlike Rio’s.

    “You’re drunk,” Regina says evenly, not a flicker of change in her expression.

    Rio scoffs.

    “I haven’t even started.”

    She takes a long sip, holding the glass just a little too tightly between her fingers.

    Regina exhales slowly, sets her phone on the table, takes a sip from her own glass, and only then replies — voice calm, steady, almost languid. “She quit because she wanted to. And you know that.”

    Rio taps her nail against the rim of the glass, rolls her eyes, exhales through her nose like a cornered animal.

    She can’t settle.

    The thoughts won’t leave her alone.

    And she hates that she cares this much.

    “She didn’t even try to talk to me.”

    Regina blinks, leans back lazily in her chair, doesn’t argue. But Rio gets it.

    She has no rebuttal.

    And that only pisses her off more.

    Rio takes another sharp sip, eyes closed, trying to push the frustration down — it doesn’t work.

    “I haven’t seen her in, what, a year and a half? Two?”

    Regina’s face doesn’t change.

    “You know exactly how long it’s been.”

    Rio lets out a hollow laugh — dry, bitter, without a trace of humor. “A year and eight months.”

    Her voice is low, laced with venom. She stares into her glass like the answer might be floating at the bottom.

    “I know that.”

    She lifts her eyes.

    “But do you think she knows?”

    A pause hangs in the air.

    Rio leans forward, lips curling into a tired, bitter smirk. “No. She doesn’t give a damn.”

    Regina doesn’t reply right away.

    She takes a slow sip, sets her glass down, then looks straight at Rio. “But you’re the one who blocked her.”

    She says it calmly — almost gently — and it’s that very softness that makes the words land like a slap.

    “You didn’t even let her speak.”

    Rio blinks sharply.

    Her fingers tighten around the glass — something in her chest does too. But she doesn’t show it.

    She takes another sip, cider burning her tongue, and turns her gaze away. She lowers the glass, runs her fingertips across the surface, as if testing its smoothness.

    “I didn’t want it to end like that.”

    Her voice is quieter now. But behind that calm, something feels cracked. Regina studies her for a long moment, then simply shrugs and takes another slow sip.

    “You know where she lives.”

    Rio lets out a short laugh — bitter, hollow.

    She tilts her head back, eyes closed for a second, then stares into her glass like the answer’s hidden somewhere at the bottom and she’s not sure she wants to find it.

    “She ran.”

    The words are even, but there's a weight in them she can’t disguise. She lifts her gaze and meets Regina’s, tired but firm.

    “That was her choice.”

    Regina stays quiet, watching Rio like someone observing the aftermath of a storm — the part where silence settles in, but the damage is done. She finishes her cider, sets the glass on the table, tilts her head slightly and says — calm, but not indulgent: “Are you sure?”

    Rio snaps her eyes to her, but doesn’t answer right away. The irritation flares in her gaze, then fades just as fast — replaced with something heavier.

    Exhaustion.

    She presses her lips together, takes another sip of wine, then exhales deeply and sets the glass on the table.

    “I don’t want to talk about this anymore.”

    Her voice is steady, but there’s a clear finality to it — sharp and unquestionable.

    Regina doesn’t argue. She just gives a lazy shrug, accepting the decision. Rio grabs the glass in one hand and snaps her fingers lightly with the other to get Heidi’s attention.

    “Come on, girl.”

    The dog lifts her head with a yawn, stretches, then obediently gets up and follows her.

    Rio doesn’t look back as she walks into the house.

    She closes the door behind her, cutting off the conversation, cutting off the evening, cutting off something inside herself that she refuses to acknowledge.

    Rio lies on her side, staring at the window, though her gaze doesn’t focus on anything. Outside, the night drifts by, smearing the city lights across the dark, but inside her everything remains tense, sharp, too alive to fade into shadow.

    She’s not asleep.

    She can’t sleep.

    Heidi breathes evenly beside the bed, curled up in her own spot. Rio knows that if she so much as breathes too heavily or shifts the blanket, the dog will lift her head and give her that quiet, questioning look.

    “Why aren’t you sleeping?”

    She wouldn’t know what to answer.

    Her body feels heavy with exhaustion, but her mind keeps running at full speed, as if afraid that if she lets it slow down, if she dares to fall asleep — she’ll see her again.

    Agatha.

    That name echoes inside her like an old wound that’s healed over but still throbs if you touch it just right.

    Rio exhales, clutching the blanket tighter between her knees, trying to find some kind of stability in the unease.

    She hates it.

    She hates how her thoughts keep circling back to her.

    How memories seep into her mind like poison she willingly let under her skin.

    How long has it been?

    A year and eight months.

    Rio knows it exactly.

    Every day. Every month.

    And not a single one of those days brought her any relief. Brief flings?

    Too shallow.

    Too quick.

    Too not Agatha.

    They left no mark.

    But Agatha did.

    She remembers every touch.

    The weight of her gaze.

    Her hands gripping her waist.

    Her voice, sinking into her skin like a bruise—

    A bruise shaped like their last encounter.

    Last?

    Rio brings her fingers to her temple, pressing hard, as if she could pull that thought out of her head.

    But it doesn’t come out.

    It stays.

    Just like the anger never left.

    The anger she felt when she found out Agatha was leaving. That she was finishing her last week.

    Resigning for “personal reasons.”

    That they would never see each other again.

    Rio didn’t go to her.

    Didn’t write.

    Didn’t say a word.

    Neither did Agatha.

    They both made the only choice they could.

    They both pretended the other didn’t exist.

    And all that time—

    Rio was furious.

    Furious that Agatha had been the one to run first.

    Furious that she didn’t come looking.

    Didn’t write.

    Didn’t say a thing.

    Angry that maybe—just maybe—it really didn’t matter to Agatha. Rio rolls onto her back, staring up at the ceiling, feeling something heavy and slow build inside her, like wet cement poured straight into her chest. She swallows, eyes fluttering shut, but there’s no peace there. Only a dull, pulsing truth: She never actually stopped waiting for her.

    Slowly, Rio reaches for the nightstand, her fingers grazing the surface until they find her phone. The screen flares to life, casting cold light across the room’s shadows.

    She doesn’t think.

    Just moves.

    Instinct, muscle memory — password, home screen, the familiar Instagram icon.

    She hasn’t opened it in forever.

    Hadn’t checked.

    What for?

    But now, in the middle of the night, when sleep refuses to come and every thought loops back to that one damn name, she can’t stop herself. Search bar.

    The profile appears too fast.

    Too familiar.

    But it’s empty.

    The last post is nearly two years old.

    No new updates.

    No lectures, seminars, or press.

    Just old photos.

    Rio grips the phone tighter, scrolling down, thumb moving slower now, more cautious.

    She knows these images by heart.

    Knows the sharp-edged aesthetic.

    The tailored suits.

    The crisp collars.

    That frozen elegance in her gaze.

    Knows what Agatha looked like when they first met — severe, unsmiling, with eyes that sliced straight through you and never blinked. She remembers how Agatha looked toward the end —

    the lines of her face a little softer, her hair slightly more tousled, her lips curved in a real smile in a few photos.

    And yet…

    Even after a year and eight months, even after their silent war, even after all her attempts to scrub the memory clean, something tightens in her chest the moment she sees those eyes. Blue.

    Deep.

    Too piercing.

    Rio forgets to breathe.

    Just like back then.

    Just like always.

    She keeps scrolling.

    Reads captions she already knows by heart, yet reads them again anyway. Her favorite is still the black-and-white one —

    Agatha in a sharp dark suit, chin slightly raised, that carved expression on her face.

    But the eyes…

    God, those eyes.

    Rio slowly drags a fingertip across the screen, as if that could soothe the ache blooming inside her. A painful tenderness spreads in her chest — familiar, maddening.

    She’s angry.

    At herself.

    At Agatha.

    At the stupid, undying instinct to reach for her.

    Even now. Even after she’s gone.

    She didn’t leave her a single chance.

    But still…

    It feels like Rio lost her.

    She exhales sharply and forces herself to lock the screen. The phone drops back onto the nightstand with a dull thud. Her eyes close.

    But those blue eyes stay with her, clear as ever.

    No.

    Not now.

    This isn’t what she should be thinking about.

    Not about the past.

    Not about what can’t be changed anymore.

    About the future.

    About the fact that now she’s here — in Regina’s house, with her dog, her suitcases, no apartment of her own, and absolutely no solid plan.

    Her lease ended, the landlord decided to sell the place, and Rio…

    Rio didn’t find anything in time.

    Didn’t want to rush.

    Didn’t even try hard enough, if she’s being honest.

    And now she’s temporarily crashing at her — goddamn brilliant — best friend’s place.

    She glances at Heidi, curled up like a fuzzy comma on the floor beside her bed.

    At least she doesn’t care.

    All she needs is Rio nearby.

    Rio exhales and lets herself fall back against the pillow. She’s not complaining.

    She’s not panicking.

    But still…

    Life could really stand to be a little less chaotic.

    Chapter End Notes Alright, here’s what I have to tell you, guys: 1. I’m giving you a few days to adjust to the new situation — and then I’ll keep translating!

    2. Heidi is actually my real-life dog 😄

    3. Just like Rio and her dog, we’re also living in emigration — and we definitely know what it’s like to lose an apartment.

    Chapter 19: Monday

    Chapter Notes Over the past few days, I’ve been incredibly angry.

    And as you might have noticed, I’ve closed access to this fanfic for unregistered users.

    Because here’s the truth: I genuinely believe criticism should be fair and grounded. If someone feels the need to insult me or my work — fine. But at least have the decency to do it openly. Don’t hide behind anonymity.

    I’ve also created a new Twitter account where you can reach me directly, if you want to talk like real people: https://x.com/marickmus?t=R9dreXnFwe2jdoI_c9ww5A&s=09 I don’t get paid for writing. I do it because I love it. Because I need it. A year ago, I was lonely. I felt empty. Then, in November, I watched this show for the first time — and for the first time in ten years, I felt something spark. I was inspired. I started writing again. That’s what this story is. It’s not just a fic. It’s a part of how I came back to life.

    So yes — you’re welcome to ask questions, to analyze, to disagree, even. But don’t try to hurt me.

    I’ve already been through enough.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes Rio ties the final knot on her white socks, pulling them over the sheer tights, and leans back, pressing her palms against the bed. The room still smells of her morning perfume — warm, woody-spicy notes with hints of tobacco and honey hang in the air, blending with the faint aroma of coffee she finished about fifteen minutes ago.

    Soft light filters through the half-open blinds, catching the dark sunglasses resting on the nightstand. Rio reaches for them, puts them on with a practiced motion, and checks her reflection in the mirror. Today, she looks perfect.

    Not flashy or dramatic — just like herself. The self she had finally found and claimed. No more awkward attempts to fit someone else’s expectations, no more bouncing between styles that never truly belonged to her. Rio now knows exactly who she is.

    The black blazer with slightly extended shoulders fits flawlessly, shaping a silhouette that strikes the perfect balance between severity and edge. Underneath — a crisp white shirt with a sharp collar, topped with a delicate gold chain that catches the light when she moves. A mini-skirt, sheer black tights, chunky shoes with thick soles. And, of course, the hair — long, sleek, perfectly pulled into a high ponytail, divided into neat sections. The contrast between the dark roots and the lighter ends gave her entire look an even sharper edge.

    Rio smirks slightly.

    More mature? Maybe. More grounded? Definitely.

    Now she knows exactly what she wants.

    She grabs her phone from the nightstand and checks the screen — nothing important. Not a single message that could throw her off. Everything is just as it should be. She drops the phone into her chain-strap purse, slings it over her shoulder, and glances toward the bed.

    The sheets are lightly creased, and Heidi is still sleeping peacefully on her dog bed by the wall, her fur gently rising and falling with each breath. Rio runs a hand down her thigh, giving herself one last once-over, then heads for the door.

    She knows it’s Monday.

    A day she intends to start without chaos.

    Because after her classes at the university, she has a date. Regina is already in the kitchen.

    She’s holding her phone, the screen casting a soft glow in the dim morning light. She scrolls lazily through her feed, sipping coffee from her favorite mug with a gold rim.

    Rio walks past her, grabs a bottle of juice from the fridge, and pours herself a glass. She notices Regina briefly glancing over the top of her phone. “Got a date?” Regina asks, still mostly focused on her screen. “Yeah,” Rio replies flatly.

    “Hm.” Regina pretends not to care, but the corners of her mouth twitch upward.

    Rio rolls her eyes, downs the juice, and sets the glass in the sink. “What, you want to talk about it like we’re in some cheap sitcom?” “Oh, no,” Regina drawls, crossing her legs and lounging back in her chair. “I’m just enjoying the fact that, for once, you don’t reek of alcohol and regret.”

    Rio snorts but doesn’t reply.

    She opens a cupboard, pulls out a small metal tin of chewing gum, gives it a shake, tosses it in her palm like she’s weighing it.

    “You coming home late?” Regina asks, throwing her a quick glance. “Maybe.”

    “Then I’ll lock the door.”

    “And toss my stuff out on the lawn?”

    “Of course. So next time, you’ll actually hire movers.”

    Rio smirks. She pops a piece of gum in her mouth, slides the tin back into the cupboard, and heads for the door.

    “Have a nice day,” Regina calls after her.

    Rio just waves her off as she walks out the door.

    She closes it behind her and heads down the steps, pulling her keys from her pocket. The cold morning air brushes against her skin pleasantly, and rays of sunlight sneak through the scattered clouds, painting her morning in shades of something almost carefree.

    She’s no longer dependent on buses, schedules, or waiting. No more morning crowds, frozen fingers, or overhearing strangers’ conversations. Now she has a car.

    Her gaze slides over the deep blue body of her baby. She trails her fingers lightly along the hood before getting behind the wheel. It’s another one of those small victories she’s earned over the last year and a half. Now she can afford more. Now she knows how.

    Rio slips the key into the ignition, grabs her phone, and quickly connects to the car's audio system. A routine gesture — part of her morning ritual. The screen lights up, and the first familiar chords begin to play. “When I Kissed the Teacher.”

    “God, this band is eternal,” Rio mutters with a satisfied grin, turning the volume up as she eases onto the road.

    She hums along, fingers tapping rhythmically on the steering wheel. Her mood is good.

    So good that even the morning traffic doesn’t bother her. Even the prospect of spending another full day at the university doesn’t annoy her. She considers stopping for coffee but, after glancing at the dashboard clock, decides she’ll manage without it today.

    Everything feels like it’s in place.

    She drives toward the university, singing along to her favorite song, feeling like the day is hers.

    Still unaware of exactly who will walk into the big gymnasium just a few hours from now.

    Rio strolls lazily down the hallway, a paper coffee cup in one hand and a half-eaten granola bar in the other — not because she plans to finish it, but because it adds to the look. The exhausted professor vibe. The barely- holding-it-together academic who balances work and tiny joys like caffeine and sugar.

    The university air is thick, filled with the usual hum of students making the most of their breaks — talking over each other, scrolling through their phones, chugging overpriced coffee from the nearest café. Rio turns the corner and heads straight toward Lilia’s classroom, her steps quick and her mood sour. This whole farewell speech circus is pissing her off. Thirty minutes of stiff ceremony, and for what? Just so she can miss something truly important — her cigarette break before the next class. She pushes the door open with her hip, not bothering to knock. Politeness is officially canceled today.

    “I swear, I officially hate this place even more now,” she announces instead of a greeting, taking a dramatic sip of her coffee and raising the cup like a toast.

    Lilia, sitting at her desk, barely glances up from her laptop, one eyebrow lifting ever so slightly.

    “Let me guess — the morning was too peaceful?”

    “Exactly,” Rio rolls her eyes and tosses the granola bar onto the nearest table like it personally offended her. “And now, thanks to this ridiculous performance, I won’t even have time to smoke before my next class.” She says it with the kind of expression you'd expect from someone whose world just came crashing down — and Lilia chuckles, shaking her head. “You could’ve just skipped it.”

    “Oh yeah? And then listen to a lecture from admin about professionalism?” Rio takes another sip of her coffee and leans lazily against the edge of the desk. “No thanks. I already see their faces more than I’d like to.” Lilia shrugs but doesn’t argue. They both know that despite all the sarcasm, Rio always shows up to these events. She likes to keep her finger on the pulse.

    “All right, let’s go,” Lilia finally says, closing her laptop. “The sooner it starts, the sooner it ends.”

    “You say that like I won’t spend the entire time cursing the fact that I’m alive.”

    “You always do.”

    Rio just smirks, pushing off the desk with a slight bow of her hand — “after you.”

    They step into the hallway and head toward the gym, unaware of what kind of performance is actually waiting for them there.

    The gym buzzed like a hive.

    Students, faculty, a few members of administration — the whole place was filled with a low murmur of hushed conversations, the occasional laugh, and the shuffling of feet. Some people were trying to grab seats up front, others hung back by the walls, clearly hoping it would all be over before it even began.

    Rio and Lilia walked in, and Rio immediately scanned the room with a skeptical look.

    “Looks like the entire damn institution came to say goodbye to the old man,” she muttered, sipping her coffee.

    “Well, he did run the university for fifteen years,” Lilia offered with a shrug.

    “So what? I’d happily retire too after a job like that,” Rio waved her cup in a vague gesture. “He’s probably thrilled.”

    Her gaze drifted over the crowd, taking in familiar faces. Surprisingly, even some students seemed genuinely interested. Maybe it was a big deal to them — a change in leadership always came with a dash of intrigue. Up on the makeshift stage — really just a slightly raised platform — the old director was already adjusting his glasses and getting ready to speak. “Well, here we go,” Lilia sighed, folding her arms.

    “Bracing for impact,” Rio rolled her eyes.

    She took one last sip of her coffee, then crushed the empty cup in her hand and tossed it onto the nearest bench.

    At that moment, the director’s voice echoed through the gym. “Dear students, colleagues, friends…”

    It had begun.

    Rio lazily shifted her gaze to the stage, mentally preparing herself for a long-winded speech full of gratitude, pomp, and dry lists of achievements. But the real bombshell hadn’t dropped yet.

    Because the most important name in this room still hadn’t been mentioned. Rio barely held back a smirk as she felt her phone vibrate in her pocket. She pulled it out, hoping it was something important — and inwardly smiled when she saw the name.

    Wanda.

    She hadn’t even realized when it had become a habit — exchanging messages with her throughout the day. It had started with a harmless comment under a photo, then flowed into chats about movies, music, and eventually… more personal topics.

    Wanda: No, I actually prefer Italian cuisine.

    Rio bit her lip, her thumb lazily gliding across the screen. Rio: That’s unexpected. I figured you’d go for something more refined. The reply came almost instantly.

    Wanda: You know how much I enjoy pleasure. And a good plate of pasta? That’s pure orgasm for the taste buds.

    Rio snorted softly, typing back quickly.

    Rio: Let’s see how much you really know. You pick the place. She hit send and, without looking up, smiled to herself.

    What exactly was she expecting from this date?

    Something light. Maybe romantic.

    But most importantly — hot, consistent sex.

    No games.

    No expectations that would eventually end in heartbreak.

    Just what Rio needed.

    Her fingers idly skimmed over Wanda’s profile again. Even through photos, it was obvious — she knew what she wanted, and she knew how to get it. The phone vibrated once more.

    Wanda: You’re going to regret that challenge.

    Rio smirked and slipped the phone back into her pocket.

    “Something important?” Lilia asked lazily, casting her a sidelong glance. “Life or death,” Rio said with a shrug.

    Lilia just shook her head and crossed her arms, and Rio turned her attention back to the stage.

    What she didn’t know — what none of them knew —

    was that her personal life was about to collide, violently, with a past she’d spent over a year trying to outrun.

    The director’s voice finally began to slow, taking on that familiar final tone — the kind that usually meant the end of a painfully long speech was near. Rio closed her eyes briefly, just to stop herself from rolling them in full view of the crowd.

    Lilia stood beside her, arms crossed, her expression matching half the faculty — patient, but clearly bored.

    “…and with deep gratitude, I want to thank all of you for these years, for your commitment to education, for your contribution to the university’s growth,” the director went on, his voice starting to dissolve into the soft murmur of the hall.

    Oh, come on, old man. Wrap it up already.

    Rio’s eyes lazily drifted across the rows of students. Some had fully checked out, whispering to each other. Others were glued to their phones. “And now,” the director continued, straightening his shoulders, “it’s time to pass the torch. I’m confident my successor will meet this responsibility with grace. This is someone with an impressive track record, extensive experience, and a unique vision for education.”

    Rio yawned and turned her head away.

    "Overacting," Rio muttered to Lilia, who simply nodded in silent agreement.

    "I am proud to announce that this person will be the next director," the former headmaster continued, his voice gaining a ceremonious edge. "Please welcome..."

    He paused. Dramatically.

    Rio arched an eyebrow.

    Come on, just say the name and let’s get this over with.

    But instead of a name, he coughed, awkwardly looked to the side, and finally added: "…who, unfortunately, is running a bit late."

    A ripple of chuckles and murmurs spread through the hall. Rio blinked.

    Lilia let out a soft snort.

    "Promising start," she said dryly, tilting her head.

    "Of course," Rio scoffed, rolling her eyes. "Most responsible director ever. Already a fan."

    She reached for her phone again just as the gymnasium doors began to open slowly.

    At first, no one noticed — too much noise, too much movement, the ever- present hum of conversations. But something shifted in the air. Something subtle, almost imperceptible, yet instinctively felt.

    Rio sensed it first. Not footsteps — a presence.

    The noise in the room didn’t stop all at once, but it started to settle, like heavy fog sinking lower and lower. Heads began to turn. Someone leaned toward a friend. A quiet murmur passed from one row to the next. And then — silence.

    Rio slowly lifts her gaze.

    She shouldn’t be surprised.

    And yet, something tightens inside her, a rush of heat crawling up her spine, leaving behind a trail of cold.

    Agatha.

    She stands in the doorway, and for a moment it seems like the world behind her blurs — as if there’s a thin, invisible line separating her from everyone else.

    That same posture — straight, deliberate, impeccably composed. That same look — calm, but sharp. Cutting. Piercing straight through. But something has changed.

    Her hair is swept into a high, elegant knot — loose enough to suggest carelessness, but precise enough to betray control. A few strands have slipped free, but they don’t ruin the look — they animate it. As if there’s a hairline crack in all that control.

    Her outfit is strict, but carries a hint of theatricality. An elongated blazer with structured shoulders frames her silhouette, making it even more commanding. Underneath — a crisp white shirt with a high collar, buttoned all the way to the top.

    At her chest hangs an antique medallion — delicate, refined, but unmistakably symbolic.

    The dark, wide-leg trousers fall in soft folds, flowing with her every movement. Not overly formal, but formal enough to make one thing clear — the new head of the university has arrived.

    Agatha steps forward.

    And even the air seems to pause.

    Rio watches her.

    Too closely. Too carefully. Too… intensely.

    She shouldn’t be looking at her like that.

    But her eyes have already caught every detail, every movement, every shade of this cinematic reappearance.

    Agatha walks through the gym with measured steps, unhurried, allowing everyone to feel her presence. Her heels echo clearly, each strike against the polished floor marking time — and with every step, something in the space seems to shift.

    She says nothing.

    Not yet.

    But she doesn’t need to.

    This moment belongs to her.

    The former director — who just seconds ago had announced her delay — falls silent, fastening a polite smile to his face. But there’s tension in his eyes. Some people in the crowd exchange glances; others can’t look away. And Rio sees it.

    She sees how both students and faculty watch Agatha — not as someone simply new, but as an event.

    She shouldn’t care.

    Absolutely shouldn’t.

    But something tightens inside her as she notices how effortlessly Agatha belongs in this space — as if it had always been hers.

    No awkward pauses. No signs of unease.

    She is the center of attention. Not because she tries to be — but because it simply couldn’t be any other way.

    And Rio watches as people begin to shift in rhythm with her presence. A student straightens subtly in his seat, not even realizing he wants to look more composed. Professors catch her gaze and instantly adjust their expressions — from bored to politely engaged.

    Agatha hasn’t just entered the room.

    She’s changed it.
