    But that’s not even the most frustrating part.

    What irritates Rio most is the fact that she doesn’t feel anything. No needles beneath the skin. No raw nerves. No storm gathering inside. She’s just observing.

    Detached. Calm.

    As if Agatha Harkness were merely someone she used to know. Just a name from the past.

    Just a memory.

    Just…

    Rio blinks.

    Of course.

    If it were that simple, her heart wouldn’t be beating this fast. The silence in the room isn’t just awkward — it’s the kind where even the faintest breath becomes audible. Agatha walks slowly toward the center, unfazed by the tension, in no hurry to break the hush. Her gaze sweeps across the hall, gliding over faces — professors, students, row by row — as if she’s memorizing every detail.

    Rio sits straight, composed, but her fingers tighten slightly around the coffee cup.

    Agatha always knew how to enter a room as if she already owned it. It wasn’t a performance, or some need to prove herself to others. It was organic. A kind of power steeped in natural charisma, wrapped in outward cold — beneath which a fire always smoldered.

    But what catches Rio most — is her expression.

    Calm. Serene. In absolute control.

    And only those who truly knew her might have noticed the faint trace of something else flickering deep in her blue eyes.

    Rio freezes for a moment.

    She doesn’t see her.

    She can’t see her — not in a crowd like this. Too many faces. Too many eyes fixed on the woman who’s suddenly become the gravitational center of this place.

    And yet…

    Something inside Rio tightens. A small, sour twist in her chest — not sharp, not violent. Just… unrelenting.

    It isn’t anger.

    And it’s not irritation.

    It’s something else.

    Something she wants to pull away from — but can’t.

    Agatha doesn’t rush to speak. She lets the moment linger, lets the air soak in her presence.

    And then, after several long seconds, her voice slices cleanly through the silence.

    “I know I wasn’t expected.”

    And just like that — it’s all there in her voice.

    Confidence.

    Frost.

    Play.

    Rio slowly lowers her gaze to the coffee cup in her hands. Here it is.

    It’s begun.

    Agatha lets the words hang in the air, lets them settle over the crowd — not loudly, but with precision, like a whisper that still manages to echo. She steps forward again, her eyes drifting over the faces in front of her as if she’s savoring each reaction. Her fingers clasp lightly around her opposite wrist — a gesture that’s both casual and commanding.

    “But since we’re here…” she continues, lifting her chin slightly, “let’s begin.”

    The former director, whose position she has now inherited, clears his throat awkwardly and steps aside. The atmosphere shifts — not with fear, but something else. Denser. Sharper.

    Anticipation.

    Curiosity.

    Rio notices students exchanging glances, a few professors tilting their heads ever so slightly, all quietly appraising the woman standing at the center of it all.

    Agatha Harkness.

    A name that hadn’t been spoken within these walls for more than a year. Her departure had been a subject of gossip.

    Her return — a shock.

    And yet, here she is.

    Rio stays perfectly still, letting her mind catch up to what’s unfolding before her.

    She’s not naïve.

    She knows full well — if Agatha is here, if she’s come back — it’s not without reason.

    But why?

    Why now?

    Her fingers tap lightly against the paper cup, but her gaze remains cold. Inside, everything is churning, pulsing, beating to a rhythm she doesn’t like. She hadn’t let herself think about Agatha too often.

    Only sometimes.

    Only at night.

    Only in moments of weakness.

    But now reality has made its move again, and Rio feels something coil tight inside her chest.

    Meanwhile, Agatha scans the room slowly.

    “You are all part of this university,” she says, calm but firm. “And I expect the same from you as you expect from me.”

    Rio tilts her head slightly, watching every movement, every syllable. Agatha is no longer a dean.

    She’s above that now.

    And any chance of avoiding this collision — is gone.

    Agatha takes another step forward, letting her last words settle into the silence. The quiet that fills the room isn’t awkward — it’s sharp, focused. The kind people lean into. Listen through.

    Rio can feel it against her skin.

    She doesn’t move. Her eyes stay locked on the woman standing center stage. Everything in her — posture, voice, presence — exudes intention. Agatha knows exactly what she’s doing. What she’s saying. And it’s infuriating.

    Rio bites the inside of her cheek, her fingers tightening around the coffee cup.

    “Now that the formalities are out of the way…” Agatha’s voice remains steady, with just a hint of dry amusement, “I’ll be taking the next few days to reacquaint myself with the university’s internal systems. I expect your full cooperation.”

    Some people in the audience nod. Others exchange glances. Rio doesn’t move.

    But inside, everything’s buzzing.

    This meeting was bound to happen eventually. Logical. Expected. Not surprising.

    But why now?

    Why like this?

    Agatha scans the room. Not looking at Rio. But something inside Rio tells her that Agatha knows. Feels it.

    Because it’s always been that way.

    A game without words.

    The director finally takes the mic again, thanks everyone for coming, and announces the meeting is over. The room starts to shift — students and faculty slowly begin to disperse, but the feeling lingers. Rio doesn’t get up right away.

    She watches as Agatha takes a step back, her profile cutting through the crowd of professors. As if nothing could shake her. As if she never left. Shit.

    Rio exhales and slowly stands.

    Agatha’s back.

    And nothing’s going to be the same.

    Rio turns sharply to Lilia, still feeling that strange tension in the air after everything that’s just happened.

    "I'm gonna go have that smoke after all."

    Lilia raises her eyebrows, arms crossed.

    "Class starts soon."

    "So?"

    Rio doesn’t wait for an answer. She spins on her heel and heads for the exit, her steps sharp, fast.

    A cigarette.

    She needs a cigarette.

    She can feel the anxiety swelling inside her, thoughts tangling and circling around one thing. Her.

    What the hell is she doing back?

    Rio moves through the hallways, ignoring the students around her, slipping out a side door and stepping into the back courtyard.

    The air here is cooler, cleaner. But what she needs is smoke. She glances around and spots a small group of students nearby — standing in a loose circle, one of them lighting up.

    She approaches without a word.

    “Anyone got a cigarette?”

    A tall guy in a leather jacket gives her a quick glance, then silently holds out a pack.

    “Take one.”

    Rio pulls one out with practiced ease and holds it between her fingers. “Light?”

    A girl with short dyed hair nods and reaches into her pocket for a lighter. She flicks it on, and Rio leans in just enough for the flame to catch. The first inhale is sharp. Deep.

    Rio closes her eyes and exhales slowly, smoke curling from her lips as the familiar, warm burn fills her lungs.

    The noise in her head quiets — if only for a moment.

    But only for a moment.

    Rio inhales deeply, holding the smoke in her lungs like she’s trying to fill the hollow that cracked open inside her the second she caught sight of that perfect posture, that gaze, that damn smile — the one that wasn’t even there, but she felt it.

    Agatha Harkness.

    She was back.

    Not like a ghost from the past. Not like a mistake that could be ignored if you just pretended hard enough it never existed.

    She came back the way she always did.

    In full view.

    Confident. Composed. Cold-blooded.

    Like nothing had ever happened.

    Rio clenched her jaw and took another drag, letting the nicotine flood her veins — but even that couldn’t dull the irritation slowly, steadily crawling under her skin.

    She hadn’t let herself think about this. About Agatha. About how it ended. About the fact that even after a year and eight months, something inside her still responded to the sound of that name alone.

    Rio exhaled slowly, releasing smoke into the chilled air. It was laughable.

    All the flings, the dates, women, men, casual kisses, unfamiliar hands, unfamiliar voices. It was all so easy. So predictable.

    None of them compared.

    None of them could erase the memory of Agatha’s touch.

    Stupid, isn’t it?

    Stupid to think that one presence could throw her so completely off balance. But it did.

    She holds the cigarette between her fingers, lazily tapping the ash off, staring into nothing.

    Her head is full of words she’ll never say.

    Accusations that will stay clenched behind her teeth.

    Agatha hadn’t even looked her way.

    Rio lets out a bitter laugh.

    Of course she didn’t.

    Because everything’s back to how it was.

    They have nothing now but icy sarcasm, clipped phrases, silence that cuts sharper than any fight.

    The game of indifference — and they’ve both always been damn good at it. She takes one last drag, presses the cigarette to the cold concrete, watching the embers scatter across the ground like crumbs of a memory. And then she flinches — barely, but enough.

    That voice.

    That voice she hasn’t heard in over a year slices through the air like a scalpel. Sharp, assured, laced with just enough mockery to send a shiver skating down her spine.

    “You’re still just as hooked on cigarettes, I see.”

    Chapter End Notes I hope at the very least you all left a kudos on this work.

    Chapter 20: Monday|Tuesday

    Chapter Notes Hello, my beautiful ladies with even bigger hearts. Thank you for all your comments! Thank you for taking the time to leave such kind and supportive words under the last chapter.

    Right now, I’m going through a pretty difficult period, and — as the whole world probably knows by now — one of the more insidious symptoms of depression is the complete lack of meaning or inner strength to do anything at all.

    That’s why I’ve been translating the chapters so slowly lately. As for Rio and Agatha... well, things aren’t actually as bad as they might seem. In the upcoming chapters, they’ll have to start talking to each other.

    Or rather, they’ll have to do a lot of talking. Like... a lot a lot 😅 And I suggest you start lighting your candles for Regina now. Because this little queen is about to make some serious moves to get Rio and Agatha back together... 😉 And yes — try to see things from Agatha’s side.

    Imagine running into the woman you’re still in love with… two years later.

    There’s no hatred here. Just… nerves. It’s overwhelming.

    (And by the way — we’ll find out the real reason Agatha got fired very soon.)

    “You’re still just as hooked on cigarettes, I see.”

    Rio turns slowly.

    Agatha stands a few steps away, perfectly composed, a faint half-smile on her lips—not kind, not cold. Just... attentive.

    Too attentive.

    Rio feels a tight twist in her chest, but doesn’t let it show. She huffs a dry laugh, flicking her eyes from Agatha to the cigarette butt. “Old habits,” she says lazily, pressing the last of it under the toe of her boot. “You know how hard they are to quit.”

    Agatha tilts her head slightly, as if weighing her.

    “I do.”

    And it sounds… strange.

    Not how Rio expected.

    Not how it should sound after the last time they saw each other. Rio lifts her chin, a spark of challenge in her gaze.

    “Gotta admit—you still know how to make an entrance. Everyone’s thrilled.”

    Agatha’s lips twitch in the corners.

    “Are they?”

    “Oh, absolutely.” Rio tips her head to the side, taking a step back and folding her arms across her chest. “You know people love a show.” Agatha studies her with a slow, barely-there amusement.

    “A show?”

    “Sure. You didn’t just come back—you returned. With flair.” “Hopefully in a good way.”

    “Not sure,” Rio says with a crooked smile. “More like… in your way.” Agatha lets out a quiet huff, glancing down at her shoes like she’s double- checking just how on-brand her grand return really was.

    “Then it’s going exactly as planned.”

    A few seconds pass in a light, almost weightless silence. Not the kind of pause they used to have—heavy, charged with everything unsaid. No, this one’s different. Warmer. Like the echo of an old conversation that once thrived on teasing, implications, verbal fencing.

    “You look good, by the way,” Agatha notes, her gaze sweeping over Rio with quiet precision.

    Rio blinks, then smiles slowly, arching a brow.

    “I know.”

    “Oh, do you now?”

    “Of course. You think I maintain this figure on coffee and breakdowns alone?”

    Agatha laughs—quietly, unexpectedly—and the sound catches Rio off guard. It sounds… real.

    “I get the feeling your nerves are doing just fine,” Agatha replies, tilting her head.

    “You’d be surprised,” Rio shrugs slightly. “Though after today…” She gestures vaguely toward the gym.

    “Oh, I’m sure you’ll manage.”

    “No doubt.”

    Another pause. And suddenly Rio realizes how easy this is—how easy it is to talk to her. As if the break, the time—twenty months, twenty-six days, not that anyone’s counting—never happened.

    But it did.

    They just haven’t touched it.

    Yet.

    Agatha gives a small shake of her head, as if considering something, then glances back at Rio.

    “Still... it’s surprising you’re still here.”

    Rio arches a brow with lazy precision.

    “Here where? The university? Or this charming little alley?” “Both, I suppose.”

    “Oh, you clearly underestimated my patience,” Rio smirks, stepping slightly to the side, like she’s about to walk away—but doesn’t. “Or maybe you thought I’d run.”

    “I wasn’t sure,” Agatha says softly, a faint smile on her lips—but something flickers in her eyes.

    Silence settles again. Not heavy, but palpable. Different now. Agatha breaks it first.

    “I should go.”

    Rio nods slowly, a faint smirk tugging at the corner of her lips. “Yeah. Of course.”

    Agatha holds her gaze for a second longer, then turns and begins to walk away.

    Rio watches her retreat—her silhouette shrinking with every step, heels echoing sharply against the concrete of the courtyard.

    And just before she disappears around the corner, Rio says: “Agatha.”

    Agatha stops. Glances over her shoulder, head tilted slightly, waiting. Rio looks straight at her, eyes steady, a little lazy, but there’s something in her voice—something even she can’t quite name.

    “Good luck.”

    Agatha hesitates, pressing her lips together for a brief moment. Then she gives a small nod.

    “Thank you.”

    And walks away.

    Rio stays where she is.

    Far too long for it to be just watching.

    Rio canceled the date before she even started the engine. She simply pulled out her phone, typed a quick “Sorry, something came up — can we reschedule?” to Wanda, and closed the messenger without waiting for a reply.

    There was no way she could focus.

    Not tonight.

    She pulled up in front of Regina’s white house, slowly easing the car into the small driveway by the porch, and turned off the engine. But she didn’t get out right away. Just sat there, hands clasped on the steering wheel, staring straight ahead.

    Her mind was elsewhere.

    Back in that alley.

    With Agatha.

    Rio had braced herself for anything. A scene, harsh words, cold indifference, maybe even that dark, cutting silence that pressed harder on the chest than any accusation ever could.

    But not this.

    Not a normal, civil conversation.

    Of course there was tension — a soft, taut string pulled tight by a year and a half of absence. Of course there was awkwardness. Of course there was something in the air, something unnamed, something that slid across her skin like the ghost of a past touch.

    But how the hell were they even supposed to speak for the first time after that?

    Rio scoffed under her breath and finally stepped out of the car. She shut the door a little harder than necessary.

    She didn’t feel like going inside.

    The house was temporary, which meant the safety it offered was, too. It didn’t belong to her. It didn’t smell like her, didn’t carry her energy, didn’t hum with her chaos. Everything in this house was curated, elegant, in its place. And that was exactly why she didn’t want to walk into it. She would’ve gone to the gym before.

    That used to be her answer to moments like this. Running — it had a way of hammering out the excess. Burning the useless thoughts away until all that was left was muscle ache, harsh breathing, and a clean, indifferent emptiness.

    But now…

    Now there was something inside her that wouldn’t even let her try to run. Rio opened the front door and shut it a little harder than she meant to. Not out of anger — there was just too much inside her, and she needed to control something.

    From the dim hallway, Heidi rushed up to her immediately, bumping her leg with a cold nose, eyes wide and insistent. Rio reached down automatically, fingers brushing through the soft fur behind the dog’s ear. She pet her — not with the usual fondness, but mechanically, as if trying to ground herself, to keep her thoughts from dragging her back into that alley. She felt heavy.

    And she didn’t bother hiding it.

    Regina appeared in the doorway of the kitchen, tucking a strand of hair behind her ear. She wore a light sweater, the sleeve on one side a little wrinkled — as if it had just been gripped by someone’s hand. Rio looked up at her, eyes tired.

    Regina blinked slowly, taking in her expression.

    “You were supposed to be out later.”

    Her voice was even, but there was something underneath it — something Rio could’ve interpreted as suspicion. Or maybe concern.

    She didn’t smile. Didn’t even try.

    “Yeah,” Rio said flatly. “I wasn’t in the mood.”

    And that was it.

    No explanation, no elaboration, no interest in talking.

    Regina didn’t respond. She didn’t ask anything. Just narrowed her eyes slightly, watching, but letting Rio pass.

    She headed for the stairs.

    The wooden steps creaked beneath her feet, but she didn’t care. She didn’t look back.

    She just went upstairs.

    Rio steps into her room, closing the door behind her with a quiet click — and finally exhales. Not a sigh of relief. Not frustration. Just the kind of breath you take when your chest has been too full for too long. It’s still there. The weight. Not sharp, not painful — just thick and dull, spreading beneath her ribs like molasses, making it hard to breathe. She kicks off her shoes without thinking, toes curling into the soft carpet. Shrugs off her jacket and hangs it loosely over the back of a chair. Pulls her shirt over her head in one rough motion. The air against her skin is cool, but she doesn’t flinch.

    She just wants the day off her body.

    Quickly, she changes into pajamas — an oversized t-shirt and loose shorts that barely register on her skin. No performance, no pretense. Just comfort. Then she heads into the bathroom.

    Warm light spills across the white tile. She braces her hands against the sink, stares at her own reflection.

    Her eyes look darker than usual. Maybe it’s exhaustion. Maybe it’s the things she’s thinking — the ones she can’t name out loud. She leans down and turns on the tap. Cold water. Crisp and clean. She scoops it into her palms, splashes her face, lets the makeup run down the drain with everything else.

    Behind her, somewhere in the house, something shifts. A soft sound. A door?

    She freezes.

    Listens.

    But nothing follows.

    Probably nothing. Probably the wind. Or the house settling. It’s just her and Regina. No one else.

    She shakes her head, lets the thought go. Reaches up and loosens the tie in her hair. The ponytail slips free, strands falling over her shoulders in a heavy cascade — dark and light blending together in a smooth gradient. She pats her face dry with a towel, switches off the light, and heads back into the bedroom.

    The bed looks like the only right place to be.

    She climbs in, pulls the blanket over her head, cocooning herself in softness. The weight of the fabric presses down just enough to feel safe. The darkness under the covers is thick, warm. The outside world disappears.

    And then — a knock.

    Soft. Barely there.

    Rio doesn’t move.

    Another knock.

    “What?” she says, voice muffled through the blanket.

    The door creaks open.

    “I know you’re not asleep.” Regina’s voice is calm. But there’s something softer in it than usual — a tenderness that only shows up when the care is real.

    Rio hears her footsteps cross the room.

    Then the edge of the blanket lifts slightly — and the scent of green tea curls softly into the space around her.

    “I brought you tea,” Regina says calmly, settling on the edge of the bed. Rio doesn’t respond right away.

    She’s still hidden beneath the covers, like a child hiding from a storm. Regina doesn’t rush her. She just sits there, holding the cup, steam curling in delicate ribbons into the air.

    Silence stretches.

    Then Rio slowly extends a hand from under the blanket and takes the cup. No words.

    She sips. Carefully.

    The tea is hot, but not too hot. Slightly bitter, with a whisper of citrus. It warms her.

    And she hates that.

    Because right now, she doesn’t want to feel better.

    Without a word, Regina lifts the edge of the blanket and slides underneath it too — moving slowly, like she’s careful not to scare Rio away. She curls onto her side, drawing her legs up slightly, her elbow resting gently against a pillow.

    “There’s no air in here,” she murmurs, wrinkling her nose as she tries to take a deeper breath.

    Rio huffs, a soft, almost-silent laugh from the depths of the cocoon. “It was supposed to be a one-person bunker,” she mutters — but her voice carries no irritation. Just a faint note of resignation, softened by something that might be a smile.

    She shifts slightly and pushes the blanket upward, letting in a trickle of cool air. Their heads emerge from the darkness, and the space between them feels just a little lighter.

    Rio sets the teacup down on the nightstand — carefully, so it doesn’t spill — then turns onto her side, facing the window.

    The darkness outside is soft, muted, threaded with the amber glint of faraway streetlights. Tree branches sway gently, casting blurred shadows across the glass.

    Regina shifts closer without a word.

    She slides a hand along Rio’s waist, then eases into a loose, quiet embrace from behind. It’s not pushy. Not sentimental. But there’s something grounding in the way she touches her — something unforced, almost instinctive.

    “Will you tell me?” she asks, voice low, but not pressing. Rio is silent for a few seconds.

    “Not now,” she says at last, almost a whisper.

    Regina doesn’t push.

    Silence settles between them again — but this time, it doesn’t feel quite so heavy.

    And then Rio speaks.

    “What about you?”

    Regina’s body tenses just a little.

    “What about me?”

    “Who is he?” A pause. “Or she?”

    Regina freezes for half a second. The fingers that had rested loosely against Rio’s side now barely tighten — a reflex more than a choice. “What?” she replies, tone laced with a familiar teasing edge. But it’s too smooth. Too polished to be honest.

    Rio turns her head slowly, just enough to meet her gaze, her eyes half- mischievous, half-something else — interest, maybe. Or the kind of challenge she’s always delivered with the effortlessness of breathing. “You know what I mean,” she murmurs, tracing a line down the seam of the pillow with one idle finger. “You smile at your phone like you’re not texting your accountant.”

    Regina lets out a short laugh, flopping onto her back and pulling her arm away.

    “Oh, and now you’re an expert on my smiles?”

    “Always have been,” Rio replies lazily, turning back to the window. A few seconds pass in silence — warm, but taut, like a string pulled just a little too tight.

    “It’s not important,” Regina says at last, rolling back onto her side. Rio lifts an eyebrow.

    “So there is someone.”

    “I didn’t say that.”

    “But you didn’t deny it.”

    Regina smirks, crooked and calm.

    “Maybe I just enjoy keeping you in the dark.”

    Rio smirks back, but her eyes are still sharp, slightly narrowed, reading every space between the words.

    “You’re not the kind of person who keeps secrets for fun. If you’re not telling me, it means you don’t want me to know.”

    Regina pretends to be amused by her persistence.

    “Oh, Rio. You dig into people’s relationships with such enthusiasm, I’m starting to think you secretly want to be a gossip columnist.” Rio lets out a quiet laugh, but doesn’t back down.

    “I just like to stay informed.”

    Regina shakes her head slowly, still smiling.

    — Maybe I just want something of my own. Without eyes on it. Without commentary.

    Rio blinks slowly, letting the words settle. She knows that tone. The one Regina uses to draw a line when she doesn’t want to dig any deeper. She doesn’t push.

    Only replies, lazily: — Well, whenever you feel like sharing, my expert opinion is at your service.

    Regina rolls her eyes.

    — How generous.

    And yet, when her fingers quietly find Rio’s side again, the touch carries something close to acknowledgment. Unspoken, but there.

    Rio closes her eyes, letting herself feel the faint warmth of Regina’s hand. It doesn’t bring true relief, but it offers something quieter. A strange, soft calm. Not answers. Not closure. Just the smallest reprieve. And sometimes, that’s enough.

    Their friendship had always been like this — unsentimental but deep, quiet but constant. They didn’t dissect each other. They didn’t demand confessions. They simply existed together. And that had always been enough.

    Rio knew she could turn to her now, toss out some dry remark, slip into sarcasm, act like everything was fine. But she didn’t want to. Not with Regina. They could share silence, and it never felt awkward. It was one of those rare things in Rio’s life that didn’t need justification. She sank deeper into the pillow, breathing in the familiar scent of clean linen, and felt the weight of her thoughts begin to ease — not vanish, but settle, like dust.

    Agatha.

    She was back.

    Not just back at the university, but back in her mind, her routine, her line of sight. So quickly, so effortlessly, as if she’d never left. Rio couldn’t name exactly what she felt. Anger? No — that was long gone. Resentment? Maybe. Nostalgia? God, yes. And perhaps most of all, frustration. Frustration that Agatha still had an effect on her. Even without trying. Even without wanting to. Her presence alone was enough to shift the axis of Rio’s carefully ordered world.

    She wasn’t going to fall into the same trap again.

    She wasn’t the same person anymore.

    But, goddammit, tomorrow was another day.

    And she would see Agatha again.

    Rio opens her eyes a minute before the alarm goes off. Not that she needed it—she hadn’t really slept. Her rest had been fractured, tangled in flashes of dreamlike threads that unraveled the moment she reached for them. Then the soft gray glow of morning crept in through the windows, and any point in trying to sleep simply evaporated.

    She lies still for a moment, staring at the ceiling, listening to the quiet tick of a clock somewhere on the nightstand. She feels the cool air settle across the room, the sun not yet strong enough to warm it. Somewhere deeper in the house, a door thuds softly, followed by the tap-tap of claws on hardwood and a low, contented woof.

    Rio finally exhales and sits up.

    She makes her way downstairs, fingers brushing along the wooden banister, unhurried. In the entryway, she nearly runs into Regina.

    Regina’s just come back from her morning walk. She’s dressed in that impossibly effortless way of hers—a light knit lounge set, perfectly tailored pants, a soft cardigan thrown over a fitted top. Her hair’s tousled from the wind but still somehow perfect. One hand holds a folded newspaper, the other a leash.

    Heidi sits obediently at her feet, black tail sweeping back and forth with lazy excitement, dark eyes bright with morning energy. The dog perks up immediately when she sees Rio and bounds toward her, tail wagging so hard it seems she might take flight.

    — You’re up already? — Regina arches a brow, giving Rio a once-over with amused surprise. — That’s new.

    — Morning nightmare, — Rio yawns, crouching to scratch behind Heidi’s ears. — It’s called insomnia.

    — Or maybe it’s just age, — Regina offers sweetly, already heading toward the kitchen.

    — You’re dangerously free with your speech this early in the day, — Rio grumbles but follows after her anyway.

    The kitchen is wrapped in that soft, early half-light that only exists when the sun hasn’t quite chased the night away. The whole space feels calm— nothing urgent, nothing demanding. Just the gentle hum of the coffee machine as Regina sets mugs beneath the spouts, and the slow bloom of fresh-ground beans drifting into the air.

    Rio settles at the table, rubbing her eyes. Heidi flops down beside her, stretching out her legs across the cool tile.

    — So… you didn’t sleep? — Regina glances at her over the top of the newspaper, already unfolded and scanned for headlines.

    — Nope.

    — Want to tell me why?

    Rio squints slightly, leaning on her elbow.

    — Just didn’t.

    Regina tilts her head a little, studying her expression, but doesn’t press. She simply sighs, flips a page, then sets the paper down beside her, stretching for her mug.

    — Well, if you want, you could at least tell me who murdered your mood. Rio rolls her eyes.

    — My mood’s fine.

    Regina shakes her head, takes a sip of her coffee, and throws her a quick, knowing look.

    — Mm-hm. Sure it is.

    They lapse into silence—and it’s a familiar one, comforting in its quiet. Nothing awkward. Nothing demanding.

    Rio takes her first sip of coffee, feeling the warmth bloom inside her, and thinks that maybe breakfast isn’t the worst idea.

    — I’ll make breakfast today.

    — You? Willingly?

    Rio takes another slow sip before setting the cup back down on the table. Her hair, tied in a high ponytail, sways slightly as she moves. — Yes, me, — she replies with a lazy smirk. — Shocked?

    — Deeply, — Regina says dryly, folding her arms over her chest and leaning back in her chair. — You do know breakfast isn’t just a cigarette and coffee, right?

    Rio rolls her eyes dramatically and finally rises to her feet. — God, yes, I’m aware. Don’t look at me like I’ve never held a frying pan. She turns toward the counter, pulls a pan from the shelf, casts a quick glance toward the fridge, evaluating, then opens the door. — Alright then, — Regina drawls, still watching her with mild skepticism. — Surprise me.

    Rio snorts.

    — That was painfully predictable.

    She pulls out eggs, cheese, herbs, tomatoes, and places them on the counter. — Omelet? — Regina tilts her head slightly.

    — With mushrooms.

    — Hmm.

    — Can you not sound like you’re doubting my skills?

    — Oh no, I’m not doubting anything, — Regina replies smoothly, though the corners of her mouth twitch in a suppressed smile.

    Rio shoots her a quick glance but doesn’t respond. She simply rolls up her sleeves and gets to work.

    The kitchen light softens, grows warmer, filtering in through the wide windows. The scent of butter and sizzling mushrooms begins to drift through the air. Heidi, catching the savory smell, lifts her head and watches the process with interest, her tail swaying lazily from side to side. Regina takes another sip of her coffee, presses her lips together, and—still watching Rio’s movements—finally says: — You do know I’m going to critique your masterpiece regardless of whether it’s actually edible, right?

    Without turning around, Rio shrugs.

    — Is there anything in this world that actually satisfies you? — Certainly, — Regina replies, leaning back in her chair. — I’m just not convinced it’s your culinary expertise.

    Rio rolls her eyes but doesn’t respond—just lets out a low chuckle. She cracks the eggs into a bowl, whisks them together with milk, swiftly chops the herbs, and mixes it all in. The butter in the pan crackles. The mushrooms are turning golden, and the tomatoes send a fresh tang into the air.

    For a moment, everything feels... simple.

    An ordinary morning.

    As if nothing had changed.

    As if she wasn’t about to face a new day.

    A new encounter.

    As if Agatha wasn’t part of the equation at all.

    Rio adjusts her tie, fingers gliding lazily over the perfectly knotted silk, and fixes her gaze on her reflection in the rearview mirror. Morning sunlight filters through the windshield, casting a soft glow across her hair—sleek, polished waves that fall just past her collarbones, framing her face in a way that makes her eyes sharper, her cheekbones more defined. She looks flawless today.

    And she knows it.

    The narrow black tie draws a stark contrast against the crisp white shirt, its fabric catching the sunlight with a subtle sheen. Beneath the shirt—an ash- gray and black plaid corset, cinching her waist and adding a note of disciplined defiance to the ensemble. Its upper edge is trimmed with the finest black lace, a quiet flirtation woven into the severity of the silhouette. High-waisted trousers hug her hips with precise intention, the cut tailored to perfection. Slightly tapered at the ankles, the fabric carries just enough weight to catch the light as she moves. A black belt with a silver buckle pulls the look together, anchoring every detail under her control. Her arms are encased in long black leather gloves, adding a touch of theatrical elegance. Her gloved fingers trail across the steering wheel as she checks the dashboard before pulling out.

    On her feet—polished black stilettos, sharp-toed and unapologetically tall. Every step they take is deliberate, melodic. The kind that makes people slow down without realizing it, just to watch her pass.

    She slides on a pair of oversized black sunglasses with squared frames. Instantly, her expression vanishes—blank, unreadable. With them, she becomes more composed, more impenetrable.

    She looks exactly how she wants to look.

    Without compromise.

    The drive is surprisingly smooth. Rio savors the quiet control of being behind the wheel, no longer forced to chart out subway routes, dodge morning crowds, or freeze at bus stops. She no longer belongs to that chaotic current. Her life now is about sharp lines, deliberate decisions, and absolute independence.

    The speakers hum to life with the familiar intro of Dancing Queen by ABBA, but she quickly skips the track, selecting something a bit more playful for the morning: When I Kissed the Teacher. Her lips curve into a smile. Fitting, really. She’s been starting her days with that song for nearly a week now.

    Softly, almost to herself, she sings along, her gloved fingers tapping the steering wheel in rhythm. Everything is going according to plan. Until a message flashes on her screen.

    Lilia: “You on your way? If yes, pick me up. This damn bucket of bolts decided to die.”

    Rio exhales, glancing at the time on the dashboard.

    “Of course,” she mutters under her breath, but turns the wheel in the direction of Lilia’s place without hesitation.

    Five minutes later, she pulls up to the curb. Lilia is already there—standing on the porch with her arms crossed, one foot tapping impatiently against the stone steps.

    “You’re a lifesaver. I owe you my firstborn,” Lilia announces with theatrical flair as she strides toward the car and swings open the passenger door. Rio slips off her sunglasses slowly, lifting her eyes with calculated indifference.

    “I know. Get in before I change my mind.”

    Lilia smirks as she climbs in, and before even reaching for the seatbelt, gives Rio a long, deliberate once-over.

    “Damn. You look like you just walked off a Vogue shoot.”

    Rio shrugs, shifting gears.

    “That’s because I am the Vogue shoot.”

    Lilia lets out a short laugh.

    “Obviously. Now drive before I remember I didn’t pay the fare.” Rio smirks and presses the accelerator.

    The day is just beginning.

    And she’s already starting to enjoy it.

    Rio slams the car door shut, adjusts her glove, and casually glances around while Lilia keeps talking about the day ahead. Her voice is rhythmic but not overbearing, and Rio nods lazily, replying with short comments as they walk side by side.

    "Well? What do you think?" Lilia looks at her, clearly expecting a response. But there isn’t one.

    Because Rio’s attention is suddenly caught by something else. Someone else.

    Agatha.

    Exactly at the moment she’s walking a little farther down the sidewalk, heading toward the university building.

    The deep, rich red of her coat stands out against the gray walls. Long, elegant, gently fluttering with each step, it emphasizes her posture and the undeniable confidence in her stride. Underneath — a flawlessly tailored black suit hugging her waist, the fabric smooth and precise, every line in place.

    On her feet — high, pointed stiletto ankle boots clicking rhythmically against the pavement. A black leather handbag with silver hardware swings lightly in one hand, and in the other — a coffee cup, held as if it’s part of the look, as deliberately chosen as everything else.

    But what Rio’s eyes lock onto most is Agatha’s hair.

    Today, her hair is gathered into a loose low ponytail, with a few strands rebelliously falling around her temples — creating that contrast between strict elegance and effortless ease that always made her style so… provocatively perfect.

    And as if sensing the stare, Agatha suddenly slows down, glancing casually over her shoulder.

    Her blue eyes meet Rio’s.

    And she smiles.

    Not broadly, not for show — just softly, almost imperceptibly. The faintest curl at the corners of her lips, but in it — an entire ocean of meaning. She lifts the coffee cup in a brief wave.

    Then, without a single word, simply keeps walking.

    Rio watches her go, a strange, hard-to-name tension running down her spine.

    Lilia catches the shift in Rio’s expression, the sudden distance in her gaze, and comments with a smirk: “Looks like someone made an impression.”

    Rio blinks, tearing her eyes away, and adjusts her glove with exaggerated nonchalance.

    “She’s just dressed well. That’s all.”

    Lilia arches a brow slightly, eyeing her friend with amused scrutiny. “Right, of course. Just a coat. Just heels. Just…” — she gestures lazily in the air — “the ever-glorious Agatha Harkness.”

    Rio lets out a low hum, hiding a smirk.

    “Would you mind not sounding like a tabloid columnist?”

    “I would. It’s part of my charm.”

    Lilia shrugs, and the conversation naturally trails off. But Rio isn’t really listening anymore.

    Because in her mind, that look is still echoing.

    That barely-there, yet unmistakably clear greeting.

    The air in the conference room is dense — filled with overlapping voices, the scratch of pens on paper, and the faint, bitter scent of coffee gone cold in thin-walled paper cups. And at the center of it all — Agatha. She isn’t just speaking. She’s conducting. Holding the entire room in the palm of her hand. Her hands move deliberately, shifting papers with practiced grace. Her voice is even, assured, just low enough to command attention without ever demanding it. That quiet authority that can’t be taught — only possessed.

    Rio isn’t listening.

    She’s watching.

    Every detail, every nuance — the unconscious flick of Agatha’s wrist as she smooths down the lapel of her blazer, the tilt of her head as she scans the notes in front of her, the deliberate pause in her voice right before she lands a key point.

    Rio sits off to the side, further back. She’s not part of the discussion. She’s not jotting anything down. She just twirls a pen between her fingers — lazily, it seems, though in truth, the motion is the only thing keeping her tension in check.

    And at some point, she realizes — Agatha knows.

    She feels her eyes on her.

    But she doesn’t show it.

    Not even a glance. Not even a flicker of acknowledgment. She just keeps leading the meeting like nothing is out of place. And if Rio didn’t know her so well, she might believe her own presence didn’t matter. But she does know.

    Because that barely-there gesture — the quiet tapping of manicured nails against the rim of her coffee cup — was just as intentional as everything else.

    She said something. Rio heard it — the words, the cadence — but the meaning didn’t register.

    “Rio?”

    Lilia’s sharp voice yanks her out of it.

    She blinks, turns her head slightly in her direction.

    “You with us?”

    She’s about to answer — something dry, something lazily deflective — but then she sees it.

    Agatha is looking at her.

    Not a glance. Not fleeting, not passive, not the kind you throw across the room by accident.

    It’s direct.

    Blue eyes, calm and composed, but there’s something behind them — something Rio can’t name.

    She feels it like a pull — a thin, tangible thread between them, strung tight and humming.

    She inhales.

    “Of course.”

    Rio says it with practiced indifference, but inside, something lingers — something quietly seismic.

    Agatha isn’t looking through her anymore.

    And that changes everything.

    Ten minutes later, the meeting wraps. The room begins to shift — voices swell, chairs scrape, laptops shut. People gather their papers, fall into casual conversation. The formality starts to fade like steam from the coffee cups. Rio is ready to go too. She leans back in her chair, tosses her pen onto the untouched notebook, and stretches her legs, slowly, deliberately. She hadn’t intended to participate — her thoughts were elsewhere.

    She feels it.

    She feels it — the air thickening just slightly as Agatha speaks somewhere to her right. Feels the way her voice slips beneath her skin, familiar and composed, but with an undertone only Rio would ever catch. Feels how even without looking, Agatha is aware of her — as if that invisible thread between them is still there, taut and humming softly beneath the noise of the room.

    Rio has grown used to that sensation.

    But this… this is different.

    It’s not the burn it used to be. Not the sharp, skin-prickling tension that made her clench her fists and pretend she didn’t care. Now it’s softer. Barely there. Like warm air after a thunderstorm. Like the faint sweetness of expensive tobacco. Like the ghost of something that once belonged to both of them.

    She realizes — she wants to linger.

    Not to talk. Not to rehash anything. Just to stay a few more minutes in the same space. To exist here, beside her. Even like this. Even with everyone else around.

    But it won’t change anything.

    Rio presses her lips together and brushes the thought away. She rises — slow, almost lazy. Picks up the notebook she never used and heads toward the door.

    Agatha says something, but Rio isn’t listening anymore.

    She leaves.

    And just before the door closes behind her, she pauses — breath caught in her chest.

    But no.

    She doesn’t turn around.

    If only she knew —

    that as she walked out the door, someone else was just as desperately searching for a reason to make her stay.

    That somewhere across the room, amid the rustle of papers and the soft murmur of voices, one breath caught just slightly. That the moment Rio stood, someone’s hand faltered, movement slowing, eyes locking onto her silhouette for one fleeting second too long.

    If only she knew —

    that for the past several minutes, Agatha Harkness hadn’t heard a single word being said around her. That the meetings, the talking, the perfectly rehearsed academic choreography — had become nothing but distant noise. That while Rio walked away, Agatha was silently, desperately willing herself to find just one compelling reason to say: Stay.

    But she doesn’t have one.

    They are two parallel lines — once collided, now drifting apart again. And whatever existed between them… it’s a closed chapter. Just ink on yellowing pages, something to reread, maybe, but never to rewrite.

    But that’s a lie.

    Because if it were true, Agatha’s chest wouldn’t ache the way it does when Rio’s silhouette vanishes behind the door.

    If it were true, her fingers wouldn’t freeze mid-note, her voice wouldn’t tremble — even imperceptibly — as she reached for something, anything, to say that might matter.

    If it were true, she wouldn’t still be looking at that closed door, swallowing the urge to rise, to follow.

    But Agatha says nothing.

    She finds no reason.

    She doesn’t say: Stay.

    She doesn’t ask: Wait.

    She simply pretends.

    Pretends nothing happened.

    Forces herself to breathe, to speak, to return to the room, to the papers, to the version of life where she no longer has the right to pull Rio back. But if only she knew.

    If they knew.

    That it’s not always the end.

    Sometimes —

    it’s just a pause.

    Sometimes the world tears people apart not to keep them from each other forever, but to give them time to become who they’re meant to be.

    Sometimes time doesn’t divide — it teaches patience, shifts the weight of things, reshuffles the priorities.

    Sometimes, pauses exist so that when the moment finally comes, they can speak again — differently this time, more clearly, more truly.

    Maybe now, finally, they’re ready.

    Ready to see each other without the filter of old wounds. Ready to stop looking for subtext in every gesture.

    Ready to no longer be hostages to pride, or fear.

    Maybe their paths are aligning again —

    not with the chaos that once drove them straight into disaster, but with something quieter.

    Something steadier.

    Maybe, this time, it could be different.

    But for now, there is still no reason to say: Stay.

    Chapter 21: Saturday

    Rio steps out of the house, tugging at the sleeves of a light, flowing coat that falls just below her knees. The fabric drapes in soft folds, concealing her slender frame yet emphasizing the effortless grace in her movements. Underneath, she wears a fitted olive knit set—comfortable and relaxed, yet undeniably stylish. White New Balance sneakers complete the look, striking that perfect balance between elegance and ease.

    Her hair falls loose in soft waves, and with a practiced motion she slides on dark sunglasses with thin metal frames. She takes a deep breath, catching the morning freshness laced with the faint scent of grass and damp asphalt. Regina had practically shoved her out the door—with her usual scoff and a remark that “a face like yours deserves at least a little sun.” Sure, the morning had started grey, but the sun hadn’t kept them waiting for long. Heidi pulls eagerly at the leash, glancing back impatiently, while Rio silently curses herself for still not finding a proper apartment. Nothing feels right. Too small, too run-down, too far across the city—or those dreaded listings: “No pets.” Honestly, why is it so hard for people to understand that a dog is a far more reasonable neighbor than some humans? She tightens her grip on the leash, steering Heidi toward the nearest park. A morning walk won’t hurt. Especially since she hasn’t been to this one before.

    And, according to Regina, there’s a fair going on there right now. Rio strolls along the sidewalk, her gaze lazily drifting over shop signs and the few passersby. Heidi trots ahead, the leash tugging now and then, but Rio doesn’t rush. The place is pleasant—broad walkways, well-kept trees, air crisp and cool, scented with damp earth and something faintly sweet, maybe pastries from the fair.

    She would probably be enjoying the moment—if not for that strange, heavy feeling pressing at her from the inside. Something feels… off. No, everything is fine. Exactly as it should be.

    Exactly as it should be.

    It’s just that… her interactions with Agatha now are nonexistent. Rio clenches her jaw and takes a deep breath, as if trying to force the thought away, but it’s lodged under her skin like the smell of smoke in clothing. They don’t argue anymore, don’t trade sharp remarks, don’t glance at each other across rooms with irritation or some hidden meaning. There’s nothing. Only smooth, neutral exchanges. Only work. Only a professional tone.

    When Agatha speaks, her voice is perfectly steady, polite, but stripped of even the faintest edge it once had. When she looks at her, those blue eyes hold no disdain, no urge to cut, no curiosity. Only emptiness. And that’s supposed to be a relief, right?

    Rio remembers how not long ago she would’ve gladly ripped out every memory of Agatha by the root. How she couldn’t bear to hear her name, see her profile online, or catch her likeness in the glance of a stranger. She had wanted to forget. To erase.

    But now, with an invisible wall between them—when all that heat has been replaced by polite distance—something inside her pulls uncomfortably tight.

    God.

    This is worse than hate.

    Rio huffs, as if trying to mock her own thoughts, but even to her it sounds unconvincing. She slips her hand into the coat pocket, fingers closing around the cool shape of her phone, and before she can realize what she’s doing, she unlocks the screen.

    The first thing to appear—a bright notification from Tinder. She swipes it away without reading.

    Not now.

    Her fingers drift over familiar icons, almost on autopilot. She catches herself in the act—at how mindless the motion has become, how unconsciously she keeps seeking the same name.

    But no.

    She won’t check her profile.

    That’s beneath her.

    It doesn’t even make sense.

    She knows there’s nothing new there—the same abandoned page Agatha hasn’t touched in ages. And yet her hand trembles with the urge—to look, to make sure nothing has changed, to confirm that nothing from the past will come crawling back.

    Goddammit.

    She snaps the phone shut, shoving it back into her pocket as if the cold slab of glass and metal had suddenly started to burn her palm. “Come on, Heidi,” she mutters, giving the leash a tug, though the dog is already beside her, leisurely sniffing something along the curb. She needs a distraction.

    Rio draws in a deeper breath, filling her lungs with the thick, sweet aromas drifting from the fair. Freshly baked bread, honey, cinnamon, apples—it all blends into a rich, warm wave that pushes its way into her mind, crowding out the emptiness.

    Good. Just walk a little. Look around. Maybe even buy something. And get Agatha the hell out of her head for at least half an hour. She lifts her gaze, scanning the rows of colorful tents up ahead. The crowd flickers between them—someone laughing, someone waving their hands, children darting around and tapping caramel apples with little sticks. It all feels… ordinary.

    So normal.

    So far removed from the storm still raging inside her.

    Rio takes her first step toward the fair.

    And that’s when her eyes catch her.

    At the far end of the square, among the people, between the stalls, in this completely unrelated, unexpected place.

    Agatha.

    Shit.

    Rio freezes.

    Rio feels her pulse slip out of its usual rhythm.

    It’s almost like some overused romantic cliche—but this isn’t a movie. This is her life. And in it, chance encounters with an ex who’s still lodged somewhere under her ribs happen without a soundtrack, without dramatic lighting, and without any kind of warning.

    Not that it makes it any easier.

    She notices Agatha leaning toward Nicholas, saying something to him, and he, clutching a stick of cotton candy, laughs. The sound does something strange to Rio’s chest—something warm, simple, unbearably real. Rio abruptly turns right.

    Just a coincidence. A normal encounter. Everything’s fine. She takes a deep breath and keeps walking toward the open field where people are walking their dogs. She keeps the leash in hand, though Heidi is calm enough. Staying here any longer is pointless. Better to change direction. Better not to look back. Better not to—

    But she does.

    Of course she does.

    Against the backdrop of the noisy fair, among the colorful tents and milling crowd, Agatha still somehow stands out. As if even the soft beige tones of her clothes can’t camouflage her in the sea of people. As if Rio is wired to find her in any space.

    Agatha turns her head.

    Their eyes meet.

    Rio freezes for a fraction of a second.

    So does Agatha.

    Time stretches.

    And then Agatha… smiles.

    Calmly, almost absently. As if she’s simply spotted an old acquaintance by chance.

    And that’s it. She turns away again, focusing on her son, guiding him down the line of stalls.

    Rio breathes out.

    It’s fine.

    No drama. No sudden confrontations. Just a fleeting glance. Just a smile. Just a feeling she can’t shake out of herself.

    She exhales slowly and, without lingering, walks toward the dog park. A damn coincidence.

    Half an hour later, Rio is sitting on a wooden bench, one leg folded over the other in a loose lotus pose. In one hand—a cold can of Coke; in her lap— her phone, chat window open. She idly scrolls, tapping out replies to Wanda.

    Wanda: Do you always take this long to pick a restaurant? Rio: I’m just being thorough. You deserve only the best.

    Rio’s lips curled in a faint, ironic smile.

    Really, she should be thinking about where she and Wanda would finally meet, about what Wanda looked like in person, how her voice sounded, how she behaved on a date.

    But none of that was in her head.

    Every thought was about Agatha.

    She kept replaying that moment in her mind—again and again. Their eyes meeting. That flicker of hesitation. That damn smile.

    What the hell?

    A year and eight months—without a single chance encounter. And yet, sometimes, Rio had caught herself wishing she could just see her. Just in passing. Just… to be sure she was still real.

    And now—this. As if nothing had ever happened. As if all that time she hadn’t been scanning every café near the university for her face. As if she hadn’t been glancing twice at every woman who looked even remotely like her.

    And the funniest part?

    Agatha hadn’t even looked surprised. As if nothing had changed. As if their meeting meant absolutely nothing.

    Rio took a sip of Coke, but the cold liquid did nothing to chill what was happening inside her.

    Because if this encounter truly meant nothing—then why couldn’t she stop thinking about it?

    Her head snapped up at the sound of a familiar name.

    “Hey, Heidi!” a child’s voice called.

    And there they were.

    Agatha and Nicholas walking toward her dog, who was bounding toward the boy, tail wagging furiously.

    God, you’ve got to be kidding me.

    Rio didn’t even try to hide her shock — she just sat there and watched. Watched as Heidi instantly rolled onto her back, belly exposed, tail thumping against the grass. Watched as Nicholas knelt down beside her, rubbing her stomach with the easy joy of a child who’d found a new best friend. Watched as he said something to Agatha, pointing at the dog, and Agatha leaned in slightly to listen, her attention soft and focused on him. It looked… far too natural. Too effortless. As if this was the way things were meant to be. As if there was nothing surreal about it. As if their lives hadn’t been running on strictly parallel tracks all this time.

    Rio blinked, a sharp, uncomfortable knot twisting somewhere deep inside. Christ. What the hell?

    She blinked again, almost as if she could clear the vision from her eyes — but nothing changed. Nicholas was still there, happily scratching Heidi behind the ear, and Agatha still stood beside him with that faint, restrained half-smile, simply watching.

    Alright.

    Rio exhaled slowly, drained the last sip of Coke from the can, and rose from the bench with deliberate slowness.

    “Well, well,” she drawled as she stepped closer, “seems my dog has a busier social life than I do — and I’m the last to know.”

    Nicholas’s head snapped up, his face lighting with an easy smile. “Hi!”

    He looked at her as if her showing up was the most normal thing in the world.

    “Hey, champ,” Rio replied, matching his smile as she crouched down beside Heidi, giving the dog a pat along her side. Heidi barked once in contentment, pushing her nose insistently against Rio’s arm. All the while, Agatha met her eyes — evenly, steadily — without saying a word.

    “Did you know,” Rio said to Nicholas, tilting her head toward Heidi, “that this sneaky girl would sell me out for a decent dose of attention?” “Of course!” he announced proudly, straightening his shoulders. “We already know each other.”

    Rio narrowed her eyes ever so slightly.

    “Do you now?”

    “Uh-huh!” Nicholas nodded, his fingers still scratching behind Heidi’s ear. “Regina brought her on Thursday when we went for a walk with her and Mom. She said Heidi had way too much energy, so she figured an extra run wouldn’t hurt.”

    Rio processed the information in silence.

    Thursday.

    Regina.

    Emma.

    Nicholas.

    And her dog.

    Her. Goddamn. Dog.

    She felt that same unpleasant tightening deep in her chest. Not that this was some kind of grand conspiracy, but…

    Why the hell had no one bothered to tell her?

    Rio slowly lifted her gaze to Agatha, standing nearby, looking utterly unsurprised by any of this.

    Oh, of course.

    She’d known the whole time.

    Drawing in a short breath, Rio exhaled through her nose, forcing a smile onto her face.

    “Well, I guess my dog’s been in on more than I have.”

    Nicholas laughed, and Rio flicked a quick glance at Agatha — but she remained perfectly composed. Only a fleeting play of light and shadow across her features betrayed anything at all — maybe a hint of fatigue, maybe something else entirely, something more layered and harder to name. Rio looked away, feigning ignorance.

    “Well then, if you’ve already got your little fan club for my dog going, maybe I didn’t even need to show up.”

    “You’re late,” Nicholas replied with mock gravity, still focused on Heidi. “We’ve already made the decision. She’s part of our team now.” Rio blinked.

    So did Agatha.

    They both turned their eyes to the boy, who’d delivered that statement with complete conviction, as if it were an official decree.

    Rio crossed her arms loosely over her chest.

    “Team?”

    “Yeah!” Nicholas straightened up, enthusiasm bright in his eyes. “When we went walking with Emma and Regina, Heidi and I had a race, and she won! So now she’s my partner.”

    Rio heard the words, but her mind snagged on something else entirely. She didn’t ask. She didn’t press. But now… she knew.

    Her mouth twitched into a slow, lazy smirk.

    “You’re telling me you lost to my dog?”

    “She’s really fast!” Nicholas said it with the kind of fierce, wholehearted defense only kids could muster — and despite herself, Rio’s smile grew wider.

    She cast another glance at Agatha, and the quiet between them felt… oddly unstrained. They weren’t within the university walls, and this — this was just a conversation. No insinuations, no accusations, no echoes of the past. It was as if time had really managed to blur the old lines between them, and the new ones hadn’t yet been drawn.

    Rio inhaled, deliberately breaking the moment.

    “Well, if she’s your partner now, that means you’re responsible for her, right?”

    “Yep!”

    Rio tilted her head, narrowing her eyes just slightly.

    “Then do me a favor — make sure she burns off at least half of her energy.” Nicholas lit up instantly, but before he could say anything, his eyes darted to Agatha for permission.

    “Can I?”

    Agatha hesitated. Rio caught it — that flicker, that half-second where she almost said no. But then…

    “Be careful.”

    That was all it took.

    Nicholas nodded eagerly, and in the next moment he was sprinting across the grass with Heidi bounding at his side, the dog instantly swept up in his excitement. She darted between vendor stalls, leapt over roots, a streak of motion weaving through the green.

    Rio watched them with a faint smile.

    Kids.

    Dogs.

    They always found a way to understand each other so easily. Unlike people.

    Unlike them.

    The quiet settled between them again — not oppressive, but still… quiet. Rio’s gaze drifted to Agatha. She was watching Nicholas with that particular expression only parents seemed to have — protective, warmly restrained, giving everything away without saying a word. Rio looked back to the boy and the dog. Heidi tore in wide circles, Nicholas laughing all the while.

    Shame things couldn’t be that simple.

    “Coffee?”

    Rio blinked.

    A second.

    Two.

    She hadn’t expected that from Agatha. After all this time without speaking — coffee? Just like that?

    She’d already had her morning dose before leaving the house, but… “Why not,” she said with a casual shrug, hiding her surprise beneath the gesture.

    Agatha nodded, and together they started toward the nearest stall. The pause between them felt almost natural this time — not the kind that sliced the air or brimmed with the unsaid. It was simply there.

    Rio felt her shoulders loosen without meaning to.

    “I’m glad you have a dog,” Agatha said.

    Rio arched a brow.

    “Oh, really?”

    “You once told me you didn’t like getting attached,” Agatha replied with a faint smirk, casting her a quick sidelong glance. “But it seems you’ve found an exception.”

    Rio tilted her head, studying her profile.

    “You might be surprised, but I’ve done a lot of things lately that once seemed impossible.”

    “I don’t doubt it,” Agatha said, the corner of her mouth lifting. The coffee stall was closer than expected. Agatha ordered without hesitation, the motion almost automatic. Rio found herself watching her — every movement deliberate, steady, neither rushed nor lazy. Agatha Harkness had always seemed like someone who kept everything under control. Even when she didn’t.

    They collected their drinks almost at the same time. Agatha paid first; Rio didn’t even bother to argue. All the while, Agatha’s gaze drifted back to her son again and again, as if pulled there unconsciously.

    “He’s grown,” Rio said evenly, lifting the cup to her lips. Agatha looked at her. Rio met her gaze — that deep, dark blue, revealing nothing.

    “Yes,” was all she answered.

    Rio gave a small nod.

    “And it seems he’s made new friends.”

    “He’s always been sociable,” Agatha replied with a hint of amusement. “Not like you,” Rio countered.

    Agatha gave a low hum.

    “And definitely not like Emma.”

    Rio let out a short laugh, shaking her head.

    “You know Emma and Regina are dating, right?”

    Agatha raised her brows.

    “Of course.”

    Rio fell silent for a beat, absorbing that answer.

    “Of course,” she echoed, glancing away. “And I’m the only one who, as usual, found out last.”

    Agatha’s eyes narrowed just slightly.

    “You really didn’t know?”

    “No. I’ve been staying with her temporarily while I look for a place, and she didn’t say a word.”

    Agatha’s lips curved faintly.

    “Now I see why.”

    Rio rolled her eyes.

    “Oh, sure. Go ahead — say I’m dramatic, irrational, that I’d blow up and ruin everything.”

    “You said it yourself,” Agatha replied, smirking.

    Rio shot her a look, eyes half-lidded.

    “You’re unbearable.”

    “I know.”

    They fell silent.

    And Rio caught herself thinking that this conversation… didn’t feel like one between people who hadn’t spoken in almost two years. It felt… like it was filling something. Something empty. Something she hadn’t admitted to herself.

    “They’ve been together for just under two years,” Agatha said evenly, not even looking at her.

    Rio blinked.

    “So, since the moment when…”

    Agatha turned her gaze on her, eyes narrowing slightly, as if weighing whether it was worth saying aloud.

    Rio froze.

    A year and eight months. And here she was, standing across from Agatha, paper cup in hand, hit by the ridiculous realisation that yes — all this time, Emma had been with Regina.

    “Yes, Rio.”

    Silence.

    Awkward.

    Dense.

    Rio took a sip of coffee, though it suddenly tasted flat. “Shit.”

    Agatha gave a small, tilted smirk.

    “Disappointed?”

    For a heartbeat, Rio met her eyes — deep, dark blue, sharply perceptive. “I don’t know.”

    And it was true. She didn’t.

    “What about you, Rio?”

    Her voice was soft, almost offhand. The kind of tone one could use for polite small talk. Or to land a hit right where it hurt.

    Rio blinked.

    “What?”

    Agatha took a slow, focused sip of her coffee.

    “Are you alone?”

    It sounded… damn strange.

    Rio felt her heart skip a beat.

    Seriously? Had Agatha really just asked that?

    The corners of her mouth twitched in the faintest smirk, though inside she was nothing but chaos.

    Her mind lit up instantly with possible replies: "Oh, you know, I actually have a date with a charming woman named Wanda, and I’m just waiting for us to finally meet."

    “No, Agatha — I’ve been in a relationship this whole time… with the memory of you.”

    Or: “Why the hell do you care?”

    But Rio doesn’t say either of those things.

    She only swallows, feeling the hot coffee burn her tongue. She looks at Agatha — at that deep, attentive gaze, at the slight tilt of her head, as if this were just a casual conversation. As if the question carried nothing personal. Rio takes another sip, turning her eyes away.

    “Yes.”

    One simple word. Too simple. And yet, somehow, it lands with far too much weight.

    Agatha doesn’t react. She doesn’t smile, doesn’t arch a brow in that familiar, mocking way of hers. She simply takes another slow sip of coffee. Rio studies her, trying to catch something beneath the calm. Something under the surface. But Agatha remains just as unreadable. “I see.”

    Just two words — and Rio feels something tighten inside her. See what? That she hasn’t found anyone? That maybe she’s been looking and failed? What does that even mean?

    “Are you surprised?” Rio asks, her tone light but with an edge that betrays the tension.

    Agatha tilts her head slightly.

    “Are you the kind of person who stays single for long?”

    There it is — something invisible but sharp, sliding between them like a blade. A subtle sting.

    Rio lets out a short huff, curling her lips into a faint smirk. “Maybe I decided to take a break from dramatic relationships.” She doesn’t specify which kind. She doesn’t need to. They both know. Agatha knows.

    “A wise decision.” She takes a sip, never breaking eye contact. “Though I doubt that’s really you.”

    Rio narrows her eyes slightly.

    “You think I can’t be alone?”

    “I think you get bored being alone.”

    The coffee suddenly tastes bitter.

    “Funny, Agatha.”

    Agatha gives a quiet huff of amusement but doesn’t reply. Her gaze shifts back to where Nicholas is still playing with Heidi.

    It buys Rio a few seconds. Seconds to loosen her grip, to keep from betraying the sudden rush of emotion. But she can’t quite let it go. “You’re not surprised,” she says.

    Agatha turns her eyes on her again.

    “About what?”

    Rio nods toward her.

    “That I’m alone.”

    Agatha doesn’t answer right away.

    Her gaze shifts, almost imperceptibly. Then she lifts her cup again, takes a small, unhurried sip, and only after that says, “I know you.”

    And that’s enough.

    Too much, even.

    Something inside Rio turns over — a whole slab of emotion shifting in one heavy motion. Warmth tangled with irritation, recognition brushing against protest. But on the surface, she only smirks, giving a slight shake of her head.

    Agatha stays quiet.

    The silence between them isn’t awkward.

    It’s full — thick, like the air before a storm. The kind of silence where saying even one more word might shatter a fragile balance. And for now, they both hold it.

    Once, it would’ve made Rio angry. She had always hated that unnerving calm of Agatha’s — the way being across from her felt like sitting under a single interrogation lamp. Every remark, every look another test. You pass, or you fail.

    Except there was never a right answer.

    Agatha always knew more than she said. Always saw deeper than she showed. And that was her power.

    But now…

    Now Rio isn’t angry.

    If anything, she feels a pull.

    Not that kind of pull.

    Though… maybe. Not now.

    What she really wants is to borrow that shameless, flawless composure. To look straight back into someone’s eyes and not flinch. To ask, steady and unshaken, the one question that’s been living inside her for one year and eight months.

    Why did you quit, Agatha?

    But she’s not ready. She knows it.

    She wouldn’t be able to hold her gaze in that moment. Wouldn’t be able to withstand it.

    So instead, she looks back out toward the field — where Nicholas comes running toward them, laughing breathlessly, with Heidi bounding alongside as if the boy has officially been promoted to her new best friend. They’re coming back. Which means Rio can keep that question to herself. For now.

    Nicholas reaches them first, flushed cheeks and wind-tangled hair glowing with unfiltered joy. He throws his arms out wide, as though he’s just won the most important race of his life.

    “She’s the best dog in the world!” he declares with pure conviction. Heidi barrels up right behind him, nearly crashing into Rio’s legs, and Rio has to shift her weight to keep from stumbling back.

    “Yeah,” Rio says with a faint laugh, crouching to scratch between Heidi’s ears, “I got lucky.”

    She can feel Agatha’s eyes on her.

    “Have you had her long?” Agatha’s voice is steady, but Rio catches the faintest shift in tone.

    “Almost a year,” she answers without looking up.

    “That’s a good age,” Agatha says evenly. “Not a puppy anymore, but still plenty of energy. By the way—” Nicholas suddenly pulls Rio out of her thoughts. “Have you tried the cinnamon doughnuts yet?”

    “I’ve already had coffee,” she answers vaguely.

    “So what? They’re doughnuts!”

    Agatha tilts her head slightly, and there’s the faintest flicker of a smile in her eyes.

    “Convincing.”

    “Of course!” Nicholas straightens proudly, then turns to his mother. “Can I?”

    Agatha pretends to consider, but Rio notices the subtle, hidden curve at the corners of her mouth.

    “I think after lunch would be better,” she says at last, and Nicholas rolls his eyes — but without any real protest.

    “Fine…” he drawls.

    Watching them, Rio feels something strange.

    A warmth — but not for her.

    For them.

    For that small, self-contained world where Agatha has a son, cotton candy, and a park.

    “We should go,” Agatha says, catching Rio’s gaze.

    Rio nods.

    “Of course.”

    It feels like being swept back into reality.

    Nicholas is already back with Heidi, scratching behind her ears and whispering something, while Agatha makes a barely-there gesture with her hand — almost a goodbye — before turning toward the exit of the fair. Nicholas runs after her, giving Heidi one last wave, then glances quickly at Rio.

    “Bye, um…”

    “Rio.”

    “Bye, Rio! Bye, Heidi!”

    “Bye,” Rio answers automatically — but she’s not really here anymore. She can still feel her presence.

    The scent of her perfume.

    Her voice.

    Her eyes.

    And only when their silhouettes vanish behind the stalls does she realize she’s been holding her breath the whole time.

    Rio drops her gaze to Heidi, who seems to sense her mood, and runs her hand along the dog’s fur.

    “Well… morning,” she exhales quietly.

    Then she pulls her phone from her pocket.

    A text from Wanda lights up the screen.

    Rio stares at it for a long moment.

    Then, without hesitation, she swipes the notification away. Not now.

    Chapter 22: Wednesday 1|2

    Chapter Summary Rio finally works up the courage to ask Agatha why she quit two years ago.

    And after today’s incident, it seems the university’s security guard could use a sedative.

    Chapter Notes See the end of the chapter for notes Rio adjusts the belt at her waist, smoothing the fabric of her blazer almost without thinking. Her wide-leg jeans skim softly over the floor with each step, the low heels of her shoes clicking lightly against the tiles. She feels good—relaxed, confident, in her element. She hasn’t tried to look overly provocative today, but, as always, she’s put together an outfit that speaks to her taste: classic with just a hint of defiance.

    Her hair is pulled into a neat bun, a few dark strands slipping free from beneath a heavy clip to brush against her neck. Simple hoop earrings catch the dim light. A small black handbag dangles from her wrist, her fingers idly toying with the strap. Everything is in its place, and that quiet control over the details soothes her in a way she doesn’t question. Today had been one of those days she despised with all her soul—the day for grading exams. And, of course, it hadn’t gone quickly. Papers, endless comments, mistakes, double-checking—by the time she’d finished, it was later than she would’ve liked. The students had long since gone, the halls were silent, and even most of the faculty had decided to leave their work for tomorrow.

    She clicks her tongue and exhales. At least there’s a hot bath waiting for her now—long, relaxing, and well-earned. Thirty more minutes and she’ll let herself sink into the warmth, washing away the day’s accumulated weight. Her steps echo faintly in the empty corridor, unhurried, her gait carrying the soft fatigue of a long day. The university’s lights are already dimmed, shadows stretch across the walls, and the air feels thicker than usual. And, naturally—because the universe has its own perverse sense of timing —her path takes her right past the principal’s office.

    By the door stands a security guard, arms folded across his chest—a middle-aged man with the loose stance of someone long resigned to the futility of his post. In one hand, he spins his phone idly, but the moment he notices Rio, he straightens slightly and gives her a brief nod. “Evening,” she says in passing, not slowing her pace.

    “Evening,” he replies.

    But something in his tone makes her stop.

    A faint, almost imperceptible thread of tension—or irritation. As though he’d rather be anywhere else right now, but something is keeping him here. Rio narrows her eyes, her gaze sliding toward the office door. The light inside is on.

    She could keep walking. She almost does.

    But something—something she can’t quite name—makes her slow down. At this hour, no one should be on this floor. And yet the guard stands here like he’s protecting something valuable.

    Or volatile.

    Rio casts a quick glance at his phone, but it’s already tucked away in his pocket. Now the man just stands there, as if waiting for further instructions. “Everything okay?” Her voice is calm, but there’s a hint of curiosity beneath it.

    The guard gives a short cough, clearly unwilling to go into detail. “Well, uh… yeah. Almost.”

    “Almost?” She raises an eyebrow.

    The man exhales heavily, as if regretting getting into this conversation at all.

    “There’s a problem with the principal.”

    Rio tilts her head slightly, her gaze drifting thoughtfully to the door. “Already?”

    The guard smirks briefly, but before he can answer, a distinct, muffled thud comes from behind the door — as if someone, in sheer frustration, had slammed their fist against the wood. Then follows a familiar, razor-sharp voice, brimming with irritation: “It’s not a problem! It’s a damn lock that, for some reason, decided to die right now!”

    Rio blinks.

    Even through the door, she can picture Agatha’s expression. Tight. Seething. Perilously close to the point where objects start flying. The guard calls back loudly, “We’ve already called a locksmith.” “Oh, how sweet,” Agatha’s voice drips with so much sarcasm it could flood the entire damn university and leave a permanent stain. “Will we be waiting two hours? Or maybe three?”

    Rio bites the inside of her cheek to keep from smirking.

    “They say about fifteen minutes,” the man replies cautiously. “Charming. Simply charming,” the voice behind the door all but hisses venom.

    Rio adjusts the belt at her waist and turns slightly toward the guard. “And you’re just going to stand here until she gets out?” The man shrugs, his whole demeanor saying, Did I have a choice? “Well… yeah.”

    Rio lifts an eyebrow.

    “Interesting. And what exactly do you plan to do if something goes wrong?”

    The guard blinks. He opens his mouth, then closes it again without managing to come up with an answer.

    Rio tilts her head slightly and lets out a quiet chuckle. “That’s exactly my point. I think you’d better get back to your post. The lock’s not going to fix itself, is it?”

    The man hesitates, eyeing her with mild doubt, but eventually decides there’s no point in arguing.

    “Fair enough.”

    He casts one last glance at the door — as if bidding farewell to some unspoken life lesson — and reluctantly walks off, leaving Rio in relative silence.

    But not for long.

    “Perfect,” Agatha’s voice is sharp with prickly annoyance. “Now I’m alone.”

    “Not quite.”

    Rio drawls the words lazily, as if savoring the situation. She leans a shoulder against the wall, crossing her arms, listening to the silence behind the door grow heavier, more tangible. There’s something charged about it — almost electric, like the air before a storm.

    Then comes a muted sound, as if someone has dropped heavily into a chair or slapped a palm against a desk in frustration.

    “Of course,” Agatha’s voice is even, but a faint thread of irritation runs beneath it. “Because this is exactly what I needed right now.” Rio smirks and, taking her time, slides down to sit on the cold tile floor. She crosses one leg over the other, lets her head rest against the wall, and closes her eyes as if settling in for the long haul.

    “So, you’re stuck?”

    “Thank you, Captain Obvious.”

    Rio chuckles openly, not bothering to hide her amusement. It’s been a long time since she’s seen Agatha like this — wound tight, at the edge. And damn her if she’s not enjoying every second of it.
