    “Well then, maybe you should use this chance?”

    “What chance, for God’s sake?”

    “To figure out what you’re being punished for.”

    A weary exhale filters through the door, and Rio can almost see Agatha closing her eyes, rubbing the bridge of her nose.

    “For what?” Her voice holds more sarcasm than the situation itself. “Probably for my sins.”

    “Oh, Agatha, if you were punished for all your sins, they’d have shipped you straight to hell without the option to appeal.”

    A beat of silence. Then a pause filled with something almost alive. “Oh yeah? And you decided to keep me company?”

    “Obviously.”

    And now the quiet between them shifts. No longer irritated or barbed, but… softer. Almost warm.

    Rio lets her head fall back fully, the cool surface of the wall pressing against heated skin.

    She hadn’t realized just how much she’d missed this.

    Because since Agatha’s return, she’s been going quietly insane. From her gaze — too long, too assessing. From her voice — too familiar, too steeped in the same cadences she’s heard in hundreds of other conversations, each time in a new way. From her sarcasm, her confidence, her presence.

    Agatha is her personal drug.

    And damn her three times over, but now — when they’re separated by nothing more than an old door, with no prying eyes, no background noise, no imposed boundaries — Rio can feel something flare hot in her blood again.

    As if she’s alive all over again.

    “Damn university, damn old lock, damn maintenance department,” Agatha grumbles.

    Rio’s smile widens.

    “You’ve always been a woman who likes order.”

    “And you’ve always been a woman who prefers chaos.”

    “There you go. Perfect balance.”

    Silence.

    Then — a short, almost involuntary laugh from Agatha.

    Rio closes her eyes.

    Damn.

    If only she could seal this moment in a time capsule and keep nothing but this.

    “So, are you planning on standing there the whole time?”

    Agatha’s voice still carries that same irritation, but now there’s something else in it — not fatigue, not anger, but a faint note of disbelief. As if she’s still not entirely convinced Rio actually stayed.

    Rio lazily opens one eye, slowly turning her head toward the door with a smirk, her tongue rolling idly against her teeth.

    “Why stand? I’m sitting.”

    “Oh, thank you for the clarification.”

    “Always happy to help.”

    She hears a short huff from Agatha, but no immediate reply. Only a faint sound — like the scrape of a chair back as Agatha leans against it, followed by the slow drag of her palms over her face.

    Rio’s smile curves just a touch higher as she tilts her head to the side. Truth be told, she could easily not say another word.

    Just sit here, feeling her breath on the other side of the door. But Rio wouldn’t be Rio if she didn’t add a little fuel to the fire. “So, Harkness?” She deliberately draws out the name, lazily brushing her fingertips against her own wrist. “How does it feel — being locked in?” “Indescribable,” Agatha replies dryly. “Almost like working with you.” “Oh, ouch. That one stung.”

    “I don’t think you know what pain is.”

    Rio lets out a low chuckle, not even trying to hide her smile. “Wow. Are you always this dramatic when you’re mad, or is it just for me?” “That’s strictly for the people who make me sit locked up in this damned office.”

    “Still blaming the lock.”

    “I blame the whole system.”

    “Of course you do.”

    They fall silent, but the quiet no longer presses down on them. This isn’t their first verbal sparring match — and it’s definitely not the last. But for the first time in a long while, it feels right.

    Just the familiar sharpness, the taste of it so achingly known. “You know, I actually missed this version of Agatha Harkness,” Rio says. “Because ever since you came back, you’ve been… well, all proper. Restrained. Moderately diplomatic. Practically a model of balanced leadership.”

    She hears something crack on the other side of the door — maybe Agatha’s knuckles, maybe the chair she’s sitting in. Or maybe it’s the thin thread of Agatha’s patience.

    “Don’t you think that’s a natural process?” Agatha asks evenly. “People grow. Evolve.”

    Rio smirks, tilting her head.

    “Sure. It’s just that you used to be able to tell someone to go to hell with such a look on your face that they’d end up feeling guilty for ever crossing you.”

    “I still can.”

    “I don’t doubt it.”

    Silence.

    It lasts just long enough for something unspoken to take shape inside it. Then Agatha speaks — slow, even, with that faint, almost lazy curiosity that makes something shift deep inside: “So… you missed me?”

    Rio feels something tighten in her chest.

    She’s not entirely sure they’re talking about the same thing. But every part of her tells her they are.

    For a heartbeat, she thinks she’s stopped breathing.

    She could play it off as a joke.

    She could throw back something sharp and sarcastic.

    Instead, she simply says: “Yes.”

    No irony. No lightness.

    Just one word.

    A word that hangs between them like something too real, too bare to be hidden.

    Silence on the other side of the door.

    Rio can’t see her, but Agatha is staring straight ahead, as if noticing something she hadn’t seen before.

    “You…” — Agatha clears her throat, as though she’s caught herself mid- thought, and finishes in a steadier tone — “Did you talk to Regina?” Rio tilts her head back, letting it rest against the wall. “No,” she answers, lazily closing her eyes. “I suppose she had her own reasons for keeping it to herself.”

    She doesn’t add anything else. Because what difference does it make now? Everything’s already out in the open, neatly arranged, and the emotions that had hit her in the park have started to settle, leaving behind only a faint aftertaste.

    Agatha doesn’t comment. Doesn’t defend Regina, doesn’t condemn her — just lets the fact hang in the air.

    Silence again.

    Only the low hum of ventilation and the faint tap of Agatha’s fingers on the desk.

    Then: “How’s the apartment hunt going?”

    Rio smirks without opening her eyes.

    “It’s not.”

    She hears Agatha exhale — a short breath, almost like a quiet laugh. “Not at all?”

    Rio shrugs one shoulder.

    “They’re either too small, too expensive, no pets allowed, or on the other side of the city.”

    Silence again.

    But now there’s less formality in it — and something else, harder to name. Or maybe it’s nothing.

    “Agatha?”

    “Yes.”

    “Since we’re apparently in the middle of some romantic movie scene… I have a question.”

    Rio exhales, feeling her heartbeat slow—yet pounding so hard it echoes in her temples.

    Thank God for this door between them. If it weren’t there, if she were staring into those blue eyes she once drowned in, she wouldn’t be able to do it.

    Her hands slowly curl into fists.

    She knows she wants the answer.

    But is she ready for it?

    “Why did you leave the university?”

    A brief silence filters through the other side.

    Then a soft, almost amused breath.

    “I was starting to think you wouldn’t ask.”

    Rio’s lips curve at the corner. Sadly.

    “It took me a while.”

    She hears Agatha shift. Maybe leaning back against the desk again, maybe folding her arms across her chest. It’s almost ridiculous—trying to picture her every move with a worn door between them—but Rio can’t help it. Then Agatha speaks.

    “Did you know there are hidden cameras in the library?”

    Rio’s eyes snap open.

    Her breath freezes somewhere in her chest, caught like an animal in a snare. She can’t immediately name what she’s feeling. Shock? Disbelief? No— something deeper. Something that makes her ears ring and sends a warm, aching rush through her—part realization, part belated fury. Of course. Of course.

    Goddamn it.

    All this time, she’d thought it was cowardice—that Agatha had run, had chosen to walk away instead of staying. And now she finds out it was so much simpler. And in some ways—so much worse.

    The irony hits so sharply that Rio lets out a short, incredulous laugh—one that quickly swells into something more raw, low, almost unhinged. She tilts her head back, feeling the muscles in her neck strain with the force of it. “What an idiot I am.”

    Her own voice sounds rough, compressed—like she’s just run a marathon and is still fighting for breath.

    From the other side of the door comes a pause, then Agatha’s lazy, almost taunting reply: “Oh, you have no idea how many times I’ve thought the same thing.” Rio rolls her eyes, covering her face with one hand.

    “Agatha, I swear to God—”

    “What?” There’s a faint rustle—probably her leaning back in the chair. “Am I wrong?”

    “Oh, you just had to use this moment, didn’t you?”

    “Obviously.”

    Rio shakes her head in tired amusement.

    “So they…”

    “Yes,” Agatha replies evenly. “They have plenty of interesting material, believe me. But, you know, this was… a special case.”

    Rio jerks her head forward, still trying to process it.

    “Shit.”

    “Yes.”

    For a moment, there’s a strange, taut silence. Not oppressive, but charged— with memories, with thoughts, with the words that slip away unspoken. Rio exhales deeply, running her hands over her face. Then she crosses her arms, tilts her head slightly, and asks: “Why didn’t you tell me?”

    Agatha doesn’t answer right away.

    Rio hears her shift something on the desk, as if weighing the response. Then, finally, she says calmly: “Would you have wanted to talk to me?”

    Rio closes her eyes.

    Oh, you damn witch.

    She’s right.

    She didn’t want to.

    She’d been too angry, too hurt, too wrapped up in her own truth to make even the smallest effort to learn another.

    And now, almost two years later, the truth finds her at the exact moment she’s least prepared for it.

    How poetic.

    “They gave you a choice?”

    Rio feels her voice drop—lower, quieter. Almost a whisper. From the other side of the door comes a short inhale.

    “Yes.”

    Agatha says it evenly, but Rio knows her well enough to catch that almost imperceptible pause before the answer.

    As if a memory has surfaced, and she’s deciding whether it’s worth sharing. But she goes on.

    “Only one of us had to leave, and then it would’ve stayed quiet.” Rio closes her eyes, leaning back against the cold wall.

    What a… fucking… farce.

    All this time, she’d imagined a hundred possibilities—ranging from the absurd to the most painful. She thought Agatha had left because she couldn’t take it anymore. Because she was tired. Because she didn’t want to have anything to do with her again. Or maybe because of Nicholas… But not this.

    Not that it had all been reduced to a choice.

    “And why did you come back?”

    A second of silence.

    Then: “Well, I’m currently sitting in the office of the person who made that condition.”

    Rio’s eyes snap open.

    Agatha had moved first again.

    Of course.

    A minute passes in silence, the kind in which both of them know this is the first conversation in a long time that’s felt truly honest. Rio exhales deeply, not looking at the door, and says hoarsely: “Thank you, Agatha.”

    It sounds simple.

    But there’s too much behind it.

    That shadow of gratitude, tangled with something far more complicated. Before either of them can say anything else, footsteps echo down the hallway.

    The guard is back, along with the maintenance guy, who will apparently fix the damn lock.

    And with that, their small, accidental moment—trapped between an old door and a long-awaited truth—will vanish.

    Chapter End Notes Hi, friends. No more secrets now 😉 What do you think?

    Oh, wait. The rest of this day is still to come…

    Chapter 23: Wednesday 2|2

    Rio stood by the column, almost motionless. Only the wind betrayed her presence—lifting loose strands of hair, tugging at the fabric of her coat, carrying with it the sharp scent of stone, of rain, and something distant, like the dusty haze of roads at dusk.

    She did not move. She only stared ahead.

    And thought: What the hell am I doing?

    This wasn’t her style—not to wait like this, without explanation, without a plan, just standing pressed against the wall of a university building like some heroine out of a bad French film, where everything is decided by long glances and silences between words.

    And yet… here she was.

    Minutes already.

    And still she hadn’t left.

    Because inside her—something unfamiliar stirred.

    Not fear. Not dread. Not even the anticipation of a fight. It was a cocktail—bitter with past words, touched with the strange sweetness of that recent honesty through the damned door, and laced with the faint, dangerous hope of what might still exist.

    If she dared.

    Her fingers tightened around the strap of her bag.

    She tried to breathe evenly.

    Tried not to wonder how it looked to anyone passing by. Tried not to think that at any moment someone else might walk through those doors. That this was foolish, awkward. That perhaps Agatha would believe she was waiting for… something more. Or worse.

    But.

    Something in her insisted—Agatha would not mind.

    There was no logic to it.

    Only instinct. The same instinct that had shivered awake that night at the door, when Agatha had spoken—truly spoken—for the first time in what felt like forever.

    The same one that quivered now, with every second that passed. With every thought of her.

    Of the woman she had walked away from—and to whom, somehow, she kept returning, even if her feet had yet to move.

    Rio adjusted the lapel of her coat. A strand of hair brushed her cheek. She let out a sharp breath.

    Soon.

    Soon she would appear.

    And it would begin—or end—all over again.

    Agatha’s heel struck the first step with a measured, deliberate beat. Each click of her shoes on stone resounded in steady rhythm, almost theatrical in its precision. Her gray wool suit—high-waisted skirt, voluminous coat swinging open as she moved—had seemed the perfect picture of restraint that morning. Now the fabric pressed too heavy, the collar of her sweater scratched, the belt at her waist so suffocating she longed to rip it free in one sharp motion.

    Inside, everything was tight. Not sharp anger—no. A dull, viscous irritation, laced with fatigue, lingering dissatisfaction, and… something else. Something formless, unnamed. The conversation with Rio still lived beneath her skin—not in words, not in meaning, but in sensation. A warmth she hadn’t quite managed to cast off. A voice, familiar, uncomfortably close, unbearably right.

    Agatha frowned.

    She was no witch. She couldn’t command her emotions into silence. She could only pack them away into neat boxes, sealed and locked. But now… now something inside refused to let the lid shut completely. Behind her, a guard’s voice drifted in—something about the weather, the late hour, the trouble with equipment. Agatha turned her head slightly, never slowing her pace: “I didn’t ask you to entertain me.”

    He fell silent. Relief washed over her—not from the words, but from the return of quiet.

    The corridor smelled of old paper and fresh metal. Familiar, achingly so. And yet—strange. As if the walls had shifted slightly, as if the air had grown heavier.

    She pressed her lips together and walked on, toward the glass door where the glow of the parking lot lamps spilled in.

    Beyond lay only the street. Only the night. Only the car. And perhaps the silence, in which she might finally think. Though… she wasn’t certain she wanted to.

    Her thoughts—ragged, tangled, clinging like thick wool—were cut short the instant the university door clicked open.

    Cold air struck her face. But it wasn’t the chill that made her freeze on the threshold.

    It was the sight of her.

    Rio.

    Leaning against the column by the entrance, posture loose but immaculate in its poise. Her hair, teased by the wind. Her lips curved with that half- smile Agatha could sketch from memory. Like an old photograph buried deep in a drawer, yet always haunting the edges of dreams. And something inside Agatha, traitorous, leaned forward.

    Rio was watching her. Steady. Attentive. As if she had been waiting. As if she had always known she would see her.

    Agatha faltered. Just half a step, no more. Almost imperceptible. But enough for her heart to lurch.

    She’s here because of me?

    It was absurd. Too absurd to believe. Too sweet to dismiss. She was no witch, she couldn’t conjure storms—but this gaze, this wanted gaze upon her—it gave her breath.

    And still… she feared to name it.

    “You stand there like you weren’t furious the whole time,” Rio said at last, pushing off the column. Her voice was calm, almost domestic, yet tinged with that familiar note—half-teasing, half-provocative. “Or maybe you were just hoping I wouldn’t leave.”

    Agatha paused. A beat, but a heavy one.

    Her fingers still gripped the handle of her bag. Inside, that low tremor remained. The whole day pressed into it—the castle, the office, Rio’s laugh beyond the door. And the way it slipped, still, so easily beneath her skin. “You were hoping I’d leave early,” Agatha replied evenly, her voice steady, unraised. “But apparently you’ve decided to run a night shift on memory duty.”

    “All for the sake of balance,” Rio countered smoothly. “You always were the one for discipline. Think of me as your moral oversight.” Agatha almost laughed. Not aloud—just the faintest movement at the corners of her lips, gone as quickly as it came. Her gaze lingered a heartbeat longer than necessary. In her eyes: exhaustion, conflict… and something else. Fragile. Tentative, like a thread of spider silk trembling in the wind. “You should consider other forms of control, Vidal.”

    “They’ve all failed. You remember.”

    For a moment, silence fell between them. Not empty, but weighted. As if they had come too close to something neither could touch. Or dared to. “Want me to drive you?” Rio asked suddenly, as though casually, though the words struck harder than they should have.

    Agatha didn’t answer at once. Not because she didn’t know what to say— but because she knew too well.

    She had a car. It was less than three minutes away. She was perfectly capable of driving herself. As always.

    And yet, something inside her shifted. Slowly. Heavily.

    This wasn’t about transport. Or the road.

    This was about a step forward.

    And that—was terrifying.

    She blinked, as if to chase away the thought, exhaled through her nose, and said, low and hoarse: “You know… I think I’m too tired to take the wheel tonight.” Her voice was quieter than usual.

    No irony. No armor.

    But carrying the one note only the two of them could hear. And Rio heard it. She understood.

    Rio couldn’t help but smile.

    Not at the answer. Not at the words. But at the way it sounded. At the way Agatha stood across from her, as though she had said something utterly ordinary—as though this wasn’t a small victory over herself. As though it wasn’t the first real crack in the silence of a year and eight months. But simply—yes.

    And Rio could hardly believe it. How simple it was. No scene. No explanations.

    “Perfect,” she said a little too quickly. Then stumbled, shaking her head. “I mean—not that it’s perfect that you’re tired. Just… you know what I mean.”

    Agatha raised her brows for a second, then the corners of her lips twitched into the faintest smile.

    “I know, Rio.”

    And in her tone—something forgotten. Warmth. Almost indulgence. They stepped forward at the same time, in sync, like people trained by years of practice. Side by side, not touching, not looking at each other—but feeling it, the closeness too sharp to ignore.

    Rio led her toward the car—down the steps, past the columns, across the university’s stone porch that had been the stage for so many of their unfinished conversations. The very air seemed to hum with tension, thick with everything left unsaid, lingering between them.

    Inside Rio, something stirred. Something she hadn’t managed to contain all this time. That damn hunger—to see, to hear, to be near. Close. Real. To the point of pain.

    And when they finally reached the car, Rio realized she wasn’t breathing. She let the air out slowly, as if afraid of spilling whatever fragile thing had just begun to take shape again.

    The doors closed almost at the same time. Soft. Nearly soundless. Only the muted click—like a subtle punctuation mark in the dense, strange silence wrapping around them both.

    Rio reached for her seatbelt first, the fabric gliding across her chest before locking into place with a low snap. Out of the corner of her eye—casually, but not really—she caught Agatha doing the same. Precise, measured, deliberate—in her style. Not a single movement wasted, and yet in each of them… something tired. As though she were holding herself together, keeping from breaking apart.

    Rio looked away.

    Her fingers settle on the ignition, and the car answers at once. The low, steady hum of the engine rises around them—not loud, not intrusive, but almost lulling, a backdrop to everything left unsaid.

    They don’t turn on the radio.

    They don’t ask for an address.

    They don’t even look at each other.

    And yet the silence isn’t heavy. It’s something else. The sense that everything needing to be understood is already understood. That everything needing to be said doesn’t yet require words.

    The headlights carve gently through the dark, laying pale ribbons of light across the road. Rio drives with one hand on the wheel, the other resting on her knee. Her pulse beats a little faster than usual—not from fear, not even from nerves, but from this quiet, improbable miracle.

    Agatha. Here. Beside her.

    As if the rest of the world could wait.

    Rio steers evenly, but her gaze keeps flicking—just for a second at a time— to Agatha’s profile. In the half-light her features seem sharper, more austere, and yet there’s something vulnerable there too. A slight crease between her brows, as though her thoughts are pressing too close. At last, Rio breaks the silence.

    “So. How does it feel? Coming back?”

    Agatha doesn’t answer right away. She keeps her eyes on the window, as if checking whether she’s ready for this conversation. Then she exhales, slow and measured, shoulders shifting just a little.

    “Harder than I thought,” she says calmly. “Not the administrative part. I can handle that. But… I’d forgotten how difficult it is, stepping back into a system where no one applauds your return.”

    Rio snorts, not unkindly but with something close to sympathy. “So, what—you’re saying there were no flowers? No marching band?” “Not even a tray of cookies from the dean’s office.” A faint, weary smile ghosts across Agatha’s lips.

    “Savages,” Rio sighs, theatrically. “I would’ve baked. But you would’ve fired me for violating chain of command.”

    “Perhaps.” Agatha’s smile warms just a little. “And then I would’ve eaten them during a meeting and pretended I’d bought them myself.” “There’s the Harkness I know.” Rio shakes her head, flashing her a quick grin before turning back to the road. “But I get it. People, trust, support— it’s never simple. Especially after—” She stops herself, leaving the word unsaid. “Anyway. You’ll manage.”

    Agatha lowers her gaze. Her fingers slowly twist the edge of her coat against her knees.

    “I thought it would be different too,” she admits. “Faster. Softer. But…” She pauses, voice quiet but steady. “Everything has to be rebuilt from the ground up. Even when you’re returning to a place you’ve already been.” “Strange, isn’t it,” Rio says without looking at her, “how you can feel like a stranger in a place where everyone knows your name. If you want my advice—start with cookies. Works better than any year-long strategy.” Agatha lets out a laugh—low, quiet, but real.

    “I’ll add it to my quarterly development plan.”

    Rio’s mouth curves at the corner as she turns her eyes back to the road. And in that second, something passes between them—something that needs no words, no confirmation. Just the simple fact of it: they are driving together. Talking. Again. For real.

    — I’ll manage, — Agatha says quietly, her eyes fixed ahead. Not for the sake of confidence. Not for appearances. Simply because she doesn’t know how to live any other way.

    The words fall almost casually, but inside them lies a deliberate effort, as if she’s persuading not Rio, but herself.

    Her gaze drifts back to the window. Streetlamps, buildings, the black ribbon of road swallowed by the cool night air sweep past. Her road. The one she knows by heart—down to the smallest cracks in the asphalt, the familiar curve behind the park where the trees grow in a thick, unbroken wall. And for the first time in a long while, she’s truly sorry that the drive is so short.

    Not because of the evening. Not because of the city. But because of how strangely easy it feels to breathe next to Rio.

    “If I’m serious for a moment,” Rio says suddenly, her voice careless, almost lazy. But Agatha knows that tone. It means: I thought this through before I opened my mouth.

    Agatha glances at her from the corner of her eye, silent, waiting. “I’ve got something of a plan,” Rio goes on. “Day after tomorrow—it’s Friday. Last of the month. Some of us faculty get together at a bar. No students. Just drinks, retold stories about who punched who in the faculty lounge, complaints about pay and the eternally broken coffee machine.” Agatha turns her head slightly, raising a brow. At the corner of her mouth, a careful, almost playful smile flickers.

    “Sounds… enticing.”

    “I know.” Rio gives a short laugh. “Look—it’s not an invitation, unless you want it to be. But if you do want it… I’ll be there.”

    The simple I’ll be there carries no expectation. No demand. Just a point on the map. A place to start from.

    Agatha lowers her gaze again, watching the blur of city lights melt into shifting shapes on the glass—like thoughts that refuse to stay still. “I’ll think about it,” she says softly. No edge to her tone. No coldness. Just honesty.

    Rio doesn’t turn, doesn’t press. She only lets a small smile curve at the corner of her lips, as if she knew this answer was the only one she’d hear. No pressure. No come on, decide already. Only space.

    Space to step forward. Or to step back, if it’s too much. “All right,” Rio replies simply.

    And it really is all right.

    Because in this car—for the first time in a long time—both of them can breathe.

    Because in these fragments of words, glances, half-said things—something begins to return.

    Something they once lost.

    They don’t rush it.

    They move slowly, as if feeling their way across the cooled embers of the past, unsure whether to stir the fire again. But the road—damn it—has already begun.

    The car eases to a stop. Smooth, gentle, almost imperceptible. As though Rio were careful not to break the fragile quiet in which they are both suspended, afraid of the next step. Agatha doesn’t move. She only registers the hush of the engine—not cut off, but dimmed, like breath held back. Rio doesn’t turn the key.

    The silence that settles isn’t cold. Not the usual kind that presses down on the ribs. It’s different. Expectant. Almost warm.

    Agatha stares ahead. At the streetlight throwing its long shadow across the pavement. At her front door, just a few steps away. All of it familiar to the point of pain. But tonight—it feels altered.

    She knows Rio is watching her. She feels it in her skin, in the pulse along her throat, in the taut line of her shoulders. But she doesn’t turn. Not yet. Her hands move to the seatbelt. The sharp click is like a gasp after too much silence. She blinks once, steadying herself, and only then turns her head.

    “Thank you for—” she begins, then cuts off.

    Because there aren’t words. Because anything she could say would be either too little, or far too much. She just looks. And to her own surprise, that is enough.

    In Rio’s eyes lies an uncertainty—hidden beneath the thin mask of composure. And suddenly Agatha wants to reach out, to still it, or perhaps provoke it, to spark that old, reckless fire between them. But… she can’t. The door opens. The night rushes in—sharp, wet with asphalt and memory. One step out. Another.

    She closes the door gently. Not a slam. Almost reverent. As though the silence between them is sacred, and must not be disturbed. Keys in her pocket. Heels striking wet concrete. One step. Two. Three. And then—her name.

    “Agatha…”

    She stops at once. Not startled. As if she had been expecting it. As if her name were already written in the air, waiting on the lips, hovering in the glow of the streetlight. She turns—slowly. The weight of her coat drags heavy against her back, as though it has grown denser in a single second. Rio sits in the car, window down, hair stirred lightly by the wind. Her face is almost lost to shadow—only the outline visible, the gleam of eyes in half- dark.

    “Yes?” Agatha answers. Even. Calm.

    And silence follows.

    As if Rio had changed her mind. Or lost her nerve. Or… realized that words would never be enough.

    “Good night,” she says at last.

    Agatha smiles. Barely. Just enough. Not out of politeness. Not out of habit. Simply because there’s no other way.

    “And you, Rio.”

    She turns. Not slow, not hurried. Just the way it should be. She climbs the steps. The key slips easily into the lock. A click. The door opens. She steps inside. No light. Just the door closing behind her. She leans her back against it.

    And only then does she let herself exhale.

    Slowly.

    Deeply.

    As if everything she’s been holding back for the last half hour finally breaks free—not as tears, not as words, but as this heavy, viscous silence pressing against her chest like a weight.

    She doesn’t know what it was. She can’t—won’t—analyze it. Not now. But she knows this much: something has begun.

    Or returned.

    And it terrifies her to the bone.

    Her eyes close. Her forehead rests against the cold wood of the door. She doesn’t even take off her coat. She just stands there. Listening to her breath. Her heartbeat.

    And she catches herself on one treacherous thought—tiny, almost imperceptible—that she wants to go back. To the car. To where it smells of coffee, damp fabric, and Rio.

    But—

    “Mom?” Nicholas’s voice, drowsy, warm, blurred, drifts down from the staircase.

    And everything comes back.

    Agatha opens her eyes.

    “I’m here,” she says.

    And lets go.

    Everything else—later.

    When Rio comes home, the silence feels unfamiliar.

    Not comforting—dense. Heavy. As if the house itself knows: something inside her is broken, and now even the most ordinary sounds ring differently. The ticking clock in the hall echoes like words left unsaid. The wind against the windows feels like the residue of stolen glances. The radiator’s soft crackle—like the fading warmth after she closed the car door on Agatha.

    Rio shuts the door slowly, almost mechanically, and the lock’s click reverberates through her head like a bell. She drops her bag to the floor. A dull thud—something discarded, something pointless, like the attempt to hold a straight face when everything inside is burning. She kicks off her shoes quickly, without turning on the light. Darkness doesn’t scare her. It’s easier to breathe in it.

    She makes her way to the kitchen.

    The light there is soft, muted, veiled. Too warm. Almost out of place—like the house had gone on with its own life while she tried to hold herself together in the car beside the woman she still can’t stop looking at. Regina is sitting at the table. Loose gray suit, hair pulled back carelessly, glasses, a book, an apple in her hand. The tired elegance of someone long accustomed to control—and therefore even quicker to notice when someone else is losing it.

    Heidi sleeps curled up at her feet. Rio stops in the doorway. For a second. For a breath. For the ache.

    Regina looks up. Closes the book.

    “Hi,” she says gently, as if she doesn’t want to wound.

    Rio stares at her. Like she’s staring at a painting she loves but can’t bear to hang in tonight’s frame.

    She doesn’t answer. Just shakes her head. Once. Breathes out: “She’s back.”

    Her voice is quiet, fractured. Like glass underfoot.

    “Agatha,” she adds, as though she has to speak the name aloud or suffocate. Regina sets the book down. Silent.

    Rio steps forward. Then again. Reaches the table, braces both palms against it. Inside—nothing but ache. As if something has been torn out and left that way. Without permission.

    “And… I know about you and Emma.”

    Regina stills. Only for a fraction of a second. But enough. Rio doesn’t look at her. Doesn’t search for excuses.

    “I’m not angry,” she says. “It’s not about that. It’s just…” She lifts her gaze. And it’s all there. The tears that don’t fall. The heart that can’t beat any differently. Everything inside her that has cracked and is still trying to take shape again.

    “I don’t know,” she whispers. “I just don’t know what to do with it.” Regina rises. Circles the table. Touches her hand. Carefully. As if afraid to break her.

    And then she embraces her.

    No words. No conditions. Just holds her.

    Rio presses her forehead into her shoulder. Inhales—and recognizes the scent. Not perfume. Not lotion. The scent of home. Of someone steady when you’re drowning.

    “It’s too much,” she murmurs. “Too fast. I thought I was over it. That it was buried, that the pain was gone. And then… she looks at me. She speaks. And it all breathes again. Loudly. Fiercely alive.”

    Regina doesn’t answer. Only strokes her back. Even. Quiet. As if smoothing her breath into rhythm.

    “She’s back at the university,” Rio says, louder this time. “And it feels like punishment. Or mockery. Or maybe… maybe a chance. Only I don’t know how to take it.”

    “You don’t have to know right away,” Regina says softly, but with certainty. Rio pulls back slightly. Sinks into a chair. Shoulders curved. Elbows on her knees. Fingers pressed into her temples.

    “But I want to know.” Her voice cracks, but she holds steady. “I still want her. After everything. After these years. After every attempt to forget. And it’s not right. It’s… so damn foolish.”

    Regina sits beside her. Not closer than needed. Just there. “Have you ever wanted something that can’t be explained?” “I’m a mayor,” Regina replies dryly, tilting her head. “It’s my job to want the impossible.”

    Rio lets out a weak laugh. Tears cling to her lashes but don’t fall. Her gaze drifts—to the floor, to memory.

    “We talked. Just… talked. I was waiting for her to lash out. To say: ‘Why didn’t you fight for me? Why were you silent?’ But she was simply… Agatha. Calm. Sharp-edged. So real.”

    She rubs her face with both hands, as if trying to scrub the feeling away. “And I’m that girl again. The one who fell in love for the first time. And it terrifies me. Because if I step into that river again… I’ll drown. And if I don’t—I’ll stand on the shore and watch her leave.”

    Heidi stirs, stretches, comes over and rests her head on Rio’s knees. Rio strokes her slowly, with the same caution she’s using now to decide how to go on living.

    “I don’t know what’s coming,” she says quietly. “But if she really came back—not just in body, but as herself, the woman she used to be—then I have to at least try. Not to destroy it all again.”

    Regina nods. Her eyes steady, anchoring.

    “That’s reasonable.”

    “It’s madness,” Rio whispers.

    Regina smiles. Almost maternal. She knows exactly what to say. “Reasonable madness,” she replies.

    And in that moment—despite the tears, the doubt, the gnawing fear—Rio feels it.

    She is not alone.

    And maybe that, in itself, is the beginning.

    The bedroom greets her with silence and half-shadow, where every object seems a little softer, a little calmer than in daylight. Everything is in its place. Everything under control. Her control. And yet—there is something about this evening she cannot shelve. Something that refuses to be boxed away under the neat label of past.

    Agatha sits on the edge of the bed, crosses her legs, and reaches for the jar of cream on the nightstand. A small gesture—and the cool texture spreads across her skin, worked in with slow, circular motions, as if her fingers are searching in this ritual not only for care but for clarity. The act is an anchor. Something constant. Tangible.

    The scent is neutral, nearly imperceptible. Just the way she prefers: without sweetness, without pink deception, without the pretense of being gentler than she is.

    And still… in her mind, she is back in the car.

    Back where the seatbelt pressed faintly through her sweater. Where the steering wheel in Rio’s hands felt less like a wheel and more like a fixed point in time. Where their conversation was at once too cautious and too ordinary to erase.

    She rubs the cream into her wrists, exhales, and lowers her hands to her knees. Her eyes face forward but see something else entirely. Everything about Rio tonight—her silence, her jokes, her glances over the shoulder—felt… familiar. Comfortable. Almost like home. Almost. And in that almost lay the entire problem.

    Agatha does not want to return to the same river. She has been in it before. Neck-deep. Burned, drowned, gasping—and she got out. Alone. Broken, scorched, but alive.

    Rio now… she seems different. More restrained. Less reckless. But Agatha knows too well what hides behind restraint. She remembers too vividly how easily nuance can be mistaken for promise. Desire—for loyalty. Not enough.

    She nearly says it aloud.

    Not enough words. Not enough gestures. Not enough to stake herself on. And yet—the thought refuses to leave.

    She slips out of her clothes, left in only a thin dark camisole, and stretches out across the cool sheets. Her back sinks into the mattress, her body slowly releasing its tension. But in her head—the car again. The evening again. Rio again.

    She remembers the way she turned at the traffic light. The softness in her gaze. The silence. And how she didn’t ask for more. Didn’t reach out, didn’t take her hand, didn’t say stay.

    Too correct to be honest.

    Agatha doesn’t trust correctness. Not from Rio.

    She reaches for the switch, and with a click, the room plunges into darkness. Only the streetlamp outside draws a pale shadow of the window frame across the ceiling.

    And then the phone lights up.

    A cold rectangle of light slices through the dark like a camera flash in an empty room. Agatha doesn’t look right away. Then, slowly, she turns her head.

    Only a name on the screen.

    Rio Vidal.

    A message waits. The content hidden.

    And in that brief pause—in that unopened secret—something inside her catches.

    Her heart skips a beat.

    All it would take is a hand reaching out. A single motion. One touch. But she doesn’t do it right away. She only looks.

    Then—as if something within whispers not yet—she sets the phone gently back on the nightstand. Her face remains composed. Her gaze fixed on the ceiling. Her breath steady. Everything under control.

    And yet, a few seconds later—just as her body begins to drift into sleep— the corner of her mouth betrays her.

    A smile.

    So faint it could be mistaken for a trick of the light.

    But it is there.

    Chapter 24: Friday

    Friday evening. The air was cool, carrying that tart freshness of spring—not yet promising warmth, but already different from the bite of winter cold. Somewhere on the horizon, a violet dusk was gathering, and the city was slowly surrendering to the night.

    Rio stood at the entrance of the bar, her shoulder resting against the cold wall. The neon sign above her head spilled its glow across her face, sliding along the metal of her rings, glinting in the shadows of her eyelids. She wore a black dress—tight, short, covered in a wide mesh that revealed the smooth, matte fabric beneath. Over it, a leather jacket, soft and dark, as though it had absorbed all the heat of her body and the tension of the past two days. Her hair was half-pinned up, while the remaining strands—dark, fading into lighter ends—fell over her shoulders, brushing the collar. Rings adorned her fingers, several of different shapes and weight. Cold metal contrasted with the warmth of her skin. Her nails, short and painted black, caught the faint gleam of her phone’s screen. Her gaze drifted along the street, over the lights, over the couples slipping into the bar, but in truth, she wasn’t looking at anyone. Everything inside her was concentrated on waiting.

    “I still don’t understand why the hell you invited her,” Jennifer muttered at her side, rolling her eyes. Her voice was low, almost masculine, tinged with irritation. “And now here we are, standing around like couriers waiting to deliver a package. The new director, for God’s sake.”

    Rio didn’t answer right away. She pressed the pack of cigarettes in her hand, then shoved it back into her pocket. She was all made of tension— every line of her posture carried that restless energy, as though her body could not quite stand still.

    “Because I wanted to,” she said sharply. “Isn’t that reason enough?” Jennifer snorted but stepped aside to light up. Lilia was already exchanging words with a man in a suit near the entrance, while the others chatted idly among themselves, seemingly oblivious to the storm gathering over Rio. But Rio felt it.

    And she couldn’t escape it.

    Two days ago, she had unlocked Agatha’s number again. Just like that— without thinking, as though it were a casual decision. And then she had sent a message. Not words. Not a plea. Only an address. No come over, no I’ll be waiting, no will you come? Just a point on the map.

    Agatha had replied. One word.

    Thank you.

    And now Rio was here. Standing with her jacket clutched in her fists, her palms sweating, biting the inside of her lip to keep from lighting up. She didn’t know if Agatha would come. She didn’t know what would happen if she did. But the worst part—the part that clawed at her insides—was knowing that it still mattered.

    It mattered too much.

    And just as she was about to turn away, just as the voice inside her was rising, whispering that she wasn’t coming—she saw the yellow taxi in the distance.

    Rio froze. Everything around her seemed to fall silent. The street noise, the chatter, the footsteps—all of it blurred into nothing. There was only the sweep of headlights, the soft sound of brakes, the metallic click of a car door opening.

    She didn’t move. She only watched. Waiting. Afraid.

    Hoping.

    It was her.

    Rio knew at once—before the cab had even fully stopped, before the door had opened. It was that feeling, that shiver across her skin, like a flash of light one could never explain. Pure knowledge. Absolute. As certain as gravity.

    The door opened.

    At first—only a leg.

    Slender, drawn in the delicate strap of an almost weightless stiletto sandal. Then the dark fall of fabric, a dress skimming her thigh like a shadow. And a moment later, Agatha emerged—not stepping out of the car so much as appearing—slowly, with that grace that required no effort, no performance. She wore black. A short, fitted dress with a plunging neckline and long, flowing sleeves that trailed behind her like extensions of each step. Like smoke. Like a train woven out of the evening itself. It did not glitter, did not demand attention, yet it shaped every curve of her body with such ease, with such audacity, that breathing became difficult.

    Her hair was long, dark, soft, falling in loose waves down her back and shoulders, springing lightly with each step, catching and bending the light. It seemed the air grew warmer by a degree simply because she had arrived. On her wrists, several bracelets—silver and black stone—chiming faintly as she moved. Her makeup was restrained, precise. A sharp line of eyeliner, a shimmer of shadow, lips painted in dark wine red that could speak too much with the smallest curve, and would have—if she chose.

    Agatha’s gaze swept over those gathered at the entrance. Quick. Even. Calm. A little cold. She marked them all in passing, as a director might check a room: one flick of the lashes—and on.

    Until her eyes found her.

    Rio.

    Their gazes met—and the world stilled.

    For the span of half a breath, everything slowed. Rio felt it as something physical: the air thickening, the ground tilting beneath her feet. Agatha’s gaze was steady, level, calm. Yet within it—something softer. Almost imperceptible. Like the shadow of a smile forming deep within her eyes. And then—her lips. Just barely. The faintest curve. A half-smile, intimate, almost not meant for the world to see. But it struck Rio like a blow to the chest.

    Her ribcage tightened. Heat flared beneath her skin. Her heart pounded so loudly she was sure it must be heard.

    It was only a glance.

    Only the ghost of a smile.

    Only Agatha.

    Yet in that instant, Rio thought: if the world chose to collapse, let it be now. Because she could endure no more.

    With unhurried poise, Agatha shut the taxi door. She turned and began to walk toward them. Each step was like a measured note in a melody. Like a scene from a film, the camera slowing deliberately so every detail could be absorbed.

    Rio did not move.

    She only watched. Too sharply. Too hungrily.

    As though if she blinked—Agatha would vanish.

    Two hours had passed.

    By then, the bar had softened, blurred, as though someone had poured a touch of wine and warm honey into the air. The music no longer seemed loud—it pulsed faintly to the side, like the heartbeat of the evening itself. Laughter—true, tipsy, deep—flared here and there. The low hum of voices, the clink of glasses, the mellow glow of the lamps—all of it folded together into that particular picture that usually belongs to midnight, when even skeptics stop keeping track of time.

    They all sat gathered at the high wooden table by the window—the one always claimed as “the teachers’ table.” The chairs had stiff backs, but no one minded anymore. The surface was strewn with almost-empty plates, limp straws, melted ice, scraps of cheese and finger food, discarded napkins. Fingers idly played with the rims of glasses, voices rose louder than necessary, and no one seemed to notice.

    Lilia—hair undone, cheeks flushed with wine—was telling some story in far too much detail. She laughed at herself halfway through, stumbling over details, and it only added charm to her tale.

    “…So there I am, standing in the middle of the hall. One shoe in my hand. The dress zipper stuck in the restroom. And this guy is staring at me like I’m his personal tragedy, and I just say: ‘You wouldn’t happen to be a psychiatrist, would you? Because I think I’m about to have a breakdown.’” The table erupted. A burst of laughter, loud enough to shake the glasses. Someone slapped the wood with their palm, Jennifer choked on her beer, Rose snorted so hard tears sprang to her eyes.

    Rio smiled, too. The corner of her mouth curved, a ghost of sound slipped out with it, quick and fleeting. But it did not linger. It passed over her face like a shadow, vanishing almost as soon as it appeared. Because all her focus, all her thoughts—were elsewhere.

    She was watching Agatha.

    Agatha sat a little apart, leaning back against the tall chair. One might have called the pose indolent—if not for the perfect line of her spine, neither too rigid nor too relaxed. As though even alcohol held no sway over her trained grace.

    She wore the same black dress. Yet it seemed more alluring now than it had two hours earlier. Perhaps because of the lamp-light resting against her collarbones in soft pools of gold. Perhaps because she had tilted her head slightly, letting her hair fall forward—heavy, glossy, alive. In her hand, a martini glass. A smooth turn of the wrist, the thin stem balanced effortlessly, the surface trembling with a single drop that never spilled. She listened to Lilia with half an ear, eyes narrowed, mouth curving faintly. A smile—soundless, weary, beautiful.

    That smile was undoing her.

    Rio felt something tighten in her chest again. Not pain. Not quite. Just tension. The kind that comes before a step into the void. She could not look away.

    Not for a minute.

    Not for a word.

    It was as if the whole scene, the drunken laughter and the clatter of glasses, existed only as background noise. As if Agatha alone carried any meaning at all.

    Lilia, breathless and gleeful, landed the final punchline: “And then—imagine this—he hugged me and said, ‘I think what you need is a vacation… and possibly an exorcist.’”

    The table burst again into laughter. Someone clapped; Jennifer shook her head, shouting through her own fit of giggles, “You’re insane!” And Rio— Rio looked at Agatha once more.

    Her eyes had lowered, her lips curved into a smile. Not loud, not for show —simply human. As though she were truly enjoying the moment. Yet there was something else in it, something quieter. As though she were present, yet also far away, half her thoughts sunk somewhere deeper, at the bottom of the glass—or in someone sitting close enough to touch, but who never would.

    Rio, as if in a dream, set down her glass. The tumbler, with its half-melted ice, slid softly across the wood. Every line of her body carried tension, a mute concentration. Laughter still rang around her, but within her chest there was only the pounding of her heart.

    It wasn’t a provocative look. It wasn’t even tender, not exactly. It was the kind of gaze that comes once in a lifetime—when you don’t know whether you want to rise and cross the distance, reach out a hand, vanish into the crowd… or remain like this for another hour, another night, a lifetime. Agatha lifted her eyes.

    And everything was there.

    Rio didn’t even have time to pretend she’d been looking elsewhere. They caught each other in the pure, unguarded instant where nothing could be hidden.

    For a fraction of a second, something flickered in Agatha’s gaze—mild surprise, perhaps. Or recognition. Or the faintest, silent admission: I know. And in that instant, Rio felt the weight of the evening shift. It was no longer just a gathering, no longer just a bar. It became a point. One of those that divides a life into “before” and “after.”

    Around them, the laughter faded. Someone ordered another round; someone else suggested karaoke for the fifth time. And Rio was still looking. At her. Only at her.

    And she could not look away.

    “Alright,” Lilia rasped at last, fanning herself as if her own story had drained her. “Who’s next? Jenny? Or perhaps Miss Harkness will open a secret door into her student years?”

    Everyone’s heads turned toward Agatha. Someone slapped the table, another froze theatrically with their cheek propped on a hand, while Jennifer narrowed her eyes and drawled, “Right, especially if she tells us how she was firing people before graduation.”

    Agatha raised a brow. She didn’t tense—on the contrary, she slipped into that state in which she was most dangerous: almost relaxed, a touch lazy, restrained yet playfully sharp.

    “I’m afraid firing anyone wasn’t within my power back then.” She lifted her glass, taking a slow sip of martini. “But once… once I did make three students retake the entire course. Their mistake was attempting to bribe a professor with a thirty-dollar bottle of wine.”

    “You’re a monster,” Lilia burst out, laughing.

    “I’m order,” Agatha replied evenly, without malice.

    Laughter rolled again. Someone flagged down the waiter, someone else shouted, “To order!” and raised a glass.

    But Rio still wasn’t laughing.

    She smiled—slowly, faintly. Yet inside, everything churned. She reached for her glass, not to drink, simply to hold it. Like an anchor. God, she’s beautiful.

    It wasn’t a new thought, not a sudden one, only sharper in this moment. Agatha in that dress—black, revealing just enough to unravel the mind—sat with a self-assurance so complete it asked for nothing, demanded nothing. And precisely that was what made everyone turn toward her. Her posture was composed. The bend of her wrist over the rim of the glass. Black heels, ankles crossed. And that dress. That cursed silhouette Rio hadn’t been able to erase since the instant she saw it.

    She wasn’t flirting. She simply was.

    And Rio was suffocating in that being.

    At some point Agatha felt the weight of her gaze again. And turned. Straight into her eyes.

    Rio didn’t look away. Didn’t pretend. Didn’t feign interest in the lamp overhead, or in Lilia—who was already launching into another tale, this time about a swimming pool, a neighbor, and bedbugs in the dormitory. She simply looked back.

    And in that look was everything she could not say.

    Agatha’s face did not change. But her fingers tightened, just slightly, around the stem of her glass.

    For a moment, the two of them ceased to exist within the company. There was only them.

    And the silence between their gazes was the loudest sound of the night. As though everything before had been a prelude. And the evening was only just beginning.

    “You know,” Jennifer drawled, propping her elbow on the table and spinning her glass between two fingers, “we’ve known each other for a hundred years, and I’ve heard your embarrassing stories in a thousand variations.” She smirked, tipping her chin toward Rio. “But I’d like something fresher. For instance… how are things with Wanda, Rio?” The silence hit like a gunshot.

    Not funereal, not staged—just sharp, abrupt, like a collective breath cut short. Someone still chuckled under their breath, someone pretended to sip their drink, someone else buried their face in a phone. But Rio felt it instantly—the shift, sudden and cold, like a hand pressed to fevered skin. And Agatha’s gaze. Calm. Piercing. Attentive.

    As though a second sight had opened in her.

    Rio blinked. Once.

    Inhaled. Exhaled.

    “It’s nothing serious,” she said simply. Her voice was steady, without a tremor. “We didn’t even see each other.”

    Jennifer’s shoulders dropped, almost imperceptibly—as though she had expected a different answer.

    But Rio gave her nothing. No fuel, no excuse. Not because she wanted to hide, but because every word spoken now would sound like a lie, no matter the content.

    Agatha did not look away.

    And that was the hardest part.

    She was still watching. Not accusing. Not condemning. Just… interested. As if studying something new. As if testing how far it could go. And Rio? Rio felt as though nothing remained in her head except one desperate thought—don’t lose this invisible thread that had suddenly stretched between them.

    She glanced aside for a second, only long enough to steady herself. A hundred explanations pressed on her tongue, excuses, even a joke or two— but she spoke none of them. Not because she feared saying too much, but because between her and Agatha there no longer seemed to be any space for falsehood. Even if everyone else at the table still lived inside that illusion. “Well then,” Lilia broke in, trying to drag the air back into lightness, “Rio, tell us instead—if not Wanda, then who is it now?”

    Rio exhaled into her glass, as though martini could supply an answer. And suddenly, before she could speak, Agatha leaned forward ever so slightly. “Perhaps we shouldn’t force anyone to answer uncomfortable questions?” Her voice was soft, but threaded with that refined, predatory intonation Rio remembered too well.

    The table fell quiet. For a beat. Two. Someone smirked, someone raised their brows, and Jennifer didn’t even look offended—just lifted her shoulders in a shrug.

    “Fine,” she said. “It was just getting dull. Don’t mistake me, professor, I wasn’t about to light the pyres.”

    “Evening’s not over yet,” Agatha replied, reclining against her chair again. Rio felt her heart leap.

    She didn’t know what exactly had just happened. A shield? A warning? Or… a sign?

    But in that brief exchange—in those glances, in that pause—there was everything they could not yet say aloud.

    The glasses were filled again. Someone raised a toast—for Friday, for the bar, for the proof that even professors still had a heart. A joke landed, an elbow nudged a neighbor, and the night rolled onward—loud, warm, alive, yet no longer quite the same as before.

    Because between the two women at the table there hung a tension—not scandalous, not dramatic, but unbearably personal.

    Like lightning between fingers.

    Like a whisper heard only by the one who has been waiting for it. Agatha moved first. She did it with startling composure. A light lift of her hand, a graceful adjustment of her hair—as if lazily covering a yawn. And then, with that practiced civility of hers, she said: “Excuse me, ladies. I need to powder my nose.”

    She rose from her chair with perfect unhurried grace, as though everything remained under her control. As though she truly were only going to the restroom, and not leaving behind a charge of explosives primed to detonate the moment she stepped away. Perhaps that was her true danger. Rio followed her with her eyes, the way one follows something impossible —with a breath caught, a stretch inside the chest, a nameless pull. Her legs almost lifted her from the chair, but she didn’t move.

    Too many witnesses.

    Jennifer still beside her. Lilia spinning another story. Someone joking. Someone clapping Rio on the shoulder. The world roared and flowed and carried on in its meaningless motion, while inside Rio stretched an abyss— sharp, raw, with jagged edges.

    She waited. She waited like a fool. Like the sixteen-year-old version of herself. Until the end of the song, until the last chord faded—only then did she rise. Slowly. As if she merely felt like stretching her legs. She snatched her phone from the table, dropped her gaze, hid her eyes beneath the heavy veil of lashes.

    Her heart thundered in her ears. Too loud. Too strong.

    As she moved across the room, through the dancers and the laughter, through the chatter and the tide of voices, no plan formed in her mind. Not a single line. Not a single defense.

    She didn’t know if she would justify herself.

    Or strike first.

    Perhaps only look.

    Or perhaps say: I missed you.

    Or: You look like someone who still remembers.

    She didn’t know. But she kept walking.

    And—God—she was grateful for the alcohol. For the soft, buzzing haze that made it easier to breathe, easier not to feel her knees trembling. Easier to do what sober courage would never allow.

    She pushed open the door to the restroom.

    And found Agatha at the mirror.

    She was bent slightly over the sink, washing her hands. Her movements were measured, precise, as always. Yet something about them was taut—as if she repeated the gesture mechanically while something far more turbulent raged inside.

    Water streamed down her fingers.

    Then Agatha lifted her gaze.

    Their eyes met in the mirror.

    Direct. Unfiltered. Unshielded.

    Blue, piercingly clear.

    She did not smile.

    And in that mirrored look was everything Rio had feared.

    And everything she had been walking toward all the same.

    “Agatha…”

    Agatha turned off the tap.

    No sharp movement—only the soft twist of the handle, the muted rush of water cutting off into a silence that rang far louder. She reached for the paper towels with deliberate ease, as though that were the most important act in the world. She dried her hands carefully, with a woman’s practiced thoroughness, the fabric of her dress brushing lightly along her thighs as she moved.

    Only then did she finally turn.

    “Don’t bother,” she said. Her voice was level—too level to be truly calm. “You don’t owe me anything.”

    Rio froze a step away. Her throat locked, tight as a fist. Yet the words tore out anyway: “It was… it was just messages. Only messages. I never even—” Agatha raised her hand. Not in anger. But with the precision of a surgeon. “I heard,” she interrupted evenly. “And, honestly? It doesn’t matter. We’re not who we were. We’re not together. We’re two separate people, and each of us has every right to—”

    “Stop,” Rio breathed, low and ragged. “Just… stop. None of that is true. Not a single goddamn part of it.”

    She pulled out her phone, thumb fumbling against the screen until it unlocked. Her fingers trembled, but still moved quickly, unerring. She opened the messenger, found the thread with Wanda, and without even glancing at it, held the phone out to Agatha.

    “This is my move,” she said. Her voice was quiet, but sharp as a blade. “Now it’s yours.”

    Agatha looked at her. Not at the phone. At her eyes. Long. Searching. As if in that one second she weighed everything: the pain, the foolishness, the old wounds, and how much damage a single choice could yet inflict. Rio stood there as though on the edge of a roof, breath caught, suspended. And when Agatha’s fingers touched her hand—lightly, from beneath, almost tender—something inside Rio shivered. Then Agatha lifted her other hand and, with a single motion, pressed the screen.

    “Block contact.”

    A hollow click.

    The phone went dark again.

    And Rio—if not for the alcohol, if not for the river of wine and martinis that had carried them here—might have simply collapsed into tears from the sheer, unbearable relief.

    But Rio only exhales. Slowly. With a muffled tremor in her chest, as if after a storm.

    “You—” she begins, but Agatha shakes her head.

    “I’m not promising you anything, Rio.”

    “I know.”

    “And I’m not asking for anything in return.”

    “But I would give it.”

    And in that silence, before the mirrors, in the drip of water and the muted glow of light, there seems to be the smallest of steps between them. Agatha is quiet for a time. Long enough that it cannot be accidental, and short enough that Rio has no chance to turn away.

    And then, at last, she says: “I’m going to kiss you right now.”

    Plain. Even. Without drama.

    As though it were a decision. As though she were announcing something inevitable.

    And still—Rio feels the floor drop out beneath her. Her heart lodged somewhere in her throat, her fingers clutching the dead weight of a darkened phone she no longer feels. There is only Agatha. Only her gaze. Only this moment, holding far too much.

    Rio exhales, lips trembling.

    “I don’t mind.”

    And she kisses her first.

    No warning. No seeking permission in a glance. She simply steps in, crushes her mouth to Agatha’s, cupping her face in both hands. Not gently. Not slowly. But with everything that could not be said, everything that had screamed inside her for nearly two years, everything that had torn at her chest until now.

    Agatha does not resist.

    On the contrary—she leans in, deeper, harder. Her hands close around Rio’s waist, fingers tightening as if to test whether this is real, not some phantom. And when she answers the kiss, it is no longer silence, no longer caution. It is hunger. It is fury. It is longing laced with memory.

    It is bitter.

    Ugly.

    Too long to be sweet, too desperate to be beautiful.

    Rio’s tongue slips between her lips, and Agatha does not hold back the sound that escapes her—a low, restrained moan, almost inaudible, yet so achingly real it steals the breath from Rio’s lungs.

    Agatha’s fingers are lost in her hair. Rio clutches her waist as if afraid she’ll vanish. Within the kiss lies everything: fear, guilt, anger, and that forbidden feeling they had buried alive.

    And then—Agatha pulls back. Only an inch.

    Forehead to forehead. Breath ragged. Lips wet, swollen, throbbing with pain and desire.

    “This was a terrible idea,” Agatha whispers.

    “The best one,” Rio answers, her eyes still closed. “The best one I’ve had in all this time.”

    And they stand there, in the cramped room, wrapped in the scent of soap, perfume, and alcohol. With strangers’ voices murmuring beyond the door and far too much “after” waiting for them on the other side. But now—only them.

    And if it lasts just a second—then it is still everything. Rio hasn’t let go. Her hands remain at Agatha’s waist, but the fury is gone, the sharp edges dulled. Only their slow breathing remains, syncing at last. She brushes her nose against Agatha’s cheek, hesitates as if gathering courage, and whispers: “I want more.”

    Agatha lifts her eyes. Her face still carries the imprint of the kiss: damp lips, flushed skin, lashes that tremble faintly. She smiles—thinly, carefully —with that quiet, adult sorrow Rio knows too well, the kind that cannot be torn out, not even by force.

    But Rio only shakes her head, fingers tightening as though she can read her thoughts.

    “Not what you’re thinking. Though, God, tonight I’ll probably fantasize about it twenty times.” She smiles too, almost guilty, almost girlish. “But no. I mean I want to do it right.”

    Agatha studies her, unblinking. In her gaze—expectation. Caution. And the shadow of pain Rio recognizes like her own reflection.

    “Right?” Agatha repeats softly.

    “By all the rules,” Rio says, leaning close until her forehead rests against hers again. “First kiss—done. Second one—on the second date. Sex? Fifth. Ideally. No rushing. No vanishing acts. No cursed cameras or someone else’s ultimatums.”

    Agatha exhales slowly, as if she can’t quite let herself believe. “That will be… difficult.”

    Rio smirks, lifting one hand to tuck a strand of hair behind Agatha’s ear. “Yeah. But honestly? I’d be disappointed if anything with you were easy.” They laugh almost at the same time. Real laughter. Honest laughter. And it sounds like—for the first time in far too long—the weight in their chests has finally lifted.

    “And what about tomorrow?” Rio asks softly, drawing back just enough to see her face, though her hands don’t let go. “The park. The one by the fair. I’ll bring Heidi. I swear she’ll behave better than me—cross my heart.” Agatha studies her for a long moment. Then she nods.

    “Tomorrow.”

    Rio smiles. Not quickly, not triumphantly, but with that fragile, wild tenderness that comes only after pain. After long, heavy waiting. “This can’t be real,” she whispers, barely audible. “Too much time, too much loneliness, and then—you. Here. Now.”

    Agatha doesn’t answer. But her eyes speak enough. They’re softened, faintly misted, reflecting the very thing Rio feels herself—fear, relief, disbelief.

    “It isn’t real, is it?” Rio continues, her voice trembling. “I’m actually asleep. Alone, in my cold bed. And this… this is just some cruel dream. And tomorrow…” She closes her eyes for a heartbeat, as if to inhale this fragile bliss, “tomorrow I’ll wake up again with empty hands.” But she doesn’t retreat. Doesn’t let go.

    “Only—” her voice drops lower, huskier, “if this really is a dream… I won’t survive unless I do this again.”

    And before Agatha can answer—if she ever meant to—Rio leans in. Slowly, reverently.

    Her fingers glide along Agatha’s cheek, tracing her as though rediscovering her features, afraid they might dissolve. Her eyes linger on her lips—so familiar, so unbearably real that it terrifies her.

    When their mouths meet again, everything changes.

    No hunger. No violence. They meld instead—slow, steady, like two fragments at last finding their rightful shape.

    Rio kisses her with care. With gratitude. With that trembling warmth that steals the breath from her lungs. Not greedily—but drinking her in, as though she has thirsted for this taste for years.

    Agatha doesn’t pull away.

    She moves closer. Slowly, like drifting into this intimacy, letting herself soften for the first time in far, far too long.

    They remain wrapped in each other, breathing as one, the world dissolving around them.

    And Rio knows—God, she knows—if this is a dream, she never wants to wake.

    Chapter 25: Saturday

    The tile beneath her cheek was cold. Not freezing—no, not the kind of cold that set teeth chattering on its own—but cool enough that the chill seeped into her breath, turning shivers into law: exhale—gooseflesh, inhale— weakness. A thin wash of sunlight filtered through the frosted pane set high in the wall, striping the white ceramic with pale, reluctant bands. They offered no warmth. They were only a reminder that the world went on, that time still moved, that Saturday had arrived. And that she was still alive. Rio lay curled on her side, knees drawn tight to her chest, as if the posture alone could keep her from unraveling. Her thighs ached. Her stomach was both empty and unbearably heavy, as though a stone had been dropped, hot and punishing, inside her. Her mouth tasted of salt, of metal, of a sour void. Not the flavor of martinis, not the echo of wine, but something rawer, more primitive—a body’s revenge against the audacity of testing its limits. Her head throbbed. Not with the percussion of a hangover, but with something else: a slow, sticky hum, the sort of resonance that felt as if someone had hooked a spoon behind her brain and was drawing it out, inch by inch, with deliberate cruelty. She hadn’t drunk that much in years—not enough to lose control. And last night, she hadn’t lost it. Quite the opposite. She remembered everything. Every word. Every glance. Every brush of Agatha’s mouth against hers. And that was more terrifying than any blackout could ever be.

    And then there was the roll.

    She couldn’t even remember how it ended up in her hands. Someone— Lilia, perhaps, or Jennifer—had said there was a twenty-four-hour café just around the corner. The air had smelled of scorched rice and soy, of onions caramelized past sweetness, of meat dripping fat. At some point, she had found herself inside, swaying at the counter, eyes blurred not by drink but by some strange hunger—was it for food, or simply for something, anything, to fill her? She hadn’t eaten all day. Not a bite. Not a crumb. The woman behind the till slid a carton across the counter, the plastic strap stamped with a faded red dragon. Careful, it’s spicy, she’d said, her tone more threat than warning. Rio had smirked. She ate it standing, walking, unbothered by sauce or utensils. She couldn’t remember if she’d had either. And then… the house.

    She remembered slamming the front door shut behind her. Climbing the stairs in darkness. Laughing to herself—not loudly, not freely, but with that strange, soundless laugh that carried relief and fear and something dangerously close to joy, all tangled together.

    On the second floor, when the bathroom light hit her eyes, when her reflection caught in the mirror—dress, makeup, the lingering trace of someone else’s perfume on her fingers—it all broke. A dam gave way inside her.

    The nausea came slowly. Not as a wave, not as a tide. But as a traitor— something crawling upward through her throat, claws dragging at soft tissue, tearing, scraping. She had no chance. No time to undress, to wash her face. She fell to her knees, jaw locked, breath stuttering, prayerless words dying against her clenched teeth.

    The toilet loomed before her, a throne of disgrace.

    And then—darkness. Not fainting, no. Just… a suspension of time. She could not say how long she lay there. Twenty minutes. Two hours. It didn’t matter.

    The floor was littered with fallen towels, with the faint chalky trace of dried soap on the tiles, a streak of toothpaste hardened at the sink. Everything felt alien, as though this bathroom belonged to someone else. (Even if it was Regina’s house.)

    Her memory contained only Agatha. The press of lips. The frost of her eyes. “I want more.” And her own reply: “I want to do it right.” God.

    She laughed. Dry. Broken. The sound scraped out of her chest like air forced down the wrong pipe.

    Rio lifted her head. Slowly. First her neck, weighted as though with iron. Then her spine, stiff, reluctant. And at last, her eyes found the mirror. A shadow stared back.

    Mascara blurred to bruises, lipstick caught in the corners of her mouth, strands of hair clinging damp to her face. Her skin was pale, her under-eyes ringed in violet blue, the color of a beating. Her lips cracked, raw. And still—she was beautiful. Brutally, unbearably beautiful. Beautiful the way ash is beautiful after fire. The way a raven fallen from its flock can still hold a glint of sky in its eye.

    Rio dragged her tongue across her lips. Dry. Bitter.

    The kiss.

    That first one. And the second.

    “I want to do it right.”

    And here she was—queen of the porcelain throne, stomach cursed by a dragon-printed roll, clinging to the chance of happiness that waited for her somewhere beyond the bathroom door.

    At first it was a knock. Soft, almost apologetic. The kind of knock you use when it’s the fifth time you’ve checked on someone who refuses, point- blank, to remain among the living. The door, of course, was already ajar— Rio had forgotten to close it sometime in the night. Or in the morning. Or… who even kept track anymore. A century had passed since then, one roll and half a bottle of water ago.

    Regina appeared in the doorway like a ghost of good taste and stubborn patience. She wore a gray sweater, loose and comfortable, the sleeves tugged down over her fingers as if to remind Rio that warmth still existed in the world. In one hand she carried a glass of lemon water, in the other a folded, freshly washed towel. Behind her loitered Heidi, loyal as ever, her expression a mixture of steady vigilance and unspoken promise—if you need me to, I’ll hug her. Or lick her. Just say the word. Regina didn’t hurry inside. She simply leaned against the frame and looked at Rio. Calm. Almost philosophical. The way you look at someone who has finally reached the bottom and, by sheer survival, has earned clean water, sympathy, and a few well-placed barbs.

    “Well then, goddess of morning rebirth,” Regina murmured after a pause, “you’re still here. Victory. At least you’re consistent about something.” Rio, still hunched over the sink like a ship bracing through a storm, didn’t turn her head. She only gave a short, humorless snort.

    “Yes. That will be my new title: Queen of the Porcelain Throne. Mission— survive a questionable roll and catastrophic emotional overload.” “Judging by how you look,” Regina continued, her mouth quirking with affectionate amusement, “I’d say you’ve just endured something epic. The hero’s journey. Practically statue-worthy. Or at the very least, deserving of a medal. Made of ginger.”

    “If you even think about feeding me ginger cookies, I’ll die on the spot,” Rio muttered, finally daring to glance at her reflection. “Though maybe that would be merciful.”

    Regina nodded solemnly. “Not cookies. Just water. A towel. And moral support. No judgment.”

    Rio exhaled, gripping the porcelain basin as if it were the wheel of a ship she had no business steering. The sound carried all the melodrama of a woman who, in less than twenty-four hours, had kissed her ex, devoured something potent enough to poison an ox, and was now paying penance before the merciless gods of digestion.

    “I feel like I’ve been flattened by a bus,” she said, dragging out the words, “a bus filled with memories and food poisoning. I swear, I’m in hell. And not the kind where Agatha strolls around in lingerie. The real kind.” Regina gave a short, genuine laugh. “Encouraging that you can still string sentences together. Means your liver hasn’t abandoned ship.” Rio closed her eyes, tilting her face toward the ceiling as though begging for deliverance.

    “And of course,” she groaned, “it had to happen right after we finally talked. Do you understand me, Regina?”

    There was silence. And then—very deliberate, very pointed— “…What?”

    Rio lowered her head again, nodding with theatrical solemnity, as though confessing before the highest court of fate.

    “Yes,” Rio said hoarsely. “We talked. We… kind of… acted like human beings. Normal. Conversational. Almost adult. And maybe… just a little… kissed.”

    Regina looked at her with the expression of someone who had just been told dinosaurs had been resurrected and trained to dance the tango. Then she sat down on the edge of the tub, folding herself into place with deliberate slowness.

    “You mean to tell me,” she said at last, drawing the words out, “that the two most stubborn, emotionally unavailable, passive-aggressive women on this side of the continent… finally… had an actual conversation?” Rio nodded. Grim. Fatalistic.

    “And kissed,” she added, her voice as flat as a verdict.

    Regina stretched her legs out, clasping her fingers across her stomach as if bracing for impact.

    “Well,” she said, “I clearly need to re-evaluate my understanding of reality. Either this is a simulation, or we’ve entered a new era, or Agatha has hit her head hard enough to knock the universe off its axis.”

    “Don’t,” Rio groaned. “I’m already punished enough.”

    Regina tilted her head, watching her in silence for a moment. Her gaze was sharp, but not unkind; it had the weight of someone who had already drawn the conclusion and was simply waiting for Rio to admit it out loud. “So,” she said finally. “Wait. If you two actually managed to talk, and there was a kiss… then obviously you were supposed to see her today. Am I right?”

    Rio’s nod was bitter, the slow acknowledgment of someone who had just been stripped of her one winning lottery ticket.

    “But then came the damned roll,” she muttered. “And now I look like depression in human form. If Agatha texted, she thinks I’m ignoring her. And I can’t. I just—can’t answer her. I’m scared, Regina. Do you get that? I’m scared.”

    For a long time Regina said nothing. Her eyes lingered on Rio’s face, on the tangle of shame and hurt and something else—something that, in another woman, might have been called hope. But Rio would never allow herself that word.

    “Wait here,” Regina said softly, without explanation. She rose, the sound of her bare feet soft against the tiles, and slipped out of the bathroom. A faint trace of citrus and expensive shampoo hung in the air where she had been. From the bedroom came a muted clink—metal against wood, the telltale sound of a phone laid too roughly on a nightstand.

    Then footsteps again. And Regina returned, phone in hand. She didn’t offer it at once. She only looked down at the screen, sighed, and finally, standing in the doorway, said quietly: “She wrote.”

    Rio froze. It was as if someone had doused her in ice water. Her eyes widened, something sharp and bright flickering inside them—fear, yes, but something more dangerous too.

    “What?” The word was little more than breath.
