    “While you were talking to your reflection,” Regina said, her tone calm but edged, “and trying to decide where you ended and the part of you that devoured the cursed roll began—Agatha texted. She just asked how you were. No smiley faces. No dramatics. Just: How are you?”

    Rio straightened slowly, clutching the sink as though she might collapse without it. Heat rushed to her cheeks, sudden and merciless, like a fever rising in an instant. Her throat was dry, bone-dry, as if words had abandoned her.

    “And you… you read it?”

    “Of course I did. I’m your friend, not a nun.” Regina winked, giving the phone a little shake. “The notification was right there on the screen. What was I supposed to do, not open it?”

    Rio bit down on her lip.

    “I don’t know what to say to her. I don’t know how to explain this. ‘Suspicious roll’ sounds like the worst excuse ever. And I… I just… I ruined everything.”

    Regina stepped closer and set her hand on Rio’s shoulder, firm and steady —the kind of touch meant to keep someone upright when they were about to collapse again.

    “Then answer with the truth. Tell her you got food poisoning. Tell her you feel like death. Tell her you miss her. Tell her you wanted to do everything right and instead ended up in a Greek tragedy. She’s not stupid. She knows you. And she’s not going to punish you for being human—even if you’re not the most graceful one.”

    Rio glanced upward, exhaling something caught between a groan and a reluctant laugh.

    “You’re sure?”

    Regina nodded, smiling with that particular blend of warmth and sharpness that Rio knew so well.

    “Want me to reply for you?” she offered, one brow arched in mock seriousness. “I’ll text: ‘Hello, this is Rio. At the moment I am partially deceased, emotionally disoriented, and very much in need of kissing you. P.S. sorry for the culinary collapse.’”

    Rio let out a strangled sound—half laugh, half cry—and then the laugh broke free. Rough, hoarse, unsteady. She buried her forehead against Regina’s shoulder as if it were the only safe place left in a world that was splitting open again.

    “God, I love you,” she muttered into the knit fabric, voice muffled, raw.

    By two in the afternoon, Rio finally began to feel lighter. Not reborn, not radiant, not twirling around the bathroom in a cloud of perfume and triumph —no. But her body had at last stopped behaving like one great, sulking organ. Her insides, it seemed, had reached a fragile truce with the cursed roll, and the pounding in her temples had slowed to something almost human. The world still swayed a little, but it no longer threatened to collapse with every step.

    And she truly didn’t know what she had ever done in life to deserve Regina. She wasn’t a saint, not an angel, not a nun, not an activist saving penguins. Yet here she was, blessed with a friend who refused to abandon her. Without Regina, Rio probably would have thrown herself out the window— not with a scream, not in hysteria, but in her signature fashion: sarcastic, in heels, leaving behind a farewell note that read, “It wasn’t all bad, until it was.”

    Now she stood beneath the steady warmth of the shower. Not hot—hot was for catharsis, for burning away grief. No, this was warmth. Even, gentle, almost lazy. Like a hand smoothing down her back. The water slid over her neck, her shoulders, her spine, gathering into clear rivulets before dripping from her collarbones, soft against her skin. Rio closed her eyes, tipped back her head, and let the hushed wetness wash some of the wreckage from her mind.

    She had already brushed her teeth. Her hands had trembled like those of a widow at a graveside, but she had managed. She had washed her face, rinsed away the smeared cosmetics, the salt, the tears, the ghosts of the night before. On the shelf beside her now lay a neatly folded set of pajamas —soft, clean, smelling faintly of home. Regina’s doing, of course. Perhaps they carried the faint sweetness of vanilla lotion, or the familiar comfort of fabric softener from a life more grown-up than Rio’s ever really felt. Somewhere beyond the door, Regina had said that by the time Rio came out, the broth would be ready. She had already changed the bedding, checked the towels, refreshed the water jug. Calm, practical, unflinching. As if this was simply what one did. And it wasn’t the broth or the towels that mattered—it was that as if. That of course. That quiet, ordinary assurance that made Rio feel, for the first time in too long, as though she wasn’t splintering apart.

    She turned off the water. Droplets still clung to her hair, her chin, her lashes. She reached for the towel, wrapped it around her head, twisting it into a turban with the ease of someone who had already risen from the grave more than once. Her skin was warm, clean, a little damp—fresh, like a rewritten page.

    Underwear first—plain, soft, the kind that neither chafes nor asks permission from anyone except the stomach begging for comfort. They clung slightly, like a second skin, but not in a way that exposed—rather, in a way that shielded.

    Then the pajamas. Loose, pale. The trousers wide, with an elastic waistband that held firmly but kindly. The fabric brushed her ankles as though in apology for the day before. The top was light, almost weightless, with long sleeves, small buttons, and a neckline that slipped a little from her shoulder. It didn’t conceal everything, but it didn’t demand perfection either. It was the clothing of a survivor. The clothing of still here. The clothing of will be. She stood before the mirror. Slowly. Not to admire herself—no. To bear witness. To mark: yes, she was still here. And, God help her, she almost looked human again.

    The towel wound about her head gave her the air of an ancient priestess, scented faintly of shampoo and mint balm. Her cheeks had regained a touch of color. The bruised hollows under her eyes had softened, now more like shadows on velvet than the marks of inner violence. Her lips were dry but unbroken. And in her eyes at last flickered something that resembled life. She exhaled.

    And for the first time in twenty-four hours, allowed herself to relax. Just a little.

    Rio turned toward the door.

    The doorknob was still warm beneath her damp fingers. Rio lingered for a few seconds, listening to the drip of water falling from her towel onto the tiles, to the faint clicks of the boiler, to the soft rustle of pages being turned somewhere outside—Regina, most likely, with her saintly patience and perfect broth. Rio had already braced herself for the expected: she would step out, walk down the hall, catch the scent of parsley and chicken, toss off some sarcastic remark about being reborn, and they would laugh. Everything would be just as it always was.

    But the moment she pushed the door open, the world collapsed. Everything she knew, everything she had prepared herself for—gone. Because there, on the bed—her bed, not in a dream, not in some fevered hallucination, not in a drunken haze of perfume and bad decisions—lay Agatha.

    Agatha.

    Calm, composed, stretched across the top blanket with a book in her hands. Dressed in a black loungewear set so unassuming it was obscene in its beauty: a soft knit, loose sleeves, wide trousers trimmed in white. Nothing clung, nothing demanded attention—and for that very reason it was unbearable to look at. On the floor, by the side of the bed, a pair of white sneakers, neatly set aside. Socks folded. A pillow propped just so behind her. She looked less like someone who had come to upend Rio’s universe and more like a guest dropping in for the weekend.

    And her hair—God, her hair. Loose, long, spilling across her shoulders and the blanket in dark strands like ink seeping into pale parchment. Alive. Too alive.

    And then the glasses.

    Reading glasses, square, transparent frames sliding slightly down the bridge of her nose. Behind them, her eyes—calm, softer than Rio remembered, as though they had discovered something gentler, something unguarded. As though this Agatha, the one who read books on someone else’s bed, was another woman altogether. The real one.

    Rio froze in the doorway—damp, wrapped in pajamas, towel askew, her heart pounding low in her belly like a faulty lightbulb on the verge of burning out. She couldn’t move. The entire world contracted to a single image: the bed, the book, the glasses, the hair, Agatha. Like a painting of a memory. Like a dream in which every gesture costs something, every word carries consequence.

    Agatha lifted her eyes from the page—not theatrically, not with any practiced flourish. Simply, as though she had felt it. And she looked at Rio through the glass. Directly. Without judgment. Without sarcasm. Without armor.

    “Hi,” she said. Quiet. Plain. Almost domestic.

    And that single hi struck Rio like a gunshot. Like a slap. Like the shock of too-cold air after too-hot water. And Rio did the only thing Rio could do. She slammed the door.

    Sharp. Desperate. With the expression of someone who had just seen her own soul stretched out on her bed in a black knit set, reading a novel about morality and desire. She stood there, forehead pressed against the wood. The towel slipped sideways. Her heart thundered so violently it might have been heard across the block. Her hands trembled. Her legs refused to move. She shut her eyes.

    “I’m dead,” she whispered. “Or in hell.”

    She drew a deep breath. Then another. Useless.

    “Goddamn Agatha,” she exhaled, and the words came out far too tender to be a curse.

    On the other side of the door, laughter.

    Low, rich, unhurried—more dusk than daylight. It rolled like a lazy tide, as though Agatha herself couldn’t quite believe any of this was real, and yet savored every frame of it, like an old favorite film watched for the hundredth time but never abandoned before the credits. The laughter seeped through the wood, settled into the fibers of the towel, and slid down Rio’s spine.

    Rio pressed her palms harder against the door, as though she could physically hold herself back from dissolving into the room beyond. Her lungs scraped for air, every breath coming shallow, hysterical. On the other side, Agatha’s voice was maddeningly composed, carrying that infuriating blend of irony and quiet warmth.

    “Of course,” she said. “Of course you’d slam the door. What else would a self-respecting woman in pajamas and a towel crown do when she finds her ex-lover sprawled across her bed?”

    The words curled under Rio’s skin like smoke. She squeezed her eyes shut, clenched her teeth so hard her jaw ached, as if she could clamp down on the panic bubbling inside her—rising, frothing, threatening to spill over like a pot left too long on the stove. The towel sagged down her temple, sliding crooked, like a toppled diadem of shame.

    “Stop it,” she rasped through the narrow seam of wood. Even to her own ears it sounded absurd, pitiful, almost comic.

    There was a silence. A long pause. Then Agatha again: “Do you want me to look away? Or… stand in the corner until you decide in what state you wish to appear?”

    A strangled whimper burst out of Rio—not real crying, but the exaggerated, desperate kind of sound that a child might make when denied the last slice of pizza. Her voice pitched higher, brittle, trembling: “I am never coming out of here!”

    The words scattered across the bathroom like soap bubbles: fragile, luminous, doomed to burst.

    Her knees gave way. Slowly, deliberately, she slid down the wall until she was seated on the tiles, her back pressed to the cool wood as though it alone could keep her from collapsing. The towel slipped entirely, drooping onto her shoulders, the damp edges brushing against her collarbones. Strands of hair clung to her damp forehead. Beneath the pajamas lingered the faint humidity of steam—warm, intimate, embarrassingly human.

    “I’ll stay here,” she muttered under her breath. “Go underground. Chronicle my disgrace. Launch an Instagram: The Girl Who Never Left the Bathroom. Content: tears, towels, and broth slid under the door.”

    This time, a soft rustle reached her—the faintest shift of fabric, perhaps the deliberate turn of a page.

    “Convincing,” Agatha murmured. “I can already see the documentary. Woman and Tile: A Study in Refusal to Live.”

    “It’s not funny!” Rio burst out, loud and plaintive. “I didn’t even have a chance. Not morally, not logistically! Look at me—or rather, don’t. I look like… like a moldy dumpling! Pajamas, hair, towel, and you, on my bed, in glasses, like some professor from a glossy poster. This is violence!” Agatha’s chuckle slid easily through the wood, warm and unbearable. “Then let it be gentle violence,” she replied. “Wool-soft, polite. With the option: stay inside until you want to come out.”

    Rio groaned again and let her face fall into her hands. She didn’t know whether to laugh or cry. Everything in her felt raw, unfinished, but alive — in her body, in her chest, in the maddening desire to disappear and, at the same time, to stay.

    “I don’t know how to talk to you,” she exhaled. “Even when I feel like hell, you look like a damn supermodel. I can’t. I’m not ready.” “But I am,” came the reply. Simple. Without force.

    And then, silence.

    Rio pressed her temple against the cool tile, hugging her knees tight. Somewhere inside her chest, warmth had crept in unnoticed, like someone had cracked open a hidden window in her ribcage. Maybe it was because of Agatha. Maybe it was simply because she was still here.

    And, damn it — she had laughed. Laughed, and Rio had laughed too. Which meant… maybe things weren’t so bad after all.

    Behind the door, quiet again. That fragile quiet, like water trembling in a glass left on a sunlit windowsill — quivering from the light, but not spilling. Rio sat with her chin on her knees, her arms locked around herself, staring at the floor as if sheer willpower could burn a hole in it. The air was damp, smelling of mint toothpaste, steam, and… and Agatha, curse her. Or not curse her — since she had come.

    Then came a faint rustle. Slow, deliberate. As if someone shifted something along the floor. And then her voice — the kind where irony always carried a shadow of something else.

    “There’s broth here. It’s cooling, by the way. Broth gets offended if ignored. It has a sense of dignity.”

    Rio made a face as though someone had suggested she chew the door itself. “I cannot drink broth while you’re sitting on my bed, in glasses, pretending this is a normal Saturday. You’re like a scene from an arthouse film, and I’m the badly subtitled trailer in Kazakh. None of this makes sense!” Outside, a short laugh. Light, almost tender.

    “Then at the very least you’re a poetic trailer. And apparently rated R. Are you still naked?”

    Rio rolled her eyes.

    “Pajamas! I’m in pajamas! But I look like I fell off a train last night and spent all night scavenging with pigeons. I can’t come out. This—this is my fortress. It’s safe here. Just me, the tiles, and what’s left of my dignity.” “What’s left?” Agatha echoed, her voice still low, unhurried. “Do you keep it in a box? In the cupboard? Under the rug?”

    “It… melted,” Rio muttered, “somewhere between the martinis and that cursed roll.”

    She shivered. Her stomach twisted again, dull and insistent, reminding her that if broth did exist, this might be the time for it to prove its worth. “It smells, by the way,” Agatha added. “The broth. I stood with it in the kitchen while Regina ladled it out. There’s garlic in it. And celery. Very treacherous.”

    Rio groaned.

    “Stop it. This is psychological warfare. You’re using soup to lure me out. That’s food blackmail!”

    “It’s called care,” Agatha answered softly.

    Rio sat a little longer, curling her fingers under herself as if she could somehow fold deeper into her own body. Then, quieter: “I just don’t know how to act. You’re here. And I… I didn’t sleep, I poisoned myself, and right now I’ve only got two personalities: ‘post-food- poisoning’ and ‘pajama breakdown.’”

    The reply didn’t come immediately. Agatha, as always, let the silence stretch. Then, in a low voice: “Then don’t be yourself. Just be… a person. Tired, hungry, in need of broth. I’m not looking. I swear. Eyes on the page, conscience in the cupboard.” Rio blinked. Once. Twice. Again. As though she were trying to squeeze tears out of herself that weren’t there. Instead, only a slow, viscous breath left her chest.

    “You really… won’t look?”

    “I won’t,” Agatha said firmly. “Not until you say I can.” Rio sighed. Once more. And again. Then, with the theatricality of early Chekhov, she shifted onto her knees, puffed out her cheeks like a soldier marching to certain doom, and without rising fully, stretched out her hand to the doorknob.

    Click.

    The door opened. First a crack. Then a little wider. Until finally Rio appeared in that narrow gap, still seated on the floor — damp towel sliding from her shoulders, hair clinging to her neck, pajamas wrinkled but intact, her face the portrait of a woman who had just crawled through a hurricane — moral, digestive, and emotional.

    She squinted. The room was brighter than she had braced herself for. Agatha, as promised, wasn’t looking. She sat on the bed, one leg tucked beneath her, the book still in her hands, hair a dark spill across her shoulders. Glasses perched on her nose. Her posture utterly at ease, as though she had been living here all along, merely waiting for the bathroom’s occupant to reemerge and join her for tea.

    Rio dropped her gaze. Right beside the threshold sat a tray with a bowl on it. No steam rose now, but the smell still lingered. Garlic. Celery. A touch of pepper. And chicken — not the kind found in cursed rolls. Her throat tightened. She slid into place on the floor directly opposite the doorframe, arranging herself like some ancient monk with post-toxic enlightenment. Wrapped the towel around herself, folded her legs into lotus as she once had in some long-shuttered yoga studio, and ceremoniously accepted the bowl of broth. It was still warm — not hot, but warm enough to breathe faint curls of steam, like a child’s memory clinging to the air. “All right,” she muttered. “I’m eating. And if you so much as glance — a single eye, a single pupil, a single hair follicle strays this way — I swear to God, you’ll be wearing this broth on your glasses. Don’t ask me how.” Agatha didn’t move.

    The book still rested in her hands. The glasses still balanced on her nose. She looked so composed, so unfazed, that for a fleeting instant Rio wondered if she had actually fallen asleep with her eyes open. Or if this was some witch’s trick — a disguise of calm intellectualism at the moment of ambush.

    Rio lifted the spoon carefully. The broth was good. No mockery in its taste. No overbearing sympathy cooked into it. Just broth — salted properly, its golden surface clear and shimmering, with tiny oil circles floating like satellites in orbit. With each spoonful, her stomach unwound a little more, and the inner voice screaming you blew it began to lose volume. She took another spoonful. Then another. Suddenly half was gone. Setting the bowl down, she muttered with solemn resignation: “That’s it. I’ve done all I could. You may bury me again.” Something shifted on the bed. A curve at the corner of her mouth, the hint of a smile, and then that voice — steady, with its familiar lazy sarcasm: “Planning to sleep on the floor too? Or shall I toss you a pillow and a blanket?”

    Without looking up, Rio extended her hand forward like a player in some absurd quest.

    “Throw it. And if you hit my face — consider it vengeance for the ruined first date.”

    But nothing landed. Rio frowned and lifted her gaze. Agatha was still seated with the book, still holding it open, still “reading.” “You haven’t turned a page,” Rio said, suspiciously slow. “How long have you been stuck on the same one? Five minutes?”

    “I’m absorbed in analysis,” Agatha replied without blinking. “It’s a philosophical novel. One must chew a paragraph and then wash it down with broth.”

    “Liar,” Rio exhaled. “You’re peeking. I can feel it.”

    “My glasses have fogged from the strain,” Agatha countered with serene detachment. “All I see is your outline in a parallel reality. Possibly in pajamas. Possibly at a yoga retreat.”

    Rio rolled her eyes toward the ceiling, staring as though salvation might be written there. Then, slowly, as if each cell of her body had to agree in turn, she rose unsteadily to her feet. The frailty was still there, but not the death in every muscle. She crossed to the bed, flung a leg over without looking, and burrowed beneath the blanket as though diving into a lake — cold at the surface, terrifying in its depths. She wrapped herself tightly, turned her back, leaving only the damp knot of hair and towel sticking out like a flag of surrender.

    “That’s it. I’m a cocoon. Don’t touch me. Don’t speak to me. Don’t breathe in my direction.”

    “How long can you last like that?” Agatha asked evenly. Rio heard the sound of the book being placed with care upon the nightstand. “Forever,” Rio muttered, dragging the blanket higher. “Until the end of time. Until humanity ceases making spicy rolls. Or until you leave.” Silence. Thin, delicate, laced with something almost amused. Then the faintest rustle. Fabric shifting, subtle, betraying a presence crossing the distance between two bodies once separated by walls of unsaid words.

    And then — weight. Careful, warm, deliberate. Agatha lay beside her. Not pressed against her at first. But closer. Closer still.     “Strategically,” Agatha corrected. “I’m a pillow. And an embrace. All in one. Very efficient. Just don’t turn around. There’s a trap waiting.” A laugh broke from Rio — first in her shoulders, then her throat, then deeper, into her heart.

    “Stupid blanket,” Agatha sighed, as though surrendering.

    The silence that followed was not heavy. It wasn’t the silence of accusation, nor of things unsaid. It was a pause that allowed them simply to breathe— steadily, together.

    Rio felt something loosening inside her. Not all at once, not in a dramatic rush, but gradually, layer by layer. As though someone was slowly uncoiling the spring wound tight within her chest. For real.

    “Because it made co-parenting Nicholas easier. On paper.” Agatha flicked her a sidelong glance. “And because I wanted to tell you when you didn’t look like a raccoon that’s just lost a fight.”

    “Oh, I see.” Rio gave a half-snort, half-laugh, and dropped back against her. “So I go through rehabilitation, and then you’ll send me the invoice for emotional damages?”

    “Only if you go near another roll. Then there will be no mercy.” Rio chuckled, pressing her face into Agatha’s stomach, breathing her in. “So that was a confession?”

    “That was a warning,” Agatha replied, calmly turning another page. For a time, they let the quiet settle again. Agatha kept reading, her fingers still resting lightly over Rio’s. Rio listened.     Agatha nodded without looking up.

    “I’m not planning to. You’re holding too tight.”

    “I’m an anchor,” Rio murmured, drowsy, almost purring. “A very sexy, exhausted anchor.”

    “With mild gastritis,” Agatha corrected, eyes sliding across the lines. “And excellent taste in women,” Rio added, already half-asleep, sinking into warmth, into breath, into scent. Into a comfort she had never dared hope she’d find again.

    The next awakening came in silence— but not the kind that presses, suffocates, alarms.

    This was the silence of an adult evening: steady, weathered, weary. In it there was no trace of breath beside her, no rustle of a turning page, no accidental shift of fabric—only the distant hum of the city, muffled, as though heard through the thickness of a pillow.

    Rio opened her eyes slowly. For several seconds she remained motionless, as if her body still debated whether to return to reality or keep wandering in the blurred, forgiving haze of dreams.

    Agatha was gone.

    The pillow still held her warmth—faint, almost intimate—but the air had already changed its texture. It had grown thinner. Freer. Solitary. The emptiness was not sharp.     But Rio did not panic. Something solid, deep within, whispered: this was not disappearance. This was a pause. A breath taken between movements. The natural interlude of someone who could not lie there forever, who had simply risen.

    The bathroom greeted her with mirrored emptiness and the faint scent of a damp towel. Her hair, dried without the help of patience or towels, was an unruly carnival. She glanced at her reflection, lifted one brow as if to say: Yes, darling. This needs addressing. She twisted the chaos into a rough knot, secured it with an elastic found by the sink, and turned on the shower. Hot water thundered down—reliable, promising. When the first drops struck her skin, Rio closed her eyes and simply stood there. Letting. Herself. The water. The present.

    It was not a shower for cleanliness. It was a shower for the body—for returning to it. She washed slowly, without automation, almost tenderly. She paid attention to collarbones, to knees, to the curve of her spine. The soap smelled of calm—sage, perhaps, or eucalyptus. Or perhaps simply safety. It slid over her skin, rinsing not just dust and sweat, but also the remnants of yesterday: the anxieties that linger like marker-stains on the flesh—trivial, yet irritating.

Genuinely. Slightly sideways, slightly crooked. But to herself. Familiar. Steady.

    Leaving the bathroom, she pulled nothing on. She simply walked—bare, alive, startlingly present. Her skin met the air, and in it there was no shame. Only truth.

    The room smelled of clean linen, of wood, and of a faint powdery trace— like someone had stripped off something expensive in haste and vanished.     Her sneakers waited. Scuffed, worn, loyal. The kind with frayed heels, but exactly the ones in which she wanted to step into the kitchen, into the night, into a new life—or anywhere at all.

    She descended the stairs slowly, but with certainty. Each tread wasn’t simply a step—it was a piece of return. She felt it in her heels, her calves, her breath. As though after a long, murky swim, she had finally broken onto shore.

    And she knew—someone was waiting downstairs. Or at least the kettle. And for once, that was enough.

    The kitchen smelled of life.

    The kind that moves slowly—with the hush of a vent, the clatter of knives against a cutting board, the muted shuffle of a playlist that never makes sense but always feels like home. Rio leaned her shoulder into the doorway, as though the threshold required permission, and watched. Regina stood at the stove, wrapped in a striped apron over something black and elegant—perhaps loungewear, perhaps a secret strategy for being a goddess even in a kitchen. Her hair was pulled into a loose ponytail, one strand trailing against her cheek. In one hand a spatula, in the other a skillet. Something hissed. Something simmered. Something promised delicious redemption after yesterday’s cursed roll.

    Rio inhaled. Her stomach growled at once.

    Regina turned at the sound.

    “Aha,” she said, not the least surprised. “The zombie lives.” “Not a zombie. A phoenix,” Rio muttered, dropping onto a stool by the island.

    Regina smirked, but her gaze swept over Rio—quick, precise. As if ticking boxes: the pallor gone, eyes clear, hair—well, hair as always, but that was her style. Pulse steady. Humor intact. Conclusion: alive. “How do you feel?” she asked, turning back to the stove. “Still think you’re dead?”

    “No. Now I think I just took a vacation from humanity.” Rio yawned, stretched, her back cracking. “And now I’m back. Hungry. Mildly anxious. But refreshed.”

    Garlic sizzled on the pan. Then cubes of pumpkin. Then sprigs of rosemary. “Oh, God,” Rio groaned, shutting her eyes. “I officially want to marry that smell. Someone get us paperwork.”

    “Get in line,” Regina shot back, deftly stirring. “That smell’s been my lover for years.”

    “You’re cruel,” Rio sighed. “I nearly died. I deserve at least one orgasm through my nose.”

    Regina smirked, but didn’t answer right away. She straightened over the stove, lifting the skillet like a conductor raising an instrument, tilting her ear toward the hiss as if it could tell her more than Rio ever could. “Wait,” she said after a pause. “Give me one more minute. Everything should finish together.”

    Rio nodded.

    The kitchen swelled with aromas until it brimmed. She closed her eyes, inhaled, and let herself simply sit. Her hands wrapped around a warm cup like an anchor. Her heart beat steady. Nothing burned. Nothing twisted. When Regina finally spoke again, her voice was lower, softer—as though she was choosing words that would leave no scars.

    “Agatha asked me to tell you,” Regina said quietly, her back still turned, “that it was already late. And she couldn’t stay.”

    The burner clicked off. A soft scrape as the pan was drawn aside. “She left. An hour ago.”

    Rio lowered her eyes to her palms. She didn’t answer at once. Only drew a slow breath, the way one does when bracing for a blow they already knew was coming—yet still could not keep from flinching when it landed. And then, at last—just one word. Quiet. Without strain. Without the mask of composure. Spoken simply, the way truths are when they matter. “Thank you.”

    Chapter 26: Monday

    Chapter Notes Hi! You can kill me — I’ll understand. But it’s already September 23, and I’m planning to finish translating this fanfic by the end of the month.

    Are you still here? 😅 Monday began with rain. Not a wistful drizzle, not the kind of gentle shower you’d stroll through in a park with an umbrella and a latte in hand, but a full-bodied, unrelenting downpour—as if the sky had been switched on to maximum, with all the generosity of a disappointed lover. Outside the window ran an entire symphony of water: over branches, across ledges, down panes of glass, dripping from railings in heavy drops that turned the world into a glistening, restrained gray postcard. And it might have been fine—if it weren’t so bright inside.

    Rio was coming down the stairs. Not walking, not stomping, not rushing— coming down, like in a music video: slow, graceful, with a sense of her own style and that faint, intimate ease glimmering in her eyes. Maybe it was the clothes. Maybe it was Saturday. Maybe it was the pulse of something new running quietly through her body. Not fresh—freshness would have been too sharp—but new.     She wore the kind of outfit that, in her opinion, deserved at least its own scene in a romantic drama: an oversized brown sweater with a deep neckline, the deliberate flash of a white collar beneath it—defiance tucked inside comfort. A black mini skirt, pleated soft, short enough to stop a heart but not good taste. Sheer smoky tights, like a sentence left unfinished. Black high-heeled boots. In her hand, a bag. Her hair was tied into a casual ponytail, with a few strands left loose on purpose, to tickle her cheekbones, to fall across her lips when she wanted to bite them. Or when Agatha would. It hardly mattered. What mattered was leaving the possibility. Every movement—soft, unhurried, with lazy chic. Like a cat who knows she is queen today—no decree, no crown, only by right of the light inside her. And Rio felt that light. Or forty, with the heart of a freshman who didn’t fear tomorrow would change anything. Because she herself had already been changed.

The bass struck the floor like heels on marble, while the singer’s voice—husky, with a drop of whiskey and the echo of night— spilled through the air, clinging to the walls, slipping under the skin. It teased the nerve endings low in the stomach, like a caress with no continuation.

Morning smelled of coffee, toasted bread, and… home. Not the home from an advertisement, not the home dredged up from memory. Rio barely noticed the figure to the side at first. But when she lifted her eyes, her heart gave a playful kick.

    Lilia. With a paper bag in her arms, staring as though she’d just witnessed the sun kissing the moon. Frozen by the hood of a stranger’s car, as if she’d forgotten what errand had brought her there.

    Rio didn’t stop. Didn’t look away. She walked past with measured calm, fully aware of how they must look side by side. Aware of the way the morning smelled when what you wore was someone’s smile instead of just a jacket.

    She even thought: I could… right now… slip an arm around her waist, pull her closer. Do it like in Twilight. The laugh stayed inside, but it was bright and sharp. That would be too much. Even for her. For now. Agatha said nothing. But the corner of her mouth told Rio enough: she’d understood.

    They crossed to the building together. Silent. Unhurried. With no need to make it otherwise.

    And then, in the hall, halfway to the stairs, Agatha glanced back over her shoulder — casual, effortless, as though she hadn’t seen faces on the lot, hadn’t felt Lilia’s stare, hadn’t breathed in that sharp air of attention — and said softly, almost lazily: “Thank you for the ride.”

    A pause.

    “I’m already halfway to that burger in my head. So don’t be late.” Without waiting for an answer, she headed toward her office — steady, fluid, carrying herself with that impossible poise. Leaving behind the faintest trail of perfume and, just beneath it, the suggestion of a hidden laugh.

    Rio watched her go.

    And knew: it was right. Better than right. It was exactly as it should be.

    Chapter 29: Wednesday 2|2

    Rio sat at the very back of the lecture hall, by the window. The light fell at an angle—warm, carving rectangles across the floor like a stage set. Bent over her desk, she looked perfectly focused. Her posture was impeccable: straight back, one elbow on the table, chin resting lightly against folded fingers. Her gaze stayed fixed on the presenting student, not a single degree off. Everything about her composure was deliberate. Academic. Convincing.

    And all of it—an act.

    A graceful, rehearsed performance: the measured movement of her left hand, the calm stillness of her face, the occasional note in her pad— everything built the illusion that she truly cared about what was happening on the stage today. As if the words pouring from the podium actually mattered.

    In truth… she’d have found it easier to listen to the faint wind against the glass.

    Or to snap her fingers and be back on the porch of her house. Where the morning air still smelled of affection—and of Agatha’s hair. Agatha.

    That morning.

    Those twenty seconds between the door and the kiss.

    Rio narrowed her eyes slightly, lowering her gaze. Not because the student had made a mistake—no. Because something stirred again inside her. Not a flare, not a fire. Something quieter. Warmer. Like a deep inner chime, the kind you hear when you realize you’ve let someone in—for real, and there’s no going back.

    There were no butterflies in her chest. It wasn’t that kind of thrill. What lived under her ribs was slower, deliberate, tender. Her fingers still seemed to remember the shape of Agatha’s jacket, her lips the breath between them. They hadn’t just said goodbye that morning. They’d outlined the day.

    Thirty-seven minutes left until the end of class.

    Then thirty-six.

    And then—strangely, as if time itself were teasing her—it began to stretch, thin and resonant, like a drawn violin string.

    The student went on speaking.

    A thin young man in a wrinkled dark sweater, trying to hold his back straight, though his shoulders kept sinking. He spoke with effort, with commas, as if every sentence carried weight. Maybe it did—for him. Maybe he really believed that defending his project was his first battle for the world’s attention.

    Rio heard him. But she didn’t listen.

    Each of his words touched something in her that had nothing to do with the topic—

    the shade of Agatha’s lipstick, the faint tremor in her hand as she tightened the strap of her bag, the tone in which she had said “Me too.”

    Her phone lay beside her, face down—as if pretending not to exist. But Rio still felt it there. Not with her fingers—with her skin. It was her anchor. Her point of reference.

    Two girls in the back row whispered over a desk; one boy stared at his laptop, unblinking. Someone laughed under their breath. The air in the room was warm and faintly dusty, carrying the last of the afternoon sun— almost September air. The day was winding down. But inside Rio, everything was just beginning.

    She nodded at the right moment when the student gave an example. Raised her brow—slightly, an inflection, no more. Everything was in order. Everything—under control.

    Except one thing.

    With every passing hour, she understood it more clearly: this was no longer intrigue. No chemistry. No game.

    This was attachment.

    Steady, alive, almost unstoppable.

    Complex, mature, touched with that quiet warmth that doesn’t fade, even after coffee.

    Since that morning in Agatha’s house, everything had changed. Not in context—in feeling. In her.

    And now she wasn’t simply waiting for evening.

    She wanted to be near her.

    Truly near.

    God knows, she wasn’t the type to throw herself at the word date like a schoolgirl. She had built an immunity to romance—refined over years, cool, clinical, almost cynical. And yet… now that word sounded different. Softer. Truer. Almost tender.

    A date.

    Not a night on some drunken kitchen floor.

    Not a desperate midweek call.

    Not a breath over the phone.

    Simply: “See you tonight.”

    In private. In a car. With a blanket that might accidentally slip, and hands she could hold—not under a table, not in secret, but openly. In the clear light of day. Without pretending it meant nothing.

    She bit her lip, barely noticeable. Not to look seductive—just to keep from smiling.

    Something inside her rose slowly, precisely. Warmth. Lightness. A quiet, improbable ease that felt unnatural for this hour.

    Because for the first time in a long while, she didn’t want to run. Didn’t want to hide behind sarcasm, or fatigue, or I have too much work. She wanted—to stay.

    With Agatha.

    There was that deliberate kind of carelessness she had, and that voice that could draw everything inside her tight, winding it into a single, unyielding knot. The way she carried herself, the tone she used—it all came together into something that lived beneath Rio’s ribs, quiet but persistent. There was that feeling again, low in her stomach—unhurried, almost comfortable. Not sharp, not burning, but warm, like the soft light that waits inside a house you come home to.

    Twenty-eight minutes.

    She glanced out the window. Beyond the glass, the world was perfectly ordinary: the campus, a little weary by evening; a student rushing past with a cup of coffee; another pressed against a girl in a kiss, her coat sliding from her shoulders, unnoticed. Someone sat on a bench with headphones in, tearing a sandwich in half as if dividing their reality into two pieces. And Rio—she felt suspended somewhere outside of it all, between these people and whatever was about to happen. She didn’t belong to the moment, but she belonged to the waiting.

    It took only a single glance from Agatha. That was enough to make the day lose its usual weight, to thin and turn transparent, like fabric that light passes through. She remembered how Agatha had said that morning, “A pity this path is so short.” There had been no flirtation in it, no pause or hesitation—only a simple, honest tenderness, unashamed in its directness. From that moment something had settled in Rio’s chest, steady and quiet, as if someone had spoken to her in her own language, and no translation was needed.

    She ran her fingers absently over her knee, her gaze drifting across the lecture hall as she counted down the minutes. The voices around her were no more than a hum, blurred and distant. She wasn’t writing anymore—not notes, not citations, not fragments of speech. The pen lay still beside her. Her breathing deepened, slow and uneven: inhale through her nose, exhale, uncertain. She looked at her phone, at the time, then at her hands. Everything was in its place, and yet she felt slightly apart from herself. Her pulse beat in her neck and her palms. Her thoughts tangled like fine threads—not anxious, no, simply alive. And for the first time—clear, sharp, as if heard through the rush of water—came the realization: I miss her. Not her body. Not her voice. But the way being beside her meant not having to explain, not having to defend, simply to exist.

    It was something new. Frighteningly calm. Rio had always known how to be in control—of events, of people, of herself. But now she was simply living inside the waiting, without a plan, without a formula. Nineteen minutes left. And within those nineteen minutes there was no torment— only hope. Slow, adult, warm hope, like a spring evening when you no longer need a scarf, but still want to keep your hand tucked inside another’s pocket. Not because it’s cold. But because you can.

    The class was over.

    Not abruptly, but in that way it ends when you’ve been underwater for too long and finally surface—sound returns slowly, muffled at first, then bright, ringing. The student’s final words drifted through her like a draft of air, leaving no trace. Someone snapped their laptop shut, someone scraped a chair, another stretched or laughed. And Rio… stayed seated. It was as if her body needed a little more time to return from whatever other dimension it had been in—back to the place where you can once again hear the rustle of jackets, the creak of the floor, the soft clap of doors closing. Then she stood, not noisily but quickly, almost sharply, as if the chair beneath her had turned hot. Her laptop tucked under her arm, notebook in hand, shoulders lifted just slightly, her breath deeper than a simple end of lecture required.

    She had only one route.

    To the parking lot.

    To the car.

    To Agatha.

    The university corridors—long, echoing—were drowning in a soft light, muted like the hush before a curtain rises. People passed by: a nod here, a greeting there. But none of it was really meant for her. It all felt distant, as if she were watching the world through glass. Or water.

    Her fingers brushed the lapel of her jacket. A strand of hair slipped loose behind her ear; she caught it in her fist and smoothed it back without glancing at a mirror. It didn’t matter now. She wasn’t what mattered. What mattered was what she was walking toward—and who was waiting for her there.

    Her heart no longer beat in her throat but deeper, lower, muffled, as though it were remembering a rhythm it had once known and was learning again. It wasn’t anxiety. More a kind of focus, as if a metronome had started ticking quietly inside her.

    When she stepped outside, everything seemed just a little different. The sun was leaning toward the horizon. The light no longer fell from above but from the side—across her cheekbones, her eyes, her collarbones. The air smelled of dust, of warm asphalt, and something in bloom—sharp and misplaced, like a lilac bush planted where it shouldn’t be. A breeze touched her face, cool and fine, and she didn’t flinch. She simply walked faster.

    As she descended the steps, she became aware of the new feeling inside her. The morning’s flutter had changed—not vanished, not evaporated, but settled. Quieter now. Denser. As if it had spread through her body, not in her thoughts or chest, but lower, in that place where real, grown things live. It wasn’t about being in love—not the dizzy, foolish kind. It was about warmth, the kind that makes you want to take off your scarf. About silence, the kind you don’t want to break. About how Agatha’s voice on the phone stayed with her all day—not because it was passionate, but simply because it was there.

    This wasn’t desire. Nor flirtation. Nor habit.

    It was… something else.

    Something Rio feared—

    and wanted all the same.

    Passion—yes, she knew its taste. The flashes, the nights, the breathless unraveling. But this time, she wanted something different. A foundation. Not a fire that scorches, but a heat that lingers even after the night cools. She wanted what makes a we—not words, but rituals. The brush of a hand against a back. Warm milk on the stove. A quiet I’m here while the world roared outside.

    And yet—vulnerability.

    What if Agatha didn’t appreciate it?

    The film Rio had chosen so carefully wasn’t fashionable or cult—just warm. The blanket was too creased, as if someone had already slept under it. Not the first time together. The popcorn—sweet, by mistake, though Agatha might have preferred salted. The napkins—from a cheap vending machine that dispensed them one pack at a time, with a groan and a rattle, as if embarrassed by itself.

    What if all of it seemed unnatural? Unstylish. Too deliberate. Too human. Rio smiled to herself—briefly, without bitterness. It wasn’t the risks that frightened her now, nor the uncertainty, nor even the thought that something might go wrong. What unsettled her were the details, the little things one worries about only when the goal isn’t perfection, but simply to make the other person feel good.

    She slowed her pace as she stepped into the shaded strip between two buildings and let out a slow breath, as though releasing something she no longer needed to carry. Don’t dramatize it, she told herself. You just want her to feel at ease. You want her to want to stay. You want the evening not to become a moment—but a memory worth repeating.

    The parking lot was nearly empty. It was that quiet hour between day and evening, when everything softens and settles. The air had grown gentler; the warm dust smelled of asphalt and something flowering nearby—jasmine, perhaps, sharp and unexpected, like a bush planted in the wrong place. And in that stillness, as though the scene had been arranged just for her, she saw her.

    Agatha.

    Leaning against the back door of the car. Calm. Composed. As though the sunset itself had rooted her in its light. The bag slung over her shoulder. The jacket taken off and neatly folded—as everything about her seemed to be, even when she wasn’t trying. Her shirt slightly creased, collar straightened but showing a faint line of wear that somehow hurt to look at: the day had been long, and yet she had come. One hand in her pocket, her head tilted as if she’d been listening to music, or to the wind. Her hair still pinned up, but a few loose strands had escaped, brushing her cheek, as though even they had decided it was time to soften.

    And she was looking. Not posing, not performing, not waiting. Just standing there. And Rio caught it instantly—that pulse in her chest that spoke louder than words: You came. I knew you would.

    Rio walked toward her—or rather, she approached. Because in moments like this, walking wasn’t movement, it was feeling. Warm, inward, as if she were stepping not on pavement but on something inside herself. Every step felt like saying yes.

    When only a meter remained between them, she stopped. Not out of fear, but because perhaps, in that second, everything had already happened. “Hi,” she breathed, softly, with that barely-there warmth that lives between words when the heart has spoken long before the mouth can. “Hi,” Agatha answered. Her voice was lower than in the morning, with a roughness deep inside it—not from fatigue, but from a day lived too fully, full of restrained emotion. It was a simple word, but in that simplicity there was something profoundly intimate, as if with it she confirmed: I’m here. Entirely.

    They were silent for a while—not from awkwardness, but because silence was truer than speech. Rio lowered her gaze, then lifted it again, gently, a little shyly. Agatha’s eyelids flickered; it seemed something inside her, too, tightened with the faint chill of anticipation.

    Rio stepped closer and reached out, taking the jacket without asking—just as if it were the most natural gesture in the world. Agatha let it go, equally wordless, easily. Their fingers brushed for a second, and in that accidental touch there was more meaning than in any rehearsed caress. They both felt it.

    “Ready?” Rio asked, her fingers tightening slightly around the fabric. Her voice wasn’t steady, but it wasn’t weak either—just honest. Agatha nodded, reserved but with that quiet expression that said she had just agreed to something larger than the evening. Then she took a small step forward—not for a kiss, not for show, but simply to be closer. To leave no unnecessary inch of air between them.

    “To tell you the truth…” she murmured, her chin brushing Rio’s shoulder, “I’ve been thinking all day about where you’re taking me. And you know what? I liked not knowing.”

    The words were soft, warm, a little tired—but real. Unprotected. Rio nodded, feeling her throat go dry. Somewhere deep in her stomach, something drew tight and released again. The light—the one she carried within her now, deep and tender—flared quietly to life.

    “Then let’s go,” she said.

    They got into the car almost at the same time. The doors closed, and the noise outside fell away—as if they’d stepped into another space entirely. Into the quiet. Into a zone where everything existed only between them. Rio turned slightly—not insistently, not to study, just enough to see her. Agatha had settled into the passenger seat in that particular way only she could: as if her posture were the result of a long, patient rehearsal, when in truth it was the purest kind of ease. One leg crossed over the other, her hand resting lightly on her thigh, fingertips grazing the fabric. Her gaze was fixed ahead, toward the glass—not unfocused, but intent, as though she wasn’t observing the world so much as feeling it.

    Rio held her breath for a moment before daring to ask, “Everything okay?” Agatha turned to her slowly, with the unhurried grace of someone who never needed to hurry. And there it was—that signature half-smile, the one that stole the air right out of Rio’s lungs. A little predatory, but never threatening. The kind that could make you forget your name. “That depends,” she said languidly, “on whether you’re planning to kidnap me… or simply seduce me with good taste and a well-thought-out evening.”

    Rio’s lips curved. “What if I chose both?”

    Agatha was quiet for a beat, tilting her head slightly to the side. Her expression didn’t shift—no surprise, no irony—only a slow, satisfied calm, like a woman who knows she’s been chosen deliberately.

    “Then perhaps,” she murmured, her voice almost a purr, “I should unfasten my seat belt in advance.”

    Rio laughed—softly, low in her chest, the kind of laugh that carried warmth and pressure all at once, like holding something fragile and alive in her hands. She turned the key in the ignition, and the engine answered with a deep, resonant hum. Almost a sigh. As if the car, too, understood that from this moment on, the evening was finally beginning.

    The wheels rolled forward—smooth, effortless, as though even the asphalt had given way to their mood. The city outside dissolved into gold dust: streets, facades, signs, all blurring into watercolor edges. Everything beyond the glass looked like a scene from a film where the world no longer obeys reality but bends itself to feeling. The light fell on skin not to illuminate, but to touch.

    Inside the car, it was warm. Not from the weather—from her. From Agatha. Rio gripped the wheel a little tighter, like an anchor, something solid to hold onto when the world inside her was shifting. She wasn’t thinking about the destination—it was only an excuse. The meaning was here, beside her, in the passenger seat. Agatha was here. With her. And all Rio wanted—for the first time in far too long—was not to be impeccable. Not to impress. Not to perform composure. But simply to be. With her. Close. As she was. As she could. As she felt.

    And maybe that would be enough.

    Silence settled between them—not the tense kind, not the empty kind, but the kind you can breathe in deeply. It didn’t demand anything. It didn’t press. It simply existed—like a soft blanket, or a song not yet playing but already known.

    Agatha kept her eyes on the road. Her profile was clean, almost sculptural, as if someone had spent years shaping it from light and shadow. A streetlight slid across her cheek and moved on, leaving behind the impression that even it had paused for a second too long. Rio stole a glance sideways—and just as quickly looked away, as if caught. As if the glance itself had been a touch.

    Agatha didn’t speak right away. Several seconds passed—intentional silence. She’d always known how to make silence mean something, how to use it like a prelude, not an absence but a preparation.

    “Tell me,” she said at last, her voice calm, low, her head still turned forward. “How was your day—before I, of course, rescued it with my flawless arrival?”

    The corner of Rio’s mouth lifted, that soft prick of warmth again—a joke wrapped in tenderness, and beneath it, genuine curiosity. She smiled. “My day?” Her brow arched, her grin crooked. “Chaos. In three acts. No intermission.”

    “Already intriguing,” Agatha noted, leaning back a little deeper into her seat, like someone about to listen to a favorite vinyl record—not as background noise, but to relive it, properly.

    “It started with me standing in front of my wardrobe for forty minutes like before a sacrificial altar. I’m not exaggerating. I was negotiating with my clothes—promising, threatening, praying. At one point I genuinely considered staying home naked and canceling everything.”

    Agatha gave a short, restrained laugh, almost through her nose. But there was pleasure in it, flickering at the corners of her mouth. “Negotiations with a skirt. Classic.”

    “In the end, the shorts won. Barely. A dirty victory, if I’m honest.” “So you’re wearing,” Agatha drawled, “a compromise tonight?” “Artfully disguised as boldness,” Rio said. “Plus the leather jacket—for moral armor. Purely aesthetic, of course.”

    Agatha nodded approvingly. For a moment, there was genuine admiration in her eyes—quiet, unannounced.

    “Fearless,” she said. “And very you. Go on.”

    “Then came the morning coffee. Spilled. Everywhere. Desk, tights, self- esteem. And then—Regina. With a bag of chips and the face of a theater critic. She sat on my bed like it was a three-act play and commented on every line. Especially the part where I wrestled with the tights.” “I hope she at least applauded.”

    “Oh, she did. Right on cue with the death of my dignity. I’m more afraid of her now than I am of you.”

    Agatha glanced at her over her shoulder—lazy, attentive, the kind of look given not out of curiosity but to remember.

    “I’m flattered,” she said, “and slightly concerned.”

    “After that—lecture time. One of the students spoke in the voice of a young Hugh Grant, as if advertising toothpaste. Everything polished, articulate, absolutely… sterile. I sat there staring out the window, genuinely wondering whether to jump or wait for the evening.”

    “And?”

    “Well… I chose the second option. Not without inner turmoil. But, you know, there was a woman waiting for me—one who wears suits better than any man, and three times more seductively.”

    Agatha snorted softly—no malice, just a quick, amused exhale. There was something almost girlish in it, disarmingly real.

    “Now I’m starting to suspect this whole evening is just about the burger.” Rio shrugged. “The burger’s a side quest. The main mission is to make it through the evening without embarrassing myself.”

    A pause followed—not silence exactly, but a pause in the musical sense, when the note still vibrates within, though all you can hear outside is breath. “You’re doing fine,” Agatha said, and her tone carried no trace of humor. “And you don’t have to be perfect. I’m not interested in the heroine of the evening. I like the one who argues with her skirt and curses before coffee.” Rio groaned, pressing her fingers to the bridge of her nose. “You remember all that?”

    “I’m observant,” Agatha said with a smile. “Especially when I’m trying to act composed. It distracts me.”

    “You? Nervous?” Rio squinted at her.

    Agatha leaned forward slightly, as if about to say something serious, but only smiled—softly, sincerely. “Of course. I just happen to be very good at pretending everything’s under control. Years of practice.” “And me?” Rio asked, half teasing, half challenging.

    “You glow,” Agatha said simply. “It’s charming.”

    Rio looked away, as if someone had brushed a nerve deep inside her chest. The warmth that spread through her body wasn’t embarrassment, nor shyness—it was recognition. Wordless, flagless recognition: she couldn’t pretend indifference anymore.

    She wanted to seem composed—and she did—but inside, nothing was composed at all. Her heart beat high in her throat, her breathing had gone shallow, her palms on the wheel felt almost foreign.

    “Thank you,” she said quietly, barely audible. “For not laughing.” Agatha turned toward her, unhurried. There was no irony in her eyes, no judgment—just a clear, steady warmth.

    “I laugh,” she said, “but only with you. Never at you.”

    The words landed deeper than they should have, loosening something small and clenched inside Rio. Just enough to make it tremble. The world hadn’t grown simpler—but right beside her sat someone unafraid of her sharpness, undaunted by her layers, uninterested in solving her like a puzzle. Someone who simply held the light. Someone who walked beside her. Rio smiled—unguarded, borderless.

    “Then fair warning,” she said, her voice lighter now, “our little drive-in cinema will probably have terrible sound, it might be chilly, and the blanket smells like car air freshener. But I do have chocolate, and possibly an attempt to kiss you at some especially dark and dramatic moment—for atmosphere.”

    Agatha didn’t laugh. She didn’t even smile broadly. She simply reached out and rested her hand on Rio’s knee. The touch was calm, assured, almost domestic—but a current ran through Rio all the same.

    “Then I’m glad I unfastened my seat belt,” Agatha said, her tone light but with a thread of deliberate seriousness.

    Rio let out a small, helpless laugh—the kind that eases the shoulders, unknots the breath. It sounded like her. Finally.

    “Honestly, Rio…” Agatha began, tilting her head slightly, her voice lower now, roughened by warmth, “I don’t care what the burger’s like. It could be vegetarian. It could taste like pure McDonald’s chemistry.” She paused, testing the weight of the words, then softened. “Though if you bring me a fish burger, I’ll need willpower and acting talent to survive. I might even run. But I’ll come back. I always come back.” Rio laughed again—quieter this time, but with a trace of gratitude. “The blanket’s optional,” Agatha went on, her voice dropping to a murmur. “Although… I might start feeling cold just to end up closer. I can be tragic when I want to be. Especially up close. So—don’t be surprised.” She lowered her gaze for a moment. Her fingers came to rest on Rio’s knee again—this time not as a gesture, but as a touch meant to stay. A quiet anchor. A silent I’m here.

    “I might not even remember the movie,” she said slowly. “Not because it’ll be bad, but because… when you’re beside me, everything else fades. It blurs at the edges. You become the focus, and everything else—just the background. Soft. Calm.”

    A faint breath escaped Rio. She turned her head slowly, looked at her directly. Her brows trembled ever so slightly, as if afraid of saying too much —but in that moment, she didn’t want to stay silent.

    “All right,” she whispered. “Then I’ll just be here. And… I’ll try not to ruin anything.”

    Agatha watched her for a long, unbroken moment, then said, without a trace of play: “Don’t ruin it.”

    A pause.

    “Just be.”

    Rio nodded—quietly, almost imperceptibly. Without another word, she turned the wheel to the right. The car glided off the main road and slipped down a side street where the city noise thinned out, leaving room for the evening air.

    She parked in front of a dark building with matte windows and a glowing sign—no name, just an abstract symbol, like a pause between notes. The facade was done in black and copper, sleek and understated, like a suit that spoke for itself. From inside, warm light spilled outward, not glaring but inviting.

    They stepped out of the car almost in sync, the doors closing in quiet harmony. Rio circled the hood, adjusted her jacket, caught her keys, and said over her shoulder, lightly, “By the way, the menu here’s great. The burger thing was a joke. It’s actually much more… refined inside.” Agatha walked beside her—relaxed, but with that feline elegance that always made Rio want to stumble a little. Just before the door, Agatha slowed, then stopped, turning slightly toward her. Her eyes caught the half- light, gleaming softly.

    “I still want a burger,” she said evenly. “And… not only that.” For a heartbeat, time faltered—or stopped altogether.

    Rio felt her chest tighten, not from fear, but from recognition. She knew that look. She knew exactly what Agatha wanted to do.

    So she did it first.

    Without questions. Without drama.

    Rio stepped closer and, before silence could fall between them again, leaned in and kissed her.

    Unhurried. Intentional.

    Their lips met softly, but with certainty—as if something long decided had finally reached its moment. Not a first kiss, and definitely not a last. It wasn’t for show. It wasn’t a question. It was an answer.

    Rio held herself still, but inside, everything was singing. It was the kind of moment that didn’t ask for haste. The kind that wasn’t frightening. The kind you simply wanted—because the person before you was the one with whom you didn’t need to be strong. Only alive.

    They pulled apart almost at the same time—briefly, like after a sip of wine that warms and gently clouds the mind. Agatha looked at her with the same composure, though her eyes were darker now.

    And softer.

    “Good timing,” she murmured. “Any longer, and I might’ve lost my composure right at the door.”

    Rio smiled—lightly, warmly—her fingers brushing against Agatha’s. “Trust me,” she said, “that was part of the plan.”

    They stepped inside together, as though crossing into another rhythm. Everything here sounded different: a quiet trace of jazz hovering at the edge of hearing, pools of warm light falling on tables, the walls—woven from coal and amber tones—absorbing the outside noise until it became a kind of gentle hum.

    At the entrance, a young woman was waiting for them—red-haired, her curls a halo that made her seem twice her size. She was so small she barely reached the counter, like a character out of a storybook, with wide eyes and a melodic voice.

    “Good evening,” she said with an easy, genuine smile. “Welcome.” Rio stepped forward, tilting her head slightly. “We have a reservation. Rio Vidal.”

    The hostess nodded, opening a leather-bound book. She flipped through a few lines with a finger painted deep wine-red, then looked up, smiling wider.

    “Of course. Everything’s ready. Please, follow me.”

    She moved through the room with a gliding ease, leading them between tables—past couples murmuring softly, candles flickering inside glass globes, and wineglasses catching the light, scattering it across dark linens like reflections on water.

    The deeper they went, the quieter it became.

    At last, the hostess stopped at a table tucked away in the farthest corner, where the lamplight fell in a soft golden circle across the wood.     Agatha stared at the ceiling. She couldn’t look her in the eye just yet. Her cheeks were flushed, lips parted, hair tousled beyond repair. And she was beautiful—intensely, honestly, achingly alive.

    Rio didn’t say a word. She simply leaned down and pressed a kiss to the same spot where, just a minute ago, the wildest pulse had been beating. At her throat.

    “Mine,” she whispered. Quietly. Without asking.

    And Agatha, eyes half-lidded, only exhaled: “Yes.”

She would remember the warmth of Agatha’s skin beneath her fingers, the damp trace of the kiss on her neck, and that word that had slipped out unbidden: mine.

    But that would come later.

    Her skin rippled with goosebumps—barely visible, but Rio saw them. Felt them.

    Agatha didn’t stop her. She only watched. And when the last button was done, she raised a hand, caught Rio lightly by the chin, and ran her thumb across her lower lip, as if she still hadn’t had enough.

    Then she looked her straight in the eye.

    “It was a very good date,” she said, her voice low but steady. “I’m really glad I came.”

    “I’ve always wanted to do that Titanic thing—you know, the hand on the glass, all that tragic romance.”

    Agatha smirked. “You should’ve brought a lace glove. Then it would’ve been perfect.”

    They both laughed quietly. The sound was tired and gentle, the kind that comes at the end of a long day—when it finally feels safe to exhale. A minute later, Agatha spoke again, her voice softer now. “Emma and I put the country house up for sale.”

    Rio turned to her, but didn’t interrupt.

    “This weekend…” Agatha continued, eyes fixed on the ceiling, “will be the last one we can spend there. And… I’d like you to come with me.” Silence settled between them—not heavy, just thick and comforting, like a warm blanket.

    Agatha chuckled, her smile already soft with sleep. There wasn’t a trace of tension in her face. She reached out, brushed her palm gently along Rio’s cheek. Her fingers were warm, cool at the tips. Steady.

    “Yes and no,” she said. “We could drive out Friday evening. Spend some time there—just us. You and me. Alone. And on Saturday, Nicholas, Emma, and Regina will join us.”

    “My schedule’s booked through 2040. You didn’t know?”

    Rio laughed under her breath, shaking her head.

    “That’s one hell of a guest list. But… an evening and a night with you? That sounds dangerously appealing. So yes—I’m in.”

    Before Agatha could respond, Rio leaned in and pressed a kiss to her forehead. Light as breath. The warmth lingered longer than the touch itself. “Sleep,” she whispered.

    “Why not baby?” Agatha murmured back.

    Chapter 31: Friday 2|2

    Rio wasn’t entirely sure how long it took Agatha to finish her drink and drift off to sleep. Ten minutes, maybe less. There was only the dull thud of plastic against the cup holder — and then a faint movement beside her, when Agatha, without a word, tried to make herself more comfortable, as much as the seatbelt would allow. No words. No performance. She turned toward the window, pulled her coat up to her shoulders, and tucked one hand beneath her cheek, like a schoolgirl dozing on a late-night bus ride home. It was a small, disarming gesture — so uncharacteristic that Rio almost reached out to touch her shoulder, just to make sure it was real. But she didn’t.

    Instead, she fixed her gaze on the road, catching herself wondering — what if Agatha had turned away not because it was comfortable, but because she didn’t want Rio to see her sleeping? Not out of mistrust, but simply… because she wasn’t used to it yet.

    And that was fine.

    They hadn’t shared much — in the grand scale of things. One full night. A few evenings. And one monumental argument. Not enough to feel entirely unguarded in each other’s presence, but enough to want to. To crave that ease. That kind of trust.

    Ever since she’d finally decided to buy a car, Rio had grown addicted to the feeling of the road — to the way the streetlights faded behind her, to how the music became rhythm, not background, but pulse. Even when she wasn’t behind the wheel of her own car — even when it was Agatha’s, scented faintly with foreign perfume and just a hint of lavender — it was still part of the pleasure.

She was retreating from the world — from choices, from weight, from the burden she carried like armor. Like a curse.
