    Agatha turned her head. Her gaze was direct, though not a challenge. It was a calibration. A test.

    "Thank you for not trying to climb through the back door," Agatha replied. Her tone was almost lazy, yet it carried that trademark, razor-sharp chill that made some people retreat and others freeze in admiration. "It’s remarkable how many people believe that a right to someone else's life is merely a matter of persistence."

    Silence followed. There was no smile, but none was needed. Natasha nodded again.

    The tablet lay on her lap. The recording was already underway. The space between them felt like a canvas awaiting the first stroke of a brush. For now, there was only the air.

    "Today in the studio, we have Agatha Harkness. Former Dean of Columbia University. And the widow of the novelist Rio Vidal, whose final book— published posthumously—has become one of the most widely read and discussed works of recent years."

    The phrase hung in the air. No theatrics. No finality. Just a truth heard by thousands and understood by very few.

    Natasha didn't rush to continue. She allowed the silence to settle, letting it become a participant in the conversation. Then, softly: "If you don't mind..." she leaned forward, her voice dropping an octave. "I’d like to start not with the book. But with you."

    Agatha shifted her gaze. She didn't answer immediately. The seconds stretched—not as a pause, but as a scan. She wasn't looking for a trap; she was looking for stability. She watched Natasha the way those who have been disappointed too often watch others—searching for the weight behind the words.

    "I don't like talking about myself," she finally said. Her tone wasn't defensive. It was a statement of fact, delivered like a diagnosis—a truth that required no further explanation. "And I particularly dislike being asked to do so publicly."

    "I’m not asking for a confession," Natasha said quietly. "I know my profession dictates that I should crawl into your soul and shake out all the dirty laundry, but I also know that trick won't work on you." Agatha gave a short, silent smirk—a mere ghost of a curve on her lips. It was almost an approval. Natasha continued: "You faced immense popularity following the release of your wife's book... which, as I understand it, she never intended to publish. Why did you make the decision to release it?"

    "You're right," Agatha replied. "My wife wrote it for her private library. The book contains many intimate details. Both emotional and physical." "Then why did you ignore her wishes?"

    "Because I lived with her long enough to know: she would have wanted more than just me to read it."

    "Did you expect the book to become this popular?"

    "Entirely. It’s a typical trope that hooks romance readers: love, hate, Tinder, a workplace affair, a university setting, a dash of drama, and good sex scenes. What else do you need?"

    "Did it not make you uncomfortable—the thought of a reader penetrating so deeply into your private life? Especially in those specific scenes?" "I am in my sixties," Agatha answered calmly. "I have nothing to be ashamed of. I love sex. I was good at it."

    She smirked with a self-satisfied air. Natasha smiled back. "And what about the emotions?"

    "That is more from Rio’s perspective. No one but me knows what I was actually feeling. You see everything through her eyes. But don’t take her too literally."

    "But..." Natasha glanced down at her tablet. "Chapter seventeen is written from your perspective. And chapter twenty-three is partially yours as well." Agatha nodded.

    "Yes. I wrote those chapters after her death. I don't think she would have minded. It was difficult to remember things with absolute precision. Но... plus or minus, that is how it was."

    "Will there be a second part? Told entirely from your perspective?" "No."

    "Why not?"

    "Because it isn't necessary. Because I was in love with my wife before she even realized it herself. Even before our 'fake' date."

    Agatha looked off to the side, her gaze cutting through the air as she remembered.

    "She never saw me watching her. But I didn't push. I just existed. And I waited."

    "And what about those two years apart?"

    Agatha gave a bitter, slight smirk. "Oh, I drank heavily. I wallowed in self- reproach. I wrote angry messages. One of them was half-confession, half- destruction. Fortunately, she never saw it. It would have been devastating." Natasha straightened her back, but kept her tone soft, attentive, and restrained.

    "You managed to maintain a very specific, almost intimate rhythm in those segments you wrote. They don't clash with Rio’s voice, yet they feel like a different timbre entirely."

    Agatha gave a nearly imperceptible shake of her head. Her gaze flickered to the microphone—as if, for a moment, it had become a symbol of the unwanted attention she could never truly escape.

    "I didn't want to argue with her," she said. "It was her book. I only added a frame. Context. Perhaps a final word—but not in a conversation. In a goodbye."

    Natasha exhaled slowly. She rested her fingers on the edge of her tablet, then let go.

    "You used the word 'was.' Is it difficult?"

    Agatha remained silent for several seconds. It wasn't theatrical; she simply let the silence unfold. Then, almost in a whisper, as if she were in a kitchen with two cups of tea and a silken quiet: "Speaking of her in the past tense is a specific skill. Not the kind that comes with age. It’s more like a limp. You don’t learn it; you just get used to it." Natasha looked down. The words seemed to catch somewhere between her throat and her heart. She didn't interrupt. She simply let the sentiment hang in the air—a fact that required no explanation.

    Agatha looked at her for a long time. Then, quite unexpectedly, she smirked. It was a weary, almost fond expression.

    "Forgive me. I didn't mean to be poetic. It’s simply the truth." "The truth is exactly why we are here," Natasha replied softly. "Or at least, a form of it that can survive being broadcast."

    A pause. Then: "What do you think... if she could see what has become of this book... of you... of this very conversation—what would she say?"

    Agatha leaned back into her chair again. Her gaze drifted past the spotlights and the cameras, past even Natasha herself. It was as if she were looking beyond the threshold. As if, somewhere on the other side of that screen, stood the one to whom the question truly belonged.

    "She would huff," Agatha said, her eyes narrowing slightly. "And she would ask me why on earth I’m wearing this necklace. She’d say I look far too serious. And then, if she were in a good mood, she’d add: 'You won’t tell them the whole truth anyway, Agatha. Never.'"

    "And would she be right?"

    Agatha turned her face back to Natasha. She looked at her directly. No shadows. Only age. Only honesty.

    "Of course."

    The silence returned. This time, it wasn't heavy; it was settled. Like the air after a rain. It was the silence of two people who understood that the essence wasn't in the details, but in the fact that someone had been there, and then they were gone. And that was enough to keep talking. "Do you still have the copy she held in her hands?"

    Agatha shook her head. Not as a denial, but as a gesture toward something larger.

    "We printed it together. The first version. She edited on paper. I did the layout. Then we had an argument—about the sex. She wanted to remove those scenes. I insisted she keep them. I told her that was the whole point. To which she replied: 'Then this isn't a novel anymore; it’s our exact biography.'"

    "And you won."

    Agatha smirked. Restrained. Easy.

    "As you can see. Though it cost me a week of making breakfast." Natasha laughed—quietly, but from the heart. The atmosphere shifted. It wasn't broken, just deepened. Like a change in lighting: it grew warmer. "And now," Natasha continued, as the smile faded, "I’m going to ask a question that isn't about the novel or the feelings."

    Agatha arched an eyebrow with almost mocking curiosity. "A dangerous introduction."

    "Why did you decide to speak at all?" Natasha asked softly. "Why now?" The answer didn't come immediately. Agatha rested her palms on the armrests. She looked down, then at Natasha, and finally, directly into the camera.

    "Because it’s no longer possible to talk to her. And silence is a fine thing. Но... it is like wine: not everything can withstand it."

    A pause.

    "And if something had to be said," she added, "I wanted it to be said correctly."

    At that, the studio went still once more.

    Still, the way a reader might freeze on the final page. Or a lover by a headstone. Or a woman who has finally been understood—too late, perhaps, but understood nonetheless.

    "Natasha, is there someone in your life?"

    The question wasn't a provocation. It wasn't born of curiosity, and it certainly wasn't for the sake of a soundbite. It was spoken calmly, in a slightly hollow tone—as if the question held the reflection of something much larger than just "relationship status."

    Natasha looked up, surprised but not startled. She paused. Then she nodded, as if weighing whether to speak, but deciding to proceed anyway: "Yes."

    A pause.

    "But it’s quite complicated. And... unwarranted."

    Agatha tilted her head slightly, never breaking eye contact. For a few seconds, she seemed to be checking the tonality, listening. And then, without changing her inflection, she said: "Imagine that, suddenly, this person was gone. Without preparation. No grand finale. No chance to say goodbye." She fell silent—not for dramatic effect, but because what came next required care. "What do you feel?" Natasha didn't answer right away.

    This wasn't a question one could laugh off. Nor could it be dismissed with a line from a psychology textbook. It was personal. Visceral. And it hadn't been asked of the journalist. It had been asked of the woman. Natasha’s gaze drifted to her hands. Her fingers were laced together with a tightness that betrayed her composure. She unclasped them—slowly. She took a breath. And only then did she speak.

    "Hollowness," she said, followed by a second, shuddering breath. "Hollowness and... a quiet, serrated fury. Fury that I didn't act sooner. That I left things for 'later.'"

    Agatha nodded. It was a slight, nearly imperceptible movement. There was no triumph in her gaze—only a shared, weary, hard-won understanding. She leaned back in her chair, maintaining her poise.

    "That is exactly what I felt," she said. "But I felt it for much longer than I should have."

    Natasha looked at her. For the first time, the journalist was gone. It was just one person looking at another. Seeing through them.

    "How do you live with it?"

    Agatha smirked—briefly. Without joy. Without bitterness. Simply stating a fact.

    "I wake up every morning. I brew the coffee. I curse the world and everything in it. Then I look at the pages that are left behind. And I tell myself: 'Fine. Today is another day.'"

    "Is that enough?"

    "For a life? Yes," Agatha nodded. "For love? No. Not anymore." Silence returned.

    This was worse than Natasha had expected. She hadn't anticipated that Agatha would be this raw, this devastatingly honest.

    After the funeral of the renowned novelist Rio Vidal, Agatha had vanished from the public eye. There had been no interviews. No grand statements, no public mourning, no paparazzi shots. She didn't offer commentary or fuel the media frenzy. She simply... continued to live.

    Mundanely. Restrained.

    As if half her heart hadn't been ripped out. As if she weren't the one who had held the ashes of the woman who was her everything.

    But that was exactly what everyone discussed. Because no one truly knew how she was faring.

    Natasha took a careful breath.

    "What would you say to her now?" she asked.

    Agatha didn't answer immediately. Her fingers curled slowly over the armrest, as if searching for something to anchor her to the present, to keep her from sliding back—into the memory.

    "I would..." her voice cracked almost instantly. She didn't correct it. She just kept going. "I would ask for her forgiveness."

    Natasha leaned forward slightly. She didn't intervene; she offered her support through her gaze, not through a question.

    "For what?" she asked softly, nearly a whisper.

    Agatha looked past the studio lights. It was as if, in that glare, she could see the face that was no longer there.

    "For the fact that I am still waiting for something."

    A pause.

    She didn't explain herself right away. She didn't elaborate. She simply left the thought hanging between them. Only when it became clear that Natasha wouldn't interrupt did she continue.

    "I catch myself doing it every day. I wait for her to leave a coffee mug on the windowsill. For her to forget her phone in the bathroom. I wait for her to call out from another room just because she wants me to come over 'for no reason.' I wait for her voice. Her irritation. Her laughter. I wait, even though I know she isn't coming back. And I am ashamed of it. Ashamed, because of her. I should be strong. I should be worthy of her. But instead... I am standing still. Like a stray dog left at a train station. Sitting there, waiting, because 'maybe.' Just maybe."

    The silence in the studio was different now. It was warm. Alive. Not hollow or heavy, but careful. Almost tender.

    "Sometimes I think I’m starting to manage. That I’ve almost learned how to breathe without her. And then... someone says her name. Or I see a shirt just like hers. Or I hear someone laugh the way she did. And it all comes rushing back. And I ask for her forgiveness again. Because I shouldn't have forgotten. Because I can't let go."

    She paused. Deeply. And in that pause, there was more love than any novel could ever contain.

    "I would tell her: I am not coping. And I’m not sure I ever will. Но if you can hear this—stay for just a moment longer. Just be near me. I still love you."

    Everything went still.

    No one dared to move. Not the crew, not the cameraman, not the assistant at the monitor. The studio lights—even and soft—seemed prayerfully motionless. Natasha held her breath. The cameras were still rolling, but even they seemed to be listening. This was a sensation.

    Agatha ran her fingers over the lapel mic. She looked directly into the lens. Calmly. Honestly. With a power that was almost cruel.

    "So, there is my answer to Rio’s book. She showed you what she felt at the very beginning of our romance. And I have shown you how I live in the aftermath. This is my final word. Thank you for the invitation. But from here on out—no comment."

    She rose. Unhurriedly, without unnecessary theater. Her fingers unclipped the mic from her blouse. Her phone went into her hand; her glasses moved to the bridge of her nose. She surveyed the crew—professionally, but with an underlying request: Understand this correctly.

    "Thank you, everyone," she said, her voice steady. "And please... try not to turn this into some distorted, dramatic spectacle."

    She turned to Natasha and gave a short nod. It was a restrained but sincere gesture—not an act of politeness, but a mark of respect. Nothing more was needed. Agatha walked toward the exit.

    The hallway outside the studio was half-submerged in shadow. The light was dim, the equipment powered down, and her footsteps echoed hollowly against the smooth floor. The air was cool—it always was in these pavilions —but now it felt particularly stagnant.

    Her phone vibrated in her hand. The screen lit up: Regina. An incoming call.

    Regina was expecting Agatha today—for apple pie. After her divorce from Emma, Regina had grown close not only to Rio, but to Agatha as well. She hadn't caused scenes, she hadn't posted cryptic quotes, and she hadn't wept uncontrollably in empty rooms. But everyone who knew her understood: she needed someone near her.

    And, of course, after Rio's death, they needed each other’s company more than ever.

    Agatha stepped outside. Only then did she accept the call. She pressed the phone to her ear and looked up at the sky—a flat, blue, almost theatrical expanse.

    She had never believed in God. She hadn't stepped foot in a church for thirty years. She didn't pray. She didn't look for signs. But now—in this cool air, in this light, with the voice of her friend on the line—she allowed herself one thing. To think that maybe, just maybe, someone really was looking down on her.

    "No, Regina," she said, crossing the street. "I am not drinking your apple cider. It’s too sweet."

    A pause.

    "Yes, I’ll be there. Yes, I remember the pie."

    She smiled—only with her lips. It was nearly invisible. And yet—it was a smile.

    A few years later, Rio’s book would be adapted for the screen. Kathryn Hahn as Agatha Harkness. Aubrey Plaza as Rio Vidal. And, of course, Lana Parrilla as Regina Mills.

    The film would be released in the fall. Some would weep in the theater; others would walk out halfway through. Some would call it the greatest love story ever told, while others would dismiss it as an "overrated attempt to pull at the heartstrings." Critics would argue, TikTok would churn out reviews, and Reddit would build theories about who was truly to blame. But all of that would only concern the movie.

    Not the life.

    Not the ridiculous accident where Rio found Agatha’s Tinder profile on Jen’s phone. Not the Agatha who took the fall and resigned because of the cameras in the library. Not those five dates that never quite succeeded in the traditional sense.

    Because the real thing cannot be reshot.

    Because none of the viewers would ever know that the scene in the orange glow of the lamp—where Aubrey Plaza’s character reaches for Kathryn’s through the silence—is, in fact, an almost exact copy of that night when Rio first said: "You are not something I can just ignore."

    No one would know that the line in the book—"I think... I love you, too"— was written by Agatha herself. Two days after Rio’s death. In a steady hand. Without corrections. And with the faint traces of tears.

    But Agatha wouldn't see that scene. Just as she didn't see the closing credits.

    She would leave just as Rio did—suddenly. Without a final word. In her sleep.

    With a cup of cold tea on the nightstand. With a book whose cover had been worn down to white. And with the ring on her finger that hadn't been removed once since the day Rio, nervous as a teenager, had said: "For richer, for poorer, in sickness and in health."

    And Agatha had replied: "To love and to cherish, until death do us part."

    And now—silence.

    No theatrics. No music. Just love. A quiet, adult love, lived through to the very end.
